[PDF 3]

## Vernünfftige Gedancken Von den Absichten Der natürlichen Dinge — Reasonable Thoughts on the Purposes of Natural Things

Communicated to the Lovers of Truth by Christian Wolff,

Court Councillor to His Serene Highness of Hesse and *Mathem. & Phil. Prof. primario* at Marburg, as also *Professore honorario* at St Petersburg, Member of the Royal Society of Great Britain and of the Royal Prussian Society of Sciences.

The Second Edition.

With the Most Gracious *PRIVILEGIO* of His Royal Majesty of Poland and Electoral Highness of Saxony.

Frankfurt and Leipzig, 1726.

To be had at Renger's Bookshop.

[PDF 5] To the Well-born, Steadfast and Most Learned GENTLEMAN, Mr Wilhelm Durham, Most Trusted Privy Councillor of Justice, of the Supreme Court of *Appeal* and of the Consistory to His Royal Majesty in Prussia, and *Advocate- General*, My especially Highly Honoured Sir and Distinguished Patron.

[PDF 6] As also To the Very Reverend and Most Learned GENTLEMAN, Mr Johann Daniel Schmiedtman, *S. S. THEOLOGIÆ DOCTORI,* Most Worthy Consistorial Councillor to His Royal Majesty in Prussia, Chief Preacher at the Parish Church in Berlin, and Most Worthy Member of the British Society for the Propagation of the Faith, My especially Highly Honoured Sir and Distinguished Patron.

[PDF 7] Well-born, Very Reverend and Most Learned, Especially Highly Honoured Sirs,

There are two kinds of truths, natural and supernatural. The former we know through reason; the latter from Holy Scripture. Of the former, philosophers who have exercised their reason are fitted to judge; of the latter, however, theologians, who possess the proper sense of Scripture. Whoever would judge of both at once, [PDF 8] must be a philosopher and a theologian at once. Natural truths are not contrary to supernatural ones. For both have their origin from God, and accordingly philosophers as well as theologians have long recognised that Scripture and reason cannot contradict one another. If both are right, then the contradiction must be merely apparent, and it arises from this, that either the same words and modes of speech are taken in different senses, or one person regards the matter in a different state from the other. In that case it is a mere misunderstanding, which begets a dispute about words, [PDF 9] which among people of understanding is removed by one party explaining himself to the other. I say deliberately: among people of understanding. For the others, who do not know how to distinguish words from things, will not be set right; rather, they would force upon the others, against their will, a sense of their words which they have imputed to them. These are people of disordered senses who love the wrangling of the schools: from whom a person of understanding, who loves the truth and takes no pleasure in wrangling, should keep away. But if there is an actual contradiction between a proposition which the philosopher, and another which the theologian [PDF 10] maintains, then one of the two must be false. Either the philosopher has erred in his concepts, or been over-hasty in his proofs; or the theologian has not interpreted Scripture correctly, or else has inferred incorrectly from the words of Scripture. In this case it is incumbent upon each to investigate, in due manner, that which he maintains, to see whether the fault that they do not *harmonise* with one another lies with him or not. But whoever supposes that the fault lies with the other must show the other where he has gone wrong. For so long as this does not happen, so long does the other wish to force his opinion upon him: which in no [PDF 11] way is admissible, since neither in philosophy nor in theology can one let the matter rest on the mere *authority* of him who says it. Nor can the theologian claim any privilege over the philosopher here, such that one ought to let his *authority* count for more in this respect than the other's: for why should he have more probability on his side that he has interpreted Scripture correctly, than the philosopher that he has framed his concepts correctly; and why should he be regarded more than the philosopher as one who infers correctly, when the philosopher has even more to do with inferences than he? It is therefore incumbent upon the theologian, [PDF 12] if he would meddle in the business of the philosophers, to show wherein their concepts are incorrect and where they have gone wrong in their inferences; and in turn upon the philosopher to show wherein Scripture has been wrongly interpreted, or wrongly inferred from. And accordingly in the first case the theologian must at the same time be a philosopher, and in the other case the philosopher at the same time a theologian. Otherwise it is said of both: Do not meddle in the business of others. And *Apelles* would say to both: Cobbler, stick to your last. Now I know well that the theologians are also pastors, and that this cure of souls obliges them [PDF 13] to see that they do not let the congregation entrusted to them be taken in by errors which extinguish faith and lead to an ungodly life. But I know no less that the cure of souls has its measured bounds, which one may not extend arbitrarily at one's own pleasure. Since faith is a trust in God on account of the merit of Christ, that he will for its sake regard us as righteous and holy, a pastor likewise has no right to set himself against the philosopher until he is in a position to prove that the latter denies a proposition on which this trust [PDF 14] is founded. For were one to concede that one might also begin a dispute with him on account of those propositions from which *consequences* could be drawn that were contrary to faith, one would open the door wide to those who have a desire to quarrel, since those who are least skilled at inferring one thing from another are wont to be the most dangerous makers of *consequences*. But as regards the other point, one must make a distinction as to whether a proposition is in itself a motive to ungodliness, or whether [PDF 15] it is merely misused to that end. In the latter case, again, the pastor has nothing to say against the philosopher, any more than the latter may speak against him when his doctrines are turned to wantonness. Your Worship and Your Reverence practise this caution in judging doctrines, and for this reason His Royal Majesty in Prussia has raised you to the Consistory. And for this very reason I take the liberty of presenting the present writing to my Highly Honoured Sirs for your judgement, a writing in which I show the benefit of my [PDF 16] doctrines in the knowledge of God and for godliness, and I remain with all devotion

Your Worship's and Your Reverence's, my Highly Honoured Sirs',

*Halle, 30 Sept. 1723. most obliged servant, Christian Wolff.*

[PDF 17]

## Vorrede. — Preface.

In my Reasonable Thoughts on God, the World and the Soul of Man, and on All Things in General, I undertook in the fourth chapter a general consideration of the world, partly to the end that I might derive from it the knowledge of God, as the wise and almighty author of nature, and partly so that in the explanation of the effects of nature I might have secure grounds by which all the fictitious imaginings that hold back the truth in this matter would be set aside. For it is certain that, in the present state of man, the natural knowledge of God must be drawn from the consideration of the world, and that, for want of grounds derived from the first concepts of things, all manner of things have been assumed in physics which could neither be proved nor intelligibly explained. The latter became evident when, in the Reasonable Thoughts on the Effects of Nature, we set forth the [PDF 18] general fundamental doctrines of corporeal things; and the former we can see from the present Thoughts on the Purposes of Natural Things, and in particular from the second chapter, where I set out the principal purpose of the world. Everyone who grants that there is a God, who brought forth the world according to his free will and arranged the course of nature according to his pleasure, acknowledges that it is a mirror of his perfections, and that the wise Creator of all things brought it forth precisely to the end that men might come to know him from it as from his masterpiece. But not everyone has distinctly perceived how the divine perfections show themselves in the world as in a mirror, and in what one is properly to seek that from which one can ascend to each perfection of God. Rather, one finds a very great confusion in this respect, and one seeks to prove each perfection indiscriminately from everything. Hence it has also come about that the proofs drawn from nature that there is a God sometimes go by great detours, and not everyone is able to find his way along them. I have [PDF 19] shown in the present Thoughts what it properly is in the world that leads us to each perfection of God, and have unravelled everything that was not a little confused in this respect. And by great good fortune the general description of the world which I gave in the Metaphysics has been found to be such that in it something is met with for each perfection of God, from which that perfection may be known as a divine perfection. That same description of the world, which I have established by the grounds of reason and confirmed by experience to be in conformity with this world in which we find ourselves, is the ladder by which we can ascend to God and see him as he is, namely as a being of unlimited freedom, of infinite knowledge, of the highest wisdom, of the greatest power, of unspeakable goodness and of the strictest justice. Now since in this way that description accords with the principal purpose of the world, one may so much the less doubt whether the general doctrines of the world which I have maintained in the Metaphysics are correct or not. Least of all, however, may one presume to say that they lead away from God, [PDF 20] unless one wishes either to betray one's lack of understanding or to lay bare one's malicious disposition. I rejoiced, and publicly declared my joy, when the Reasonable Thoughts on God, the World and the Soul of Man were printed for the second time, that the truth had so soon penetrated the minds of people of understanding; but I rejoice still more now, since I have had sure proofs that people of understanding have not let themselves be led astray in the truth once known. And now it will serve to strengthen them when they see how, according to my doctrines, the world becomes so glorious a mirror of the perfections of God. And certainly this is the true touchstone by which one can tell whether the general doctrines of the world are of any use or not: that one investigates how the perfections of God show themselves according to them. Whoever is not pleased with the arrangement of the world that I have given in my Metaphysics, let him give another, and that according to all the articles found there; then we shall see how, according to him, the world will become a mirror of the divine perfections, and whether my world or his, with respect to the [PDF 21] principal purpose for which God made it, has the preference.<sup>a</sup> But whoever is not able to accomplish anything of the kind has also no right to speak to the matter; and if he presumes to do what he is not fit for, he must put up with having his judgement despised as vain and rash. Upright people strive to do better what they suppose others have not yet hit upon, and leave the judgement to people of understanding. For they love truth and desire no renown but that which grows unforced out of the truth. And I have at all times made it my concern to hold with them, and I do not yet regret that I made this resolution. Therefore one will also not find that I attack others in my writings and speak of them in a disparaging manner, much less that I load anyone with odious names by laying his errors to his charge. Indeed, if anyone disparages me or [PDF 22] despises what is mine, I pass it over in silence, and forgive him his rashness all the more readily, the less he is in a position to see into the matter and to defend his objections. In time many a one comes of himself to the knowledge that he has been on an erroneous path, and regrets that he innocently attacked, and even slandered, those who were better than he. Truth must, after all, always remain true, and has no need that one should quarrel on its account with people who are hostile to it, especially when what they bring forward consists in mere assertion, and the truth finds all the more approval precisely because they know of nothing else to bring forward against it. The present writing will speak sufficiently for me that my doctrines of the world, both the general ones in the Metaphysics and the particular ones in the Physics, are very much in the interest of the honour of God. And whoever reads it through with deliberation will in particular learn to know the wisdom, power and goodness of God through manifold proofs, and will at length come so far that in everything which meets him in nature he thinks of God and delights inwardly in his unspeakable perfection. But although I [PDF 23] have principally undertaken to show how God in nature has always made one thing for the sake of the other, and at length, by means of his particular purposes, attains his ultimate principal purpose, I have nevertheless also indicated the causes of many natural events and shown how it is possible that one thing can exist for the sake of the other. Hence those also who wish to exercise themselves in the knowledge of the causes of natural events will find various things that will be of service to them. But although I have both proved in the Metaphysics in general, and set forth here in particular, that one thing always exists for the sake of the other, and that everything in nature has been connected together by the wisdom of God, I have nevertheless also shown that from this there follows no unavoidable necessity, nor any *fatality* in natural events: which I have also recently set forth in a Latin writing, with such grounds that no one has yet dared to venture upon it, much less been able to overturn it. When I speak of the benefit which we have from natural things, I have indicated it only in general, partly because the rest may be learnt by oneself [PDF 24] from experience, provided only that one has been roused to attentiveness, partly because the more exact knowledge is to be sought in the history and science of the arts, which may yet be expected in time, when the *Academies* of sciences, or, in their absence, private persons who make it their concern to promote the common good, take heed of what I have set out on this in the Ethics, and especially in the Politics. I did indeed also have a mind to set forth the benefit of the parts in the bodies of men and animals, as also in plants; but I found that this work would turn out too extensive, and have therefore reserved it for a separate work, with which I intend to conclude the writings belonging to philosophy in our mother tongue, unless an opportunity should meanwhile present itself to treat some particular matters which are not yet to be found settled by others. For in these writings, in which I explain philosophy, one finds abundant grounds from which one can derive much more particular knowledge, once one has first properly grasped them and made them thoroughly [PDF 25] familiar to oneself. The greatest part of what I have treated here I delivered some years ago in the *Lectionibus publicis*, as I also mentioned in my *Ratio Prælectionum*; and as at that time many were thereby brought to a delight in God, and I myself found no less delight in him in delivering it, so I also wish and hope that those who read with deliberation what I write, and ponder it within themselves, will likewise become partakers of this delight.

<sup>a</sup> Although my adversaries have hitherto, notwithstanding every objection made against them, ceaselessly reviled the doctrine of the world as I have treated it and *applied* it to God, yet none of them has so far ventured to produce a work of this kind; as indeed it has long been well enough known that it is impossible for them.

*Halle, 20 Sept. A. 1723.*

[PDF 26]

## Vorrede — Preface

to the Second Edition.

There has as yet been no one among the philosophers who has made it so much his concern to turn all the knowledge of philosophy into a ladder on which one may safely climb up to God, and in turn to apply the knowledge of God thereby obtained and confirmed to godliness, and this to a zealous exercise of all virtues. One need only read this book together with the Ethics, which I have published under the title of Reasonable Thoughts on the Actions of Men, and one will be sufficiently convinced of this. And for this reason I proceed with joy to the second edition of these my Thoughts on the Purposes of Natural Things, and present them to all the impartial and truth-loving as a powerful testimony against my enemies, who, in order to justify the persecution they began from *interested* motives, still continue to persuade others [PDF 27] that the reader is led away by my doctrines from God, from virtue and from all honesty. I very greatly regret the offence that is thereby given to minds prejudiced against religion. I can, however, afford them no better means against this scandal than that, together with the Metaphysics, the Ethics and the Politics, they read the present writing with deliberation: for by this they will not only be firmly grounded in natural religion, but will also be led to revealed religion in such a way that examples of this kind can no longer make any impression upon them. What the learned *theologian* who, for the information of foreigners, has vindicated my innocence and palpably shown the mischief of my adversaries<sup>a</sup> confesses of himself, namely that, after he had read my writings, he became stronger in the truth, more apt for everything [PDF 28] that a scholar can learn, more equitable towards the authorities and more ardent towards God, that everyone will experience in himself who goes through my writings with deliberation. How this may be accomplished, however, I have taught at length, with regard to the different parts of philosophy, in the recently published Account of my Writings. And from this very confidence I still let my writings speak against the imputations and slanders of my adversaries, warmed up anew so many times. Now although foreigners who are not masters of the German language cannot in this way properly know where they stand, since one would persuade them that Messrs **Bilfinger** and **Thümmig** do not present the matter according to my meaning, which I must nevertheless acknowledge that they do, and here publicly confess it; yet my Latin writings too will soon speak for me among them, and they will then see for themselves how sincerely Mr **Bilfinger** defends my cause and how well Mr **Thümmig** has hit my meaning. But they may then also judge of the sincerity of those who would persuade them otherwise, and consider for themselves what necessity drove them to so empty [PDF 29] a pretence. I have now, God be praised! prevailed through the best approval, and proceed with joy to the Latin works, so that thorough knowledge may be spread still further. No one has yet opposed me but has laid bare his lack of understanding and his partiality to people of understanding and to the impartial. No one has hitherto been able to point out a single proposition that one could with any ground of truth hold to be offensive. One has had to make shift either with mere words, with distortion or with groundless *consequences*, and, since one has now for so long a time searched through my writings anxiously and with the greatest labour and yet been able to find nothing, one must keep making a new sauce for the first dish, because it will go down the throat of no one who has had a taste of it. I thank God! who has hitherto most powerfully supported my undertaking to gather together the truth that is in the world, to show its ground, to increase it with truth as yet unknown, and to apply it to the benefit of the human race, notwithstanding that heaven and hell have been moved against it to thwart it, because men love darkness more than light, so that one may pretend to great wisdom in ignorance and, under an outward show of [PDF 30] piety, to great holiness, and set oneself in esteem with others to one's own advantage. Therefore, the more I am belittled and slandered, the more zealous I shall become in my undertaking, and those who love the truth shall soon see a proof of this. For this reason, too, I have begun to augment my writings with more marginal notes, so that one may see the better what is to be sought in them, and may apply them to greater benefit. And this very thing has been done in the new edition of the present one, which has at the same time been revised anew and here and there augmented with some additions. God grant that everyone who reads this book may thereby receive the gift of thinking of him in everything that he beholds in the world, and of thereby constantly assuring himself anew of his perfection.

<sup>a</sup> *In Commentat. de Deo, mundo, homine & fato sect. 2. §. 22. p. 93. Deum sincere veneror, nilque magis, quam peccata mea detestor, nec quidem Wolfium novi, vel unquam vidi; id tamen non possum non testari, me legendis Wolfianis scriptis euasisse robustiorem in veritate, aptiorem ad quævis studiorum genera, æquiorem in magistratus verendos ac ardentiorem in Deum. Nemini heic palpum facio, vel contradico de industria, sed eorum, quæ heic scribo, rationem me Deo Sanctissimo esse redditurum sancte & prudenter scio & perpendo.*

*Marburg, 6 Sept. 1726.*

[p. 1]

## Vernünfftige Gedancken von den Absichten der natürlichen Dinge. — Reasonable Thoughts on the Purposes of Natural Things.

## Der Erste Theil. Von den Absichten der Welt und der Welt-Cörper. — Part One. On the Purposes of the World and of the Cosmic Bodies.

### Das I. Capitel. Von den Absichten der natürlichen Dinge überhaupt. — Chapter I. On the Purposes of Natural Things in General.

*What we understand by the purpose of natural things.*

§ 1. Just as the purpose in general is the name given to all that which a rational and free being intends to obtain by that which it wills or desires (§ 910 *Met.*), so we too can understand by the purpose of natural things nothing [p. 2] other than that which God intended to obtain by them, in resolving to bring them forth.

*That such purposes exist.*

§ 2. And in this sense I have already proved elsewhere that the principal purpose of the world is the manifestation of the glory of God, that is, that God resolved to bring forth the world, and also actually brought it forth according to his decree, to the end that one might come to know his perfections from it (§ 1045 *Met.*). Indeed, in this very same sense it has been proved against *Spinoza* and his followers that everything which follows from the essence of things is a purpose of God, which he sought to attain thereby, and that he brought forth these things precisely in order that he might attain it (§ 1028, 1032 *Met.*); in such a way that even fortunate and unfortunate accidents, which with regard to God do not happen by chance but, as much as everything else, according to his decree (§ 1003 *Met.*), are to be counted among them (§ 1030 *Met.*), just as, no less, the benefit of the works of art is to be regarded as a purpose of God in nature (§ 1031 *Met.*).

*The present undertaking.*

*First part of the same.*

*Second part.*

§ 3. And from this one sees the whole constitution of our present undertaking. For since we wish to explain the purposes [p. 3] of natural things, we must above all show that the world is so arranged that one finds in it clear and distinct grounds from which one can infer God's perfections and thereby attain some concept of them, so far, namely, as it is possible for our understanding to reach a concept of what is in itself infinite. After that we must investigate how one thing in the world always exists for the sake of another, so that we may learn to comprehend what use one thing in the world is to another, and why each thing happens.

*First benefit of this knowledge: the confirmation of the doctrines concerning God.*

*Means of grounding oneself in the knowledge of God.*

§ 4. Since we find natural things so constituted that we can infer from them, as from infallible grounds, the perfections of God (§ 3), they serve us as proofs of what we have either come to know from natural grounds (ch. 6 *Met.*) concerning the attributes of God and otherwise, or have learned of it from Scripture; and we are thereby made all the more certain that both kinds of knowledge are truth, and in particular that we have inferred correctly in the natural knowledge of God, just as in arithmetic we are made certain that the sum is correct and that we have made no mistake in calculating when the check we have carried out agrees with the *facit*. And thus [p. 4] we are firmly grounded in the knowledge of God.

*The second benefit: in the explanation of the attributes of God.*

*How the possibility of the concepts of God's attributes is known.*

§ 5. Again, since in the present investigation we obtain a concept of the divine perfections, so far as it is possible for our understanding to reach some concept of what is infinite (§ 3), this investigation makes God's attributes more intelligible to us, and in this way we perceive his invisible being from his works. And since we otherwise obtain the concepts of the essence and the attributes of God by considering our own essence and attributes and leaving out their limitations (§ 1076 *Met.*), consequently by *determining* in another manner that which is *determined* in a certain manner in our soul (§ 30, ch. 1 *Log.*), we here come to know from experience that the concepts which we have of the divine attributes are possible (§ 34, ch. 1 *Log.*), and accordingly contain nothing contradictory in themselves (§ 12 *Met.*), and consequently are no empty sound (§ 4, ch. 2 *Log.*). But in experiencing the correctness of our concepts of the attributes of God, we taste them and see them with our eyes, indeed we grasp them, as it were, with our hands (§ 1, ch. 5 *Log.* and § 220, 221 *Met.*).

*The third benefit: in attaining certainty in the knowledge of God, and in promoting the honour of God, virtue and our happiness.*

[p. 5] § 6. We therefore know from experience that the concepts which we have of the divine attributes are possible (§ 5), and that the judgements that such attributes belong to God, or to the author of nature (§ 945 *Met.*), actually hold thus and not otherwise (§ 4). In this way we attain, through the knowledge of the purposes in nature, to certainty in the knowledge of God (§ 389 *Met.*), and are thus convinced of it (§ 1, ch. 13 *Log.*), whereby our knowledge becomes living (§ 169 *Mor.*) and thereby a means of honouring God and promoting his honour (§ 658 *Mor.*), the practice of virtue and the avoidance of vice are made easier (§ 656 *Mor.*), and consequently our happiness is promoted (§ 660 *Mor.*).

*The fourth benefit: in the use of natural things,*

*and in attaining dominion over them, and also the advancement of the arts.*

§ 7. When we know how in the world one thing exists for the sake of another (§ 3), and thus regard the one as the means by which the other is obtained (§ 1033 *Met.*), we learn what is necessary in the world in order that this or that may happen, and consequently how we can use natural things for the attainment of our purposes. Now since in this way we are in a position to have natural things perform at our pleasure whatever we find necessary and would gladly [p. 6] see accomplished, we become masters of them (§ 162 *Polit.*). The more, therefore, we come to know the purpose of natural things, the more our dominion over them increases. We are then in a position to bring into actuality much in nature which nature would otherwise not attain. And in this way the advancement of the arts is at the same time promoted (§ 366 *Mor.*).

### Das II. Capitel. Von der Haupt-Absicht der Welt. — Chapter II. On the Principal Purpose of the World.

*How the world must be arranged with respect to the principal purpose.*

§ 8. The principal purpose of the world is this, that we should come to know God's perfection from it (§ 1045 *Met.*). Now if God willed to attain this purpose, he must also have arranged the world in such a way that a rational being, from the contemplation of it, can draw grounds from which his attributes, and whatever else can be known of him, may be inferred with certainty. Were it arranged otherwise than thus, it would not be possible for one to behold his invisible being, and what lies hidden [p. 7] therein, in the world as in a mirror.

*How it is possible to know from the world that there is a God.*

*Why from a necessary order one cannot infer someone who made it.*

*Why the contingency of the world leads to the existence of God.*

*Why the Author proved it in this way.*

§ 9. If the world were necessary, we could no longer come to know from it that there is a God, that is, a being distinct from it in which the ground of its actuality is to be found (§ 945 *Met.*). For although everything in it were ever so orderly, one could nevertheless not know from the order that it had sprung from another thing, since in that case this order too would be necessary and one would thus need no one to put the world into it. To one who understands mathematics the matter can be made more distinct and comprehensible without trouble. There we meet with the most beautiful order, in which the numbers proceed to infinity. But one cannot on that account infer from it that there is someone who has placed the numbers in such an order. Their order is absolutely necessary and cannot be otherwise: it can be comprehended from the nature of numbers, and one thus finds in the thing itself a sufficient reason for it; consequently one need not seek any further reason outside it. Take, for example, the order in which the *powers* of numbers proceed, on account of which a general concept of their generation can also be [p. 8] formed (§ 91 *Algebr.*): take the infinite series which Mr von Leibniz has given for the circle and which proceeds in the most beautiful order (§ 163 *Geom.*), as Mr von Leibniz himself has shown more fully than the first glance reveals<sup>a</sup>. Notwithstanding this, however, it flows from necessary grounds of mathematics (§ 104 *Analys. inf.*) and needs no further ground if one wishes to comprehend why it is so. Of other examples I will not now make mention. The world is a series of changeable things which exist alongside one another and follow upon one another, but are all together connected with one another (§ 544 *Met.*); and the connection consists in this, that each of them contains in itself the ground why the other exists alongside it at the same time, or has followed upon it (§ 545 *Met.*). Mr von Leibniz's series for the circle consists of many terms which follow upon one another, and the preceding one contains in itself a ground why the other next to it is of the magnitude it is and has the sign it has, as people of understanding will very easily perceive for themselves. Therefore between the world and such an [p. 9] infinite series of numbers we find a similarity (§ 19 *Met.*). And accordingly one need only, in what has been said of the Leibnizian series of numbers for the circle, put in place of the terms the bodies of which the world consists; then what was said before of the order in this series is at once understood of the world, provided one assumes that the world is necessary in itself. But so that no difficulty may be made in the matter, I must explain myself more distinctly as to what the necessity of the world signifies. We would, namely, say that it was necessary if space and time could not be filled in yet another way than that in which they are filled. For if it were merely possible that there could be such kinds of corporeal things as we meet with in the world, and no others, and that they could merely be placed alongside one another in such a way as we find them in the present space, then we would need no further ground why this world existed and no other, or why corporeal things were ordered thus and not otherwise (§ 544 *Met.*). On the other hand, if space and time can be filled in yet other ways than we find in this world, that is, if yet other kinds of world are possible (§ 553 [p. 10] *Met.*), then one finds in the world no sufficient reason why it is thus and not otherwise, and accordingly one must seek it outside the world (§ 30 *Met.*). In this way the contingency of the world (§ 175 *Met.*) makes it fit for one to know from it that there is a God, that is, that it has an author and is not of itself. And for this reason we also chose this way when we set out to prove that there is a God (§ 939 *Met.*), since we proved that more than one world is possible (§ 569, 570 *Met.*), but that time and space are by no means necessarily filled in such a way as we find them filled.

<sup>a</sup> *in Actis Erud. A. 1682. p. 44. & seqq.*

*An objection is answered.*

*Whether one can know the contingency of a world from its possibility.*

*The Author does not love needless dispute.*

*The Author is glad when he can learn from others.*

§ 10. Perhaps someone might object that, even if the world were necessary with respect to time and space, that is, with respect to the order in which corporeal things are to be met with alongside one another (§ 46 *Met.*) and in which they follow upon one another (§ 94 *Met.*), this would nevertheless be merely a necessity of its essence (§ 552 *Met.*), which remains even if many other worlds could exist in its place (§ 38 *Met.*). Even if only one world were possible, it could for all that still be contingent with respect to its actuality, in which alone, in any case, [p. 11] one has to seek contingency (§ 576 *Met.*). It would not, after all, be necessary on that account that the world actually existed, even if it were the only one; and hence one would still have to seek a being in which a sufficient reason could be found why it actually existed. I find nothing incorrect in this: only I do not see how one would then prove that the world, in which everything is absolutely necessary, need not also have actually existed, or, what amounts to the same, that no world at all could have been actual, so long as one does not yet know that there is a God who freely brought it into actuality (§ 980 *Met.*), but is rather first to arrive from its actuality at the actuality of God. But it is not necessary for us to enter into lengthy disputes with anyone on this account: for since we know that more than one world is possible (§ 569 *Met.*), we need not ask what would be the case if only one were possible. The whole dispute that one might wish to begin over this would serve only to pass the time. Indeed, if anyone can show that the world is able, through something other than its contingency, to lead us to an unshakeable knowledge of the actuality of God, [p. 12] then I am not opposed to it: for although I cannot go further here than my insight permits me, it has nevertheless never displeased me when another sees something that I have overlooked, provided only that he has seen rightly, or has been able to show it to me so that I have seen it myself as well; for I am not of the sort who, to please anyone, says that I see what I nevertheless do not see. For I seek truth and desire to know it as truth.

*How the world can be a mirror of God's free will.*

*Difficulties if one is to infer God's free will from the possibility of a world.*

§ 11. The very contingency of the world makes it a mirror of the freedom of the divine will. Where many things of one kind are present, from which one can choose the one that pleases one most, and nothing is present that would compel one, against one's will, to take what pleases one less than one of the others, there the choice indicates most distinctly the freedom of the will (§ 519 *Met.*). More than one world is possible (§ 569, 570 *Met.*), and even if not everyone comprehends that only a single one actually exists (§ 948, 949 *Met.*), he nevertheless sees that this space could at this time be filled otherwise, and that other changes could be brought about in it than are now found there. Now since God is *independent* of all things outside him (§ 938 *Met.*) [p. 13] and therefore nothing is present that could have compelled him to fill this space at this time in this way rather than in another, the free choice is certainly shown thereby, and the world is for this reason a mirror of the freedom of the divine will, since space contains nothing in itself on account of which this body rather than another fills it, nor time on account of which it is devoted to this change rather than to another, inasmuch as space considered in itself, just like time, shows in a uniform representation not the least difference in its parts. It is indeed true that the freedom of God could still subsist even if no more than one world were possible, in so far, namely, as it stood free to him either to bring it into actuality or to leave it in the mere state of possibility and to allot it no further place than in his understanding. But here again it would be very difficult to perceive this from the consideration of the world. For here again it would depend on one's being able to know from it that its actuality was not necessary, and also that nothing is found in God that compelled him to produce a world. But since the proposition that no more than this single world is possible is incorrect, it is [p. 14] once again a futile dispute whether in that case the world would still remain a manifest mirror of the freedom of the divine will.

*Admonition.*

*What is required to understand the Author's proof.*

*How the concept of the actuality of the contingent is attained.*

*Whence the possibility of many worlds becomes evident.*

*Who understands the writings of the Author.*

§ 12. No one can doubt the proof which I have given (§ 569 *Met.*) that more than one world is possible, except someone who is either too weak to see into it, or will not take the trouble to read through my book deliberately in its complete order and to weigh well what is contained in it, and also to allow time the course it must have if concepts to which one is not yet accustomed are to become clear in the understanding. The proof is not grounded in the simile of the clock, which I adduce merely by way of illustration (§ 569 *Met.*), but rather in the concept of the actuality of contingent things, which I have set out distinctly (§ 579 *Met.*), after I had first proved at length everything that is required for it (§ 565 ff. *Met.*). Whoever rightly sees into my doctrines as I have presented them knows that everything that actually exists is individual things; that individual things can have nothing *undetermined* in them (§ 180 *Met.*); that the various species and genera arise when things [p. 15] are *determined* in the same way in certain respects, while the rest in them can be *determined* in different ways (§ 179, 181 *Met.*); that, if something is actually to be *determined*, a cause must be present through which this happens (§ 29, 30 *Met.*); that in nature, in which nothing happens by a leap (§ 686 *Met.*), not everything is *determined* at once, but little by little; that no complete *determination* takes place in things that are contingent except through a series of innumerable things that follow upon one another (§ 579 *Met.*); that finally, when everything is *determined*, nothing more is present that could prevent the thing from presenting itself, and in this way its possibility obtains its complete fulfilment: in which we have accordingly established the definition of actuality (§ 14 *Met.*). These are the grounds through which the proof of the possibility of innumerable worlds finally unfolds in the thoughts of those who read my writing in the manner I described above. I know well that what I have written here will not be clear and distinct to everyone who reads it; but neither do I give it out as such, except merely for those who have *treated* my Metaphysics in [p. 16] such a manner as they must *treat Euclid's Elements*, if they would comprehend and digest everything contained therein. I name this touchstone with good deliberation, so that no one may be over- hasty and have cause to be angry with me when he has exposed his weakness.

*How the world is a mirror of the infinite knowledge of God.*

*1. By the multitude of things.*

*2. By the uni-*

*versal harmony.*

*3. The actuality of the contingent.*

*How the infinity of God's understanding becomes comprehensible.*

§ 13. The more things are met with in a world, and the more changes show themselves in it, the greater the concept we obtain of the knowledge of God, since God knows the world entire, down to its smallest parts (§ 964 *Met.*). Now since space is infinitely filled both in the small (§ 84, 85 *Met.*) and in the great (§ 180 *Phys.*), and time can be divided as finely (§ 692 *Met.*) as matter can ever be divided further (§ 3 *Phys.*), the world thereby becomes a mirror of the infinite knowledge of God, and by the contemplation of it one begins to taste the infinity of his knowledge. This concept, however, becomes much clearer still when we consider that more than one world, indeed that innumerably many worlds are possible, all of which God comprehends in complete distinctness (§ 952 *Met.*). But as the magnitude of the knowledge which a world can afford can reach the greatest degree, in such a way that it [p. 17] cannot possibly be increased further, if each thing in it, even the very smallest, relates in a particular manner to all the others; so the universal harmony of things (§ 600 *Met.*) first makes the world a true mirror of the infinite knowledge of God. No less, however, does what we have adduced concerning the actuality of contingent things (§ 579 *Met.*) contribute to this, since it is evident precisely from this that God, in the actuality of each thing, beholds the whole interconnection of the things that belong in one series, that is, the whole world (§ 544 *Met.*): which is the finest thing that we know of the divine understanding through reason, although it was not sufficiently perceived hitherto, before I proved it distinctly (§ 964 *Met.*). By this the knowledge of God is extended in an incomprehensible manner, and yet we understand something of this extension, and the riches of God's knowledge do not remain merely a thing which we admire out of ignorance, on account of the weakness of our understanding. We taste and see the infinity of these riches (§ 5) and know quite properly from their constitution why we are not capable of comprehending them fully (§ 77 *Met.*).

*Why the world is a mirror of the wisdom of God.*

*What is properly required for this.*

*How the connection of things in the world teaches the wisdom*

*of God.*

*How it shows the magnitude of God's wisdom.*

*It is confirmed by the contrary.*

*Ground of the magnitude and perfection of God's wisdom in the world.*

*When the greatest wisdom of God shows itself in the*

*world that can find place in it.*

[p. 18] § 14. Wisdom is a science of so arranging purposes that one becomes a means to the other, and in turn of choosing such means as lead us to our purposes (§ 914 *Met.*), and that without detours, by the shortest way that can be had (§ 918 *Met.*), and completely, not merely in part (§ 917 *Met.*). If, now, the world is to become a mirror of the wisdom of God, we must meet with divine purposes in it and perceive means by which he attains these his purposes. We must find, upon investigation, that when something happens in the world, and it could be brought about in more than one way, it comes to pass in that way by which one arrives at it without detours. Indeed, there would have to appear such an agreement of the means and the purposes that neither more nor less is produced by the means than the purpose requires, nor yet, among the various purposes, is one contrary to another. I have proved that in the world everything is connected with everything else according to time and space (§ 548 *Met.*), and that each thing has in itself a sufficient reason why the other exists alongside it at the same time (§ 546 *Met.*) or follows after it (§ 547 *Met.*). And [p. 19] this is the cause why one thing can become a purpose and the other a means (§ 910, 912 *Met.*). Now if this connection runs through the whole of space and through the whole of time, then one purpose can always be made the means to a further one. Indeed, if that which happens now is *determined* by all that has gone before (§ 579 *Met.*), then everything preceding becomes a means to the following purpose (§§  *cit. Met.*). And accordingly the connection of things in the world with one another makes the world a mirror of his wisdom, and at the same time makes known the magnitude of his wisdom through the multitude of things connected with one another (§ 916 *Met.*), so that here once more, as before with his knowledge (§ 154), we learn to understand its infinity and begin to taste and see it. This connection of things in a world according to space and time is therefore a glorious attribute of it, since it makes it so excellent a mirror of one of the most glorious attributes of God. Suppose that not everything in the world were connected with everything else according to space and time, but that things were rather separated from one another, so that each thing would no longer [p. 20] relate to everything, then not only does the universal harmony of things vanish (§ 600 *Met.*), but many purposes in the world are also lost, and many means which are otherwise to be found in it (§ 910, 912 *Met.*). Indeed, the purposes are then no longer all connected with one another, as before, so that one always becomes a means of another. And in this way it is clear that there no longer remains so much of God's wisdom in the world, nor is so great a divine wisdom met with in it, as before; for the more purposes are attained by suitable means, the more effects of wisdom are present (914 *Met.*), and the more purposes are so connected with one another that one always becomes a means of another, the more perfect is the wisdom (§ 916 *Met.*). But if now all the things of the world are so connected with one another that we can always regard one as a purpose of another, and the other as a means of attaining that purpose, and in this way, with respect both to space and to time, one thing always exists for the sake of the other, then all the wisdom is to be met with in the world that could have found place in it, and not the least remains wherein a further proof of wisdom could have been [p. 21] given. Indeed, it is not possible to invent a world in which more wisdom should be found, or which would have been ordered with greater wisdom. And accordingly the connection of all things with one another in the world makes it such a mirror of the divine wisdom that one clearly beholds in it that God displays in his works the very greatest wisdom that is possible; indeed, it at the same time makes an infinite knowledge the source of his wisdom (§ 13).

*An objection is met.*

*Who they are that, over the connection of things,*

*raise difficulties.*

*Whether the world acquires an unavoidable necessity through the connection of things.*

*How there is none with respect to God.*

*Nor is any to be found in the events of nature.*

*Why things must come about as God has ordained.*

*What the necessity of nature is.*

*That such a necessity is also found in what we have resolved upon.*

*How it cannot possibly be absent from the world.*

*Poor reflection of the opponents.*

*Whether God has ordained everything from eternity.*

§ 15. One would not suppose that anyone who grants a God of infinite knowledge and unsearchable wisdom could be displeased with such a picture of the world; much less should one believe that anyone would judge of him who drew it that he was contrary to the honour of God, indeed that by his world men would be led away altogether from the knowledge of God. And in fact it is also hard to believe that anyone who has rightly looked at this picture and knows it could fall into such thoughts and say so with sincerity. Indeed, if we also attend to experience as to who they are that form such strange thoughts about this, we find that they are people who, in their judgements, [p. 22] are over-hasty and reject the matter before they have rightly looked at it and come to know it, either from a prejudice which they have once conceived, or perhaps from evil *affections*, which either blind them so that they do not see the truth, or else lead them to censure, against their conscience, what they themselves know to be good. For those who are not pleased with this excellent picture of the world, through which it becomes the true masterpiece of an artist endowed with infinite understanding and perfect wisdom, pretend, and would persuade others, that by it an unavoidable necessity is introduced into all things; from which each then infers further whatever either his memory suggests to him or his manner of reasoning puts into his hands. But whoever looks at the matter with right eyes cannot perceive at all whence the necessity is to come which is given out as unavoidable. For, in the first place, there is no unavoidable necessity present with respect to God; since I maintain that corporeal things can be connected with one another according to time and space in innumerably many other ways than they are connected with one another in this world (§ 569 *Met.*), and hence there was no inner [p. 23] constraint in God obliging him to take the present connection of things; rather, he chose it freely. Thus it has come into actuality through no necessity but through a free choice, and is therefore contingent in its actuality, since it could also have been otherwise. Next, there is also no unavoidable necessity in the events of nature on account of all things being connected with one another through a free decree of God. For this indicates nothing more than that the means which God ordains for attaining his purposes suffice for them. But by the fact that means have been chosen by which one attains one's purpose completely and certainly, the matter does not become one of unavoidable necessity. Admittedly, however, it cannot happen that things should not come about as God has ordained beforehand; for this would be just as much as if he had chosen means that did not lead him to his end, as is wont to happen with us men, that our counsel miscarries because we have chosen means by which the proposed goal cannot be attained. And this certainty, which is in things because God by his decree has established means [p. 24] which lead him to his purpose, is precisely what the theologians<sup>a</sup> have called *necessitatem physicam*, or the necessity of nature (§ 575 *Met.*), for whose sake we have retained this word, although I have shown that it ought rather to be called a certainty than a necessity. But a necessity of this kind is also found when we freely resolve upon something, provided only that we do not err in the choice of the means. For if no sufficient reason were contained in the means why the purpose follows from it, it would also not be possible to attain the purpose thereby. Therefore, even if one were to suppose (contrary to the reason and wisdom of God) that not all things in the world were connected with one another, yet in the separated parts of the world, too, the cause would at all times remain connected with its effect in the same manner in which we have stated everything therein to be connected with everything else, that is, there would always be something to be found in the cause from which one could understand why the effect followed from it (§ 545 *Met.*): which, moreover, no rational man has ever in all his days ventured to deny. And thus, [p. 25] with respect to the necessity of nature, it remains the same in the one case as in the other, whether the parts of the world be separated from one another or connected with one another. It is all one whether I grant something in detached parts or in the whole. It therefore betrays poor reflection when one seeks the ground of an unavoidable necessity in the connection of all things in the world with one another, especially since we admit no further connection than that which obtains between right means and purposes, and which proceeds from the wise arrangement of God, who according to his free and unconstrained will chooses the best (§ 545, 980, 985, 1026 ff., 1034, 1086 *Met.*). I should hope that no one will take offence when we maintain that God has from eternity established this connection, according to which things are to follow from one another, and that it is now not necessary that he, like a craftsman who does not himself fully know his work nor can foresee all its accidents, should alter anything in it (§ 999 *Met.*): for whoever would so far err must not yet rightly know God, nor have read what Scripture says of God's works.

<sup>a</sup> *Gerhardus in Loc. Theol. Loc. 2. §. 255. f. 316.*

*Wherein the wise connection of things is distinguished from a fatality.*

*That the rules of motion are not necessary.*

*Why there can be no unavoidable necessity in the connection of things.*

*Difference between fatality and a wise connection.*

*The Author's confidence in the just cause.*

*Why the Author writes against his custom.*

*Further difference between fatality and a wise connection.*

*Third difference.*

[p. 26] § 16. But in order that I may clear away every offence in a matter in which the honour of God (§ 653 *Mor.*) and our own satisfaction (§ 715 ff. *Mor.*) are so greatly *interested*, I further point out this, that the connection of things through the most wise decree of God is something entirely different from what has been called *fatality*. The changes of corporeal things have their ground not only in their essence (§ 614 *Met.*) but also in motion (§ 615 *Met.*), and one must look to both if one wishes to explain them intelligibly (§ 616 *Met.*). The rules of motion are grounded in maxims which have no necessity and which therefore also cannot be demonstrated geometrically: as Mr von Leibniz first remarked, and hence justly claims for himself<sup>a</sup> that he was the first to remove thoroughly the unavoidable necessity of the Spinozists. The maxim which the *mathematicians* commonly employ when they wish to demonstrate the laws of motion is this: that the action of one body upon another and the reaction of the other are at all times equal to each other, or that a body, in communicating its motion to the [p. 27] other, is pressed back by it as strongly as it presses it (§ 669 *Met.*). Mr *Newton*, excellent *mathematician* as he is in the highest degree, has<sup>b</sup> nevertheless been unable to give any *demonstration* of it, but has only assumed it as a thing grounded in experience. And in fact people have been willing to accept this proposition without much scrupling, because they saw that the rules of motion which are derived from it with the help of mathematics agree with experience, as *Mariotte*<sup>c</sup> in particular has shown. I have indeed (§ 669 *Met.*) proved it from certain grounds established beforehand, so that even those who do not themselves recognise how the rules of motion derived from it agree with experience might nevertheless be convinced of it, and so that one might also in mathematics itself make use of this proposition with greater reliability: but if one turns back to it, one will find that it has not been demonstrated entirely from necessary grounds, but [p. 28] only derived from simpler experiences. Now since the rules of motion flow from grounds of this kind, they have no unavoidable necessity (§ 36 *Met.*), and consequently no such necessity can be met with in the changes that occur in natural things either, and therefore not in their connection with one another (§ 547 *Met.*). From this, however, there now further appears a very great difference between a *fatality* and a wise connection of things with one another. For 1. where there is *fatality*, or unavoidable necessity, there things cannot be otherwise; rather, everything has had to happen just as it comes, and neither is any perfection to be met with in things nor any image of wisdom contained in the whole, as all those who maintain it concede, so that I have no need to prove it at length. On the other hand, the connection of things which I have established in my Metaphysics is free from all necessity, both with respect to the actuality of corporeal things, which rests upon God's free decree (§ 980 *Met.*), and with respect to the laws of nature, which God has chosen according to his wisdom, as follows from [p. 29] what I have just now proved. And hence not only do natural things have a perfection (§ 712 ff. *Met.*), which the defenders of a *fatality* would deny them, but the whole world taken together becomes the most excellent image of the wisdom of God (§ 14). Indeed, I do not see how anyone who wishes to maintain an unavoidable necessity can bring forward anything against the grounds we have advanced, be he as profound as ever he will, of which I would not at once show that it does not stand the test. If anyone wishes to see the proof, let him send me his supposed grounds for *fatality* in writing; I will answer them publicly and furnish the proof. But if some should also suppose that *fatality* has not yet been sufficiently distinguished from an unavoidable necessity, or that the world has not yet been represented as the most perfect image of the wisdom of God, let him work out both better from his own grounds. Then we shall *confront* the two worlds with each other and see which will better *legitimate* itself.<sup>a′</sup> But whoever does not venture to do this [p. 30] has no honour in speaking, but must by rights be despised as one who does not understand the matter. I write against my custom: for I am *interested* in the honour of God not with the mouth but in deed and in truth. There appears, however, 2. between *fatality* and the wise connection of things this further difference also, that with the former one must await the outcome as it comes, whereas with the latter the outcome follows that accords with the means one has chosen. Hence also, for example, the Turks, who, as is commonly alleged, have an unavoidable necessity in mind, remain in the plague in the hope that it will not harm them, unless they are meant to perish in it. On the other hand, whoever is assured that the connection of things is so ordered that one thing is always to be regarded as a means by which the other is attained as the purpose, also seeks the means when he desires the purpose. Finally, 3., to pass over other things in silence for the sake of brevity, one finds no tranquillity of mind in *fatality*, since it is a poor consolation that we must be precisely that creature to whom no good fortune in the world has been allotted; but one does find it in the wise connection of things with one another, [p. 31] whereby what is adverse becomes a means to what is better and a safeguard against greater evil (§ 1060 *Met.*).

<sup>a</sup> *Theodicee §. 345. p. 13. edit. sec.*

<sup>b</sup> *Princ. Phil. Nat. Math. p. 13. edit. sec.*

<sup>c</sup> *Traité de la Percussion ou Choc des Corps.*

<sup>a′</sup> From the time when this was first written, no one has yet been found who would venture to do so; instead, people have taken to slander, abuse and persecution, as a sure testimony against themselves.

*How the world becomes a mirror of the wisdom of God through the laws of motion.*

§ 17. But nature also becomes a mirror of the wisdom of God through this, that the rules of motion prescribed to it have no unavoidable necessity, since the maxims on which they are grounded are not of such necessity and cannot be perfectly demonstrated like the necessary truths (§ 16). For it is precisely from this that it comes about that the creature, by virtue of its essence, can act in accordance with the wisdom of God, since all effects take place through motion (§ 615 *Met.*), while bodies in their motion must conform to the rules which God according to his wisdom has prescribed to nature. But since no one can comprehend this truth except one who has advanced so far in mathematics that he grasps the *demonstration* of the rules of motion (§ 381 ff. *Mech. Lat.*) and is at the same time practised in the true metaphysics, I do not wish to adduce anything further about it in this place.

*How through the pre-established harmony,*

*and the universal harmony of things.*

*Mirror of the great knowledge of God.*

*How it promotes the honour of God.*

§ 18. Since by the pre-established harmony between body and soul the wisdom of God is exalted above all that can be thought (§ 1050 *Met.*), [p. 32] the world thereby becomes a mirror of the wisdom of God; and indeed, since no creature is capable of comprehending this harmony with its reason (§ 916 *Met.*), although one can understand it (§ 276 *Met.*) and know its possibility (§ 767 *Met.*), the wisdom of God is thereby represented as unutterable and unsearchable. This mirror is enlarged still further by the universal harmony of things (§ 600 *Met.*): for since, by virtue of it, the internal state of every simple thing in the world agrees at every moment with the internal state of all the rest therein, the view of the wisdom of God is here *multiplied* according to the number of the things that have been set in harmony with one another (§ 916 *Met.*). Now since matter loses itself in its parts in the infinite (§ 3 *Phys.*), the simple things, or the elements, must all the more lose themselves therein (§ 582 *Met.*). And in this way the infinity of the wisdom of God is indeed made intelligible through the universal harmony, so that we must know it as infinite. Finally, the unutterable variety of things also contributes a great deal to the wisdom of God (§ 591 *Met.*), as that by which the universal harmony becomes much greater [p. 33] (§ 600 *Met.*). And this same variety bears witness also to the great riches of God's knowledge (§ 13). In this way, both by the pre-established harmony and by the principle of indiscernibles (§ 589 *Met.*), the honour of God is mightily promoted (§ 653 *Mor.*), so that everyone who is *interested* in it must come to love this doctrine, even if he is not yet in a position fully to comprehend its correctness, unless evil passions blind him and lead him astray. And this is the very reason why I make it my concern to make these important truths known. Those who imagine that this doctrine is so dangerous labour under the false delusion that freedom is thereby abolished. But it is well enough known to those who see rightly into the matter that freedom fully subsists with it (§ 833 *Met.*)

*How the world becomes a mirror of the power of God.*

*How one knows what he can accomplish naturally.*

*How one knows the magnitude of the power.*

§ 19. Through the world's being contingent in its actuality, it becomes at the same time a mirror of the power of God. For from its contingency one knows that it has its actuality from God (§ 10), and indeed through his free will (§ 11). But in perceiving the actuality of the world as *dependent* on God (§ 938 *Met.*), one knows that God [p. 34] is able to make the possible actual, and in this way one sees his power from the world (§ 1020 *Met.*). Now whoever learns from experience what takes place in the world also comes to know all manner of things in particular that God is able to do. And since physics explains what can happen through the forces of bodies, and pneumatology what can be effected through the powers of spirits (§ 12 *Proleg. Log.*), but both come about in a natural way (§ 630, 757 *Met.*), experience, when it is held against the doctrines established in these sciences, teaches us what God can do even when he proceeds naturally and performs no miracles (§ 633 *Met.*). The magnitude of power is known partly from the magnitude and multitude of the works, and also from their variety: which I here assume, without further proof, as a thing grounded in experience. Accordingly the world bears witness to the magnitude of the power of God, not only when one looks at the size of the space which God has filled with bodies and considers the size of the cosmic bodies, but also when one descends to what is small and weighs the great variety of things that one meets with everywhere.

*How the*

*world becomes a mirror of the divine reason.*

*How one knows the magnitude of God's reason.*

*How one knows it as unfathomable.*

§ 20. Insight into the connection [p. 35] of truths is what we are accustomed to call reason (§ 368 *Met.*). Now whoever can connect things with one another through his understanding before they exist must have reason. Therefore the connection of things in the world, which comes from God's understanding (§ 975 *Met.*), makes the world a mirror of the divine reason. Now since reason is so much the greater, the more connection of truths we perceive (§ 905 *Met.*), the world also shows the magnitude of the divine reason, not only through the multitude of the things that have been connected with one another, but also through the multitude of the universal truths that agree in this connection. Therefore, since in the world everything, from the greatest down to the smallest, is connected according to time and space (§ 548 *Met.*), the world furnishes the most powerful proof of the divine reason, inasmuch as it could afford none greater. And since no being endowed with finite understanding is capable of comprehending or distinctly knowing this connection (§ 591 *Met.*), the divine reason thereby makes itself known as higher than all other reason; indeed, one beholds it as unfathomable.

*How the world becomes a mirror of the goodness of God and of his government.*

§ 21. In that God has brought those things [p. 36] into actuality which, after their kind, have the greatest perfection, of which I have given an example in the eye (§ 710 *Met.*), and of which we shall find still more in what follows, he has thereby given proofs of his goodness (§ 1062, 1063 *Met.*). The like shines forth further from this, that he has so connected the things in the world with one another that one is always conducive to another: of which we shall again meet with a great many proofs in what follows. Finally, these too are proofs of the goodness of God, that he has so bound together the events in the world with one another that even the adversity which befalls us, and the evil of men, must turn out for the best (§ 1060 *Mor.*). But if one attends to this connection of contingent events, and notes the wisdom of God therein (§ 14), the world becomes for us at the same time a mirror of his government, and whoever attends to it can, through manifold examples, arrive at general maxims according to which God governs the world, as I have also already remarked elsewhere (§ 709 *Mor.*), and therefore have no need to dwell on it any further.

*How the world becomes a mirror*

*of the justice of God.*

§ 22. But if God connects natural evil with the vices of men, and likewise fortunate and unfortunate accidents with their [p. 37] conduct in general, as they require it (§ 30, 31 *Mor.*), one learns from the events of the world that God, for all his goodness, gives away nothing of his wisdom (§ 1063, 914 *Met.*), and in this way the world becomes a mirror of his justice (§ 1084 *Met.*). Indeed, one learns from it at the same time that God takes no pleasure in man's misusing his freedom, but rather that he at the same time obliges him to a way of life conformable to the law of nature (§ 30 *Mor.*).

### Das *III.* Capitel. Von der Zahl der Welt-Cörper. — Chapter III. On the Number of the Cosmic Bodies.

*The number of the cosmic bodies shows the magnitude and immensity of the divine understanding.*

*What makes the variety in the multitude of the cosmic bodies incomprehensible.*

§ 23. Around our single sun there move, besides the earth (§ 176 *Phys.*), five further primary planets, Saturn, Jupiter, Mars (§ 173 *Phys.*), Venus and Mercury (§ 171 *Phys.*), and over and above these five secondary planets, namely one moon with our earth (§ 172 *Phys.*), four with Jupiter and five with Saturn (§ 107 *Phys.*). The number of the fixed stars that one can see with the naked eye extends to about [p. 38] 2000, and perhaps, when someone sharp-sighted comes to it, still higher; but through telescopes there appears so great a number that it is not possible to bring them all into a catalogue (§ 108 *Phys.*). All are nothing but suns (§ 154 *Phys.*), and without doubt also have their planets about them, like our sun (§ 155 *Phys.*), so that the number of the planets is far greater still than the number of the fixed stars, although on account of their feeble light one cannot see them as one sees the latter. Accordingly one finds an inexpressible number of cosmic bodies, of which the edifice of the world is composed. Now since, further, there cannot be two things in the world that would be similar to one another (§ 587 *Met.*), each cosmic body must also be different from the other: indeed, everything that is found in the space which they fill must be different in each, down to the smallest, indeed down to the very last (§ 586 *Met.*). And because this very thing is true also of the changes with respect to time (§ 586, 587 *Met.*), what is changeable that occurs in one cosmic body must also be different from what is changeable that takes place in another. Namely, in everything some difference is present, although there is also much along with it which remains the same: for here we are speaking only of a perfect [p. 39] similarity, since an incomplete similarity ceases to be one in so far as a difference is to be found. In this way an incomprehensible variety shows itself in the inexpressible multitude of the cosmic bodies. God beholds all this at once in complete distinctness (§ 955, 963 *Met.*), and knows the whole world in its smallest part, in such a way that the past and the future too are present to him at the same time (§ 964 *Met.*). Therefore the inexpressible number of the cosmic bodies, with their incomprehensible variety, gives a proof of the magnitude of the divine understanding, and we thereby obtain some concept of its immensity.

*The number of the cosmic bodies also shows the magnitude and immensity of the divine power, and its particular kind.*

*Difference between the power of God and that of men.*

*How God's force of creating out of nothing is known.*

§ 24. The faculty of making the possible actual is power (§ 1020 *Met.*). Now since all the cosmic bodies, and what is in them, receive their actuality through God (§ 988 *Met.*), their inexpressible number shows the magnitude of the divine power. Accordingly, the more cosmic bodies one meets with in the world, the more distinct becomes the concept of the magnitude of the divine power (§ 13, *ch. 1 Log.*). If, then, we set the power of men against the power of God, in so far as we perceive the latter from the present consideration of the edifice of the world, we find that it is immeasurable. [p. 40] For where we wish to measure something, there we must have a standard of measure and seek the ratio of that which we wish to measure to it (§ 62 *Met.*). Let one here take the power of men: then it will not be able to furnish a standard of measure for the divine power. One will not be able to say how many times the power of a man must be *multiplied* so that a power results which is equal to the divine power to which the edifice of the world bears witness (§ 65 *Arithm.*). Indeed, even if one wished to *multiply* the power of a man at pleasure in order thereby to obtain a standard of measure, one would nevertheless labour in vain herein. Whoever wishes to consider this more exactly has yet two things to heed. Namely, first one must bear in mind that the power of men shows itself little by little, or through repeated action, and that one must therefore take care, in estimating a man's power, not to set it higher than is fitting. For example, whoever can carry 30 pounds can, little by little, namely in ten turns, carry 300 pounds from one place to another. But on that account his force is by no means as great as that of another who can carry 300 pounds at once. Next, one must also not forget that men often have their power [p. 41] outside themselves, and through the help of others, who must be at their bidding, accomplish what they would not be capable of accomplishing by their own force. For example, a mighty king has his power outside himself in his soldiers and his money (§ 444 *Polit.*). God brought forth the world by his own power without anyone's help (§ 938 *Met.*). Moreover, the power of God is also of an entirely different kind from ours, of which we are speaking here. For we need actually existing things when we wish to make actual something that was not before: but God brought forth the world when previously nothing actual existed out of which he could have made it. For he communicated actuality to this world when it previously was not, and when another was just as possible as it (§ 951 *Met.*). Since the world takes its origin from simple things as elements (§ 582 *Met.*), and the inner state of each is governed by the others that are around it (§ 595 *Met.*), indeed by the composite things and by the whole world (§ 596 *Met.*), the elements of things must be different if the world is different; consequently God must also have imparted their actuality to the simple things, and in this way God can make actual where nothing is present, and [p. 42] consequently his power is entirely different from the power of men. Therefore, since a ratio can be sought only between things of one kind (§ 117 *Arithm. Lat.*), human power can have no ratio to the divine, and the latter remains immeasurable (§ 62 *Met.*)

*Remark on the advantage of the Author's Metaphysics.*

*Harm of the opposing doctrines.*

§ 25. Here we have once again a proof of how conducive the grounds maintained by me in the Metaphysics are to the honour of God and to the confirmation of the common doctrine that God made the world out of nothing, that is, when previously nothing was present outside him. This latter is a necessary consequence of the two grounds that the elements of corporeal things are simple, each of which is constantly different from the rest in its inner state, and that more than one world is possible, God however having chosen this one out of innumerable ones according to his good pleasure. Whoever abolishes the difference of the elements and assumes only one world as possible may see whether he can prove from this that God made the world out of nothing and that he has a power to bring forth what is possible without anything having to be actual beforehand which he would have to use in order to make the other actual, as we need to. *Epicurus*, who had other grounds, opposed to ours, therefore maintained the [p. 43] eternity of nature and *fatality*, as may be seen from *Lucretius*.

*The number of the cosmic bodies proves the fullness of God's goodness.*

*How one knows the good that God shows to each creature.*

§ 26. God shows to each thing as much good as is possible (§ 1062 *Met.*), and accordingly his goodness increases with their number (§ 1063 *Met.*). Therefore, since this number is inexpressible (§ 23), it also shows an inexpressible wealth of the goodness of God. If one wishes in particular to know the good that God shows to each kind of creature, one must not only consider the perfection of its essence (§ 707 *Met.*), but also heed the general maxims that can be observed in the government of God, and in particular compare with each other what now follows and what would come about hereafter if these were to be changed. But this elaboration does not belong in this place, and one thing and another will present itself when we come to the particular kinds of bodies with which our earth is adorned.

*Likewise the immensity of his presence*

§ 27. Again, since God continually goes on communicating actuality to the things in the world (§ 1054 *Met.*) and is accordingly present to all things, this same inexpressible number of the cosmic bodies shows the immensity of the divine presence. If we consider the things to which we are present at one time, [p. 44] it can be placed in no comparison whatever with the things to which God is present, and thus no measurability has place here (§ 62 *Met.*).

*Whether everything on the surface of the earth has been made merely for the sake of men.*

§ 28. Since so many bodies exist which we either never get to see at all, or in which at most we perceive nothing more than a little light (§ 23), everyone will easily comprehend that they have not been made for the sake of us men who dwell on the surface of the earth. And accordingly we recognise that God has not made everything in the world merely for our sake. Men could persuade themselves of this so long as the number of the cosmic bodies was not known and one had a very narrow concept of the edifice of the world. It is indeed true that we can draw some benefit even from the far distant bodies which we merely see shining in the sky, and that we must to that extent concede that they too have been made for our sake (§ 1029 *Met.*); but when we come to know the benefit, it will easily be shown that this is by no means to be reckoned among the foremost purposes for which God made them, but that one must rather reckon it among the subordinate purposes which he has sought to attain together with others.

*Narrow limits*

*of the knowledge of nature.*

§ 29. Now since, however, there are so many cosmic bodies of which we do not even [p. 45] know that they are to be found in the world (§ 23), and since we can also examine closely no more than a single one, namely our earth, the number of the things which we do not get to see is very greatly larger than of those which we are permitted to examine. And accordingly we recognise how very narrow are the limits set to us in the knowledge of nature, since even then we should still have to say that we had comprehended only a little, even if we knew everything on the surface of the earth with complete exactness. But since even on the surface of the earth itself the least part is properly known to us, the limits of our knowledge become narrower still, since of the little we comprehend the least. But precisely from this one sees clear as day that nature contains within itself an inexhaustible sea of knowledge, and that one need have no fear that we shall go too far, since we are of ourselves forbidden to go as far as we might perhaps desire.

*How God extends the knowledge of himself in the creatures.*

*Difference in the knowledge of God.*

*1. General proof of it.*

*2. Particular proof.*

§ 30. The number of the planets exceeds the number of the fixed stars and is still more inexpressible than this (§ 23).
