All planets are inhabited by rational inhabitants (§ 149 *Phys.*), and the principal purpose itself requires it, as it would not otherwise be attained (§ 8). Now since everything in the world happens according to God's will, and nothing can [p. 46] come about but what he has decreed (§ 999 *Met.*), God has willed that the knowledge of him should also be infinitely increased in the rational creatures taken together, although in any one of them it is enclosed within very narrow limits. And it is altogether fitting that of God, whose perfections have neither measure nor bound (§ 1083 *Met.*), the actual knowledge too should have neither measure nor bound. Not only the principle of indiscernibles (§ 589 *Met.*), but also the way by which rational creatures in the world attain to the knowledge of God, shows clearly that in this way the knowledge of God is infinitely increased not only with respect to those who attain it but also with respect to itself. For since, by virtue of the principle of indiscernibles, two actual things can have no complete similarity, the thought which one person has of God's perfections must also always comprise something which is not to be found in another. But however strange this may appear to some from the general proof, so distinctly will it show itself when we add the other proof as well. We perceive God's invisible perfections from the visible things in the world (§ 8 ff.). The things which one person sees are always somewhat different [p. 47] from those which another sees, especially if we place the spectators of the works of God not in one but in quite different cosmic bodies. Therefore each has before him a particular proof of this or that divine perfection (§ 4). Although, then, the general remains the same, e.g. that one receives no other concept of the wisdom of God than another, yet each sees something particular of the wisdom of God which is represented to him in the particular proof he has before him. And the case is the same with the knowledge of all the other attributes of God.

*What joy arises from this among men.*

*First cause.*

*Second cause.*

*Why this joy is not found in everyone.*

§ 31. This extension of the knowledge of God causes very great joy in those who love God and men. Whoever loves someone is ready to take delight in the other's good fortune (§ 449 *Met.*). God has all perfection (§ 1083 *Met.*) and possesses it in tranquillity (§ 1086 *Met.*); therefore he can obtain nothing further from the creature in the world than that through it he attains his principal purpose for which he made the world, namely that those who have reason should know him from his works (§ 8), since by this he knows both his wisdom (§ 1048 *Met.*) and the perfection of things (§ 152 [p. 48] *Met.*). Whoever therefore loves him, and consequently lets God's delight be his delight, must take great delight in it when he recognises how the knowledge of God is spread abroad through the rational creatures (§ 30). Now a great delight constitutes joy (§ 446 *Met.*). And accordingly one who loves God must rejoice at the spreading and enlargement of the knowledge of him. God shows every creature as much good as is possible (§ 1062 *Met.*), and accordingly the goodness of God also increases with the number of the creatures. Now since one who loves others draws delight from the good that befalls them (§ 449 *Met.*), a great delight is thereby awakened in us too when we consider how far the goodness of God is spread abroad; consequently we rejoice at it (§ 446 *Met.*). It may well be that many on this account feel no delight within themselves, much less rejoice at it at all: but these lack either a zealous love of God and of other men and creatures, or they are not assured of those truths which I have set forth concerning the number of the cosmic bodies and their similarity to the earth. But we ourselves have given joy a place in no other case than where these circumstances are found together.

*How man is thereby made eager to spread God's honour and to serve others.*

*Who encourages himself to good by the contemplation of nature.*

[p. 49] § 32. Whoever loves God endeavours to do that in which he takes pleasure (§ 693 *Mor.*). Therefore, when he recognises how greatly he has it at heart to spread his honour (§ 30), and perceives from this his zeal for it, he is thereby also made more eager to spread his honour. Again, when one sees how God has it at heart to spread his goodness (§ 26), one recognises from this his earnestness that it should go well with everyone. Therefore, since he who loves God seeks to show himself pleasing to him (§ 693 *Mor.*), he is thereby encouraged to seek every opportunity, and to observe with the greatest care, where he can serve others and promote their good. One sees from this example, as from a specimen, how one can encourage oneself to good through the contemplation of the works of God in nature, when one's mind has first been changed and furnished with good inclinations, especially where one sincerely loves God. But where there is no love towards God, and one is still very far removed from all good inclinations, there the contemplation of natural things has no influence on the will either, and kindles no zeal for God's honour, nor does it impel one to the service of others.

*The stars make it bright at night.*

*Why this happens.*

*That it happens even under an overcast sky.*

*Benefit of it for men and animals.*

[p. 50] § 33. At night-time we do not enjoy the light of the sun, unless it be at the season when the sun shines through the whole night (§ 189 *Astr.*). Nor does the moon shine constantly at night, and so it would be entirely dark, as it is wont to be in a dark cellar that is firmly secured against all access of light, where one cannot see the very least thing. A star can give the earth little light, because on account of its distance from it it appears very small. But since there is so great a number of them, they make it so bright on the surface of the earth that one can still see something in the street at night. Indeed, even when the sky is covered with thick clouds, so that one supposes there is now complete darkness, one still has something of the starlight to enjoy. For let the sky be as overcast as it will, one can still see something white, which sufficiently indicates that there must still be light present which is *reflected* from it, since in a dark place where no light at all can come one is also unable to see anything white. But however little the light is which the stars let us enjoy on the surface of the earth at night-time, it nevertheless does men and animals manifold benefit, as, among [p. 51] other things, the experience of travellers and of other persons who have something to do at night under the open sky bears witness.

*That this also happens in the planets.*

The planets, like the earth, are likewise illuminated by the sun on one side only (§ 142, 143, 144 *Phys.*), and accordingly have night on the other side. They also rotate, like our earth (§ 175 *Phys.*), on their axis (§ 146 *Phys.*), and accordingly there is in them too an alternation of day and night. The fixed stars stand round about all the planets as round our earth, since in our part of the world the sun lies in the middle, all the planets move round it (§ 177 *Phys.*), and the fixed stars are farther away from it than Saturn (§ 150 *Phys.*), and there is moreover a very great space between Saturn and the fixed stars (§ 549, 575 *Astron.*). Therefore from the other planets, just as from the earth, one can survey half the sky at night and see the fixed stars shining in it. And accordingly what we have just proved concerning the advantage which they procure for the earth holds of them too.

*Whether the fixed stars have, by their light, an influence on the*

*earth.*

*Their light has no noticeable influence.*

*An imperceptible one is left in doubt.*

*Hidden effects are rejected.*

*Whether one has experience of it.*

*What is to be thought of the grounds of Placidus de Titis.*

*Who praises them.*

*Light is supposed to be the cause.*

*He takes grounds from the Aristotelian philosophy.*

*Infers from sunlight to the light of the planets.*

*Assumes hidden causes from astrology.*

*Why the Author adduces this.*

§ 34. Light penetrates into the interstices of bodies and moves the matter of heat present in them (§ 130 *Phys.*), whereby furthermore some parts of the permanent matter can also be moved (§ 75 *Phys.*). But this motion produces [p. 52] change in the body. The effects of burning-glasses (§ 136 *Pt. II Exper.*) and burning-mirrors (§ 138 *Pt. II Exper.*) show this plainly, because the strong sunlight brings about a noticeable change. Starlight is a very poor thing with respect to sunlight, and therefore it can hardly produce any noticeable changes. But whether something noticeable can come out of frequently repeated imperceptible changes, since it is in any case nature's way to bring things forth gradually and, as it were, imperceptibly, as we perceive, for example, in the growth of trees and animals; and whether, given the great subtlety of matter (§ 3 *Phys.*) and especially such small creatures present everywhere, as microscopes show (§ 85 *Met.*) and as *Leeuwenhoek* adduces a great many of them in his writings, changes may not thereby take place in small things: this may indeed be conjectured, but cannot be determined with certainty. But other hidden effects, which were formerly ascribed to the stars before people had become accustomed to explaining natural things in an intelligible manner, are granted without any ground. It is indeed true that in this matter one appeals to experience and, with *Ptolemy* [p. 53]<sup>a</sup> maintains it, as if the matter had long since been settled thereby. But whoever has learned to judge of experience according to the rules prescribed by me will very soon perceive (§ 11, *ch. 5 Log.*) that there is a want of sound experience in this respect. And for this reason, too, the influence of the stars, together with the soothsaying grounded upon it, has nowadays been rejected. Nor am I unaware that a certain monk in Italy, *Placidus de Titis*<sup>b</sup>, intended to prove from natural grounds the forces of the stars by which they act upon the bodies on the surface of the earth, and that some are also of the opinion that he hit the mark very well, among whom is *Petrus Paulus Bonettus*, who wrote a preface to the book and filled it with many encomiums: but whoever considers the matter more closely will perceive very great leaps in the inferences and will find himself not at all convinced by the supposed *demonstrations*. It is indeed true that he means to explain the influence of the stars by manifest causes and to confirm the grounds he has assumed by experience: but there is [p. 54] still a great deal wanting in the correctness of the proof. Since nothing comes from the stars to the surface of the earth but their light, he<sup>c</sup> gives this out as the cause of their influence. But that this light has a force to produce all manner of changes, indeed all kinds of changes, in the bodies on the surface of the earth, he proves from the grounds of the Aristotelian philosophy, because it can alter the so-called first *qualities* or contingent properties of bodies, heat, cold, moisture and dryness. Here, however, he further infers from the sun to the stars, since the sun, according to the difference of the four quadrants in the ecliptic, causes dryness in spring, heat in summer, moisture in autumn and cold in winter: which inference to the planets, at which he is properly aiming, no one will grant, if one considers that all forces have their measured degree, which a body cannot exceed in its effect. Moreover, he also assumes something hidden from the old astrology, which no one will concede to him nowadays and which experience contradicts (§ 39 *Geogr.*), namely as if light suffered some change through the heavens in the different parts of the ecliptic, whereas rather the cause [p. 55] why, for example, things dry more easily in spring than in autumn is to be sought in the state of the surface of our earth, and by no means in the difference of the sunlight: which I could very easily prove from what has been adduced concerning the four seasons (§ 227 ff. *Phys.*), if it were necessary. I have adduced this merely to the end that one may have some taste of his pretended *demonstrations*, and that the groundlessness of astrology may thereby become all the more evident.

<sup>a</sup> *Lib. 1. de iudiciis c. 2. f. m. 379. Oper.*

<sup>b</sup> *in Cœlesti Philosophia naturalibus huc usque desideratis ostensa principiis.*

<sup>c</sup> *Lib. 1. c. 1. p. 4.*

### Das IV. Capitel. Von der Grösse des Welt-Gebäudes. — Chapter IV. On the Size of the Edifice of the World.

*Why the size of the edifice of the world was not recognised.*

*How small the edifice of the world*

*was imagined.*

*First cause: lack of observations.*

*Principal cause: prejudice concerning the structure of the world.*

*What sustained it, namely persecution of the*

*philosophers*

*and the reviling of them.*

§ 35. The ancients had quite childish thoughts about the size of the edifice of the world, in that they imagined it so small that it was not at all commensurate with the infinite perfection of God. They imagined that the earth was the only dwelling-place, for whose sake all the rest had been made. This lay in the middle of the world, and it was no farther to the fixed stars, even [p. 56] by the reckoning of *Tycho Brahe*, who held with the ancients, than 14000 semi-diameters of the earth, or 12040000 German miles (§ 15 *Geogr.*). That is, they made the whole globe of the world 14000 times as large in diameter as the diameter of the earth: which will indeed seem large to those who have nothing to do with the measurement of magnitudes, but with respect to the infinite power of God is something paltry. The fixed stars were, in their imagination, all equally far from the earth, and with them the world came to an end. The cause why such lowly thoughts of the size of the world were held arose at first from this, that there was a lack of such *observations* as would allow the distance of the planets and fixed stars from the earth to be exactly determined; and, as is sufficiently known to astronomers, one erred at all events in setting the distances far too small. The principal cause, however, was precisely the preconceived opinion of the structure of the world which I have just now cited, and which everyone invents who looks at the world with the naked eye alone. But that the philosophers and *astronomers* also remained in this opinion was brought about by the persecution which those had to suffer who wished to depart from it. For when *Aristarchus* maintained the motion of the earth about its axis and about the sun, an uproar was raised against [p. 57] him, as if he were introducing heresy and teaching things prejudicial to the religion of the Greeks. And since those who were the cause of the uproar were of the opinion that prudence required them not to come forward themselves, they instigated *Cleanthes*, who had to accuse the good *Aristarchus* of having offended the goddess *Vesta* in her honour, in that he had moved her seat from its place<sup>a</sup>. *Pliny* denounced as godless, without doubt because such was then the fashion, those who had presumed to ascribe to the earth a motion about the sun<sup>b</sup>. In such circumstances it was not possible to view the size of the edifice of the world with the right eyes; and, since men contended for the honour of the gods with lack of understanding and malice, they were greatly prejudicial to the true honour of God, inasmuch as they prevented God from being known from his work as he is, and men from being moved to a true esteem of him.

<sup>a</sup> *Plutarchus in libello de facie in orbe Lunæ.*

<sup>b</sup> *Hist. Nat. Lib. 2. c. 23.*

*How its size came to be better known.*

*Who in particular contributed to this.*

*How this knowledge was applied in England to the honour of God.*

§ 36. But the times of ignorance have at last passed away, and in our days one has learned to discern the astonishing size of the world [p. 58] better. For after *Copernicus* had made known the true structure of the world; *Marius* and *Galileo* had begun to apply telescopes to the contemplation of the heavens; Kepler had happily discovered the laws of the motion of the planets, and *Newton* had *demonstrated* how they agree with the orbit in which they move; nay, *Johann Bernoulli* had proved that according to these laws they can move in no other orbit than the one Kepler specified; moreover *Huygens* had shown how the apparent magnitude of the planets can be determined more exactly than before; and finally *Dominicus Cassini* had shown how their distance from the earth can be ascertained with greater certainty than had been done before: so one has also acquired quite different thoughts of the size of the world, far more befitting the infinite God whom we worship, and has thereby also very forcefully promoted the honour of God. In England in particular *Derham*, a learned *theologian* and *philosopher*<sup>c</sup>, used the majesty of the edifice of the world to this end when, by virtue of the foundation of the famous Boyle, he delivered the sermons against the atheists. And the Royal Court Preacher there, Mr John [p. 59] Clarke, in the very same situation, namely when this office was entrusted to him in the year 1719, applied the new discoveries concerning the edifice of the world to that end<sup>d</sup>. Indeed, the *theologian Cotton Mather*, known among the English for his piety and his zeal for the purity of Christian doctrine, likewise made use of the truths of the *astronomers* concerning the edifice of the world when, following his impulse of piety, he wished to show how one ought to apply the knowledge of natural things to the praise and glory of God<sup>e</sup>. Now it would be good if others too followed the laudable example of the English theologians and, so that they might do the like, learned sound sciences, instead of persecuting them and striving to suppress them.

<sup>c</sup> *Astro-theology.*

<sup>d</sup> *An enquiry into the cause and origin of evil.*

<sup>e</sup> *Christian Philosopher.*

*Astonishing size of the edifice of the world.*

*Who worked it out*

*How it may be made comprehensible.*

*1. By Huygens's method, as far as the sun.*

*2. By my method, as far as that.*

*3. By Huygens's method, as far as Saturn.*

*4. By my method, as far as that.*

*How the magnitude of the space of the world may be represented still more clearly.*

*How our thoughts lose themselves in the extent of the world.*

§ 37. The knowledge of the size of the edifice of the world is grounded principally on the knowledge of the distance of the sun from the earth, which could not be exactly determined by the methods known to the ancients and the *instruments* customary in their *observations*. Therefore, when *Huygens* wished to *determine* it, he sought [p. 60] (§ 560 *Astron.*) to arrive at it by conjectures, and brought it out equal to 25086 semi-diameters of the earth<sup>a</sup>. After *Cassini* had invented his new method (§ 541 *Astron.*), he found by it the mean distance of the sun from the earth to be 22000 semi-diameters of the earth (§ 549 *Astron.*), and in this obtained the approval of the famous *astronomer* in England, Flamsteed<sup>b</sup>. *De La Hire*, on the other hand,<sup>c</sup> brings it out at as much as 34377 semi-diameters of the earth. One sees, therefore, that *Huygens* was not unlucky in his guessing, since his distance falls between Cassini's and de La Hire's. But it has always been the custom among astronomers that in doubtful cases one chooses the mean between the greater and the smaller. Now the mean between the two is 28188, to which Huygens's distance of 25086 comes fairly near. Accordingly, the space which the orbit of the earth encloses, that is, the circle in which the earth moves about the sun, is to be regarded as a sphere which in diameter is, by *Cassini*'s reckoning, 22000 times, but by *de La Hire*'s 34377 times, as great as the [p. 61] earth. Therefore, since the semi-diameter contains 860 German miles (§ 16 *Geogr.*), there are from the earth to the sun 18920000 by the former's reckoning, or as many as 29564220 German miles by the latter's; and the diameter of the spherical space first chosen contains in the first case 37840000, but in the second 59128440 German miles. In order to make this distance somewhat more comprehensible, one need only calculate, after *Huygens*'s example, how much time would be required before a ball that is shot from a cannon, if it continued to move with the velocity with which it issues from the cannon, would come from the earth to the sun: for which he brings out by his reckoning 25 years<sup>d</sup>. But one can also gauge it from this, if we compare the sphere which the orbit of the earth encloses with the earth. It is known that spheres are to one another as the cubes of their *diameters* (§ 241 *Geom.*). Now the diameter of the earth is [1], and the diameter of the orbit of the earth 22000. Therefore the space which the orbit of the earth encloses is 10648000000000, or 10 billion and 648000 million, times greater than the earth (§ 43 *Arithm.*). From which it is at the same time [p. 62] evident how small a part the earth occupies of that space which lies all round between it and the sun, in which it moves. And it is certain that we still bring out too little, since we take the mean distance of the sun for the semi-diameter of the orbit of the earth, as one is accustomed to do in such calculations, where probability also has its place. But this magnitude is still something very slight when we set it against the space which the planets all together occupy round about the sun as far as *Saturn*. The distance of Saturn from the sun is to the distance of the earth from it as 95 to 10 (§ 486 *Astr.*). Therefore from the sun to *Saturn* there are, on *Cassini*'s grounds, 179740000, on *de La Hire*'s 280860090 German miles; and a cannon-ball which takes 25 years before it can come from the sun to the earth would need 237 years before it could come from the sun to Saturn, even if it moved on constantly as swiftly as it issues from the cannon, when it travels 600 feet in one second, or within one pulse-beat, as *Huygens* assumes from *Mersenne*'s *observations*. *Huygens* brings out as much as 250 years, because he has the distances of the planets to one another *proportioned* somewhat otherwise than Kepler. [p. 63] If, further, we represent to ourselves the space which the orbit of Saturn encloses about the sun as a sphere, its diameter is, on *Cassini*'s grounds, 359480000, on *de La Hire*'s 561720180 German miles: from which one can easily gauge how very small the earth is with respect to this space, the earth whose diameter does not amount to more than 1720 German miles. If one wishes to represent the magnitude of this part of the space of the world still more clearly, let one suppose that someone could travel a great German mile every quarter of an hour, and it will be found that one would need over 5000 years on *Cassini*'s and over 8000 years on *de La Hire*'s grounds before one could come, on so swift and uninterrupted a journey, from the sun to *Saturn*. The distance of the fixed stars from the earth is still a great deal greater. *Huygens*, who invented an ingenious way of determining it by conjectures (but we have already seen above how fortunate he was in astronomical conjectures), puts it at 27664 times greater than the distance of the sun from the earth<sup>d′</sup>. Now since a ball that is shot from a cannon would need 25 years before it could come from the sun to the earth, [p. 64] it would have to have 691600, that is, nearly 700000 years' time before it could come from the earth to the nearest fixed star. If we travelled one mile every quarter of an hour, we should come from the earth to the sun only after 84 years: therefore whoever were to travel from the earth to the nearest fixed star would, although he covered a great German mile in a quarter of an hour, nevertheless arrive there only after 2323776, or far more than two million, years. Whoever reflects but a little upon all this will be astonished at the size of the space that lies around our earth as far as the fixed stars: for if we were to travel from one fixed star past the earth to the other opposite it, we should need for this a time of more than four and a half million years, although we covered a great German mile in a quarter of an hour without interruption. But we must now look further out, and then our thoughts will at length lose themselves altogether in the inexpressible extent of the world. For however astonishing the size of the space which we have hitherto endeavoured to represent to ourselves with some clarity, it nevertheless reaches no further than to the fixed star, and belongs for the most part to one sun. Now whoever considers that each fixed star is a sun [p. 65] and that not only up to about 3000 of them can be seen with the naked eye, but that a far greater, indeed an innumerable, multitude shows itself through telescopes (§ 23), can easily judge that around each fixed star so astonishing a space is to be found, and that in this way the whole space of the world multiplies itself out into infinity, that is, becomes so great that neither senses nor reason can reach it (§ 180 *Phys.*).

<sup>a</sup> *System. Saturnin. p. 80.*

<sup>b</sup> *Vid. Whiston in Prælect. Astron. p. 82.*

<sup>c</sup> *in Tabb. Astron. p. 6.*

<sup>d</sup> *in Cosmotheoro lib. 2. p. m. 106.*

<sup>d′</sup> *in Cosmotheoro p. 115.*

*How God's immensity is known.*

*Why this knowledge is to be made common.*

§ 38. This tremendous size of the edifice of the world shows the immensity of the power, wisdom and knowledge of God (§ 13, 14, 19). And a man who is devoted to the honour of God finds no little delight in the fact that the world is so fitting an edifice for an infinite master-builder. And for this very reason it were to be wished that the astronomical truths concerning the edifice of the world were made better known, so that many might taste the immensity of God, since such knowledge (§ 169 *Mor.*) would be most conducive to them for the promotion of the honour of God (§ 658 *Mor.*) and the exercise of virtue (§ 673 *Mor.*).

*Motive to humility.*

§ 39. In particular, however, man is brought by this taste of the immensity of God to the point that he recognises his lowliness and impotence, and [p. 66] exalts himself neither on account of his power nor on account of his knowledge. I need not be lengthy in the proof here. Everyone loses heart when he meets with something lofty to which what is his own, on which he sets some store or on which he even prides himself, does not come near at all. Indeed, I know from my own experience that people who like to cherish a great opinion of themselves have not wanted to hear of the size of the edifice of the world, because the thought of it at once cast them down and disturbed their pleasure and delight, in which they had found themselves so well and in which they had sought their happiness.

*Proof leading to trust in God.*

§ 40. Whoever regards the wisdom, power and knowledge of God as immeasurable (§ 38), and is himself convinced from the contemplation of the world that God is able to do exceedingly abundantly above all that we understand and can imagine, is thereby *disposed* to trust in God (§ 716 *Mor.*), even in such cases where he himself does not see how counsel and help are to be had, indeed where to human eyes all help seems to be impossible.

*Narrow bounds of the senses and of the*

*imagination.*

§ 41. Finally, when we consider how small the great space looks which we take in at once with our eyes as we look up at the sky, and yet consider that in itself it is so inexpressibly great [p. 67] (§ 37), we thereby learn to know the narrow bounds of our senses and of our imagination, and to estimate how slight their degree of perfection is (§ 826 *Met.*)

*Inner constitution of the representation of the senses.*

*Whether creatures with more perfect*

*senses than ours are possible.*

§ 42. But I must here put forward one more particular thought, which will not occur to everyone at once. Every representation of the senses is nothing other than an image of the world (§ 753 *Met.*), and in it is to be found everything that the space of the world contains (§ 773 *Met.*). Therefore, when we look at the sky, the image of the part which we survey contains everything that is to be found in it, only that we cannot properly distinguish everything in it. Now since an unspeakable multitude of matter is to be found in this same space (§ 37), of which even the very smallest parts are different from one another (§ 587 *Met.*), one recognises that the representations of the soul contain infinitely much, and it is therefore no wonder if acute men and men practised in reflection can draw much out of them: for let one draw out as much as ever he will, it nevertheless remains, with respect to what is contained in them, only something very slight, indeed it is to be held as almost nothing. But one recognises from this at the same time how many kinds of representations can be conceived [p. 68] that are more perfect than ours, and consequently how other creatures may be supposed which have far more perfect senses than we. But whether such are actually present elsewhere in our world cannot as yet be determined, since in particular the difference of the state in the various planets has not yet been weighed as maturely as might be necessary for this, and as could very well be done, if only able heads would apply due diligence to it.

*That corporeal things must in themselves be quite otherwise than they appear to us.*

*Principal source of the difficulties concerning matter.*

§ 43. Now since, however, the representations of corporeal things contain so very much in themselves (§ 42), as in particular the observations made with microscopes also show (§ 82 *ff. Pt. III Exper.*), that things go downwards in the small just as they go upwards in the great, they must also in themselves be something quite other than they are represented by our senses. And indeed microscopes always show us another shape, the further one can unfold by them what the senses have wrapped up in one another and confused. But precisely because every mote of matter contains an infinite variety of differences in itself (§ 684, 587 *Met.*), no man is able by reason to unfold what the senses confuse in it. And it is a great [p. 69] error that we take the confused for something unfolded: for this is the principal source of all the difficulties which philosophers have raised concerning matter, which *Descartes* began to stop up, namely with respect to colours and other sensible properties, of which we have no distinct concept, but not completely, since he excepted from them the extension of matter (not to mention other things), of which he nevertheless had as little a distinct concept as of colours and other sensible properties. The cause was indeed this, that he did not grasp the difference between clear concepts, and did not consider that clear concepts can be distinct and indistinct (§ 13, ch. 1 *Log.*)

### Das V. Capitel. Von der Sonne. — Chapter V. On the Sun.

*The sun preserves the changeable state of the earth.*

*This is confirmed still further.*

*What the sun contributes to the principal purpose of the world.*

§ 44. The sun illuminates our earth, and by its light at the same time warms it (§ 130 *Phys.*). The heat of the sun makes great changes on the surface of the earth, as the difference of summer and winter [p. 70] shows in common experience. In summer the water is fluid, dew and rain fall, the trees and plants grow and bear fruit, the animals generate their like. In winter everything ceases. The water freezes, the earth is covered with snow, the plants perish, the trees lose their splendour and stand there as if dead, the animals that must remain out in the cold do not generate their like. Whoever compares the state of summer and winter with each other will find that, as soon as one took the sun away, a perpetual winter would follow with a constant night, consequently in the end the generation of plants, animals and men would wholly cease, and the earth would become a desolate and empty lump. If anyone wishes to be still more assured of this truth, let him look up in the Physics for what causes summer and winter are so different (§ 227 *f. Phys.*), why water is fluid (§ 55 *Phys.*), how vapours are generated (§ 248 *Phys.*), how it rains and dews (§ 276, 271 *Phys.*), how plants and trees can grow (§ 400 *Phys.*), and how animals generate their like (§ 444 *Phys.*); and he will find how in all this the sun is so far *interested* that without it nothing can happen. [p. 71] Accordingly we see that God made the sun to the end that the changeable state on the surface of the earth can be preserved in its proper order, and that it can be inhabited by living creatures, men and animals. Now since it is precisely men who are the rational creatures that perceive God's invisible being from the contemplation of the world, the sun also contributes something to the attainment of the principal purpose (§ 8), in so far as without it man cannot be preserved, nor his race propagated.

*Why some have taken the sun for God*

*What confirmed them in their opinion.*

*How this error becomes clear as day.*

§ 45. We accordingly have a very great benefit from the sun on the surface of the earth, since without its help it can by no means subsist in that state, nor be inhabited by men. But precisely through this it has come about that the most rational heathen peoples fell into the error of taking the sun for God. For they knew, namely, that God is that being in which a sufficient reason is to be found why the others exist (§ 945 *Met.*), and that the world subsists by his force and cannot continue without it (§ 1054 *Met.*). Now since they looked about in the world to see whether they could find such a being by whose force the world subsisted, and found that the sun by its force keeps the earth in its state, [p. 72] and that it at once becomes a desolate lump if the sun's action and presence are wholly withdrawn from it, they finally inferred that the sun is God, and therefore venerated it as their God. But they were confirmed in their opinion all the more because they supposed that the earth makes up the principal part of the world and that all the rest exists merely for the sake of the earth, and moreover did not recognise that the fixed stars are nothing but suns, and stand so far away from our sun that it could accomplish nothing in them by its action. In particular, they were confirmed still more in their opinion by the error that the world had been made out of a desolate and empty lump, and that the stars altogether were desolate and empty globes, that is, by the erroneous opinion concerning the structure of the world (§ 35). As soon as one gives up these prejudices, in particular the error concerning the production of the world out of a desolate and empty lump, and concerning the constitution of the edifice of the world, one also sees, clear as day, that in the sun one finds no sufficient reason why the world is present and is preserved in its state, but that one must go further and seek another being which uses the sun merely [p. 73] as a means to preserve the changeable state of the earth.

*That the sun also preserves the other planets in their changeable state.*

*Why the effect of sunlight is not the same everywhere.*

§ 46. All the planets are bodies like our earth (§ 148 *Phys.*), are illuminated by the sun (§ 142 ff. *Phys.*), and turn on their axis, some within 24 hours, some within fewer (§ 146 *Phys.*), and are thus shone upon and warmed all round, little by little. One also conjectures, with no small ground of probability, that they too are adorned with herbs and flowers and inhabited by men and animals (§ 149 *Phys.*). Therefore one can easily judge that the sun procures for them too the advantage that it grants to the earth (§ 44), and in part experience itself also shows this, in so far as the changeable spots give us to understand that vapours in the air of the planets draw together into thick clouds and dissolve again into rain or snow (§ 146 *Phys.*). Since the force of sunlight decreases with the differing distance from the sun (§ 148 *Pt. II Exper.*), while Mercury and Venus are nearer to the sun, but Mars, Jupiter and Saturn farther from it, than the earth (§ 170 *Phys.*), the sun must also shine much more strongly on the first two, but much more weakly on the last three, than on the earth. And accordingly the effect [p. 74] of sunlight, too, cannot be of one and the same kind everywhere. The sun accordingly produces many kinds of changes in the different planets, with various differences among them, which, however, we shall not investigate more closely. It is enough that we see that God made the sun not only for the sake of our earth but also for the sake of the other planets, and thus also to the end that they may be preserved in their changeable state and need not remain desolate and empty lumps.

*Benefit of sunlight.*

*The first benefit.*

*The second.*

*The third.*

*How one learns to grasp the benefit of sunlight.*

*How one recognises its benefit to science.*

§ 47. By its light the sun makes the day, not only on our earth but also in the other planets (§ 144 ff.). But daylight brings us great benefit: for by it we can conveniently undertake our tasks, which in the evening can in part not be undertaken at all, or at least not so conveniently, and with some expense, which the art of making it light requires. Animals can seek their fodder by day, which by night they would in part not find at all. Moreover, we also have the sunlight to thank that we can see everything that is on the surface of the earth, not only near at hand but even at a distance, and can make out both things near at hand exactly and [p. 75] their kind at a distance: which again procures us manifold benefit, not only in human life, for our necessary tasks and on journeys, but also, in particular, in the knowledge of nature, since this is for the most part founded on such *observations* as we obtain by the help of sight, and which we cannot have without sunlight. Whoever wishes to grasp properly this advantage which the sun procures for us, let him only consider for a month, in everything he undertakes, how it would stand with him if it were not day but night; he will then be sufficiently convinced of it by his own experience, especially if he has many tasks to carry out in the street or in the field. Likewise, if anyone wishes to perceive the more distinctly how much daylight, or sunshine, serves for the advancement of science, let him attend to his concepts and to the grounds of proof, and to how he acquired them. And if he finds that he took them from experience, either by himself or because others who had attended to it by themselves reminded him of it, let him further investigate whether he could have had them without daylight, or without sunshine. Then, no less than before, his own experience will [p. 76] convince him that without the sun one would be deprived of a great deal of the knowledge which we now have, indeed probably of most of it.

*An objection is met.*

*Objection.*

*Reply to it.*

§ 48. Some might perhaps hold up to me the example of the blind *Professor of Mathematics* at *Cambridge*, who has advanced as far in mathematics and philosophy, and in particular in physics, as many do not attain who have eyes to see and can enjoy the light of the sun that makes the day. And hence it will be supposed that the sciences could be attained even without daylight and sunshine. But this scruple can very easily be removed. It is one thing to learn a science which has been discovered by others, and another to discover it. We are not speaking now of those who learn what others have discovered, but of those who first find the thing out for themselves. If nothing of science were yet known, and men were all blind, so that they could see nothing of what is in nature and goes on in it, a great deal of the knowledge which we now have would remain wanting. Indeed, even if they were not blind, but merely had no light of the sun, there would again be much which by other light they could not have observed so exactly, nor indeed have had the opportunity to observe. Whoever [p. 77] wishes to recognise this in particular in the case of the blind *professor*, Mr **Saunderson**, need only read what Mr **Thümmig**<sup>a</sup> has written of him, and in particular consider what he has adduced<sup>b</sup> on how one who is blind by nature can learn mathematics and physics.

<sup>a</sup> Versuch einer gründlichen Erläuterung der Begebenheiten in der Natur *part. 2. c. 4. p. 96. & seqq.*

<sup>b</sup> *§. 6. & seq. p. 102. & seqq.*

*Why the sun is so large, and its rays have such great force.*

*How one can measure the size of the sun.*

*Cause of its size.*

*The magnitude of the sun's force.*

*Cause of it.*

*How much weaker it is on Saturn than on the earth.*

§ 49. The diameter of the sun is 152½ times as large as the diameter of the earth (§ 817 *Astron. Lat.*). Now the latter is 1720 German miles (§ 16 *Geogr.*): therefore the diameter of the sun contains 262300 German miles. Again, since we have to regard the earth and the sun as a pair of spheres (§ 118, 182 *Phys.*), they are to one another as the *cubes* of their diameters (§ 241 *Geom.*), that is, since the diameters are nearly as 1 to 152, as 1 to 3511808. In this way the sun is more than three and a half million times larger than the earth. Whoever wishes to grasp the size of the sun still better may also seek the space which it fills in *cubic* miles (§ 237 *Geom.*), or else measure its circumference in the same manner as we earlier (§ 37) made [p. 78] the distance of the earth from the sun and the size of the edifice of the world more comprehensible. The circumference of the sun is to the circumference of the earth as its diameter to the diameter of the earth (§ 163 *Geom.*). Now the circumference of the earth under the line, or in general when one travels round in a great circle, is 5400 great German miles (§ 15 *Geogr.*). Therefore the greatest circumference of the sun is 823500 German miles (§ 113 *Arithm.*). Accordingly, if one travelled a mile in a quarter of an hour and continued the journey without interruption, one would need more than 23 years before one came round it, whereas at such a velocity we could complete the journey round the earth in less time than three months. The earth occupies 2662560000 *cubic* miles of space (§ 17 *Geogr.*): now since we have seen that the sun is 3511808 times as large as the earth, it must occupy a space of 9350399508480000 *cubic* miles. When we consider what an important office has been entrusted to the sun, namely that it is to preserve all the planets in their changeable state and prevent them from becoming desolate lumps (§ 44, 46), the enormous size of the sun will no longer seem strange [p. 79] to him. For since the space around the sun as far as Saturn holds more than five hundred and fifty million German miles in breadth (§ 37), and the sun must nevertheless extend its force perceptibly through this whole space, one also easily sees why God made it of so enormous a size. The effects of burning-glasses and burning-mirrors, especially **Tschirnhaus's**, sufficiently confirm that the force of the sun close at hand must be exceedingly violent (§ 136, 158 *Pt. II Exper.*). But precisely for this reason, since it is to extend itself through so great a space and nevertheless continually decreases, like the light (§ 43 *Opt.*) by which it is conveyed, it must be of an altogether uncommon magnitude and penetrating beyond all measure. Whoever wishes to consider this more exactly, let him reflect how much the force of the sun has decreased before it extends as far as Saturn. The distance of Saturn from the sun is to the distance of the earth from the same as 95 to 10 (§ 486 *Astron.*). The force of the sun on the earth is accordingly to its force on Saturn as 9025 to 100 (§ 43 *Opt.*). And in this way Saturn enjoys scarcely the ninetieth part of the force which on the surface of the earth it [p. 80] has. Now if, with so great a diminution, the sun is still to have some part of its force left over, by which it can produce a noticeable effect, it must indeed be, in and of itself, of quite uncommon magnitude.

*The sun bears witness to God's power.*

*And indeed to a particular circumstance of it.*

§ 50. Now since the size of the cosmic bodies bears witness to the power of God (§ 19), the sun in particular, which is of quite enormous size (§ 49), must give a powerful testimony of the power of God. And one can rightly say: he must be a mighty God who made it. But one also knows the power of God from this, that he does not lack the means to accomplish that for which a quite uncommon force is required; for the sun gives a clear example of this. Accordingly, those to whom it matters to be convinced of the magnitude of the divine power, and of this particular circumstance of it, must represent to themselves as distinctly as possible the size of the sun and its force. And this confirms what I said above (§ 30), that different creatures bear witness of God with some difference.

*The sun bears witness to the wisdom of God.*

§ 51. If one chooses such means as the purposes require, one acts wisely (§ 916 *Met.*), and accordingly thereby gives a proof of one's wisdom. [p. 81] Now since God made the sun of such size as was necessary in order that all the planets might be preserved by it in their changeable state (§ 44, 46), he has thereby also given a proof of his wisdom.

*And to his goodness. First proof.*

*Second proof.*

§ 52. Indeed, the size of the sun is also a proof of the goodness of God. For since the sun is of so enormous a size, so many planets can be preserved in their changeable state, and thus many creatures can be brought into actuality in them which would otherwise not attain it (§ 46). Now since the number of creatures increases the goodness of God (§ 26), a proof of the divine goodness is also given by the size of the sun. In particular, we too, and other creatures in other planets, have a proof of the goodness of God in this, that the sun procures us manifold advantage by its light and its heat (§ 47): for since good is thereby done to us (§ 422 *Met.*), God thereby shows his goodness towards us and towards other creatures (§ 1063 *Met.*).

*What the contemplation of the sun contributes to virtue*

§ 53. Since the certainty of the wisdom, power and goodness of God promotes trust in him (§ 716 *Mor.*), and in particular the knowledge of their magnitude increases esteem and reverence towards [p. 82] him (§ 710 *Mor.*), the contemplation of the sun likewise produces many good motions in our mind, when everything that we have adduced concerning it (§ 50 ff.) is duly weighed.

*What the sun does with regard to time.*

*An objection, which is answered.*

*Why the Author mentions such things.*

§ 54. From the sun one can know when it is noon (§ 45 *Astron.*), and by knowing this time exactly, clocks can be set correctly, which is of great importance especially in astronomy. One can also find from the altitude of the sun the hour and the minute, indeed still smaller parts of time (§ 133 *Astron.*), and in this way know exactly the time at which something has happened, which again is of very great importance in astronomy and in other things *depending* on this science. And thus the sun also helps us to the knowledge of time. Perhaps some will suppose that here, before all things, it ought to be mentioned that we owe to the sun the alternation of the four seasons, and that it gives the measure for the year and the months: but, not to mention that this comes from the sun only in appearance, and has other causes, as we shall see hereafter, and that here, since we are seeking the means by which God attains his purposes, we [p. 83] must look not to the appearance but to the essence, this consideration also belongs in general to the chapter in which we treat of the structure of the world and shall investigate its purposes. Yet I could not pass over this reminder altogether, lest anyone should fall into the thought that I had forgotten something which is otherwise known to everyone; for I find in any case that some people suppose that with me one must criticise everything in the sharpest manner, in order to extort something wrong and force it upon me against my will, although they are scarcely wont to weigh their own words and propositions on the hay- scales, let alone on the gold- or jewel-scales. I have experienced this still more since I have been persecuted in such a manner that they must be ashamed before the impartial world, and now know not how to save themselves, since they cast their slanders and calumnies now into this form, now into another, in order to prejudice the unwary against me.

*How the sun serves to distinguish*

*the regions of the world.*

§ 55. By the help of the sun one can find the meridian line (§ 40, 44 *Astron.*), and then further distinguish the regions of the world (§ 62 *Geogr.*), which one needs to know if one wishes to learn to know the winds (§ 223 [p. 84] *Phys.*), and consequently find the cause of the changeable kinds of weather (§ 246 *Phys.*). In this way art owes to the sun the wind-indicators, such as the learned Jesuit *Paolo Casati*<sup>a</sup> describes.

<sup>a</sup> *Mechanic. lib. 5. c. 9. prop. 1. p. 568. & seq.*

*Likewise the hours of the day.*

§ 56. The meridian line is the ground of sundials (§ 16 *ff. Gnom.*), and in general we should have no sundials if we had no sun. But since the light of the sun is not sufficient for this, and it is rather the motion of the earth about its axis in particular that contributes the most to it, we shall also set forth more about this in its proper place (§ 54).

*Benefit which the sun affords in geography.*

§ 57. From the meridian altitudes of the sun the latitude of a place, that is, its distance from the Line towards the pole, can be determined (§ 101 *Astron.*). *Cassini* has also devised a method by which, from the observations of solar eclipses, one can determine their longitude, that is, how much farther to the east one lies than the other, and the difference of the clocks at the two places<sup>b</sup>. But the longitude and latitude [p. 85] of places are of very great consequence in geography: for as soon as one knows them, one can enter every place correctly on the map (§ 72 *Geogr.*), and one would have no maps, and would also not know many geographical truths, if one had not found the longitude and latitude of many places.

<sup>b</sup> *Vid. de la Hire in Tabb. Astron. præc. 13. p. 53.*

*And in navigation at sea,*

*1. in finding out the latitude of a place;*

*2. in the use of the magnetic*

*needle.*

*An incidental remark.*

*What the Author passes over.*

§ 58. The sun also serves seafarers at sea as a faithful guide. For when they wish to know whether they have sailed too far towards the north or the south, according as they sail on this side or the other side of the Line, they ask the sun, and it must tell them, since they work out from its meridian altitude the latitude of the place where they are (§ 334 *Geogr. Lat.*). Indeed, although the magnetic needle in the compass is the ordinary guide which mariners use at sea, as appears from the 10th and 11th chapters of my Latin Geography and Hydrography, they nevertheless may not trust it alone; but when it is about to lead them astray, or has led them astray, the sun must set them right again, since from the quarter where it rises and sets one determines the declination of the magnetic needle from the meridian line (§ 299 *Geogr. Lat.*). The sun must in fact tell [p. 86] them how far one may trust the magnetic needle. But since one can also find the declination of the magnetic needle on land by means of the meridian line (§ 36 *Astron.*), the needle thereby also becomes fit for various uses on the surface of the earth, for which one could not otherwise well use it, as e.g. in the drawing (§ 19 *ff. Gnom.*) and use of sundials (§ 18 *Gnom.*). I will not now mention what has hitherto been attempted in order to determine by the sun the longitude of the place at sea where the ship is<sup>c</sup>: since in this respect nothing has yet been accomplished, just as in general no one who has undertaken anything in this respect has yet succeeded.

<sup>c</sup> *Dorothei Alimari longitudinis investigandæ methodus Lond. 1715. in. 8.*

*Also in geodesy.*

§ 59. Since the bodies on the surface of the earth which are illuminated by the sun cast a shadow, and this is governed by their height (§ 55 *Opt.*), one can also find the height of a body from its shadow (§ 57 *ff. Opt.*). And in this way the sun also affords us a benefit in geodesy which we should not have without it, and which I have proved in particular in the Latin work on the whole of mathematics (§ 171 *ff. Opt.*).

[p. 87]

### Das VI. Capitel. Von den Fixsternen. — Chapter VI. On the Fixed Stars.

*The office of the fixed stars is the same as the office of the sun.*

*The cause of this.*

§ 60. The fixed stars are nothing but suns (§ 153 *Phys.*), and likewise have planets about them, which they illuminate and warm (§ 155 *Phys.*). Now since our sun is made to the end that it should make fruitful the planets which are about it, and preserve them in their changeable state (§ 44, 46), the fixed stars without doubt also have this function, that they warm the planets which are about them, and thereby preserve their changeable state. We cannot point out the least ground why it should be otherwise. Why should one sun have so important an office, and, on the other hand, so many thousands, indeed innumerably many suns, have been made either for mere pastime or for a meaner purpose? We cannot say that they were made merely for the sake of our earth: for, not to mention that the proposition that everything in the world has been made for the sake of the earth is erroneous (§ 28), it is also evident of the fixed stars in particular that they cannot [p. 88] have been made for our sake, because we do not see most of them, and consequently the light of most of them is so weak that we cannot suspect even the very least change on the surface of the earth from it (§ 34). But even astrologers of understanding have themselves recognised that one may grant the stars no further effect than that which proceeds from their light (§ *cit.*). Now where their light either does not reach at all, or arrives so weak that nothing can be seen by it, but a thick darkness remains, there one can also suspect no effect from the light.

*Prejudice of the senses concerning the fixed stars*

*1. How it arises.*

*2. Whence it has its strength.*

*3. How it shows itself.*

*4. How it is removed.*

*5. Why it is hard to dispel.*

§ 61. If the fixed stars looked as large as the sun, and shone as warm as it does, no one would doubt that they performed just such effects in nature as our sun. But since they look so small, and we also do not feel the slightest heat when their light falls upon us, since it is coldest of all in winter, when the stars shine most brightly in the sky, we have judged from our youth up that the fixed stars are something quite different from our sun; and this prejudice of the senses has become so rooted in us through long habit that we find it hard to give it up. When we are to assume the contrary, [p. 89] it is as if there were something in us that contradicted us, namely the sensation of the supposed certainty of our judgement, carried on through so long a time. Therefore one cannot free anyone from it otherwise than by the astronomical truths that have been discovered concerning the fixed stars, which, however, do not lie so immediately before one's eyes that everyone could perceive their certainty. We know from astronomy that the fixed stars are not smaller than our sun, and look smaller merely because they are farther away from us (§ 575 *Astron.*). Indeed it is also known that one is farther away from the earth than another (§ 178 *Phys.*), and looks smaller than the other merely because its light becomes weaker on account of the greater distance (§ 43 *Opt.*). For otherwise they are all so far away that even the nearest would not be visible by reason of its magnitude (§ 570 *Astron.*). One can further prove that, if we were as near to a fixed star as we are to the sun, and were on the other hand removed as far from our sun as we are from the fixed stars, the fixed star would appear to us as large as a sun, and the sun on the other hand like a fixed star (§ 43, 82 *Opt.* and § 549, 575 *Astron.*). Whoever carefully considers all this [p. 90] will very soon free himself in this respect from the prejudice of the senses. Admittedly, however, much knowledge is required before one can have a conviction of these optical and astronomical truths, as anyone may easily grasp who looks up in my mathematical writings the §§  *cited* in the proof, and at the same time considers what has been set forth elsewhere (§ 4, 6, ch. 13 *Log.*) concerning convincing. And therefore it is no wonder if so very many remain in the prejudices of their senses, and even defend them obstinately. Much is already required before one comes into the state of being able to judge what one understands and what one does not understand. And since so many do not attain to it in the whole of their lives, it is no wonder that so much useless dispute is begun, and why people of understanding will have nothing to do with disputing.

*What benefit the fixed stars are to their planets.*

§ 62. Now since the fixed stars are just as large as our sun, and have just as strong and powerful a light as it (§ 60), everyone sees without further reminder from me that every fixed star must procure for its planets, that is, for the dark bodies that move about it (§ *cit.*), the very same advantage that our [p. 91] sun procures for the earth (§ 54 ff.). And accordingly it would have to be regarded as something superfluous if we wished to repeat this here once more.

*Benefit of the fixed stars in geography, navigation,*

*common life,*

*astronomy,*

*on journeys,*

*in geography,*

*at the appearance of comets, and in the course of the planets.*

§ 63. With the help of the fixed stars one can ascertain the latitude of a place on the surface of the earth, or how far it lies from the Line towards the pole (§ 91, 101 *Astron.*), on which a very great deal depends in geography (§ 72 *Geogr.*). One can see from the stars in the sky in which part of the world one is, whether in the northern or in the southern; which is exceedingly serviceable to seafarers at sea who travel from one part of the world into another and on the open sea see no mark of where they are. Indeed the stars also serve them to *determine* exactly how far the ship is from the Line on this side or the other (§ 336 ff. *Geogr. Lat.*). From them one has also sought to *determine* the longitude of the ship, or its distance towards the east, although one has not yet been able to succeed in it<sup>a</sup>. Not only can the peasants, from the position of the stars, particularly [p. 92] of the Great Bear, or of others which do not set with us, roughly gauge the time of night; but in astronomy one can also determine it so exactly by the help of a fixed star (§ 201, 204 *Astron.*) that one can set and *correct* clocks by it, when such cases occur in which one needs to know the time quite exactly and without the least error. Star-clocks have also been invented, from which one can know the hours of the night when the sky is starry (§ 170 *Gnom. Lat.*), to say nothing of other advantages which have regard to time. The pole star, and the stars in general, also serve us to know the quarters: which is of signal service especially to travellers, if perhaps they have lost their way in the evening or at night, as also to those who are overtaken by night in the field or in a forest and who have to direct themselves by the quarters if they wish to find the way that leads home. But since by means of the fixed stars one can also exactly *determine* the meridian line, and hence correctly fix all the quarters of the world (§ 112 *Astron. Lat. &* § 62 *Geogr.*), they also help us to the benefit which we commended above in this respect in the case of the sun (§ 55). The [p. 93] astronomers not only know how to determine, by the help of the fixed stars, the place of the comets and of whatever else unusual occurs in the heavens (§ 936 *Astron. Lat.*), but they also owe them much in that they have brought the course of the planets, and in particular of the moon (§ 652 *&c. Astron.*), into correct order, and are still able to improve in it whatever is lacking. Astronomers understand how much this benefit signifies. Not every truth can be made comprehensible to everyone (§ 3, 6, ch. 13 *Log.*)

<sup>a</sup> *Ricciolus in Geogr. Reformata lib. 8. c. 7. f. 320. & Svvedenborgius in Methodo nova inveniendi longitudines locorum.*

*The fixed stars are signs of the kinds of weather: 1. of the constant.*

*2. Of the variable.*

*Why in this case they are probable signs.*

*Why they are signs of the tasks*

*in the country.*

*Origin of astrology from the weather.*

*What further supports it.*

§ 64. The constant kinds of weather in the four seasons arise from the course of the sun through the zodiac (§ 232 *Phys.*). Now according as the sun enters this or that celestial sign, so it also rises and sets with these or with other stars; indeed, accordingly, other stars also rise or set shortly before it, and others stand after its setting on the western horizon, so that they are about to set soon after it. Indeed, according to the difference of the various seasons, now these, now other stars may also stand in the south while the sun rises or sets. And in this way one can make the fixed stars signs of the seasons, indeed even of the months, and of the constant kinds of weather occurring in them [p. 94] (§ 292 *Met.*). For example, it has been taken as a sign of the greatest heat in summer when Sirius rises and sets with the sun, which time has on that account been called the dog-days. The variable kinds of weather, it is true, are not the same one year as another; yet at certain seasons some kinds of weather are more common than others. For example, at the beginning of April there is commonly unsettled weather; around St John's Day and at the beginning of September, as also around Martinmas, rainy weather is apt to come, and indeed in the first two seasons downpours commonly set in. Therefore one can also, on the grounds just adduced, make the stars signs of the variable kinds of weather. But since these kinds of weather do not set in with certainty at the same time every year, but are only wont commonly to occur, the stars can in this case be merely probable signs, whereas in the preceding case they were infallible signs. Again, since in agriculture and gardening, in the rearing of cattle, and in husbandry generally, not only are certain tasks to be undertaken at certain seasons, but in doing so one must also, according to the variable kinds of weather, [p. 95] direct oneself, the stars too can furnish signs of the tasks in husbandry. And since in former times men had no almanacs, as they have now, there is also no doubt that the countryman used the stars as an almanac and guided himself by these signs, partly in judging the kinds of weather, partly in his tasks. And hence, in my opinion, it afterwards came about that the stars were even given out as causes of the kinds of weather, and that an effect upon the growth of plants and upon other changeable things on the surface of the earth was ascribed to them. In this prejudice men were afterwards still more confirmed. For since it was known that natural effects must have a cause (§ 29, 30 *Met.*), and yet, for lack of knowledge, men could not find it on the surface of the earth, while they were nevertheless caught in the prejudice that the stars had been made for the sake of the earth (§ 28), they also took them for the cause of the effects on the surface of the earth, since they supposed they had none for them. Whoever weighs this will be all the less led astray by the alleged constant experience by which it is sought to maintain the influence of the stars [p. 96] upon subterranean bodies.

*What the fixed stars contribute to godliness.*

§ 65. Since the fixed stars are nothing but suns, which all have such functions as our sun (§ 60), they all likewise give such proofs as it does of the wisdom, power and goodness of God (§ 50 f.) and afford us motives to honour him as a God (§ 53): all of which I consider it unnecessary to repeat here anew in detail.

### Das VII. Capitel. Von der Erde und den Planeten. — Chapter VII. On the Earth and the Planets.

*Why God made the earth.*

*That it is to be a dwelling for men and animals.*

*How one is made more certain of this.*

*How God's goodness is to be known from it.*

*Unrecognised benefactions of God.*

§ 66. We learn from daily experience not only that men and animals are found on the earth, but also that it is in every respect so arranged that men and animals find everything that belongs to the necessities and convenience of their life. Now since all things that happen in the world and are attained through the essence of things are divine purposes, and this very essence is to be regarded as a means by which God attains them (§ 1027 ff. *Met.*), one can [p. 97] also say no other than that God made the earth to the end that it should be inhabited, and for that reason so arranged it that it should be fit to be a dwelling for men and animals. Man finds here everything that may in any way be serviceable to him for nourishment, clothing, dwelling, the attainment of science and art, and for the fulfilment of his duties, which I have explained in detail in the Ethics and the Politics: of which various proofs will appear in particular in what follows. And whoever will pay heed to everything that comes before him in his occupations in the course of the day, and reflect upon it, will find far more of them every hour, indeed every moment. I consider it unnecessary to pursue this further here: I shall only call one thing more to mind. Since we have to regard the whole arrangement of the earth as nothing other than a means ordained by God for attaining all that we need for our necessities, for convenience and for recreation, God shows us his goodness in all this (§ 1063 *Met.*), and we have to regard all this as unrecognised benefactions, to which men ought to pay more heed so as to be moved thereby (§ 720 *Mor.*). It would therefore be serviceable if people made it more their concern, whenever anything serviceable to our necessities, convenience and recreation [p. 98] comes before us, to think of God and to pay heed therein to the footprints of his goodness.

*Whether all the planets are dwellings of living creatures.*

*Every planet is a theatre of the perfections of God.*

§ 67. All the planets are nothing but terrestrial globes (§ 148 *Phys.*) and, just like our earth, are maintained in their changeable state by the sun (§ 46). Therefore, since the earth was made by God to the end that it might be inhabited by men and animals (§ 66), one may readily judge that he has prepared the other planets also as dwellings of the same kind. And in this way is confirmed anew what we conjectured elsewhere on another ground, that the planets are inhabited by men and animals (§ 149 *Phys.*). Now since each planet was made to the end that it may afford animals and men a convenient dwelling, every one of them is a theatre of the divine wisdom, power, goodness and the other perfections of God, and thus the principal purpose for which God made the world is attained everywhere (§ 1045 *Met.*). And from this it is sufficiently evident that the doctrine of present-day astronomers and philosophers concerning the inhabitants of the planets is very conducive to the honour of God.

*Whether the light of the*

*planets produces a noticeable change on the surface of the earth.*

*Why it is very weak.*

*That apart from light the planets have no force*

*by which to act on the earth.*

*When this was falsely ascribed to them, and why.*

§ 68. The light of the planets, even that of the moon itself, is exceedingly weak: [p. 99] for even if it is caught by the most excellent burning-glasses and burning-mirrors and thrown upon the ball of a weather-glass, not the slightest change is thereby produced in the *spirit*; rather, it remains standing motionless (§ 137 *Pt. II Exper.*). Nor is it to be wondered at that it is of so feeble an effect. For it is merely a *reflected* light (§ 141 ff. *Phys.*), which is much scattered by the *reflection* and thereby weakened (§ 21, 43 *Opt.*). Whoever would grasp this more distinctly, let him consider how weak sunlight becomes before it comes from the sun to the planet, and how much this weakened light is weakened anew as it is brought from the planets to the earth (§ 43 *Opt.*). Here, however, one must not forget that from dark bodies too, such as the planets are, not all the light that falls upon them is reflected. Therefore it remains settled that the planets can produce no noticeable changes on the surface of the earth by their light (§ 34). But apart from light no force comes from the planets to the earth. For the force of corporeal things cannot be separated from motion (§ 624 *Met.*); but motion cannot be without matter. And [p. 100] from the planets no matter comes down to the earth. Whoever maintained such a thing would be positing something that he could not prove, and that a person of understanding, for his part, will not grant for nothing. And accordingly no ground remains that the influence of the planets upon the surface of the earth could have in its favour. One therefore easily sees that such a thing was merely invented at a time when the similarity of the planets to the earth (§ 148 *Phys.*) was not understood. For once people had persuaded themselves, from an erroneous delusion, that the fixed stars contributed their share to the constant kinds of weather and to the usual variable ones in the four seasons (§ 64), no other inference could well be drawn than that the difference of the variable kinds of weather must also come from the stars. Now since the fixed stars do not change their position relative to one another, but the planets do (§ 65 *Astron.*), no ground could be found in the former, and therefore recourse had to be had to the latter.

*Whether the moon has a noticeable influence on the earth.*

*Experience that is alleged.*

*Cause that has been assigned.*

*The groundlessness of this is shown.*

§ 69. The moon has a very bright light, and one would suppose that by its light it could produce a noticeable change on the surface of the earth. The astrologers, moreover, have alleged that with the waxing moon the cold and [p. 101] moisture increase, but with the waning moon both decrease. And from this they have sought to explain all manner of changes which they suppose to be grounded in experience: e.g. that oysters, mussels, snails &c. are fuller at the waxing than at the waning moon; that flowers transplanted at the full moon become double; that wood has more moisture at the waxing moon than at the waning; that wethers have more marrow and fat at the former time than at the latter, &c. Namely, it was believed that moonlight makes things moist and increases the cold, just as sunlight dries and warms. Now it is true, indeed, that moonlight is nothing other than sunlight which is merely reflected by the moon (§ 133 *Phys.*); notwithstanding this, however, it might nevertheless undergo some change in the moon. And the learned Jesuit *Honoré Fabri* also undertakes to show that this happens<sup>a</sup>. We know that there is much water in the moon (§ 136 *Phys.*), and since the sun shines upon it on one side for 14 days, because it does not rotate on its axis like the other planets (§ 314 *Astron.*), not only must many vapours [p. 102] rise (§ 248 *Phys.*) in its air (§ 137 *Phys.*), but they must also stand very high in it (§ 247, 252 *Phys.*), that is, recede very far from the centre of the moon and approach the earth, which the moon faces. Now *Honoré Fabri* supposes that these vapours, which are subtilised by the sun (§ 252 *Phys.*), might be brought nearer to the earth than they are to the moon. And then their gravity towards the moon would be diminished, and they would acquire a gravity towards the earth, and consequently they would fall into our air. And in this way the moisture from the moon would be brought down with its light to the surface of the earth. Vapours rise in the air because they are of a lighter kind than it (§ 247 *Phys.*). Therefore it is a hard thing that we should be asked to grant that they rise out beyond the air and, through the subtle celestial air, which they cannot possibly equal, approach the earth. But there is no need for us to dispute much about this. For the experience with burning-glasses and burning-mirrors to which I appealed above (§ 68) shows the contrary. Nor can what was adduced above as examples from experience be confirmed by it with any certainty. [p. 103] *Rohault* assures us<sup>b</sup> that for many years in succession he went to the meat-stalls at the waning and the waxing moon, and always found now much and now little marrow in the bones of the wethers. When he enquired whence the butchers obtained the fat and whence the lean meat, he was given the answer that in the first case the cattle had been kept and fed in the stall for some time, but in the other had been slaughtered soon after being driven a long way overland. Here everyone will grasp where the difference comes from, without needing to call the moon to his aid. The remaining examples are in no better case.

<sup>a</sup> *Phys. Tract. 8. lib. 1. prop. 53. & seqq. p. 250. & seqq.*

<sup>b</sup> *Tract. Phys. part. 2. c. 27. p. m. 319.*

*How one planet benefits another by its light.*

*Strong light*

*of Venus.*

*How strongly the planets illuminate one another.*

*One is the light of the other.*

*Benefit that comes of it.*

§ 70. Now although the light of the planets is so very weak that even the nearest of them can produce nothing noticeable by it in another (§ 68), it is nevertheless sufficient to illuminate it brightly by night. We see this in the moon, which shines very brightly at night, even when it is not full. But what the moon does for our earth, and what can be proved of it with regard to the earth, the moons of Jupiter must likewise perform for Jupiter and the moons of Saturn for Saturn, and it must be possible to prove the same of both with regard to Jupiter [p. 104] and Saturn. Indeed, in general, what is true of the moon with regard to the earth must hold of every planet that is near another. For not only the moons of Jupiter and Saturn but also all the other planets are illuminated by the sun (§ 142 ff. *Phys.*), like our moon (§ 133 *Phys.*), and cast their light back onto others, as the moon casts its light onto our earth. For since the light of Saturn, which is the farthest from us (§ 170 *Phys.*), reaches our earth, as otherwise we could not see it, it must all the more reach the planets that are nearer to it. And this inference holds also in the other similar cases. Indeed, since we can see the moons of Saturn and Jupiter through telescopes (§ 106, 107 *Phys.*), and thus a light, weak indeed but nevertheless some light, comes down to us from them, a far stronger light must be *reflected* from them onto Saturn and Jupiter, since light decreases very greatly (§ 43 *Opt.*) over so great a distance (§ 549 *Astron.*). Indeed, that the light of their moons must be very noticeable on these planets can be recognised from the fact that they are very near them (§ 318, 335 *Astron.*). Indeed, we find on our earth too that Venus, when it is near the earth, illuminates things [p. 105] so strongly that they cast a shadow: as I myself remember having observed that the post-wagon with its horses cast a shadow by its light, no otherwise than when the moon shows itself again some days after the new moon. And it further occurs to me that, when for many years I had my bedchamber to the south, at midnight, when in winter- time Jupiter came onto the meridian, it shone so brightly through the window that a straw which I held against the wall by which the bed stood cast a shadow. If a body is to cast a noticeable shadow, the place where the shadow falls must be noticeably illuminated (§ 3 *Opt.*). And accordingly it is clear that Venus noticeably illuminated the earth, and Jupiter the wall standing opposite the window at a distance of more than 16 feet; consequently that both planets, when they come near the earth, make the nights bright, or rather that the former makes part of the night bright. From these and other grounds needed for the purpose (§ 549 *Astron.* & § 43 *Opt.*) it could very easily be shown in more detail how strongly the remaining planets illuminate one another, if it were necessary to enter into so many prolixities. But what I have adduced suffices to this end, [p. 106] that we may see that one planet is also the light of another (§ 3 *Opt.*), as in particular the moon, and after it Venus and Jupiter, are lights of the earth, which stand it in very good stead, especially in winter-time, when we have long nights. But by becoming the light of another, one planet procures it much benefit. I have already remarked this of the weak light of the fixed stars (§ 33), and the same holds still more of the stronger light of the moon and of the planets mentioned. Daily experience, moreover, speaks of the various advantages that we enjoy from it; and since other planets are just like our earth (§ 148, 149 *Phys.*), the same may be inferred of the rest also.

*How the earth benefits the moon and other planets by its light.*

*That it is a moon to the moon.*

*The earth shines brightly in the moon.*

*The earth, in*

*Venus and the other planets, is a star.*

§ 71. Nowadays it is a settled matter that our earth is a planet which, like the others, moves round the sun in the free celestial air in the midst of them (§ 174, 175 *Phys.*). But it can also be proved that to some it is a moon, to others, on the other hand, a star. It is a well-known matter in astronomy that the horizontal *parallax* of a star is equal to the angle under which the semi-diameter of the earth is seen from the star, or when the eye is placed in the star (§ 817 *Astron. Lat.*). Now the horizontal *parallax* of the moon, [p. 107] when it is greatest, is 1° 1′ 25″<sup>a</sup>; consequently the earth is seen in the moon, when the moon is nearest to it, under an angle of 2° 2′ 50″, that is, very nearly under an angle of 2° 3′. The diameter of the moon at this same distance is 33′ 30″.
