And in this way the apparent magnitude of the moon is to the apparent magnitude of the earth as 33½ to 123, or 67 to 246 (§ 74, 75 *Arithm.*). Accordingly the earth appears in the moon almost 3⁵⁄₇ times, or very nearly four times, as large as the moon appears on the surface of the earth. Now it is known that the half of the surface of the earth which can be surveyed at once from such a distance contains 4644000 square miles (§ 17 *Geogr.*). Therefore the area of the earth which appears in the moon as large as the moon appears to us is 1161000 square miles, each of which is a mile long and just as broad; consequently an area that is a mile long and broad appears no larger than ¹⁄₁₁₆₁₀₀₀ of the full moon does to us, that is, smaller still than a millionth part of it. It is therefore not possible that one could recognise and distinguish a single body on the surface of the earth from the moon; consequently the whole surface of the earth appears indistinct (§ 21, ch. 1 *Log.*). Meanwhile, [p. 108] since the earth and the bodies found upon it throw back the light, and the rays *reflected* from one body have this colour, those from others another, while the variously coloured rays mix with one another and therefore no longer represent colours but light (§ 158 *Pt. II Exper.*), the earth too must look light all over, as the moon does to us. And in this way the earth appears in the moon like a bright shining plate four times as large as the moon, in which, however, nothing distinct can be discerned; consequently the earth is a moon to the moon. But the light of the earth in the moon is also fourteen times as strong as moonlight on the earth (§ 555 *Astron.*), so that it can even be seen on the earth itself (§ 556 *Astron.*). And in such a way the earth is a light to the moon, and indeed a still greater light than the moon is to us, which stands it in particularly good stead, because in it there is night for 14 days at a time, during which it has the earthlight to rejoice in. Accordingly the earth benefits the moon still more by its light than the moon benefits it by its own. Again, the diameter of the earth is smaller by a fourth part than the diameter of Venus (§ 561 *Astron.*). Therefore the earth appears in this planet still [p. 109] smaller than Venus appears to us, and consequently, since it hovers in the free celestial air just as she does, it appears nothing other than a star. The diameter of the earth is greater by a third than the diameter of Mars (§  *cit.*). Now since in this way the diameter of the earth appears in Mars greater by **⅓** than his diameter appears to us, whereas that of Venus appears to us greater by ¼ than ours appears to her, the earth is very nearly to this planet what Venus is to us. Accordingly the earth can procure for Venus and Mars as much benefit with its light as Venus and Jupiter procure for us with their advantage (§ 70).

<sup>a</sup> *Phil. de la Hire in Tabb. Astron. n. XVIII. p. 27.*

*How the planets have given occasion to the sciences and to erudition.*

*What erudition is.*

*Prudence of the Chinese.*

*Men are encouraged by the planets to the use of reason.*

§ 72. The planets, by their changing position in the heavens, have encouraged men to enquire into their motion. But since in astronomy one cannot get on with this before one has mastered geometry and arithmetic, this has further been a motive why mathematics too, to which these two sciences properly belong, has been investigated and little by little *perfected* more and more. Since, further, astronomy makes use of optical grounds, it has given occasion to these also. And after it had been found that the astronomers [p. 110] had perceived progress in the knowledge of nature, men also turned their thoughts to physics. And so, little by little, men arrived at the sciences. Indeed, this has also given occasion to erudition, that is, to historical knowledge and to those disciplines which are grasped by the memory, just as the sciences are grasped by the understanding. Hence one also finds that those peoples among whom astronomy was held in esteem distinguished themselves above others in the sciences and disciplines. The ancient Chinese are famed for their great prudence in political matters, and I have found that the practice of their moral doctrine and art of government was excellently grounded in the nature of the soul, such as one does not meet with among other peoples. But the great astronomer *Johannes Hevelius*<sup>a</sup> has also proved, from the famous Jesuit *Martini*'s *Historia Sinica*, that in most ancient times they surpassed all peoples in astronomy, namely at the very time when they so greatly distinguished themselves in moral and political prudence. One could also demonstrate the same of more recent times among ourselves, how astronomy gave occasion for the sciences and erudition to come once more into [p. 111] flourishing; but I will set this aside to avoid prolixity. One sees already from what has been said that God has encouraged men on the surface of the earth, by means of the planets, to the right use of their reason. Whoever considers how wonderfully everything in the world is connected together (§ 543 *Met.*), and how at times, in an altogether singular and unexpected way, one thing continually gives occasion to another, will not find this strange.

<sup>a</sup> *in Præfat. ad T. I. Machinæ cœlestis.*

*How the excellence of human reason came to be recognised.*

*God's earnest will that reason should be used.*

§ 73. Since men at length came so far in the knowledge of the course of the heavenly bodies that they could foretell with certainty what would happen in future, they recognised the excellence of reason and were convinced by an infallible proof how far one can go, by the right use of reason, in the knowledge of natural things. And therefore it is no wonder that men were afterwards encouraged by astronomy to make an experiment in other sciences as well. Indeed, since God has used such remote means to encourage men to the use of reason, and has shown them such a way, whereby they could bring to light what is most hidden and at the same time make infallible proofs of how far the truth had been brought to [p. 112] light, one sees from this the earnestness of God that man should exercise himself in the right use of reason (§ 1007 *Met.*) and at the same time recognise his goodness (§ 1063 *Met.*) in having endowed him with reason. Accordingly those act contrary to the will of God who will not exercise themselves in the use of reason, and those who despise reason despise a precious benefaction of God.

*The planets are a theatre of the divine perfection.*

*Each planet is an entirely particular theatre.*

*The planets are inhabited.*

§ 74. On our earth there is found an exceedingly great multitude of kinds of particular bodies in the three so-called kingdoms of nature: the kingdom of minerals, that of plants, and that of men and animals. To the first are reckoned all lifeless things that are found on the earth and for the most part are dug out of it. To the second belongs everything that grows out of the earth, such as trees, shrubs, herbs, &c. To the last comes everything that lives and moves, such as all birds, fishes, four-footed animals, all vermin, and finally man himself. Whoever opens a natural history will be astonished at the multitude of the different kinds of things in all three kingdoms, and at the difference that remains among them notwithstanding their similarity. It were to be wished that, since the writings belonging to natural history are in part rare, and for the most part very costly and prolix, [p. 113] someone would bring them into an order for convenient use, so that more people might learn to know the riches of the works of God. Now since the remaining planets are altogether just such bodies as our earth (§ 148 *Phys.*), and some of them larger than it (§ 561 *Astron.*), but are likewise, like it, adorned with all manner of particular kinds of bodies (§ 149 *Phys.*), there is no doubt that no smaller store of different kinds of bodies will be met with in them than on and in the surface of the earth. Yet since we not only know that God has very greatly loved difference in nature (§ 586, 875 *Met.*), but also recognise in particular that the force of sunlight manifests itself in different planets in an entirely particular manner (§ 549 *Astron.* & § 43 *Opt.*), and at the same time see on the surface of the earth that things are of different kinds in different countries according to the difference in the force of sunlight, everyone will very readily grant that the kinds of things in one planet are not entirely as they are in another, and hence that God, who knows them all, observes for himself an infinite difference among them in the whole world. Now at the origin of each thing, and for as long as it lasts, all manner of changeable things take place, and each has its [p. 114] cause (§ 29, 30 *Met.*) through which it becomes actual, and gives us to know God's wisdom (§ 14, 17), power (§ 19), reason (§ 20), goodness (§ 22) &c. Therefore each planet is a theatre of the divine perfections, as the principal purpose of the world requires (§ 8), and each one convinces its rational inhabitants that God fully attains that purpose in everything. And so we see on yet another ground that it is not without reason that inhabitants are placed in the planets.

### Das VIII. Capitel. Von dem Baue der Welt. — Chapter VIII. On the Structure of the World.

*Unspeakable size of the world.*

*What it effects in the mind of man.*

§ 75. In former times it was believed that the world came to an end at the fixed stars, and it was looked upon with very small eyes (§ 3, 5). But after *Copernicus* had shown that around our sun, besides the earth, five further primary planets move (§ 23); after *Giordano Bruno*, having considered that the fixed stars were nothing but suns (§ 153 *Phys.*), had also conjectured that around them there is a great space in which planets move, as they do around our sun (§ 155 *Phys.*); and after the multitude of the fixed stars which have been discovered through magnifying glasses, and their different distances from the earth (§ [p. 115] 178 *Phys.*), and indeed also the great space that lies between those of equal magnitude, which presumably stand at almost the same distance from the earth, had confirmed all this still further, the world too came to be looked upon with entirely different eyes than before. For if one considers the unspeakable number of the fixed stars (§ 108 *Phys.*), and reflects at the same time that around each one there is to be found as great a structure as we have found around our sun (§ 37), one will obtain an entirely different picture of the world from that which one had before, and will be astonished at the size of the world: for not only our senses and imagination, but also our understanding and our reason lose themselves in infinity, since we do not see where we can find an end.

*Why God made so large a world.*

*How far God has therein also had regard to himself.*

*Prejudice that arises here.*

§ 76. Now since the prodigious size of the world brings man to the knowledge of the magnitude of the divine perfections, and in particular sets their immensity before his eyes (§ 38 ff.), while the principal purpose which God had with the world is none other than this, that his invisible being, with the infinite perfections proper to it, should be beheld as in a mirror (§ 1045 *Met.*), one easily sees why God has made the world so large. A God of infinite understanding, infinite wisdom and immeasurable power must bring forth a work [p. 116] that is of unspeakable size, if the greatness of his art is to be gathered from it. When an artist makes a lesser work, although he could make a far better one, one can indeed see from it that he is a good artist; but one cannot yet judge the greatness of his art from it, and he himself does not take so great a pleasure in it as in that which he holds to be his masterpiece. And hence, although no creature can survey or think through the size of the world, and one might therefore suppose, with respect to creatures, that God had had no need to make the world of so enormous a size (§ 8), he has nevertheless, with respect to himself, made it of so astonishing a size. For since he surveys it entire, from the greatest down to the smallest (§ 964 *Met.*), he is also in a position to judge that he has made everything in the best and most magnificent manner, and finds his pleasure therein (§ 404 *Met.*). I could moreover point out a prejudice which arises in this scruple from the notion that everything in the world were directed towards our earth, the contrary of which I have nevertheless already proved (§ 28); but I will set it aside for this time, because what I have adduced concerning the size of the world with respect to God is sufficient [p. 117] to remove the doubt that has been raised. And now one understands still better why the most learned among the theologians in England assent to the **new structure of the world** (§ 36).

*Whether there is just such a space with planets about the fixed stars as about our sun.*

*Kepler's objection.*

*It is set forth distinctly.*

*Wherein Kepler erred.*

*The contrary is confirmed.*

*A doubt is met.*

*Why one cannot arrive at complete certainty here.*

**§ 77. Kepler**<sup>a</sup>, who did indeed recognise that the planets are bodies just like our earth, and that they are inhabited as it is, nevertheless<sup>b</sup> doubted whether there is a structure of planets about every fixed star as there is about our sun. Therefore, since he is held, and with great right, in great esteem where it is a matter of such truths as are maintained by astronomers, we have cause to examine more closely what he objects, and to confirm by further grounds the proposition itself, which so uncommonly enlarges the honour of God (§ 67) and makes the world so befitting to his essence (§ 75). It is accordingly to be noted that, if there were such a planetary space about every fixed star as there is about our sun, only very few fixed stars with their planets could stand round about our planetary space: in **Kepler's** opinion, no more than twelve. Whoever wishes to be assured of this without having to engage in geometrical [p. 118] demonstrations, let him picture each sun, or each fixed star with its planetary space, as a sphere, just as we too above (§ 37), with another purpose, represented to ourselves the planetary space about our sun under this image. It is therefore just the same as if we were to place other spheres of equal size about one sphere. Therefore, if he does this, he will find that only a few can be placed round about one sphere. Now we must imagine that the fixed stars are at the centre of these spheres. In this way we obtain very few stars that are at the same distance from our sun. If one now sets still others of the very same size against the spheres that have been placed round about the one, and again imagines that there is a fixed star at their centres, they soon come to be two, three, four times &c. as far away as the first. All this is correct and cannot be called into doubt. Now it is known from optics that, if a body (one is speaking, however, of those which are some distance away) is twice as far away as another, it appears only half as large as the other. If it is three times as far away, it appears only as large as the third part of the other, and so on (§ 205 *Opt. Lat.*). [p. 119] Accordingly **Kepler** supposes that the fixed stars which were twice as far away as the first must also appear only half as large as they, and that those which were three times as far removed as the first must look equal to only the third part of the first. The magnitude of the fixed stars of the first magnitude is so small that it can neither be enlarged by a telescope nor, in this enlargement, can the angle under which they are seen be measured (§ 570 *Astron.*). Therefore, if they were to decrease in their apparent magnitude in this manner, as ½, **⅓**, ¼, **⅕** &c., they would soon become so small that we could no longer see any of them; consequently very few stars would be visible in the heavens: which, however, is contrary to experience, which shows not only a very great number to the naked eye, but in particular an inexpressible multitude through telescopes. **Kepler** would be right if the constitution of bodies that have their own light were no other than that of dark bodies. These can no longer be seen as soon as their image in the eye becomes so small that it is to be reckoned equal to a point. And this would certainly have to happen very soon with the stars if there were such a planetary space about them as about our sun. But luminous bodies [p. 120] can be seen as long as the light remains strong enough to affect the eye perceptibly, however small they may appear in themselves. And that this holds of the stars is shown by experience, according to which they do not appear enlarged through even the very largest telescopes, which nevertheless enlarge the planets uncommonly; rather, the stars that look large, as well as the others which one takes to be smaller with the naked eye, remain only like indivisible points (§ 570 *Astron.*). Accordingly **Kepler** has objected nothing by which the **new structure of the world**, as it is customarily called especially in England, would be overthrown. But although the conjectures which in nature are taken from the similarity of things are very strong, and yield little to the certainty of a proof, as one sees abundantly in astronomy in particular when one has mastered its whole course, yet beyond this the excellent *mathematician* and late *Astronomiæ Professor Savilianus* at Oxford, *Gregory*<sup>a′</sup>, has already shown that the differing magnitude of the fixed stars very well [p. 121] agrees with the new structure of the world. For since a sphere can be touched all round by thirteen others of equal size, there must be 13 fixed stars of the first magnitude, provided they are suns which have a planetary space about them like our sun. Now although the *astronomers* do not entirely agree with one another in judging the magnitude of the stars, as may be seen from *Hevelius's Catalogus fixarum*<sup>b′</sup>, and likewise from the most complete of all, which **Flamsteed** has given<sup>c</sup>, yet it is beyond dispute of 13 that they are of the first magnitude. Now since, further, 4 times 13, or 52, spheres can still be placed around these 13, there must be 52 stars of the second magnitude. Now *Hevelius* counts 50 of them, *Tycho*, and with him **Kepler**, 58. Therefore, although men are not entirely agreed in judging the magnitude of the fixed stars, one nevertheless sees this much, that the number given for those of the second magnitude comes fairly near to that which the new structure of the world requires. Now it is true that the fixed stars of the second magnitude do not stand in quite such an order behind and [p. 122] beside one another as ought to be the case if they were suns having as great a space of the world about them as ours; but since it holds fairly well with those of the first magnitude, inasmuch as 6 of them are seen in the zodiac, 3 towards the north and 4 towards the south, this need not trouble us. For since we find that the planets are of very different sizes, and also that the small and the large stand among one another (§ 561 *Astron.*), one cannot presume either, especially since nature admits no complete similarity (§ 587 *Met.*), that all suns are of equal size. And for this very reason it is also not necessary that there be just as many planets about every sun as about ours, nor that they be of the same size as ours and occupy just as great a space. For the rest, it is not to be wondered at that in this respect one cannot have complete certainty and perceive everything with the desired distinctness. What is so far removed from us, and of which so little can be observed, certainly cannot be known with such distinctness as other things, which we can either look upon as they are because of their nearness, or of which, because their distance is not too great, we observe much from which their constitution may be inferred; [p. 123] since everything must be settled either by experience or by reason through correct inferences. Up to now no grounds of reason are known to us by which we could get so far.

<sup>a</sup> *Epist. 156. f. 251.*

<sup>b</sup> *Epist. Astron. Copernic. lib. 1. p. 35. & seqq.*

<sup>a′</sup> *in Element. Astronom. Phys. & Geom. lib. 2. prop. 21. f. 159.*

<sup>b′</sup> *in Prodomo Astronomiæ f. 143. & seqq.*

<sup>c</sup> *in Historia cœlesti Britannica part. 1. f. 1. & seqq.*

*Why the planets move about their axis.*

*That day and night may alternate,*

*and the planets on both sides*

*be habitable.*

*That other causes still lie hidden.*

§ 78. The earth (§ 175 *Phys.*) and the other planets (§ 146 *Phys.*) move about their axis, whereby it is brought about that the sun shines upon them by degrees all round. For since all cosmic bodies have a round figure like the earth (§ 182 *Phys.*), the sun can shine upon only about half of a planet at once. But if the planet turns about its axis, the sun's light passes ever by degrees from one part of the surface to another. And from this arises the alternation of day and night. One sees, accordingly, that God uses the motion about the axis to this end, that day and night may alternate. But since sunlight at the same time warms the surface of the earth (§ 130 *Phys.*), and its heat is the very thing by which the earth is made fertile and maintained in its changeable state (§ 44), the motion about the axis is the means by which the earth and the planets are made habitable on both sides. If the earth and the planets had no [p. 124] motion about their axis, there would remain perpetual night on one side, and in that perpetual darkness no other state could obtain than the one we described above, as it would be if the sun were taken away altogether (§ 44). But since the sun too, which has light on all sides, moves about its axis (§ 117 *Phys.*), this motion must have some other significance in its case than in that of the earth and the other planets; and it may therefore well be that in the case of the earth too it has some further significance: which, however, cannot well be determined at present. For we should perceive this better if it had first been settled what causes the motions of the cosmic bodies have: which, however, has not yet been brought to certainty at present.

*Benefit of the alternation of day and night:*

*in the torrid zone.*

*Error of the ancients.*

*General benefit in all places on the earth.*

*How it is known more distinctly.*

*That the other planets also partake of it.*

*That there are inhabitants in the planets.*

§ 79. In the torrid zone on the surface of the earth there is an unbearable heat (§ 36 *Geogr.*), as is also confirmed by the experience of those Europeans who have travelled thither by sea. Now if the sun shone continuously, the heat there would become so great that no one could remain there, especially in the place where it would be perpetual noon. But since, on account of the motion of the earth about its axis, the sun does not [p. 125] shine with equal warmth the whole day long, and after this, when it sets, stays away again for a good while, the air that has been heated during the day can cool again towards evening and through the night, and so the heat abates again somewhat. And here it is of particular advantage that throughout the whole year in this zone of the world day and night remain equal to one another (§ 42, 57 *Geogr.*), of which more shall follow hereafter. The ancients, who paid no attention to this, therefore also imagined that the torrid zone of land was uninhabited; of which, however, experience shows the contrary, since not only do seafarers hold out in these warm places at sea, and could hold out still better if only they did not lack food and drink, but many inhabited lands are also met with there, in which no small wealth of natural goods is found. One finds, moreover, in the alternation of day and night the benefit that men and animals who have grown tired during the day can refresh themselves again by sleep. It is indeed true that one can also sleep by day and work by night; but just as not all work can be done as well by candlelight as [p. 126] by day, indeed some work is so constituted that it can be undertaken only by day, such as the work in the garden, in the field and in the forest, so it is also certain that one sleeps better by night than by day, because everything is much stiller and quieter, since neither men nor animals make a noise, nor does the light hinder sleep. Meanwhile the night, on the contrary, serves for certain tasks that cannot well be undertaken by day, such as occur in fowling and fishing. Whoever in human life pays attention to his own tasks, as no less to the tasks of other people, and considers therewith what advantage it brings him that it is dark or light, will perceive the benefit of the alternation of day and night from day to day through new proofs. Since we have no cause to doubt that the other planets too are inhabited like our earth (§ 67), the alternation of day and night has with them the very same benefit that we have from it on the earth. Indeed, since we find that the motion of the earth about its axis, from which the alternation of day and night comes, is of such consequence on the earth, and is to be regarded as a means (§ 1032 [p. 127] *Met.*), by which God attains his end, namely that the earth is habitable all round and everywhere (§ 66), but we find that the other planets, through their motions about their axes, have the very same alternations of day and night (§ 77), then what we have already conjectured on other grounds (§ 149 *Phys.*) and corroborated (§ 67) is thereby confirmed anew, namely that they too are adorned with plants and inhabited by men and animals. For why should God do in the planets everything that he undertakes on the earth to the end that it may be habitable, if he did not wish that they too should be habitable? One sees accordingly that the opinion of **Kepler**<sup>a</sup>, *Huygens*<sup>b</sup> and others, who take the planets to be inhabited terrestrial globes, agrees excellently with other truths, and is therefore confirmed more and more, the more one investigates the truths connected with it.

<sup>a</sup> *Epist. 156. f. 25.*

<sup>b</sup> *in Cosmotheoro lib. 1. p. m. 29.*

*Why the secondary planets do not move about their axes.*

*The doubt is removed.*

*Why the moon has no need of a motion about its axis.*

*A further doubt is removed.*

*How the heat in the moon is preserved.*

*First cause.*

*Second.*

*Third.*

*How far this may also be applied to the moons of Saturn and Jupiter.*

*Doubt concerning the outermost*

*moon of Saturn is removed.*

*First ground.*

*Second ground.*

*Third ground.*

§ 80. The moon, which as a secondary planet moves not by itself alone about the sun, but rather merely about the earth as [p. 128] a primary planet (§ 72 *Phys.*), has, to all appearance, no motion about its axis (§ 314 *Astron.*). But since we have nevertheless declared it to be inhabited no less than the other planets (§ 149 *Phys.*), it seems as if what we have advanced concerning the motion of the planets about their axes (§ 78) would not square with it, or, in case one could not contradict that, as if one must not declare the moon to be inhabited. This, indeed, has some plausibility at first, but it soon vanishes when one reflects carefully on the matter. The moon, as a spherical body, is always half illuminated, stand where it will in the sky, except when it is eclipsed in the shadow of the earth (§ 259 *Astron.*); but we see now nothing, now only something of the illuminated part, according to the position it has with respect to the earth. Never do we see the illuminated part whole except when the earth stands midway between it and the sun, as is wont to happen at full moon, when the sun sets as the moon rises. The observations of the astronomers testify that the moon constantly turns one side towards the earth. And accordingly it is clear that the moon is illuminated by degrees all round, and that therefore every day in one [p. 129] place the sun rises, but in another it sets. This illumination is governed by its position with respect to the earth, and this arises from its motion round the earth, which is completed in about 29 days if one at the same time pays attention to the sun (§ 492 *Astron.*). Therefore the alternations of light and darkness, or of day and night, which in the primary planets are obtained by the motion about their axis (§ 78), come about through the motion round the earth; and in this way it was not necessary that the moon should move about its axis, since it already obtains by another means what it would have had to expect from that motion. And this agrees with the general maxim of nature, which is grounded in wisdom (§ 1049 *Met.*), namely that nothing happens in it for nothing. It is possible for the motion of the moon round the earth to take the place of the motion about its axis, because it does not need too long a time before it comes round the earth (§ 492 *Astron.*). Were whole years needed for this, it could no longer be done, since the overlong night would make it far too cold. Perhaps some might imagine that this doubt still remained, even though the moon spends only a month [p. 130] before it comes round the earth, since in it there is day for as long as 15 days, and night for almost as long. But, not to mention that the earthlight illuminates the moon very brightly when it is night there (§ 71), it is to be noted that, since the sun shines for almost 15 days in succession without interruption, and at that time there is almost constantly clear weather (§ 138 *Phys.*), it must also become very warm indeed in the moon (§ 230 *Phys.*): just as we also find on the surface of the earth that in places situated far towards the north it often becomes warmer than with us, on account of the great length of the day. Now heat does not go away all at once, but is lost little by little (§ 76 *Phys.*). Therefore the great heat cannot be lost again at once in the lunar night either. Heat is very greatly promoted by warm winds (§ 244 *Phys.*), and we find that for this reason warm nights come on the surface of the earth too, in which, as a rule, more grows than in several days. Therefore in the moon also the warm winds can prevent the air from cooling too greatly in the long night. And one has cause to suppose such winds there. For since the sun shines without interruption for almost 15 days on the air of the moon from one side [p. 131] and greatly warms both it and the soil, there arises from this a wind which carries the warm air over to the other side, where it is dark or night (§ 206 *Phys.*). Mr *Newton*<sup>a</sup> has proved that the moon is much denser and heavier than the earth, although the latter is almost 52 times larger than it (§ 554 *Astron.*). I recall that Mr von Tschirnhaus therefore conjectured that the moon must have many rocks where we see the great mountains (§ 135 *Phys.*). Now as this conjecture is not without ground, while on the other hand it is known from experience on our earth that in such places where rocks and much sand are found it becomes warmer than in others, we have a cause why the heat in the moon can increase greatly, so that it is not entirely lost through a long night of 14 days. As the moon moves round our earth and excludes the sun, in just such a way the five moons of Saturn and the four moons of Jupiter also move round their primary planets (§ 106, 107 *Phys.*). Therefore, as in the moon the motion round the earth accomplishes that which in [p. 132] the primary planets is obtained by the motion about their axis, there is no doubt why the alternation of light and darkness should not likewise come about in the moons of Saturn and of Jupiter through their moving round their primary planets. Now since the first of the moons of Jupiter comes round Jupiter in one day 18½ hours, the second in 3 days 13 hours, the third in 7 days 4 hours, the fourth in 16 days 18 hours; and the first of the moons of Saturn comes round Saturn in one day 21 hours, the second in 2 days 18 hours, the third in 4 days 14 hours, the fourth in 16 days, the fifth in 74 days 8 hours (§ 317, 330 *Astron.*), day and night alternate more quickly in all of them, except the last moon of Saturn, than in our moon. For example, in the first moon of Jupiter the night under the line is not much longer than 21 hours; in the first moon of Saturn not much longer than 22½ hours. And this is of excellent advantage to these planets, since on account of their distance from the sun (§ 549 *Astron.*) they are not illuminated as strongly as our moon (§ 43 *Opt.*). Now it is indeed true that the outermost of the moons of Saturn seems to be very badly off, [p. 133] if it has no motion about its axis. For on account of the great distance of Saturn from the earth (§ 549 *Astron.*) the sun must shine very weakly in it (§ 43 *Opt.*), especially when it roams still far out beyond Saturn; and yet under the line a night there lasts 37 days. But this does not yet mean that we may take this moon for a desolate lump. For, in the first place, we have no reason at all why we should maintain that in all cosmic bodies the particular kinds of bodies must be the same as those we meet with on the surface of the earth; rather, not only the proposition of indiscernibles (§ 589 *Met.*), but also the difference of the animals, the plants, and indeed of men themselves in the various parts of the surface of the earth where there is a noticeable difference in heat and cold, brings us to quite other thoughts. Next, we shall see shortly hereafter, by the example of the lands lying to the north of us (§ 83), that God has means to increase the heat when the sunshine is weak. And finally, whoever is versed in geography will find in the lands lying towards the north just such a state as may perhaps obtain in the outermost moon of Saturn, but will notwithstanding find that men [p. 134] and animals have their dwelling there, although one who should judge merely by the experience he has in his own country might think it impossible to live there, as the ancients also imagined. For who, for example, could persuade himself that men could live contentedly under snow lying house-high above them, in caves of the earth, without a stove or chimney? And yet the example of the Lapps teaches us this.

<sup>a</sup> Princip. Phil. Nat. Mathem. lib. 3. prop. 37. Cor. 3. 4. 5. p. m. 430.

*Why the planets move about their axes in unequal times.*

*Why we cannot fathom*

*all of it.*

§ 81. Venus, Mars and our earth move about their axes in nearly the same time (§ 146, 174 *Phys.*); Jupiter, on the other hand, needs less than 10 hours for it, somewhat more than a third part but less than half of the time which our earth takes. Jupiter is much further from the sun than Mars, the earth and Venus (§ 549 *Astron.*), and therefore has less of the force of its light to enjoy (§ 43 *Opt.*). Therefore it stands it in very good stead that the alternations of day and night are very rapid, for otherwise the little heat which the sun causes during the day would soon be destroyed again by the long nights. But there may indeed be much more good contained in the motion about the axis that we do not attain to, because we are unable to inspect anything of the particular state of the planets for ourselves, [p. 135] but must guess at everything by conjectures. Nor has science yet risen so far that we could say what advantage may follow for a cosmic body from the motion about its axis.

*Purposes of the annual motion of the planets round the sun.*

*A new ground that the planets are inhabited.*

§ 82. Our earth (§ 174 *Phys.*) and all the primary planets, Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus and Mercury, move round the sun in a certain time (§ 171, 173 *Phys.*), and carry their secondary planets or moons round with them at the same time. This motion makes the difference of the four seasons and of the constant kinds of weather in them (§ 232 *Phys.*). It is indeed not to be denied that on the surface of the earth this arises chiefly from the fact that the earth moves round the sun in the ecliptic and not in the *equator*, that is, in an orbit which cuts the *equator* or the line at an oblique angle (§ *cit.*); but it is also known that the other planets have their particular orbits, which, for each one at a particular angle, cut the ecliptic and consequently also the *equator* (§ 464 *Astron.*). Therefore the sun must cause in the other planets just the same difference of the seasons and of the constant kinds of weather that we perceive on the surface of the earth. [p. 136] Now, further, just as the alternation of the seasons makes the earth habitable on both sides, on the northern as well as on the southern side (§ 39 *Geogr.*), so the same is likewise achieved in the other planets in a similar way. But precisely because we find that in the other planets, as well as on our earth, summer and winter alternate, and that this alternation, moreover, takes place on the earth to the end that it may be habitable on both sides (§ 1007 *Met.*), the opinion that the planets are all inhabited is thereby confirmed anew (§ 149 *Phys.*). For why should the four seasons with their constant kinds of weather alternate in them merely to pass the time, when on the earth they have so glorious a benefit, and the planets are so constituted that the very same benefit can be attained in them too (§ 148 *Phys.*)?

*What their inequality signifies.*

*Illustrated by what happens on the earth.*

*Particular traces of the wisdom of God.*

§ 83. Saturn goes round the sun in nearly 30 years, Jupiter in 12, Venus in 1½, Mercury in 3 months. Therefore the four seasons in the various planets are of very different length. In Saturn the summer lasts 7½ years, and each of the other seasons is just as long; in Jupiter [p. 137] this length is 3, in Mars half a year, in Venus, on the other hand, not fully 2 months, in Mercury scarcely 22 days. Mercury is very near the sun (§ 486 *Astron.*), and the sun shines very warm there (§ 43 *Opt.*). And if one were to investigate everything exactly, it would appear that winter there is still to be reckoned equal to a summer in another planet, and consequently that there is as little a true winter there as with us in the torrid zone. The further one goes from the sun, the more this difference signifies, because the difference of the sunshine in the various seasons becomes ever more noticeable. Thus, since Saturn is very many times further from the sun than the earth (§ 486 *Astron.*), the sun also shines there very much more weakly than with us (§ 43 *Opt.*). Therefore it is very fitting that the summer is short where it is very warm, but long where it is not so warm. We find also on the surface of our earth that God has so arranged it. Towards the north, where one is either near the frigid zone or actually within it, it does not grow warm as soon as with us, and the warm days also do not last so long. On the other hand, not only does the experience of those who come to us from those lands testify that it is there [p. 138] much warmer in the hot summer days than with us; but, since growth is also quicker there, and the experience of gardening, on the other hand, clearly shows that greater heat hastens it, one can at the same time gauge the greater heat from this. And God makes use for this purpose not only of the motion in the ecliptic, whereby the days grow ever longer the further one goes towards the north (§ 51 *Geogr.*), but also of other causes, such as the great rocks and stony crags, and likewise the abundance of sand, whereby the heat is increased and retained longer. The wisdom of God shows itself everywhere, since one sees particular ways which he employs to make the earth habitable in many places (§ 1036 *Met.*). And there is no doubt that in the other planets also God does not lack means to attain this end, notwithstanding that we grow uneasy when we are to say how it may be attained. But it is precisely such reflections that give us a higher concept of the wisdom of God. For the more impossible it seems how a purpose could be attained, the more we delight in the wisdom of God when at last we nevertheless find how he has devised a means by which he attains it.

*An objection is met.*

§ 84. It is indeed true that the alternation [p. 139] of the four seasons in the various planets, and their difference in the various parts of the surface of the earth, seem to have their necessary causes, namely the motion about the sun and the inclination of the ecliptic to the *equator*. For since the distant planets have a very long path about the sun, they also come round later than the others, which have a short path to run about it. In like manner, because the ecliptic is inclined to the *equator*, the days grow ever longer towards the north, and, because the sun shines longer, it quickly grows warm, although the sun shines weakly. But this is precisely the common mistake, that one at once declares a thing necessary and, with *Spinoza*, would exclude wisdom wherever some things are so connected with one another that one cannot choose the one without the other, or indeed would even declare a thing necessary when the means is sufficient to attain the purpose. And I wonder not a little how people who would be theologians can go so far astray, as those have actually done who began a dispute with me without cause, and how others can keep silent at the attempt to *canonise* Spinozist errors.

*Why the planets stand so far from one another.*

*A doubt is met.*

*Whether Mars has moons about it.*

*Particular caution in judging the wisdom of God.*

§ 84. The planets which move round the [p. 140] sun stand very far from one another. If one divides the distance of the earth from the sun into 10 parts, the distance of Mercury from it receives 4 of them, that of Venus 7, of Mars 15, of Jupiter 52, of Saturn 95 (§ 486 *Astron.*). Accordingly, if one imagines the centres of all the planets to stand in a line drawn from the centre of the sun to the centre of Saturn, and the whole line to be divided into 95 parts, then Mercury stands at the end of the fourth, Venus at the end of the seventh, the earth at the end of the tenth, Mars at the end of the fifteenth, Jupiter at the end of the fifty-second, and finally Saturn at the end of the ninety- fifth. In this way Mercury and Venus stand 3 parts apart, Venus and the earth likewise 3, the earth and Mars 5, Mars and Jupiter 37, Jupiter and Saturn 43. All the planets move about the sun, and therefore they must stand at different distances from one another, so that one does not come too near another in its motion. In particular it is known that, since they are all together illuminated by the sun (§ 142 f. *Phys.*), they cast behind them a shadow which [p. 141] lies opposite the sun (§ 50 *Opt.*). Therefore they must stand so far from one another that none is troublesome to another through its shadow. The nearer the planets are placed to one another, the more easily can one come into the shadow of another, and the more deeply fall into it. Therefore we also find that the cosmic bodies which are large, as Saturn and Jupiter (§ 561 *Astron.*), stand much farther away from one another than the others, which are smaller, because they cast a longer shadow than the other, small ones (§ 53 *Opt.*). To this is added that Saturn and Jupiter at the same time have many moons which move about them (§ 106, 107 *Phys.*), and hence regard must be had not only that these have room in their motion, but also that neither they fall into the shadow of the nearest primary planet, nor this into theirs. This purpose is confirmed by our finding that the space between Saturn and Jupiter is greater than that between Jupiter and Mars, while Saturn has five moons about it (§ 107 *Phys.*), Jupiter only four (§ 106 *Phys.*), and moreover Saturn is quite uncommonly larger than Mars (§ 561 *Astron.*). The distance between the earth and Mars seems to be contrary to this. For the space between [p. 142] the two is greater than that between the earth and Venus, which is the same as the space between Venus and Mercury, and yet Mars is much smaller than Venus. With the distance between Venus and Mercury there is no great difficulty, for Venus is much larger than the earth: but with the space between the earth and Mars it will not appear how the doubt is to be removed. If, accordingly, the foregoing grounds are weighed, it may be conjectured that Mars too has one or perhaps several moons about it, which, however, in proportion to its size are so small that we on the earth cannot discover them even with our telescopes. It may also be that they *reflect* the light very poorly, and hence on account of the weakness of the light cannot be *observed*, even if their size permitted it, as we have remarked the contrary in the case of the fixed stars (§ 16). I have so much the less hesitation in bringing forward this conjecture, because Mr *Derham*<sup>a</sup> too has already granted it a place. And it is further strengthened by our finding that the earth has one moon, Jupiter four moons, [p. 143] and Saturn five, consequently the number of the moons increases with the distance from the sun. For thus it does not seem credible that Mars should have no moon at all, and Jupiter at once four, especially since there is room for them. But even if one should hesitate to give assent to this conjecture, in that one wished to regard it as a thing invented merely to please one's own opinion, our proposition which we confirmed above is not on that account overthrown. For God, by his infinite knowledge which he has of every thing (§ 964 *Met.*), can have more than one purpose in a thing, and seek to attain many at once by a single means. Therefore it is possible that in the contemplation of nature, when we inquire after the purposes, something may at times occur to us which will not agree with respect to one, although it agrees very well when one regards them all together. This I remark once for all, so that no one may let himself be deterred from investigating the purposes of natural things, and from using the knowledge of nature in accordance with its principal purpose (§ 8).

<sup>a</sup> *Astro-theology lib. 7. c. 5. p. m. 185.*

*Why the planets stand so*

*very far apart from one another.*

*First cause.*

*Second cause.*

§ 86. If we wished to determine the length of the shadow which a cosmic body casts while [p. 144] it is illuminated by the sun (§ 50 *Opt.*), by calculation (§ 142 *Lat. Opt.*), it would appear that their distance from one another is much greater than the length of the shadow of the lower one, which stands nearer the sun, and that consequently the distance could be much smaller than is required in order that one may not come into the shadow of the other. And from this very fact one sees that there are yet other purposes which God had in the distance of the planets from one another. Therefore we must reflect further on the matter, whether perhaps we are permitted to perceive some of the remaining ones as well. We know that God, who has the very highest reason (§ 974 *Met.*), and therefore will undertake nothing without ground, loves difference in nature (§ 586 *Met.*). Now since the planets, although they are all bodies similar to the earth (§ 148 *Phys.*) and inhabited like it (§ 149 *Phys.*), must on account of their so very different distance from the sun (§ 549 *Astron.*) experience such very different effects of sunlight (§ 43 *Opt.*), this is a means whereby God has increased the difference in the state of the planets and of the bodies found on them, and in this way has been able to give more proofs of his wisdom and power (§ 14, 19), as also of his other perfections.

*A doubt is met.*

*Statement of the doubt.*

*Answer to it.*

*Further answer.*

*General reminder concerning caution in judging the purposes of God.*

[p. 145] § 87. If the planets were not so far from one another as they are found to be, one could at least discover something of their particular state through large telescopes. Therefore, since God made the world to the end that we should learn to know him from it (§ 1045 *Met.*), it would seem more in keeping with his principal purpose if they were so near to one another that from one of them something of the particular state of another could be discovered through telescopes. And one would suppose that, by virtue of the principal purpose, God ought to have had regard to this before other things. This has some plausibility, but it very soon vanishes again when one considers the matter more exactly. The moon, as a secondary planet, is so near to our earth that it even comes at times into its shadow (§ 259 *Astron.*); and yet nothing of its particular state can be made out even through the most serviceable telescopes. Therefore, if from one planet one were to see anything of particular bodies in another, the latter would have to be much nearer than the moon. And even if this were to happen, it would signify nothing more than that we might perhaps behold a lofty range of mountains or a wide sea with some (not with complete) clearness. For even a [p. 146] large building or a high tower keeps its distinctness for only a few German miles. Therefore, let one planet come as near to another as it would, it would still not be possible for us to know more of its particular state than we now know of the moon (§ 134 ff. *Phys.*) and can further attain concerning the others, with the help of the knowledge obtained of it, by well-grounded conjectures (§ 145 f. *Phys.*). Accordingly little would be gained if the planets were to be brought closer together than they now stand one after another. And on the other hand all the rest would be lost that is now obtained, since they stand at such a distance from one another and make up so great and magnificent a structure about the sun. But neither is it necessary that we should first seek, in the other planets, proofs of the perfections of God from the contemplation of the particular kinds of bodies. We find so much on the surface of the earth that we are not able to investigate everything here, but, after all the diligence that has been bestowed on the knowledge of nature, bring out only a very little. Accordingly the principal purpose of God, which we have explained at length above (§ [p. 147] 8 ff.), is attained far more surely when the planets stand so far from one another round about the sun than if they had been brought together into a narrow space. Meanwhile one learns from this in general that, in the consideration of the purposes of God in the works of nature, one must not be overhasty in one's judgements. Likewise we see that one is safe in holding to what is grounded in experience, although at times some circumstances seem to be against it. When in time one goes further in considerations of this kind, all manner of grounds will also appear which make conjecture more certain and easier.

*Benefit of eclipses.*

*First benefit.*

§ 88. But although God has with wisdom avoided that one primary planet should come into the shadow of another, he has nevertheless permitted that a secondary planet, or moon, can at times come into the shadow of the primary planet about which it moves. We have the example in our moon, which is eclipsed by the shadow of the earth (§ 259 *Astron.*), and the eclipses of the moons of Jupiter and of the moons of Saturn (§ 144 *Phys.*) likewise confirm the same. But these eclipses afford us various [p. 148] benefits which we should not otherwise have. The eclipses of our moon have constantly been used to determine the difference of the hours at different places on the surface of the earth, and from this, further, their longitude (§ 27 *Geogr.*). The eclipses of the moons of Jupiter have also been used for this (§ 56 *Geogr. Lat.*), and the French in particular have them to thank that they have been able to prepare the maps of France with greater accuracy than before. The eclipses of the moons of Saturn would have this very same benefit, if only they were not so difficult to observe. Next, they lead us to certain knowledge of the proper nature of the planets (§ 144 *Phys.*), and, since all truth is fruitful, this knowledge in turn gives us occasion for further knowledge.

*The course of the planets testifies to God's wisdom and understanding.*

§ 89. The planets all move according to regular rules, which **Kepler**<sup>a</sup> first discovered, and which *Newton*<sup>b</sup> found, on geometrical grounds, to accord with the line in which, according to **Kepler**'s discovery, the centre of the planet moves about the sun. Where rules [p. 149] are observed, there is order (§ 141 *Met.*), and one recognises at the same time from this that God, who has prescribed to them so orderly a motion, made the world with deliberation; consequently they bear witness to his understanding (§ 954 *Met.*) and to his wisdom (§ 1048 *Met.*).

<sup>a</sup> *in Commentariis de Stella Martis.*

<sup>b</sup> *in Princip. Philos. Nat. Mathemat.*

*Reminder of the universal order in all things of nature.*

*Means of coming to know it.*

*What in the motion of the planets still remains to be*

*discovered.*

§ 90. There is no doubt that in all other things likewise there is an order founded on general rules, although at present we do not know them. How long have men applied themselves to astronomy, and how many able minds have investigated the motion of the heavenly bodies, before it came so far that the order present in it was found out! Therefore one need not doubt that, if one continues with diligence in the investigation of nature, and pays careful heed to what takes place in it, and also, where the way is closed to us, opens it by experiments, one will in time come to know the order of nature still more. Indeed, even in the motion of the planets one has not yet fully succeeded. The rules which **Kepler** gave concern only the orbit of the planet; but there are still other things to be observed in their course, such as the change of the *aphelia*, or of the points in the orbit where [p. 150] the planet has its greatest distance from the sun, of the nodes, or the points where the orbit of the planets intersects the ecliptic, and whatever more there is of this kind. Indeed, the great *mathematician* in England, Mr *Newton*, has already shown in the example of the moon that with the secondary planets, such as the moon is to our earth, particular rules besides hold good, which are prescribed to them as secondary planets and by which the primary planets need not be governed<sup>a</sup>.

<sup>a</sup> *in Princ. Phil. Nat. Mathem. lib. 3. p. m. 394. & seqq.*

*The ring around Saturn shows that God is unsearchable in his works.*

*Affords us no benefit.*

*An objection is forestalled.*

§ 91. Among those things whose purposes are to be accounted unsearchable belongs in particular the ring around Saturn (§ 147 *Phys.*), which, so far as the edifice of the world is known to us, has nowhere its like. We can therefore no longer infer from other known cosmic bodies to Saturn by similarity with them, and yet one does not see either what benefit Saturn or its moons should have from it. This therefore belongs among the proofs that God is unsearchable in his works. But this much is certain, that the ring of Saturn affords no [p. 151] benefit to us who live on the surface of the earth. And hence we cannot say that it was made for our pleasure: which is a fresh proof that not everything has been made for the sake of the men who dwell on the surface of the earth (§ 28). Indeed, even should a benefit for us be found after some time, it would nevertheless become evident that this could not be the principal benefit. For although the moons of Jupiter serve us to the end that from their eclipses we may know their nature and that of Jupiter, and can *determine* the longitude of places on the surface of the earth (§ 88), and although it may not be denied that this belongs among the purposes which God had in view in choosing this world (§ 1029 *Met.*), yet everyone will understand that there are nearer purposes for Jupiter than for us, and likewise that for the particular bodies which are found on them there must be nearer purposes still than for Jupiter.

End of Part One.

[p. 152]

## Der Andere Theil. Von den Absichten der besonderen Arten der Dinge. — Part Two. On the Purposes of the Particular Kinds of Things.

### Das I. Capitel. Von dem Nutzen der Lufft. — Chapter I. On the Benefit of the Air.

*When one learned to know the benefit of the air.*

*Difference of experiments, and their benefit.*

*Air is a principal means of the changes on the earth.*

§ 92. Since the learned Guericke, well practised in the sciences, invented the air-pump (§ 63 *Pt. I Exper.*), and by its means the two principal properties of the air, namely its expansive force (§ 80 *Pt. I Exper.*) and its weight (§ 86 *Pt. I Exper.*), have been established with undoubted certainty, and its manifold effects, which formerly were either not known at all or at least not heeded, have also been made known through all manner of experiments, partly astonishing, partly fruitful, by the first of which the investigators of nature were roused to attention, but by the second were enabled to advance in science, as among other writings on the subject my three parts of the Experiments, by which the way to the knowledge of nature [p. 153] is prepared, sufficiently testify; so one has also learned to perceive the manifold benefit of the air far more distinctly than before, and one finds it to be a principal means by which God brings forth the changes on the surface of the earth, and which he without doubt employs for like purposes in the other planets, which, as well as our earth, are surrounded with an air (§ 146 *Phys.*). And hence it is also no wonder that, after the invention of the air- pump, men have advanced much further in the knowledge of nature in a few years than formerly in several thousand: although still other causes furthered the progress of science in the preceding century, of which we do not intend to speak just now.

*The air preserves the same quantity of matter in every cosmic body.*

*Proof from our earth.*

*Proof from the other cosmic bodies.*

*Every cosmic body has its*

*unchangeable share of matter.*

§ 93. Into the air there rise not only vapours, but also other materials into which bodies are dissolved. Both of these we need here only assume from experience. That water evaporates when it is warm, common experience confirms; and that no small quantity evaporates in the course of the day, the experiments made to that end show (§ 86 *Pt. II Exper.*). Indeed it is also known that water evaporates in the cold (§ 87 *Pt. II Exper.*), and that no less than in the heat: [p. 154] concerning which Mr Thümmig<sup>a</sup> has thoroughly explained the experiment made by the celebrated *Perrault*. Everything that dries out in warm weather, or also from the wind, goes into the air. No less there goes into it what is daily *transpired* in great quantity from the bodies of men and animals (§ 25, 422 *Phys.*) and from plants (§ 345, 394 *Phys.*). Likewise there finds its abode what is consumed by fire, or also by decay. And all this confirms that the air is the universal receptacle of the matter which, either by decay or by other ways of nature, is very subtly dissolved and kept in store for the generation of changeable bodies. Now since, however, everything that floats in the air is sustained by the resistance of that air which equals it in its kind of weight (§ 85 *Pt. II Exper.*), whereas the air becomes ever thinner and of a lighter kind the higher one goes, and indeed at last loses itself altogether in the exceedingly subtle celestial air (§ 189 *Phys.*); so no mote of all the materials that go into the air can rise any higher than to where it is of one [p. 155] kind of weight with it, or at least comes very near to it. But that such materials as disperse themselves in the air and rise up in it cannot be of a lighter kind than any air, however subtle it may be, can be established by experiments with the air-pump (§ 85 *Pt. II Exper.*). One sees, accordingly, that the air is made to the end that nothing of the matter which belongs to the earth can get away from it, but rather that, by the air's continually decreasing density, which it receives from its weight and expansive force (§ 189 *Phys.*), everything that is scattered into it as dust is kept in store as in a universal receptacle. Now since there is air about other cosmic bodies (§ 137, 146 *Phys.*), even about the sun (§ 120 *Phys.*), and consequently about the fixed stars themselves (§ 153 *Phys.*), as there is about our earth; so by it, too, the matter belonging to each of them must be retained in every other cosmic body: which one will concede all the more readily, the more certain it is that all kinds of changes show themselves in the cosmic bodies, even from afar, owing to the foreign matter that comes into the air (§ 189 *Phys.*). Since, therefore, we find that the air which surrounds a cosmic body brings it about that no mote [p. 156] of the matter belonging to it can get away from it; so we know at the same time that God has assigned to every cosmic body a certain share of matter, and that it is his will that a certain quantity of matter be constantly preserved in each (§ 1007 *Met.*). This maxim of nature with respect to matter agrees with another which obtains with respect to motion, namely that the same moving force is constantly preserved in nature (§ 426 *Mech. Lat.*)

<sup>a</sup> *part.* 1. of his Versuch seiner Erklärung der merckwürdigsten Begebenheiten in der Natur *Num. 4. p. 32. & seqq.*

*The air preserves the changeable state of the planets.*

§ 94. Now since the matter which, in decay and other dissolution of mutable bodies, goes into the air is the very same from which everything that grows out of the earth, and likewise men and animals, have their growth, and to which they must ascribe their preservation (§ 25 *Phys.*), and which indeed, according to experience, also contributes its part to the generation of many other corporeal things, to which in particular all the events in the air bear witness (§ 291 ff. *Phys.*); so by the air the changeable state on the surface of the earth, and consequently also on the other planets (§ 146 *Phys.*), is preserved.

*It serves for all kinds of*

*changes in bodies.*

§ 95. It is not only in all fluid materials (§ 148 ff. *Pt. I Exper.*), [p. 157] but also in other solid bodies (§ 159 *ff. Pt. I and* § 64 *ff. Pt. II Exper.*) that air is found in abundance. Now since this air is expanded by heat but drawn together into a narrower space by cold (§ 133 *Pt. I Exper.*), and the former cannot happen except that the parts are carried further apart from one another and room is made in the enlarged pores for other, foreign matter (§ 6 *Phys.*); whereas in the other case foreign and inconstant matter is driven out, if the parts draw closer together and the pores become narrower, or else, where these remain unchanged, other matter must come in (§ *cit. Phys.*); the air makes bodies apt for all kinds of changes (§ 18 *Phys.*). And this is precisely the cause why the experiments with the air-pump extend so far, and why the knowledge of nature has thereby gained so much advancement: all of which those will comprehend more circumstantially who read through with care my Reasonable Thoughts on the Effects of Nature.

*Men, animals and plants can neither live nor be healthy without the*

*air.*

§ 96. Plants, men and animals not only obtain from the air that matter by which they are nourished and maintained in their growth (§ 94); but it is also absolutely not [p. 158] possible that men and animals should live without the air, and that plants should grow without it. To the first the experiments bear witness which I have adduced with birds, four-footed animals, frogs and fishes (§ 103 *ff. Pt. III Exper.*); of the second, on the other hand, it has been found by similar experiments that in a space void of air they do not continue to grow as they do outside it. Animals and men cannot live for long if they can no longer draw breath, as experience teaches us, although one can hold out longer than another when it is prevented from breathing; but for breathing the air is absolutely necessary (§ 437 *Phys.*). If plants are to grow well, they must *transpire* strongly (§ 394 *Phys.*); but this cannot happen without air (§ 247 *Phys.*). Indeed, since *transpiration* is necessary in men too (§ 422 *Phys.*), and it is not unknown to *physicians* what dangerous diseases can arise if it does not proceed properly, the air serves at the same time for the preservation of health and for the avoidance of many diseases in men and animals. Indeed, since all the food and all the drink that men and animals consume has much air in it (§ 159 *ff. Pt. I Exper.*), while the air serves for all kinds of [p. 159] changes in corporeal things (§ 95), much that is changeable must also be effected by the air in the bodies of men and animals. And to this is further added that the air mingles with the blood in the lungs (§ 417 *Phys.*).

*What benefit the air provides in the illumination of the earth.*

§ 97. If the celestial air continued uninterrupted down to the surface of the earth, or to the planets, and there were no other, grosser air about these cosmic bodies, then the light would also pass through unrefracted (§ 147 *Pt. II Exper.*), and in this way we should not get to see the sunlight before the sun actually rose, nor should we have the enjoyment of it any longer than until the sun set. And the same would be the case with the light of the moon and of the stars. And thus there would be no break of day, nor any evening twilight (§ 191 *Phys.*); nor could the day shine through the whole night anywhere or at any season (§ 193 *Phys.*); consequently a smaller part of the earth would be illuminated than now, those towards the north would not have their long night shortened nor their day lengthened, nor would the health of our eyes be preserved unimpaired (§ 192 *Phys.*). Now since the other planets likewise have an air about [p. 160] them (§ 137, 146 *Phys.*), in which the light is refracted (§ 137 *Phys.*), with them too the light must be brought by refraction to such places as cannot be illuminated by rays falling directly upon them. And accordingly the air everywhere serves to have the dark cosmic bodies illuminated further than would happen without its assistance.

*How God makes it his concern to avoid darkness.*

*First means.*

*Second means.*

*Third means.*

§ 98. Not only does the air contribute a great deal to the light on the dark cosmic bodies being extended by the air that surrounds them (§ 96); but this same thing also comes about from another cause. All the planets are much smaller than the sun. For the primary planets are very much smaller than it (§ 559 *Astron.*); but the secondary planets, or moons, are smaller than the primary planets, since of our moon it is certain from astronomical grounds that it is much smaller than our earth (§ 554 *Astron.*); whereas the moons of Jupiter and Saturn are not seen otherwise than through telescopes, while one sees their primary planets Jupiter and Saturn with the naked eye (§ 106, 107 *Phys.*), and that accordingly they must be smaller still than these. Sun, earth and planets have the figure of a sphere [p. 161] (§ 118, 182, 184 *&c. Phys.*); but a luminous sphere which is larger than a dark one illuminates more than half of it (§ 48 *Opt.*), whereas a smaller one illuminates less than half of the larger, and, when both are of equal size, no more than half has light (§ 111 f. *Opt. Lat.*). Now since, besides the illumination being extended partly by the size and partly by the *refraction* in the air, the moons and fixed stars moreover at night, in those places to which no light from the sun can be brought, either drive away the darkness (§ 33) or diminish it (§ 62), one cannot conclude otherwise than that God has employed every means to increase the light and to diminish the darkness (§ 1032 *Met.*); to which is further added this, that he has set the planets so far from one another that none of the primary planets can come into the shadow of another (§ 84), while the secondary planets fall into it only now and then, and do not remain in it long either, as the astronomical *observations* of the fixed stars show. Accordingly the proposition remains correct: God has increased the light in the world as abundantly, and on the other hand diminished the darkness as much, as was ever possible.

*Whether the air is disadvantageous to astronomy.*

*Caution to be used in*

*judging perfection.*

[p. 162] § 99. It is indeed true that through *refraction* the stars appear to be higher than they actually stand, and hence none is seen in its true place except one that stands either at our zenith or at least near it (§ 199 *Phys.*). Now it is certain that astronomy thereby meets with a great hindrance, since it is known that the greatest part of what one knows and calculates in astronomy concerning the motion of the stars is derived from the meridian altitudes of the sun and stars, which are too great on account of the *refraction*. It therefore seems as if it would be better if there were no air and the light could come to the surface of the earth unrefracted. Certainly no one will deny that it would be an advantage for astronomy if the air were not there. But this small imperfection had to be admitted, so that greater perfection might not be disturbed (§ 166 *Met.*). It matters more that the earth and the other cosmic bodies are habitable, which cannot be maintained without the air (§ 96), than that astronomy be made easier, since in any case means are after all found in the end whereby one can determine the magnitude of the *refraction* (§ 221 *Astron.*). Meanwhile we see from this, as from an example, what has been taught in general elsewhere (§ 170 *Met.*), [p. 163] namely that in judging perfection one must look not at one thing alone but at everything together, and that it is sometimes not possible to retain everything that would be good, because contradictions arise whereby one thing, as it were, drives out the other. Those who have a narrow insight do not consider that, where many things exist together, not everything is possible that might very well be if the one thing existed alone. They do not consider that, when one adopts one thing and establishes it by a voluntary and entirely unconstrained choice, other things thereafter necessarily flow from it and can no longer be *determined* at pleasure. And pray, what use would it be in the end that the earth were fitted in the most perfect manner for astronomical *observations*, when it would thereby at the same time be put into a state in which it could have no *observer*? It would be a magnificent *observatory* lacking the *observer*, who is nevertheless the most necessary thing in *observations*.

*Air is necessary to the changeable kinds of weather.*

*Why the knowledge of the air has brought physics to advancement.*

*Great benefit of the air.*

§ 100. The cause of the changeable kinds of weather comes chiefly from the winds and from the vapours and clouds (§ 238 ff. *Phys.*). Now there can be no wind where there is not air variable in its weight and expansive force (§ 205 ff. *Phys.*); nor can [p. 164] any vapours rise and gather into clouds except where there is air (§ 247, 261 *Phys.*). Therefore without air no changeable kinds of weather can take place. Indeed, we find also in particular that without air variable in its weight and expansive force no mist can arise (§ 256 ff. *Phys.*), no dew, rain, snow or hail can fall (§ 270 ff. *Phys.*), nor can other phenomena and events take place which either have their seat in the air or are generated in it (§ 291 ff., § 321 ff. *Phys.*). And accordingly nothing changeable can occur in the kinds of weather, whatever name it may have, unless the air contributes its share to it. And from this one again sees a reason why the knowledge of the air has brought physics to such great advancement: namely because the air contributes its share to so many effects of nature, and one therefore cannot understand nature without the properties of the air (§ 29 *Met.*). But one also recognises clearly how much one has to thank the air for, since the change in the kinds of weather is of no small consequence on the surface of the earth, of which more will appear hereafter in its place, especially in the third and fourth chapters (§ 114 ff.). That is, one must [p. 165] in judging the benefit of the air, look not merely at that which proceeds immediately from it, but in particular also take into consideration everything else that would not take place without it.

*Air serves for hearing and for speech.*

*How one learns rightly to know the good.*

§ 101. Sound is carried along by the air (§ 6 *Pt. III Exper.*) and would not take place without it. If no sound were excited, one could also not hear (§ 427 *Phys.*). And accordingly we have the air to thank that the ear is not there for nothing. Speech does not take place without the air which is driven out of the lungs (§ 430 *Phys.*), and thereby it procures for men an uncommon benefit. If there were no air, it would signify just as much as if we were all deaf and dumb; which pitiable state one can best picture to oneself if only, on every occasion when we either hear or speak, one deliberately considers how it would stand with us if at that time we could neither hear nor speak. The more one investigates the causes of natural things, the more one will also find how great a part the air everywhere takes in them.

*Air testifies to*

*God's wisdom and goodness.*

§ 102. Now just as the air is used by God as a means by which he accomplishes a great deal in nature [p. 166] which would otherwise not be attained without it (§ 93 *ff. Phys.* II & § 14, 912 *Met.*), so God's wisdom also shows itself thereby (§ 1036 *Met.*). Indeed, since we draw much advantage, partly from the air immediately, partly from other things which cannot exist without the air, as well for necessity as for convenience and also for the delight of life, as is evident not only from the benefit of the air described hitherto (§ 93 *ff.*) but also from daily experience, if we will only diligently take heed of the things wherein the air is *interested*, so the air testifies very powerfully to the goodness of God (§ 21).

### Das II. Capitel. Von dem Winde. — Chapter II. On Wind.

*Wind cools the air.*

*Why great heat is unbearable.*

*How God uses it for punishment.*

*How to do good to man.*

*An objection is answered.*

§ 103. Ihave already described one or two benefits of the winds (§ 214 *Phys.*), because at that time I did not yet intend to publish this part of natural knowledge in which the benefit of natural things is explained. Among these I have shown, by a remarkable example of an unbearable [p. 167] heat in calm weather in England, what it signifies that the wind cools the air. But we also find it in our own experience that the heat is unbearable when no wind at all is blowing, so much so that even the common man is wont to give it as a cause of the unbearable heat that no wind blows. One says: it is a quite unbearable heat, not the slightest breath of air is blowing. This great heat, however, is unbearable because the air then becomes quite unfit for breathing, so that it is no different than if one were about to suffocate. We recognise, accordingly, that God can use the wind to punish men (§ 37 *Mor.*). For if he lets it drop on bright summer days, there arises an unbearable heat, so that men and cattle must languish. Hereafter still more examples will present themselves where the wind turns to the harm of man. On the other hand, he can also use it to do good to man: for when they are about to languish from heat, he can refresh them all at once by a wind that cools the air. And of this too still more proofs will come to light hereafter. Let no one say that this is contrary to what I have taught concerning the connection of things with one another, confirmed from eternity by [p. 168] the decree of God, in the Metaphysics. For God has indeed already resolved from eternity when he wills to use the wind for punishment and to do good to men, and has no need only now to deliberate anew. This, however, does no prejudice to the matter. It is all the same whether something is resolved now or from eternity. If this happens with respect to men conducting themselves in such a way and no other, then God has also foreseen this before it comes (§ 968 *Met.*), and does not need first to wait, in order to make a resolution, until men conduct themselves thus and not otherwise. For example, if he has willed to take heed of the praying of men, he has already known from eternity that they will pray (§ 970 *Met.*), and therefore has also resolved from eternity what he will do in hearing the prayer (§ 999 *Met.*), and consequently has also made the arrangements for it, so that he has no need first to deliberate what he shall do.

*The wind also makes it warm and promotes*

*fertility, or else barrenness.*

*How one and the same thing in nature, for man's good and for his punishment,*

*can serve.*

*Man's duty in this.*

*Origin of the unavoidable necessity of Spinoza.*

§ 104. The wind brings warm air from the lands where it is warmer into others where it is not so warm (§ 214 *Phys.*). We find it so and not otherwise in experience among us when the south wind blows, which even in winter makes it so mild [p. 169] that it does not freeze, even should the severest cold rage. Now if, particularly in spring and summer, when it has previously rained heavily and the soil has been moistened, south winds blow at night, one has warm nights, and one marvels how much has grown in a single night. In this way the wind promotes fertility, and God can use it as a means of doing good to the creature (§ 1033 *Met.*). If, after calm weather on bright summer days, when the heat becomes unbearable (§ 103), warm winds blow besides, and the rain stays away, then the ground dries out, and all sap departs from the grass and the other plants, so that at last everything is, as it were, burnt up and withered by the heat. And in this way God can also use the wind as a means of punishing the ground with barrenness (§ 37 *Mor.*). We see from this single example that one and the same cause, which in itself acts one time just as another, can now act for the good of the creature, but now must act to its harm, according as the constitution of the other circumstances entails. And accordingly it arises from the order in which natural events follow upon one another that now the creature, and in particular [p. 170] man derives advantage from that from which at another time he suffers harm.
