Now since this order is not necessary (§ 577 *Met.*) and subsists through God's free decree (§ 980 *Met.*), but he has had his purposes in all things, for which he also wills (§ 1057 ff. *Met.*) to permit the evil in which he has no part (§ 1056 *Met.*), so man certainly has occasion, in this order in which natural events follow upon one another, to think of God and to learn from it his wise government (§ 657 *Mor.*). And precisely this is necessary if one wishes to know the perfection which is in nature, that is, the excellent harmony of all events with one another (§ 701 *Met.*), which *Spinoza*, together with the other fatalists, has taken away from the world, in that they admitted no more than the possibility of one world, and in this way brought the actuality of things under an unavoidable necessity, and moreover even held the laws of nature, according to which motion takes place, to be unalterable: the groundlessness of which I have shown above (§ 16). It is remarkable that those who would ascribe to me an unalterable necessity adopt these grounds of *Spinoza* and found the accusation on the opposite doctrines.

*Winds make it cold, and are thereby serviceable and harmful.*

*How we sweat.*

*Why we do not sweat when it is cool.*

*How the dry wind does harm.*

*Cause of the cold summer.*

*Of the great winter.*

[p. 171] § 105. The winds cool the air by their motion, and also bring air from cold countries (§ 214, 243 *Phys.*). Therefore, when there is great heat and a strong wind arises, the heat is thereby moderated, so that it does not fall so burdensomely upon men and animals. When there is great heat, the air is very warm, and hence not so much heat can pass out of our body as otherwise, when the air is cooler (§ 76 *Phys.*). We also notice quite distinctly that what the body gives off in perspiration cannot disperse so freely through the air: for this is why what *transpires* collects, and the sweat stands upon us. Now if the wind blows, it carries the air which is near the body away from us and constantly brings other air with it. Now since one can clearly show by means of weather-glasses (§ 55 *Pt. II Exper.*) that there is more heat in the bodies of men and animals than in the air, the air can also take up heat from our body (§ 76 *Phys.*), and the wind carries it away from us together with what is perspired. In this way not only do we become cool (§ 74 *Phys.*), but no sweat collects either. Meanwhile, since the wind dries strongly, as we see particularly in spring, it increases the drought when, in continuing dry weather, it follows upon great heat, [p. 172] and promotes all the harm which is wont to ensue in great drought and may be observed from experience. If, at the time when the sun should shine at its warmest and warm the earth, cold winds blow, particularly in overcast weather, there is a cold summer, and very often growth is hindered, so that the fruits either spoil right at the beginning or do not attain their proper ripeness. If such winds blow in winter, and one has overcast weather with them, extraordinary cold sets in, which does great harm (§ 246 *Phys.*); an example of this we had *Anno* 1709, which I set out in more detail at the time.<sup>a</sup>

<sup>a</sup> in *Dissertat, de hieme A. 1709.*

*What winds do in rain, and how they thereby benefit and do harm.*

*Cause of the cold summer and of the wet one.*

§ 106. The winds carry the vapours in the air from one place to another, partly dispersed, partly drawn together into clouds. And thereby one receives abundant dew and rain in such places as would otherwise most lack them. Who does not know that most vapours rise from water (§ 248 *Phys.*)? And accordingly the winds must carry them over the dry land, so that it may be moistened with dew (§ 270 *Phys.*). The vapours rise most of all from the sea, because evaporation takes place in proportion to the size of the [p. 173] upper surface which the water presents (§ 86 *Pt. II Exper.*): as we also see that water in vessels which are wide open evaporates more than in others which have a narrower opening. And for this reason the winds which blow over the sea bring rain- clouds (§ 221 *Phys.*). If, therefore, we had no wind, we should also have no proper rain sufficient to soak through the dry and parched soil as the growth of plants requires. Indeed, since the origin of springs most probably comes from dew and rain (§ 343 ff. *Phys.*), the winds also promote the springs; indeed, since they convey the water to them, without winds there would be no springs at all. If winds which bring clouds and rain come at a time when the sun ought to warm the earth most, they make a cold summer (§ 246 *Phys.*), and, if they bring too much rain, an overly wet one. Both can do much or little harm according to the constitution of other circumstances, as we may learn from experience, which in general can greatly multiply considerations of this kind and give natural philosophers occasion for further investigation.

*Winds in general produce the changeable kinds of weather.*

§ 107. Indeed, in my *dissertation* [p. 174] on the severe winter *Anno 1709* I have shown in detail that the principal cause of the changeable kinds of weather is the winds, which I have since also set forth anew in my Thoughts on the Effects of Nature, *ch. 4*, and brought into the chain of those truths which we know through the right use of reason. Therefore all the advantage that we have from the changeable kinds of weather on the surface of the earth we must ascribe to the winds as their principal cause. Indeed, we must also attribute to them the harm that we experience through the changeable kinds of weather.

*Whether the winds have a regular cycle.*

*At present still uncertain.*

*How one may get to the bottom of the matter.*

§ 108. The winds alternate constantly, but not in the same way each time. One wind does not blow as long at one time as at another; nor does one wind follow upon another in the same way each time. In both there is a great difference, and one year is not like another. And for this very reason the changeable kinds of weather also do not come in one year as in another (§ 107). Now since we find that in the motion of the cosmic bodies, too, much that is changeable occurs, so that it is not constituted at one time as at another (§ 354 ff. *Astron.*), but that notwithstanding the most excellent order is nevertheless to be found therein, which is grounded in unaltered rules [p. 175] (§ 89), and after the lapse of some time comes to an end again and begins anew; so men have hit upon the thought whether the winds too, inconstant as they seem to be, may not have a regular cycle, which runs to its end after a certain number of years and thereafter begins anew. No one doubts that, if such a thing should exist in nature, it would be of uncommon benefit if one could search it out, since we should thereby be put in a position to foresee the changeable kinds of weather as well as the constant ones. But at present nothing can yet be conjectured about this with probability, let alone determined. If, however, one is to have hope that in future times it will be possible to speak of this with greater certainty, then one must diligently observe the winds and their alternations in many places through many years, indeed a hundred years, investigate their causes (§ 205 ff. *Phys.*), and compare with one another the alternations observed in each place. It is indeed a labour that is toilsome and of long prospect: but in astronomy, too, one meets with truths of this kind which have emerged only after some thousands of years, and we should not yet know them if our forefathers [p. 176] had not also been willing to take pains purely for our good. But so long as we know nothing of an order in the alternations of the winds, we can infer nothing further from them.

*What the wind accomplishes by its strength.*

*1. For benefit in common life.*

*2. For mariners at sea.*

*3. With mills.*

*4. In navigation.*

*How one comes to know the strength of the wind.*

*Harm that storm-winds cause.*

*What effects they have in general.*

*Why one marvels at them.*

*Why God can use the winds as a punishment.*

*How God can reward and punish men through nature in general.*

§ 109. The strength of the wind is not slight, and by it the wind is able to procure us more than one benefit. Even if a wind is quite weak, it can still drive the clouds and turn the weathercocks. And since from the drift of the clouds and the position of the weathercocks we can judge from what quarter the wind blows, the wind brings us to the knowledge of the cause of the changeable weather (§ 217 ff. *Phys.*), and puts us in a position to make, concerning the weather through the day or the coming night, such a conjecture as may suffice for the ordering of those human affairs in which one has to pay regard to the weather. It serves mariners at sea in particular to know from what quarter the wind blows, not only because they have to govern themselves by it in steering the ship, but also because a great deal depends for them on judging the winds by the kinds of weather which they are wont to raise. The wind drives the windmills: which is of great benefit in such places [p. 177] where one has no water, or at least the water is not in a state to drive mills. The wind also drives ships, both at sea and on rivers rich in shipping, by means of the spread sails into which it blows, so that one can make uncommonly swift progress by water. And there is no doubt that it can be put to still more use than men have hitherto been accustomed to put it to. It has its strength from the velocity with which it moves (§ 215 *Phys.*), and this makes up for the want of matter in the very thin air (§ 86 *Pt. I Exper.*) that is moved (§ 205 *Phys.*). Whoever, therefore, wishes to understand more distinctly how the wind is capable of accomplishing this or that, must note from experience the velocity with which the wind moves. But this belongs to the mathematical knowledge of nature, with which we have nothing to do at present. And I have already laid the grounds for it in the Latin *Aerometry*. Storm-winds do great harm not only at sea, and elsewhere on the water, but also on land. At sea they cover the ship with waves and dash the water into it, so that one has difficulty in saving oneself and keeping the ship from sinking. They drive it to places where it does not wish to go, so that it often [p. 178] runs aground on sandbanks, or else is wrecked on a rock. Indeed, in a storm it sometimes perishes in yet other ways besides. And it is precisely the storm-winds that make for the greatest danger at sea. These same storm-winds sometimes tear down tall trees in forests. They strip tiled roofs and even throw towers to the ground. In a word: on the bodies that resist them they produce such effects as one may expect from a great violence. So it is apt to happen here with us in Marburg, where the wind can pass through the windows of the tower by the bells, that, when it blows strongly and passes straight through, it moves the heavy clapper of the bell and makes it strike. That one marvels at these effects of the storm-winds comes solely from this: that in the case of the air one looks to the small quantity of matter that is moved (§ 86 *Pt. I Exper.*), and in common experience mostly sees violent effects produced by such bodies as have a great weight and consequently a great quantity of proper matter (§ 13 *Phys.*). Although one also perceives the velocity of the wind, one cannot properly gauge how great it is. [p. 179] But whoever attends to the latter (§ 215 *Phys.*) is not surprised at the magnitude of the effects. Now since storm-winds can cause great harm, and the whole of nature is a work of the wisdom of God (§ 14), free from all *fatality* and unavoidable necessity (§ 16), God can also use them as a punishment (§ 37 *Mor.*) when he finds it advisable; indeed, he lets them come with this purpose (§ 984 *Met.*). And this is called to mind once and for all, so that in what follows we need not repeat it every time. For as often as we shall show in what follows that a creature procures advantage for man, so often must we also remember that this is a means by which God can do good to man (§ 1032 *Met.*). And as often, on the other hand, as we shall see that a creature can harm man, we may regard it as a means by which God is in a position to punish evil without working miracles (§ 37 *Mor.*).

*That the winds make the air healthy.*

*1. by preventing it from becoming stinking.*

*2. disperses the harmful evaporations,*

*3. makes the air dry,*

*4. suited to transpiration, and*

*5. fit for breathing.*

§ 110. There remains yet one principal benefit of the winds which does not at once strike everyone's eye, notwithstanding that we constantly enjoy it. Namely, if there were no winds, the air would be unhealthy and would give occasion to many harmful [p. 180] pestilences and diseases. The winds, on the contrary, keep it healthy and fit for breathing. The cause is easily understood. When the air stands still for a long time, it becomes stinking, especially in such places as many stinking evaporations enter, or else such fumes as become stinking. One finds this in chambers that are always shut. If, for example, one has been away travelling, and the room remains locked for several days, so that no one comes into it, but it is so situated that the sun can shine freely into it during the day, then, when one comes into it again, it smells quite foul, and one must open windows and doors so that fresh air can come in, if one does not wish to be *incommoded* by it. In the same way one finds in buildings where there is a narrow courtyard and no free access of air that the air there smells very foul. Now if there were no wind, the air would everywhere stand still. The evaporations that came into it would remain in the place where they had risen, and it would fare with the air everywhere as in a closed room, or else in a courtyard where it has no free access. That the body becomes unhealthy from stinking air, especially if one had to remain in it without any change, no one doubts. [p. 181] It is a settled matter among the *physicians* and all who pay heed to experience that a pure and fresh air is conducive to health. Now since the wind moves the air (§ 205 *Phys.*) and carries it from one place to another, it is thereby not only prevented from becoming stinking, but the noxious evaporations which have drawn into it are also dispersed, so that one is no longer aware of them and they cannot *incommode* men. This is confirmed by experience in such places as lie low and have a swampy and marshy country about them, where the air is found to be very unhealthy when no wind blows, of which one meets with examples here and there in the travel accounts. To this belongs also that moist air, when it becomes very warm, causes headaches and indeed even death. We have a clear example in common experience. When in winter wet linen is hung up in a warm room, especially where the stove has been strongly heated, so that the air becomes moist from it, one gets severe headaches, becomes at the same time quite dazed in the head, and even falls down as if dead. Such a state would prevail almost everywhere on hot summer days if there were no wind, [p. 182] especially over the water and in places lying near it. Thus it is also known how suddenly moist air can kill when it is not only heated but, in particular, a sulphurous fume is added to it (§ 108 *Pt. III Exper.*). Indeed, one has also to consider that men and animals cannot *transpire* so well in a moist air filled with vapours as in a pure one, since, namely, the air takes up only a certain share of fumes (§ 405 *Phys.*). But what hinders *transpiration* also hinders health, since everyone feels in himself that, when he has not *transpired* well in the night, he finds himself weary and heavy in his limbs; whereas, when he has *transpired* well, he is quite brisk and light in all his limbs. That the air would be unfit for breathing if there were no wind at all can also be very well understood. When we let out our breath, the air does not go back out of the lungs dry, but carries much moisture out with it. One sees this quite plainly in winter, when in the cold air the breath issues from the mouth or through the nostrils like a fume and clings to the cold panes of glass in the window. Accordingly the air becomes moist through the breath. The breath [p. 183] which comes out of the mouth is also warm, and therefore warms the air. Now it is found in experience that, when one is shut up in a narrow space where one has only very little air about one, one is soon like to suffocate if one remains in it for a while. But in a narrow space the air becomes warm and moist from the breath. And accordingly one sees that moist and warm air is unfit for breathing, indeed that one must at last suffocate altogether if one cannot draw fresh air. Now if there were no wind, all fumes would remain in the place where they rise, and the air would at the same time be very warm (§ 103). Therefore it would be unfit for breathing.

### Das III. Capitel. Von den Witterungen in der Lufft. — Chapter III. On the Kinds of Weather in the Air.

*Whether in the constant kinds of weather a purpose can be sought by us.*

*First cause why it applies.*

*Second cause.*

§ 111. It seems as if, with regard to the constant kinds of weather, one could not ask why God willed that they should alternate, since the alternation is a necessary consequence [p. 184] of the motion of the earth about the sun, and indeed even in the torrid zone the alternation of the constant kinds of weather is by no means found with such a difference as among us, since there, if summer is to be judged by the warmth, there is indeed a perpetual summer. But notwithstanding that the motion of the earth about the sun, since it is moved in the ecliptic and not in the *equator*, brings with it that summer and winter, spring and autumn must alternate (§ 232 *Phys.*), God can nevertheless have a purpose in it (§ 1028 *Met.*); and we too, who live in such places where the alternation takes place, can have a benefit from it, which is likewise to be reckoned among the purposes of God (§ 1029 *Met.*). Moreover, this arrangement of the course of the earth about the sun is surely not absolutely necessary, but the whole world could also have been arranged otherwise (§ 569 *Met.*). We cannot give it out as absolutely impossible that the world could have been so arranged that there would have been perpetual summer over the whole surface of the earth. And perhaps, if a skilful *mathematician* were willing to reflect upon it, it would not prove all too difficult to devise such an arrangement. But precisely [p. 185] then it would appear still more clearly than now why God has preferred the present arrangement, in which the alternation takes place, to another in which a constant summer would be maintained, as under the torrid zone. Notwithstanding this, however, one can see even now, if we merely attend to the present arrangement, for what causes God has been pleased to choose such an alternation.

*The alternation of the constant kinds of weather makes the earth more habitable.*

*What the kinds of weather*

*would be if the sun moved in the equator.*

*Which lands would be uninhabited.*

*What the kinds of weather would be*

*if the sun moved in the meridian.*

*What would happen if the ecliptic passed through the poles, or the sun moved in a parallel circle.*

*How all this can most easily be*

*comprehended.*

*How the alternation of winter and summer is to our advantage.*

§ 112. I did indeed remark above (§ 82) that the alternation of the constant kinds of weather in the four seasons serves to make the earth habitable in more places than would otherwise be the case; but since this was done only in passing, I must set it out more circumstantially here, in its proper place. If no alternations of the constant kinds of weather were to take place on the surface of the earth, the sun would have to move either in the *equator*, or in the *meridian*, or in a parallel circle to one of the two, as *Burnet* and perhaps the Chaldeans supposed it to have been at the beginning of the world, of whom the former maintains the motion of the sun in the *equator*<sup>a</sup>, the latter, on the other hand, in the *meridian*<sup>b</sup>. If the sun were to remain constantly in the *equator*, [p. 186] it would shine the whole year through just as at the beginning of spring and autumn, and we should have somewhat warmer weather than is usually the case in spring, or somewhat colder than is usually the case in autumn when it begins (§ 37 *Geogr.*). I say deliberately: somewhat warmer weather than at the beginning of spring, and somewhat colder than at the beginning of autumn; for it is known that the weather at the beginning of spring is governed by the preceding state of the winter, and that at the beginning of autumn by the preceding state of the summer. But to this we cannot pay regard here, where no alternation of the seasons takes place. That one must pay regard to it now, however, is known not only from the grounds of metaphysics, by virtue of which the preceding state contains within itself a ground of the following (§ 548 *Met.*), but it is also confirmed by the difference of the weather at the beginning of spring and of autumn which still remains even when one disregards the changeable kinds of weather (§ 232 *Phys.*). Accordingly there would be almost constantly, over the whole surface of the earth, the very same state that we meet with when either spring or autumn begins. At that same [p. 187] time there is an unbearable heat under the line and in the places lying near it, whereas with us little or nothing can come to its growth. It is at least certain that with us no corn, no proper tree, and also many plants of the earth that we now have would not grow; indeed we could have no fruit- bearing trees here either. And thus we should already have here a Lapland, or other lands lying towards the north at the same distance from the line. Further towards the north nothing at all would grow; indeed Sweden and Russia, and very likely even Denmark and Livonia, and whatever lies in one *climate* with these lands, could no longer be inhabited at all; but least of all could lands be inhabited that lie still further towards the North Pole. The case would be the same on the other side of the earth, or in the southern part, which it is unnecessary to set out in more detail. One sees, therefore, that in the first case, where no alternation of the constant weather would take place, a great part of the surface of the earth would remain uninhabitable which is nevertheless habitable now with the alternation of this weather. If the sun moved in a *meridian*, that is, in a circle that passed through the present [p. 188] poles and completed its course round the earth in it every day, it would be just the same as if the poles came into east and west, and the *equator* were turned round into the *meridian*, which it cuts at right angles. Therefore the state of the earth with respect to the weather would also remain entirely the same as before, only that the cold and uninhabited lands would now become habitable, whereas warm and inhabited ones would at the same time become uninhabitable. If one wished, properly speaking, to suppose with the ancient Chaldeans that the poles and the *equator* remained in one place, and that the ecliptic, in which the sun completes its course round the heavens within a year, passed through the poles, the constant kinds of weather would alternate, and accordingly this position of the sun does not belong here. If, on the other hand, the sun were to move in a parallel circle, the torrid zone would thereby become smaller, since the circumference of the sphere becomes ever less the nearer one comes towards the poles; whereas the frigid zone on the one side would become larger, and accordingly in this case too the earth would remain less inhabited than now. Whoever wishes to comprehend all this more easily need only take in hand a terrestrial globe made by art (§ 65 *Geogr.*), and he will thereby be able to represent all this to himself with the greatest [p. 189] distinctness. It therefore remains true in general that the earth can be inhabited in more places when the constant kinds of weather alternate than when they remain the same. Meanwhile, although in some places there is thereby a very severe winter, and nothing grows all winter long, we learn from experience that this does no harm. For not only does so much grow in summer that men and animals have their nourishment from it in winter as well; but one also finds occupations in winter which cannot so well be undertaken in summer, and which are as necessary to human life as others that must be undertaken in summer. Whoever has some experience of country life, or merely looks up the household almanacs in the books written on country life, will be sufficiently assured of this. Now since winter, even in lands lying far to the north, or in general towards the poles, is nevertheless so constituted that it does not make the earth there uninhabitable, God has had so much the less cause to maintain constantly the same weather over the surface of the earth.

<sup>a</sup> *in Theoria Telluris.*

<sup>b</sup> *Diodorus Siculus.*

*The alternation of the constant*

*kinds of weather makes a greater difference of things.*

§ 113. If we consult the travel accounts and natural history, there is found a great difference of the [p. 190] plants and living creatures according to the difference of the lands, with respect to the constant kinds of weather found there. Therefore if they no longer alternated, and the inhabited lands were thereby brought together into a narrow compass, there would no longer remain so much difference with respect to the constant kinds of weather as before, and at the same time many plants and animals would thereby be banished from the surface of the earth. Accordingly the present arrangement of the earth, in which a fit alternation of the constant kinds of weather takes place, has the benefit that more kinds of particular bodies can exist on the surface of the earth: which is in accordance with the proposition of indiscernibles (§ 589 *Met.*), as that which requires the greatest variety of things that can find room. And precisely thereby God at the same time also shows his wisdom (§ 917 *Met.*), in that he most completely attains his end, that the earth should be habitable (§ 66).

*Whence the difference of fertile and barren years comes.*

*How experience confirms this.*

*Cause of the difference of fertile years.*

*Why, for growing and ripening,*

*the same weather is not good.*

§ 114. If the kinds of weather at one season were as at another, fertility too would be the same one year as another. For the causes why plants, and whatever else grows out of the earth, thrive are partly rain and dew, which supply nourishment (§ 392 *Phys.*), partly heat, [p. 191] which promotes and accelerates growth in various ways (§ 397 ff. *Phys.*), partly wind, which serves for *transpiration* (§ 405 *Phys.*). Now if the heat were as great one year as another, and also alternated one year as another; if rain and dew fell one year in as great a quantity, and at just the same time, as another, and the winds likewise blew one year as another: then all the causes of fertility would be the same, and one therefore cannot see why there should be more fertility at one time than at another. One may not say that hail and tempests could do more harm at one time than at another: for these things also belong among the changeable kinds of weather, and, if these were not to take place, hail and tempests too would have to come one year as another; consequently they could not do more harm at one time than at another. Therefore, that fertile and barren times can come intermixed arises solely from this, that the kinds of weather at one season are not as at another. And experience agrees with this. For if we will take note what weather there has been when we have fertile years, and how, on the other hand, the weather has been constituted when we have had a barren [p. 192] year, the difference will show itself clear as day. And hereafter, in the particular consideration of the various kinds of weather, we shall give examples. But this too is still to be noted. From the difference of the changeable kinds of weather at a season it also comes that one year is more barren than another, and in particular that some fruits turn out well while others, on the contrary, spoil, or at least turn out badly. The cause can easily be guessed. Not everything grows at the same time, nor does everything ripen at the same time. Indeed, each thing has its season at which it thrives best. There can therefore be good weather for ripening but bad for growing; or good for growing but bad for ripening; or good for one kind of plants but bad for others. Therefore in the first case what is to grow may lag behind, while what is to ripen thrives. In the second case, on the other hand, what is to ripen suffers harm, and what is growing thrives, and may also afterwards have weather convenient for its ripening. That the same weather is not convenient for growing and for ripening can easily be proved. When something is to grow, it must have nourishment enough and be warm withal. Therefore [p. 193] warm rainy weather serves for this. On the other hand, if something is to ripen, it must not have much moisture, but heat and wind, drying and softening causes, according to the different constitution of the fruits. The particular kinds of plants require particular kinds of weather if they are to thrive, and we shall hereafter obtain particular examples of this.

*Changes of the weather cause signs of the impending weather.*

*How this is possible.*

*How experience confirms it.*

*One kind of weather points to another.*

*How the rules of country people are to be examined.*

§ 115. In nature no change happens all at once, but little by little (§ 686 *Met.*). It commonly takes an imperceptible beginning, and thereafter becomes ever more perceptible, as we also find in experience if we pay attention to it. Therefore the change of the weather is likewise of just this constitution. It does not change in an instant, but little by little, and here too the change takes an imperceptible beginning, which thereafter becomes gradually more and more perceptible, until at last the complete change is present. And although sudden changes of the weather also seem at times to occur, yet their constitution is no other than that of all sudden events, namely, either many small parts or many small motions come together in a very short time [p. 194] (§ 691 *Met.*). Of the first we have an example when the clear sky is suddenly overcast with clouds; of the second a strong flash of lightning and clap of thunder, coming unexpectedly, furnishes an example. But although the incipient changes of the weather do not of themselves fall within our senses, yet they can cause changes in other things which do fall within our senses, and in this way one can know from them beforehand that the weather is at the door and will soon set in; consequently they are signs of the incipient weather (§ 292 *Met.*). Experience agrees with this. For such changes occur in the cosmic bodies, in the air, in animals and in lifeless creatures, from which one can judge of the impending weather. Those who live in the country have brought their experiences into rules<sup>a</sup>. But although I will not approve everything indiscriminately, neither can I reject everything outright; rather I find that much has very good ground, as I shall shortly show more fully. Indeed, the kinds of weather themselves carry such a difference with them that from them [p. 195] one can judge of those that follow, because of the connection of what follows with what precedes (§ 547, 548 *Met.*), as examples will show in their place. For the present I merely note this in general. Whoever would judge the rules which country people have made from their experience must investigate what causes they have; and for this what I have set forth in the Physics concerning the causes of the kinds of weather<sup>b</sup> and of aerial events<sup>c</sup> can serve him. For what has its natural causes, that one must also admit; and in so far as something has natural causes, in so far must one also admit it. By this proof what has ground is most readily distinguished from what is not sufficiently grounded. One must not let the matter rest on experience alone. This yields only a probability (§ 11, *ch. 5 Log.*), and in judging it practised people, let alone common people, can deceive themselves all the more easily, the less is known at present of serviceable rules by which probability can be judged. It is always good when reason *directs* experience.

<sup>a</sup> *Florini* kluger Hauß-Vater *lib. 2. c. 72. & seqq. f. 457. & seqq.*

<sup>b</sup> *cap. 4. §. 226. & seqq.*

<sup>c</sup> *c. 5. & seqq. §. 247. & seqq.*

*Baglivi's rule for foreseeing the weather from the present.*

*Whether the rule is to be held an astrological one.*

*What time is most convenient for observing it.*

*Which places are inconvenient for it.*

*Why the Author doubts the correctness of the rule.*

[p. 196] § 116. Here I am reminded of a rule which *Giorgio Baglivi*, a famous *physician*<sup>a</sup>, has given for judging the coming weather from the present, and which he received from a learned clergyman who had travelled much by sea, where one has time and opportunity to observe the changes of the weather, and more cause than on land to attend to them. The rule is this. Two to three hours after the new moon one should observe the weather, and the winds in particular; for as one finds it at that time, so it will continue until the third day of the moon's age. If now on the third day, at the same hour, the weather does not change, it will remain unchanged until the fifth, and so on from the fifth to the seventh, from the seventh to the ninth, from the ninth to the eleventh, from the eleventh to the thirteenth, from the thirteenth to the fifteenth, from the fifteenth to the nineteenth, from the nineteenth to the twenty-first, from the twenty-first to the twenty-third, from the twenty-third to the twenty-seventh, [p. 197] from this, finally, to the end of the lunar month. Although the rule seems to be an astrological one, yet it cannot properly be held to be such. For it might be that the winds, at least at sea, where they are not hindered as on the surface of the earth, have their regular alternations, which accord with the course of the moon as ebb and flood do (§ 355 *Phys.*), or else merely coincide with it by chance (§ 11, ch. 5 *Log.*). Therefore it would not be unserviceable if one paid attention, not only on land but also, in particular, at sea, to how far this rule is to be trusted. And since the moon is new more than twelve times every year (§ 48, 49 *Chronol.*), the *observation* can be repeated many times in a short while, and one can the sooner get to the bottom of it. Meanwhile, since one must always observe every other day, and may easily meet with hindrance, or forget it on account of other business, those months are the most convenient for it in which the new moon falls scarcely a couple of hours before noon, so that one has to observe about noon, before or after dinner. I have indeed several times resolved to pay attention to it; but on account of manifold other business, and other hindrances that got in the way, it has [p. 198] again fallen into oblivion. Since the wind strikes against mountains and is *reflected*, those places are the most inconvenient for paying attention to it where there are many mountains, which can produce all manner of changes, and for other reasons besides. Meanwhile I scarcely suppose that this rule will have ground enough in experience on its side. For the clergyman from whom it comes seems indeed to have been prepossessed with the common prejudice that the moon is a principal cause of the change of the weather. But it is known that when anyone observes with a prejudice, he believes he finds experience as he would have it, provided only that it does not wholly contradict him. Had he given a record of his *observations* (§ 2, ch. 5 *Log.*), one would be able to judge of it with greater certainty. But as it is, it remains to be put to one's own experience if one would reject it with good ground.

<sup>a</sup> *in Appendice ad Specimen de fibra motrice & morbosa p. m. 242.*

*The sun and the moon by their colours give signs of the*

*weather.*

*How this is possible.*

*Warning against false interpretation.*

§ 117. The sun and the moon do not always have the same colour. In the moon one can see it, wherever in the sky it may stand, because its light is weak and never dazzles the eyes; in the sun it is to be noticed especially when it rises, or when it is about [p. 199] to set, because then, when it stands low, the brilliance of the rays by which the eyes are dazzled is lost. And since moonlight is incomparably weaker than sunlight (§ 137 *Pt. II Exper.*), a change can also be perceived in it more easily than in the latter. The colours of the sun and of the moon arise from the *refraction* of their light in the air (§ 158 *Pt. II Exper.*). Now since this changes when the state of the air is changed (§ 151 *Pt. II Exper.*), one can also infer the changed state of the air from the changed colour of the moon and of the sun. But the state of the air is changed when a change of the weather is impending (§ 115): therefore one can judge of the change of the weather from the colour of the moon and of the sun. But in asserting this I concede nothing further than what I have proved, and on that account I do not yet approve any of the particular rules which have been made from experience for judging of the weather from the colours of the sun and of the moon, until their correctness has been proved in particular. Since it is incumbent on me, as a natural philosopher, to investigate the causes of what happens in nature, I shall also show in particular from what causes this or that [p. 200] colour of the sun and of the moon portends this or that kind of weather.

*The sun rising bright signifies fine weather.*

§ 118. When the sun rises in the morning quite bright and clear, it signifies fine weather through the day. For when the sun rises with clear and bright light, that light is not altered in the air by *refraction*. Therefore the air must be free of fumes and other evaporations. Now since the weather does not change instantaneously all at once, but the air first begins imperceptibly to change (§ 115), one cannot but expect a bright day. Since, accordingly, the present rule is grounded in physics, reason agrees with experience in it, and one has no cause why one should not be willing to trust experience.

*When the sun rises or sets pale, it points to rain.*

*A doubt is met.*

*Why the rain- wind does not at once*

*bring clouds.*

*Why vapours are present before rainy weather.*

*How more exact observations are to be made.*

§ 119. On the contrary, when the sun rises or sets pale, rain is to be expected. For when the sun looks pale, its light must be weaker. Now since it is not weaker in itself (since at the very time when the sun rises or sets pale with us, it does not look pale in other places where it is raised above the horizon, nay, even in other places where it rises at the same time, it has no such pale colour), it must be weakened in the air before it comes to our eyes. And accordingly [p. 201] there must be a coarser matter in the air than the air itself, that is, since what is changeable in the air is the evaporations, there must be watery vapours in the air. Watery vapours give rain- clouds, and these bring the rain. Therefore in the present case one has to expect rain. It is indeed true that a rain-wind must blow if it is to rain, as with us the west wind (§ 223 *Phys.*): but this does not stand in the way of what was said before. For if watery vapours are present in the air, the air must be light (§ 263 *Phys.*), and accordingly the air presses into ours from another place, where it is heaviest (§ 186 *Phys.*). The air is heaviest where the vapours are subtle and, in great quantity, have been dispersed throughout in such a manner that they are one with the corpuscles of air and help to increase the weight of the air (§ 40 *Pt. II Exper.*). Therefore the air with its vapours presses into ours, and not only brings these to us but also thereby becomes rarer in its own place, consequently of a lighter kind, so that there the vapours gather together into clouds (§ 263 *Phys.*). And this is the cause why the rain-wind does not at once bring clouds with it, but at first only vapours, the harbingers of the rain-clouds and of the rain [p. 202] that follows from them. We therefore see a twofold cause why watery vapours can be present in the air when rainy weather is about to set in: namely, the first is the lightness of the air in our place; the other is the wind which arises from it and brings us watery vapours before the rain. Now if, at a sign of the weather, as here at the altered colour of the rising and setting sun, one were at the same time to pay attention to the barometers (§ 22 *Pt. II Exper.*) and manometers (§ 45 *Pt. II Exper.*), as also to the hygrometers (§ 93 *Pt. II Exper.*) and thermometers (§ 55 *Pt. II Exper.*), one would know their causes with greater certainty, and after this would also observe the difference between the signs more exactly, and determine how far they are to be trusted.

*The pale moon signifies rain.*

*Why in this respect between the sun*

*and the moon some difference is found.*

*Why the moon indicates rainy weather earlier than the sun.*

§ 120. What we have proved of the sun (§ 119) holds still more of the moon. Let it stand in whatever place of the heavens it will, it signifies rain when it looks pale. And the ancients remarked this long ages ago, so that the poet too wrote it down as a well-known thing: *Pallida Luna pluit*, or: a pale moon signifies rain. Namely, the weak moonlight can be weakened in every position with respect to the earth: for that, with the sun, only at its rising and [p. 203] setting do such signs occur, comes from no other cause than that, in its oblique position with respect to the earth, it is robbed of its rays and its light is thereby weakened. The moon, however, never has a lustre shooting forth rays about it, let it climb as high as it will. Meanwhile the moon must grow pale through the vapours in the air even sooner than the sun at its rising or setting. For since moonlight is uncommonly much weaker than the light of the sun (§ 137 *Pt. II Exper.*), even the slightest loss can be perceived in it which is not noticeable in the sun. And in this way it can grow pale from fewer vapours in the air than the sun. Now since the changes in the weather come about little by little (§ 115), the moon shows them earlier, but the sun later. Therefore when the sun rises or sets pale, the rainy weather sets in at once, very likely on the same day or the same night: but the moon can well indicate it by its paleness some time beforehand. All this must be confirmed still further by more exact *observations*. Where one has not measured, or cannot measure, the forces of natural things and the magnitude of their effects, there one must unite reason and experience with one another.

*Whether the red colour of the moon indicates winds*

*What difficulties arise here*

*First doubt.*

*Second doubt.*

*Whether the forecast is on that account to be rejected.*

*Conjecture concerning the cause.*

*Why certainty is still lacking.*

[p. 204] § 121. It has also been observed that when the wind blows strongly the moon looks red, and hence from the red colour in calm weather winds are expected. And the same poet, as he goes on, *rubicunda flat*, or: a red moon signifies wind, bears witness that already in ancient times the moon with its red colour was, from experience, made a harbinger of the wind. When a strong wind blows, the air with us usually becomes very light beforehand (§ 44 *Pt. II Exper.*), and if there is no rainy weather with it, it is clear of vapours. Now it is indeed true that light air is rarer than heavy (§ 189 *Phys.*), and that in rare and clear air the light can come down more freely and is also less refracted (§ 151 *Pt. II Exper.*): but from this one does not yet properly see why the moon should acquire a red colour. If few rays of its light are lost on the way, the light merely remains strong. Now if moonlight were to look red when it is strong, redness would have to be its usual colour: which, however, one cannot well say, since we find the moon in calm and clear air, after the rain has passed, golden- coloured rather than reddish. Nor can the lesser refraction of the light well be the cause, [p. 205] since we find the contrary in the red of evening and of morning. For this arises through *refraction* in the watery vapours (§ 203 *Phys.*). But who does not know that it soon vanishes when the sun comes up, and lasts no longer than while the rays fall very obliquely into the air and are consequently strongly refracted? Accordingly the cause seems hard to guess. Meanwhile, although, as we see, the matter has its difficulties, this is nevertheless not enough to call the rule into doubt, as to whether it be in accordance with experience or not. Rather, we must let this urge us to pay more careful attention to it, and at the same time to consult the *instruments* devoted to the observation of the weather (§ 119). Since colours cannot possibly arise otherwise than through *refraction* in the air (§ 158 *Pt. II Exper.*), there must indeed at that time be a matter in the air which refracts the moonlight as it passes through the air. And since we have found that strong *refraction* in watery vapours can produce a red colour, it might well be that this cause also obtains here. For when the air is light, the vapours run together (§ 85 *Pt. II Exper.*). They may, however, be scattered singly, so that one does not see them, and [p. 206] perhaps also be held up already by a wind in the upper air, so that they do not fall down, a wind which, however, we cannot *observe* below, where it is still calm. If the *refraction* is to be strong, the vapours must be very watery, or else very small frozen pellets of hail must be present in the air, by which, however, the air is nevertheless not darkened (§ 307 *Phys.*). These, however, on account of their weight must be held up by a wind. Meanwhile this cannot yet be given out with certainty. For since, besides the fumes, still other evaporations rise into the air, and in it other compound ones can arise out of some simpler ones by mixture, the kind of matter present in the air might also contribute something to a red colour's coming out through the *refraction*: although on this view one does not see how the red colour of the moon is connected with the wind and can serve as a harbinger of it. The cause of this rule remains still uncertain, and perhaps this is an indication that the rule in itself still needs some limitation. It commonly happens that one carries experience beyond its bounds where one does not understand the least thing of its cause.

*When it becomes day early, and the sun rises too early, it signifies rain.*

§ 122. It is easier to comprehend [p. 207] that when day begins to break early, or the sun also rises earlier than it should, this signifies rain. The break of day comes from the rays of sunlight being refracted and *reflected* in the air (§ 191 *Phys.*). When there are many watery vapours in the air, the light is not only strongly refracted (§ 151 *Pt. II Exper.*) but also abundantly *reflected*. And accordingly it can come to the earth earlier than at other times, and also in greater quantity. Therefore in such a case the day must break earlier than at other times, and the sun also be seen above the horizon earlier than it rises (§ 198 *Phys.*), and indeed much earlier than at other times, when the air does not have so many vapours. Now it is certain that many watery vapours in the air are a harbinger of rain (§ 119). Therefore the early break of day and rising of the sun are also a harbinger of it. One sees without my reminding that what has been said of the sun must also hold of the moon.

*What the figure and size of the sun and the moon at their rising and*

*setting signify.*

*Why it does not always certainly signify rain.*

*When it signifies dew and hoar-frost.*

*How fallible signs become infallible.*

*What the moon rising full while waning signifies.*

§ 123. It comes from a *refraction* in the air, and thus from watery vapours, that the sun and the moon, both at their rising and at their setting, have an oval figure and look very large (§ 200 *Phys.*). Now since watery vapours in the air are a harbinger [p. 208] of rain (§ 119), the sun and the moon with their oval figure and unusual size on the horizon can also signify rain. Yet it is not exactly necessary that rain should always follow upon it quite infallibly, or that we in our own place should receive the rain. If no rain-wind blows with it which causes a settled rain, as with us, for instance, the west wind does, it may well give rain only in the place that lies to the east of us or to the west, according as either the rising or the setting heavenly bodies show a change in their figure and size. Further, it is also possible that the vapours merely gather together into clouds, but that the cloud does not rain down, being instead carried off by the winds to another place. Indeed, when the sun and moon change their figure and size at their setting, the vapours may also fall down in the night in a heavy dew (§ 271 *Phys.*), or in the cold season there may be a heavy hoar-frost (§ 273 *Phys.*). And in such a case the changed figure and size signify heavy dew and hoar-frost. If the air changes in its weight, and having hitherto been light now becomes heavier and denser, the vapours may disperse (§ 262 *Phys.*), and neither rain nor dew can [p. 209] follow upon it. All this shows sufficiently that these are not infallible signs of rain, but that still other circumstances must be taken together with them before one can give them out as such. One will also very readily see that this is to be understood of all the remaining signs which bear immediate witness merely to a multitude of watery vapours, and point to rain no further than in so far as these are a harbinger of it. Since it can also very well happen that, from the very same cause whereby the moon is changed into an oval, the waning moon may rise full (§ 201 *Phys.*), and from the very same cause the waxing and the waning moon may rise and set full, one may also from this event pass the same judgement on the impending weather as the changed figure and size of the sun and the moon in the horizon furnish.

*Indication of the weather by the clearness of the stars.*

*When it signifies good weather.*

*What care is needed in this.*

*When a bright sky signifies cold.*

§ 124. When at night, especially at the season of the year when the day does not glimmer through the whole night, the sky is very bright, so that not only can the Milky Way be seen very distinctly, but even the smallest stars can be quite well made out, it points to bright weather. For then the state of the air must be so constituted that the light of the stars is not weakened; consequently there must be no [p. 210] matter present which *reflects* it. Now since the coarse and watery vapours *reflect* the light, there can be few of them to be met with. But we know from what has gone before that the weather does not change in an instant, but little by little. Therefore, in such a state of the air, one can expect no worse weather. Whoever wishes to pay heed to this indication of the weather must learn by experience to judge the differing clearness of the starry sky, so that he does not take a wrong ground for the right one, and the supposed indication of the weather afterwards deceives him. When in winter the sky is bright and the stars sparkle greatly at night, it points to cold. For when the sky is very bright, a wind blows over the mainland, as with us the east wind (§ 221 *Phys.*). But these winds bring cold in winter (§ 220 *Phys.*).

*When the stars indicate overcast weather.*

*1. When few are seen.*

*2. When they twinkle greatly.*

§ 125. One sees that, on the contrary, the sky points to overcast weather when one sees fewer stars than usual, although neither is the moon shining nor does the day glimmer through the whole night, and when, moreover, the stars which one does see, especially those of the first and second magnitude, do not sparkle. For this is a [p. 211] clear indication that the light in the air is weakened, and thus that a matter is present in it which *reflects* it abundantly. Now since this matter is nothing other than coarse watery vapours, inasmuch as the subtle ones do not hinder the transparency of the air (§ 157 Pt. II *Exper.*), but these vapours are a harbinger of overcast weather (§ 119), one may from this appearance of the sky reckon on overcast weather. When I speak of watery vapours, one will judge without my reminding him that the coarse frozen vapours which were previously watery are to be included with them: which is also to be understood of other such cases. When the stars twinkle greatly, it is an indication that the sky will soon be covered over with clouds. This is so certain that no one doubts it, because it follows all too soon, so that often the sky becomes overcast all at once while one is looking at it, as has happened to me myself more than once when I have been teaching lovers of astronomy to know the stars. The cause is this. When coarse watery vapours are in the air, they do not stand still in it, but move up and down. The rays of the stars are refracted as they pass through the vapours (§ 147 Pt. II *Exper.*), and [p. 212] the refracted ray moves up and down as the vapour moves up and down, which can also be shown by the experiment with which I am accustomed to show the *refraction* of light in a darkened chamber (§ *cit.* Pt. II *Exper.*). Now since the stars are raised by *refraction* (§ 199 *Phys.*), they are raised by the wavering rays now much, now little, and therefore begin to twinkle.

*Why more signs of this kind are not adduced.*

§ 126. There are still more changes in the light of the heavenly bodies which may be regarded as harbingers of the impending kinds of weather. For, at least with the help of telescopes, we find, for example, something in the sun which has some similarity to the twinkling of the stars (§ 125), namely the trembling of the sunlight round about its edge when the air is very full of vapour. So too one sometimes perceives a halo round the planets and the great fixed stars, as round the moon, and other such things. But since it is not our undertaking to treat the signs of the kinds of weather at length, but we wish only to give some examples of them which serve for the understanding of the rest, we let the matter rest with what is sufficient as a model if the rules that exist for such signs are [p. 213] to be examined and improved, or perhaps even others made anew. Therefore, with this very purpose, we shall examine in the same manner only a few more signs of another kind.

*When stones and walls indicate a slackening of the cold.*

§ 127. When in winter the stones in the street have been coated with thin ice as with a glaze, one hopes that the weather will soon change and a thaw set in. Where ice freezes, water must come. Now since water can come to the stones from nowhere else than from the air, but in the air it is present in the form of vapours (§ 247 *Phys.*), watery vapours, and indeed coarse ones, must be present in the air when the stones are coated with ice as it were with a glaze. Sometimes one also sees something like a drizzle falling, or perceives as if a mist were coming down; but sometimes one sees nothing of either. In the latter case things go on just as when the windows sweat (§ 272 *Phys.*), and I have no need to explain it more distinctly. Since, accordingly, there are many watery and coarse vapours in the air, it is a sign that rainy weather will set in; and in winter a thaw-wind is wont to bring this with it (§ 242 *Phys.*). If, however, one asks why the watery vapours which [p. 214] melt on the stones freeze, this does not, it is true, properly belong here; yet it can be explained quite easily. In the cold weather the stones have become very cold (§ 76 *Phys.*). Now when the moisture from the air, or a drizzle, falls on them and wets them, the little water is deprived of its heat by the cold stones (§  *cit. Phys.*), and thereby it freezes (§ 119 *Pt. II Exper.*). When the cold strikes out of the buildings, the air is mild and moist (§ 274 *Phys.*). Therefore this too is a sign that the cold is now about to slacken.

*Harbingers of rainy weather, as 1. the sweating of walls &c.*

*2. the moisture of salt.*

§ 128. Much the same is the case in autumn and in spring, or perhaps also on cold summer days, and when it is not really cold in winter, with the sweating of walls, partitions and other things of wood and stone, or also when the square flagstones with which a hall is paved, where the outside air can freely reach it, become quite wet, as I have often experienced in my ante-hall. For the difference between the striking-out of the cold and the sweating of the walls is merely this, that in the first case the walls and other things are colder than in the other, and hence in the other case not so much heat escapes from the moisture as would be required if it were to [p. 215] freeze (§ 119 *Pt. II Exper.*). We also have other things which at all times readily attract the moisture of the air, that is, the watery vapours present in it, whether they be warm or cold. Of this kind is salt, and everything into which salt has soaked. Thus I still remember quite particularly that, when in my youth I was still living in my native country, I observed quite exactly how a floorboard in the anteroom, where a piece of salt in the form of a millstone had lain, such as is usually brought from Poland, always became damp when rain was about to set in. There is no need to remark anything further here than that this indicates a multitude of watery vapours in the air, which are harbingers of overcast weather and of rain (119).

*When the preceding signs are harbingers of good weather.*

*Why the rain- wind changes in such a state.*

*Why the rules of weather- forecasting do not always hold true.*

§ 129. Yet I also cannot omit to remark that these are harbingers of rain only when there has previously been good weather. For when such things follow after rainy weather, it is rather an indication that the rainy weather will change. When after rainy weather the air is very moist, it is an indication that the coarse watery vapours have sunk down in the air (§ 252 *Phys.*). And in this way the upper air becomes clear of vapours. Accordingly, provided that no wind blows which [p. 216] brings in new vapours, the air must become clear, and consequently fine weather must follow. But that the rain-wind must commonly change in such a state can also be understood. When the vapours fall down from the upper air into the lower, the air must become very thin and light (§ 276 *Phys.*). In this way the wind which blows must bring in less air than flows away from us elsewhere. Therefore one sees that still other causes must come in, by which the way is prepared for a new change, since in nature everything changes little by little (§ 115). And accordingly one can reckon upon a change in the rainy weather, and consequently upon fine weather. One sees from this that the rules which have been made concerning the times of the weather are not so constituted as they would have to be if they were always to hold true. Namely, one has not sufficiently *determined* in them all the circumstances to which one must pay attention if one wishes to judge with certainty (§ 330 *Met.*). And this is the common oversight in experience, for which more than one pair of eyes is needed if one wishes to note everything correctly.

*Other signs*

*of rain: 1. the snapping of strings.*

*How one makes the hygrometer into a weather- prophet.*

*2. Cracking of the boards.*

*3. The cracks in woodwork swelling shut.*

*Caution to be used in some cases*

*Why wood splits along its length*

*and shrinks across its breadth.*

*When the cracking of boards signifies dry weather.*

§ 130. When the strings of gut on musical instruments are tightly stretched [p. 217] and snap, as it were, of themselves, this is taken as a harbinger of rain. A string snaps when it is stretched too tightly. Therefore, since it snaps here where no one is present who might stretch it further, there must be a cause present in the air in which it lies by which the greater tension comes about. It is known that a string draws the moisture of the air into itself and is thereby much shortened (§ 98 *Pt. II Exper.*). Now if it is tightly stretched on the instrument and the moisture penetrates into it, it ought to become shorter thereby, and this is no longer possible. Therefore it is just the same as if it were drawn too hard, and it must accordingly snap. In this way the snapping of the string shows that there is much moisture in the air. But much moisture in the air is a harbinger of rainy weather (§ 119). And accordingly one can also use hygrometers as weather-prophets. But if one wishes to know by them which degree of moisture points to rainy weather, and which degree of dryness to clear weather, one must continue the observations for some time, so that one learns to *determine* these degrees by experience. It also tends to happen that at times the boards [p. 218] and the things made from them, such as chests, cupboards, tables, benches, doors &c., crack. Wood likewise draws moisture into itself and swells up from it (§ 100 *Pt. II Exper.*). Hence it goes as with the string, that the wood at times splits, as indeed one can also at times see the crack where it has split, when it dries out again and shrinks. But even if nothing of it is to be seen, a small crack which one does not see can nevertheless produce a noticeable sound when the wood splits. When doors that have split and have cracks swell up so that one no longer sees the crack, this too is a sign that the air is very moist. Meanwhile this too is certain, that things made of wood tend to split when they dry out too much. Experience attests it, and it has therefore become a rule in architecture that one should use well-dried wood for everything. One can also quite well understand how it comes about. When wood dries out, it shrinks. Now if it is so held at the ends on the sides that it cannot give, it must split. But it splits along its length and shrinks across its breadth. For it consists of fibres which run down along the length, but at the sides are joined to one [p. 219] another. When now the moisture goes out of the pores between the fibres, they come closer together across the breadth, and, if this cannot take place throughout the whole, some must tear loose from the others. Accordingly the cracking of boards can also be a sign of very dry weather. And accordingly one has all the more cause to *determine* the particular circumstances more exactly, since one and the same thing can have contrary significations.

*How a strong smell points to rain.*

*Whether smell is carried more easily through thin air.*

*Whether it is carried more easily through moist air.*

*Why flowers smell more strongly in the evening and at night.*

§ 131. When flowers and herbs that have a strong smell can be smelt far off, this is taken as a mark that rainy weather will set in. When rainy weather is about to set in, the air becomes light (§ 41 *Pt. II Exper.*) and moist (§ 115). The question therefore is whether the smell is carried along by the thin air or by the moisture, or whether both contribute something to it. Smell consists in an outflow of a subtle matter which disperses itself through the air (§ 116 *Pt. III Exper.*) and causes the smell when one draws it into the nose with the air in drawing breath (§ 431 *Phys.*). And accordingly one would suppose that the smell could spread better in thin air than in dense. But another experience at once stands against this, if one wishes to maintain it. [p. 220] When the sun shines warm at noon, the air becomes thin (§ 133 *Pt. I Exper.*), and yet the flowers smell least, indeed some not at all, precisely because the smell spreads out far too much and is thereby weakened. On the other hand, when it grows cool towards evening and the air becomes denser again (§ *cit. Pt. I Exper.*), they smell much more strongly. Indeed some flowers which have a very delicate smell, such as the night-violets, do not smell at all by day, but only at night. We must therefore seek another cause than the lightness of the air. If a smell is strong, the outflow of the subtle matter which causes it must remain very close together and may not spread out much. Therefore, if the smell is carried further than usual, there must be a cause present which holds it together. Such are the watery vapours. For it is known from experience that water draws smells to itself. We are accustomed to pour water on things that stink badly; then the stench is all drawn into the water and does not go into the air. If, therefore, there are watery vapours in the air, the smell is drawn into them, and, since these do not so readily let it go again, they bring it densely into the nose. And precisely [p. 221] this is the reason why flowers smell more strongly in the evening and at night. For when the air cools, it also becomes moist (§ 271 *Phys.*), as one can indeed quite distinctly perceive when one walks about in the evening air, especially in the open field or in a garden that lies open. Since, therefore, a strong smell carried into the distance is an indication of watery vapours in the air, it is no wonder if it betokens rain (§ 115). To this belongs also the fact that the strong stench from privies announces rainy weather, and indeed persists during such weather.

*When candles betoken rain: namely 1. by crackling.*

*2. by a halo.*

*When the halo round the candle acquires colours.*

*Caution in this weather- forecasting.*

§ 132. Candles too are held to be rain-prophets when the flame crackles. When water gets into fire, it crackles. For in water there is much air (§ 148 *Pt. I Exper.*). The air is expanded by the heat (§ 133 *Pt. I Exper.*) and forms little bubbles, which at length burst and thereby cause the sound (§ 16 *Pt. III Exper.*). One also sees quite distinctly that this is the true cause when one lets a drop of water fall on hot iron or on a glowing coal: for at once it turns into little bubbles, which vanish one after another. The watery vapours in the air are little bubbles which have a [p. 222] thick shell of water and air inside (§ 125 *Phys.*). As soon, therefore, as they come to the flame of the candle, the air expands and stretches the little bubbles so strongly that they burst with a little crack. Therefore when the flame of the candle crackles, it may be gathered from this that there is much watery moisture in the air, and this is wont to be a harbinger of rain. It is counted under this head when the candle has a halo, which at times even plays with colours. It is known in general that through the reflection of light from the air round about the candle a strong brightness is produced (§ 86 *Opt.*). When this circular brightness becomes so strong that it can be quite distinctly seen, it is called a halo, because it has some affinity with the halo round the moon and the stars (§ 306 *Phys.*). It is therefore certain that, if a halo is to be produced round the candle, a matter must be present which *reflects* the light more strongly than the air. Now since at the time when the air is very moist many vapours are present which *reflect* the light more strongly than the air, we have a cause by which the halo round the candle can be produced. And accordingly one can recognise from it the great moisture of the air, which as a harbinger of rainy weather [p. 223] is wont to occur. If colours are to arise, a matter must be present which *refracts* the light (§ 158 *Pt. II Exper.*). And in fact the triangular glass *prisms* also show that the light of a candle, such as one is wont to burn in the evening, is changed into colours by refraction. Vapours also refract the light so that it can be changed into colours, as one can quite distinctly see when one sets steaming water or *coffee* in the sun and keeps the eye in the dark, so that one looks sideways at the steam rising from the surface of the water. Therefore if the halo round the candle is to acquire colours, very large vapours must be found round it in great quantity. If one wishes to judge from the constitution of the flame of the candle of the state of the air, and further from this of the kinds of weather, one must be certain that in the place where the candle burns the air is constituted just as it is outside under the open sky.

*How animals become weather-prophets.*

*What changes in the air cause in flowers.*

*That the air has an influence on the actions of animals.*

*How the weather-forecasting of animals is to be investigated.*

§ 133. Before the weather changes noticeably, imperceptible changes already take place in the air, as in its weight, in its density, in its heat and in its moisture (§ 115); indeed, the instruments by which we ascertain the magnitude of these changes, such as the [p. 224] barometer, manometer, thermometer and hygrometer, show this plainly (§ 22 ff. *Pt. II Exper.*) if one attends to their changes several times a day. The air has a great influence on the life and motion of animals (§ 101 *ff. Pt. III Exper.*). Therefore it may well be that its incipient changes also produce something changeable in them and *determine* them to certain motions and cries, from which one can take an indication of the impending change of the weather. One need not wonder at this: for we find still other events in nature where we are certain that the change in the air alone produces them. Thus tulips and other flowers open in the morning and remain in that state through the day; but when evening comes on, when the air becomes cool and moist, they close again. That nothing but the change of the air is the cause of this is proved by the solar eclipse which occurred *Anno* 1706 in May. For the tulips had then opened; but when towards 9 o'clock the eclipse greatly increased and the air took on an appearance as if evening were coming on, they closed. The pigeons, too, flew from the field [p. 225] home, and the bats came flying out, which are otherwise not to be seen at that time (§ 251 *Astron.*). From which it was sufficiently seen that the animals do not keep their regular time merely because they have been accustomed to it, but rather that they are *determined* to it by the external state of the air and the changes of the light. Since at that time everything happened as is wont to happen when evening is coming on, the air must certainly have undergone such changes as are wont to occur in it towards evening. The then Royal *Astronomer* at Berlin, the late Mr **Hoffmann**,<sup>a</sup> observed the changes in the air by means of the barometer, thermometer and hygrometer, and found some change in every one of them, but most of all in the moisture of the air, which accords very well both with the closing of the tulips and with the other events. And accordingly I hold it to be a matter which one may no longer doubt, that the air through its changes can also produce changes in animals and plants, even though these do not of themselves strike the senses. If, therefore, one wished to investigate the rules [p. 226] in which animals are represented as weather- prophets, or intended to find out others as well by one's own experience, one need only attend to the unusual actions of cattle and of animals, and at the same time note the changes of the weather-instruments. The multitude of observations will show what is to be made of the matter; and if one investigates, from the doctrines of physics, the ground of the rules that have been made, it will become apparent whether the changes of the air were the cause of what is observed in the animals or not. Since hitherto observations have not been made with such caution that attention was at the same time paid to the imperceptible change in the air, then, as is wont to happen in such cases, without doubt much will turn up along with them that has not sufficient ground.

<sup>a</sup> *Miscellan. Berol. p. 238. 239.*

*Whether the extraordinary crowing of the cock signifies a change of the weather, and other similar*

*examples.*

*That the extraordinary crowing has an external cause.*

*That it shows itself in a sensation of touch which is caused by the changed air.*

*How the proof*

*from the crowing of cocks is to be applied to the actions of other animals.*

§ 134. I must adduce one or two further examples, partly so that we may learn to know what truth there is in this respect, partly so that one may see, as in a pattern, how one has to proceed herein when one wishes to seek the ground of the rules. Nothing is better known than that from the crowing of the cocks at an unusual time one conjectures a change of the weather. It is said: [p. 227] the cocks are crowing a great deal, the weather will change. The crowing of the cocks must have a cause by which they are *determined* to it. It will perhaps readily be granted me that this cause is to be sought outside the cock. Yet if anyone should have misgivings about granting it, it can also well be proved. The crowing of the cocks agrees with the crying out of men, and thus with an action that is governed by the appetite. And indeed we also find that the crowing of the cock is governed by its appetite. For it varies its crowing according to the constitution of its appetite, since the crowing is different when it calls the hen and when it runs after her, different when it has trodden her, and different when it crows at an unusual time. Even in men the appetite takes its origin from a present sensation (§ 879 *Met.*): for although man does not strive after a thing necessarily, but freely (§ 519 *Met.*), and thus the appetite does not necessarily spring from the sensation, yet the sensation gives occasion to it, and the appetite would not have followed without it. If I am to acquire an appetite for a thing, I must first think of it; but that I think of it happens either because the senses represent the thing as present [p. 228] to me, or because what they represent to me brings something else into my memory (§ 238 *Met.*). And accordingly it will readily be believed that in animals too those actions which are governed by their appetite take their origin from the sensations, and consequently that the crowing of the cock which happens at an extraordinary time must also come from a sensation. Whatever causes the sensation must touch the organs of the sensations. Now the cock crows in the dark and in the quiet, when it neither sees nor hears anything. Therefore the sensation by which it is *determined* to crow is a sensation of touch. Now since there is nothing around the cock that touches its body except the air in which it finds itself, this must cause an extraordinary sensation of touch in the cock, by which it is *determined* to crow. But if the air is to cause a different sensation of touch from before, a change perceptible to the cock must have taken place in it. Therefore, since such a change in the air is a harbinger of the change of the weather, the rule that the crowing of the cocks at an unusual time is a sign of the change of the weather has a ground in nature. Just the same inference holds when other [p. 229] animals at certain times undertake unusual actions or motions, when no ground for them is to be found in themselves, nor does anything strike the eye from without that could *determine* them to it. For the proof conducted from the cock is derived from general grounds, and can accordingly be brought to bear in all similar cases. In particular, the crying of the night-owl by night agrees with the crowing of the cock, which, since it happens in good weather, one is accustomed to interpret as betokening rain. For this very reason the bathing and diving of ducks and water-fowl with a cry, when it is about to rain, and the unusual croaking of frogs in the morning, fall under the very same case.

*Why it betokens rain when the bees fly out early, but not far.*

*Cause why they fly out early.*

*Cause why they soon turn back.*

§ 135. But in order that one may also recognise that one need not always rest content with this general cause, but may also proceed to particular ones, I have had to give one more example of it. It is a well-known thing that, when it is about to rain, the bees set out early to gather their honey, but do not fly far out, and rather soon turn back again. I presuppose that bees have a very subtle sense of smell, because I find this grounded in experience. I have constantly observed that, when in spring [p. 230] the apricots and peaches begin to blossom, the bees also appear in great numbers in the gardens in the town as soon as the blossoms open, although no one keeps bees nearby, and afterwards not a single bee comes there again the whole year through. Now since there is no one who tells them that trees are blossoming here from which they can gather honey, it cannot be otherwise than that they are led there by smell. We have seen in what precedes that, when rainy weather is about to set in, flowers smell more strongly than at other times (§ 131). And accordingly we find a cause why the bee sets out earlier than at other times: for it follows the smell which it perceives. But why does it not go far, but soon turn back again? This happens either because it has found flowers nearby, to which it was led by the smell, or because it finds a difficulty in flying. The first might indeed be said with some appearance of truth; but the second has more ground in its favour, and, since it alone is a sufficient cause why it soon returns to its hive, the first also loses its probability. We know in general that not only the bees but all flying vermin are not [p. 231] able to fly when their wings become wet. Before rainy weather sets in, there are many watery vapours in the air (§ 115), which draw themselves into those things that become damp (§ 93, *Pt. II Exper.*). Therefore, since the wings of bees and of other vermin become wet, they too must become damp in the moist air. And in this way the bee feels some difficulty in flying, by which it is induced to turn back again. And this is in general the cause why flying vermin creep away out of the open air when rain is about to come.

*Signs of the weather that smack of superstition.*

*What is to be thought of weather- forecasting*

*from Christmas Day and Christmas Night.*

*Distinguishing mark of superstition.*

*How it creeps in.*

*How people hit upon Christmas Day.*
