*Why the 12 nights were adopted.*

*Why people looked to the day of the week.*

*Ground of the superstition.*

*Is refuted.*

*A doubt is removed*

*Yet another objection is met.*

*Proof of the explanation*

*of the Author.*

§ 136. Since the signs of the weather explained so far, and the like, are grounded in nature, and also have clear experience on their side, which assures us of their correctness and gives us occasion to *determine* them more exactly, so that they may come true still more correctly (§ 117 ff.), people have let themselves be persuaded all the more readily that there were yet other signs of the weather as well, in which, however, superstition manifestly has a share, and which were long ago put into German rhymes under the title of the *Peasants' Practica*, and afterwards, when the German language had become more polished, somewhat improved. From the weather on Christmas Night people would judge [p. 232] of the weather of the whole year, and indeed even from whether Christmas Day falls on a Sunday or on another day of the week, of the changeable kinds of weather over the following year. To this belongs further the rule of the 12 days and 12 nights reckoned from Christmas Day, from which one *prognosticates* the constitution of the 12 months of the following year. For example, that Christmas Day falls on a Sunday is supposed to signify a warm winter, strong winds, many tempests, a pleasant spring, a hot and dry summer, a moist and cold autumn. Since the kinds of weather have their natural causes from which they arise, as I have set out at length in my Thoughts on the Effects of Nature *ch. 4 ff.*, but Christmas Day, and its falling on this or that day of the week, by no means belongs among these, one easily sees that superstition has a share in these rules. And it can also be very well understood how men can have been brought to such rules by superstition. Christmas Day falls at the end of the year, when the countryman, to whom the weather matters, recalls whether he has had a good year this time or not (§ 238 *Met.*), and during the holidays [p. 233] he has time to think of it, because he rests from his work. If he has had a good year, he rejoices at it (§ 446 *Met.*); but if he has had a bad one, he becomes sad (§ 448 *Met.*), although he soon knows how to raise himself up again if he is assured of the wise government of God (§ 728 *Mor.*). Now since he at the same time thinks of the coming year, whether it will be good or not, he certainly wishes that the former may be the case, but not the latter (§ 492, 493 *Met.*), and acquires a longing for the former (§ 483 *Met.*), especially when particular circumstances are added which make the fertile year agreeable to him and the barren one repugnant (§ 441 *Met.*). Now since, along with the longing, there is still fear whether the fertile year might not perhaps fail to come and the barren one set in (§ 483 *Met.*); but fear disquiets the mind (§ 476 *Met.*), and one is therefore glad to be rid of it (§ 432, 436 *Met.*): there arises from this a desire to know what one may well have to expect in the coming year. Now from ancient times the superstition has crept in among us that the common man imagines that on Christmas Night or on Christmas Eve one can judge of future fortune or [p. 234] misfortune, and in general of what is to happen in the future. Therefore a countryman very easily came to the thought that one would also be able to judge, on Holy Christmas Eve or on Christmas Night, of the impending fertility and barrenness of the year (§ 1, ch. 4 *Log.*), and consequently also of the weather, as the cause of fertility and barrenness, which is known to a countryman from constant experience (§ 325 *Met.*). But now the weather differs, and one year is not like another, and the difference of the weather at a certain season promotes fertility in one and hinders it in another (§ 114): therefore it was very soon seen that one could not make do with a single night, and on that account a day was taken for each month of the following year. Since, moreover, no difference occurs in Christmas Day in itself except that it falls either on this or on another day of the week, and yet the difference which is supposed to be found in the thing signified must be inferred from the difference found in the sign, as the definition of a sign readily suggests (§ 292 *Met.*), so one has also had to look for other kinds of weather [p. 235] according as Christmas Day falls on this or on another day of the week. But now since in all these rules it is presupposed that Christmas Day, and the days next following it, are on its account to have something prophetic about them, which, however, consists in a mere fancy and cannot be confirmed by the least ground, they are in the end all founded on a false opinion about the birth of Christ (§ 394 *Met.*), and such groundless opinions are reckoned as superstition. And the groundlessness of this opinion also becomes clear from the fact that Christ was not born precisely in the night which we are accustomed to take for it<sup>a</sup>. Accordingly, even if one were willing to grant that Christ had endowed his birthday with a prophetic power and thereby distinguished it from the other days of the year, one would still not be permitted to assume 25 *December* at pleasure, but one would have to know the actual day of the birth, the memory of which God would in this case also have preserved. Should anyone say that the way I have indicated of arriving at this superstition is far too contrived for one [p. 236] to be able to ascribe it to the countryman, then it is not hard to answer this doubt that has been raised. It requires reflection and knowledge of the soul if one wishes to explain how one comes by degrees from one thought to another; whereas he who comes from the one to the other need not concern himself with how it happens. But neither will anyone object that it is superfluous to trace so deeply the origin of a thing which, as one will readily be granted, is rightly rejected. For whoever would allege this would not know what is incumbent upon a philosopher. The knowledge of a philosopher consists precisely in this, that he shows how that which happens can happen, or is possible (§ 5 *Proleg. Log.*). Therefore, since I have done nothing more than show, from the nature of the soul as I have explained it in my Reasonable Thoughts on God, the World and the Soul of Man, how it was possible that people could arrive at the *Peasants' Practica*, which stems from superstition, I have gone no further than my undertaking requires, which obliges me to furnish that knowledge which a philosopher ought to have of natural things. But that I have not erred in my explanation, one will believe me all the more readily [p. 237] even if one is not in a position to weigh the grounds adduced, since people have presumed to judge from the weather on Christmas Day and on the days following it also of other fortunate and unfortunate events of men which have no connection whatever with the weather, for example of war and peace, of the fertility of women, and so on.

<sup>a</sup> *Vid. Ricciolus Chronol. Reform. lib. 8. c. 2. f. 300.*

*Signs of the fertility of the year.*

*Example.*

§ 137. That fertile and barren times come one among another, and also that the year is fertile in one thing but barren in other things, arises from the constitution of the changeable kinds of weather in a season (§ 114). Therefore the changeable kinds of weather at this or that time of the year can furnish signs of fertility and barrenness, both in general and also in particular as to whether this or that will turn out well (§ 293 *Met.*). But one easily sees that whoever wishes to judge from these signs must be very well experienced in agriculture and gardening, so that he knows what weather each thing needs when the seed goes into the soil, when it comes up, when the plant grows on, when it blossoms, when the fruit ripens. Accordingly, before one can judge of it with certainty, one must [p. 238] first bring husbandry of the field and gardening into the form of a science by sound grounds, through good experience, well-conducted experiments and the investigation of what one has observed, so far as is necessary for this purpose. Therefore, although country people have carried about with them since time immemorial many rules from which they are accustomed to *prognosticate* in this respect<sup>a</sup>, yet one cannot well judge of them where one has neither experience of one's own nor has made the experiments necessary for it. For before I can foretell the effect from the cause, or also announce from one effect another which has a like cause with it, I must first know well that this is the cause of this effect. But I will here adduce some particular examples, so that one may judge from them how one has to proceed in other cases. For example, it is commonly said:

<sup>a</sup> Florini kluger Hauß-Vatter *lib. 2. cap. 88. f. 483. & seqq.*

A dry March, a wet April, a cool May, Fill barns and cellars, and bring much hay.

*What a dry March contributes to fertility.*

*What the wet April signifies;*

*What the cool May.*

*Why the rule taken from this*

*is only probable.*

*First cause.*

*Second cause.*

*Why it is suited to the hay.*

*How far to the vine.*

*Why it admits of exception here.*

*How one obtains greater certainty.*

If we wish to investigate this rule, we have to look to three things. For [p. 239] 1. it is to be shown why the corn thrives well when March is dry, April wet and May cool; 2. what this contributes to the growth of the vine; 3. how this weather is serviceable to the hay. In February the snow begins to melt, at least by day, and penetrates abundantly into the soil. If it thaws completely either at the end of this month or in March, still more wetness draws into the soil. The cold has driven the soil apart while it was frozen (§ 120 *Pt. II Exper.*). Now if it thaws again, the wetness finds all the more room in it to stand in between. In this state the soil is unfit to be well ploughed and dug and sown. Now if March is dry, the soil dries out and settles together again, so that it does indeed remain soft and loose, and moist as well, but yet not too much wetness stays standing in the all too many pores of the soil and perhaps even spoils there. Accordingly the soil can be well ploughed and dug, and the seed well harrowed in and set; and in loose soil everything can take root well. Now since one tills the summer fields in March and at the beginning of April, in which one sows barley, oats and [p. 240] other summer corn, peas, lentils and vetches, one finds the field for them in such weather as one could wish. Now if rainy weather sets in in April, the field and the ground in the garden are not only kept constantly moist, but they also constantly receive fresh moisture, so that the seed can germinate and come up well. Finally, if May is cool, the corn does not overgrow itself, nor does whatever else one has sown in spring run too much to grass, and hence the plant retains more force for the seed; as indeed we find by experience that when the corn grows all too much into straw, it gets the fewer grains, or at least lighter grains, in the ears. Likewise, if it is cool in May, the soil does not dry out at the time when the corn most needs nourishment for its growth, and it can therefore also grow more into tillers<sup>a</sup>, and consequently obtain more ears, than when the weather is so dry that there is a want of nutritive juice. The rule is therefore grounded with respect to the corn. But since other causes may join in which [p. 241] may do harm to the fruit, then by virtue of this rule alone one can certainly expect the outcome only with some probability. When the meadows are all too moist, no good grass grows on them: from which one sees that excessive wetness is also a hindrance to the growth of grass. Indeed, one also sees that the grass on the meadows only truly begins to grow when the ground has settled and yet at the same time is loose and moist. Since the wind dries strongly in spring, according to experience, there is a want of moisture when April is dry, and so the grass finds no nourishment at the time when it most needs it. In May everything on the meadows grows fully up and attains its proper ripeness, at which time it has its best strength<sup>a′</sup>. But if May is too warm, the plants *transpire* too strongly and the ground becomes too dry: hence no strong and vigorous grass grows. The weather, therefore, that is stated in the rule is suited to the hay as well. The vine gains its buds in April and towards May begins to put forth leaves. In May it actually puts forth leaves and sets its grapes. But it needs much nourishment, because it belongs among those plants [p. 242] which shoot very vigorously; and therefore the ground where it grows must be provided with sufficient nourishment, and the *transpiration* too, while the new shoots are still young, must not be too strong: to which a moist April and a cool May after a dry March are fitting. But in the following months the vine still needs a great deal more weather, and therefore nothing can be judged of its success from these alone. Therefore the rule admits of exception nowhere more than with the vine. Should one wish to prognosticate of the vine with greater certainty, one would have to pay heed at the same time to the kinds of weather of the following months. For this particular observations would be required, which can also be made with other plants. Namely, one would have to record the kinds of weather diligently for many years and at the same time write down whatever changes show themselves in the vine-stock; thus more would show itself in experience that would give occasion for reflection.

<sup>a</sup> Von Vermehrung des Getreydes *c. 2.* §. 22. *p. 17.*

<sup>a′</sup> Thümmig's Versuche *part. 3. num. 10. p. 250.*

[p. 243]

### Das IV. Capitel. Von Dünsten / Nebel und Wolcken. — Chapter IV. On Vapours, Mist and Clouds.

*What use the vapours are.*

*Hidden benefit.*

§ 138. The vapours do not rise out of the water and moist bodies into the air to no purpose: for they serve to this end, that the air can become moist and does not always remain dry (§ 93 *Pt. II Exper.*), that mist (§ 255 *Phys.*), clouds (§ 261 *Phys.*), dew (§ 270 *Phys.*), hoar-frost (§ 273 *Phys.*), rain (§ 276 *Phys.*), snow (§ 281 *Phys.*), hail (§ 287 *Phys.*), and also the rainbow (§ 291 *Phys.*) and other aerial phenomena can be generated in nature (§ 307, 313, 317 *ff. Phys.*). And in this way the vapours are used by nature for a great many things, and very much in the world would not take place if there were no vapours in it. This benefit strikes the eye at once of anyone who looks about him even a little in the world, or leafs through a book in which natural events are explained. But they have yet another benefit [p. 244] which does not so immediately strike the eye, and of which I consider myself all the more obliged to treat, the less everyone is able to see it for himself. But if we consider only this which strikes everyone's eye at once, we already have a ladder by which we can climb up from the creature to God, and behold a particular circumstance of his wisdom which has a great influence on the actions of man, and especially on the tranquillity and joyfulness of his mind. Therefore, although I do not lead from the creature to God on every occasion, because the general consideration which I treated above (*ch. 2* § 8 *ff.*) is sufficient for everyone to be able to apply for himself in all particular cases what was there shown in general, yet I can by no means pass over those occasions where we are led to particular circumstances of the divine perfections which it is very useful for us to know.

*How the vapours lead us to the knowledge of particular circumstances of the*

*wisdom of God.*

§ 139. The vapours, if one considers them in themselves, are something mean, nothing but little bubbles of water (§ 85 *Pt. II Exper.*) which disperse in the air and vanish before our eyes as they rise (§ 247 *Phys.*). As long as [p. 245] they are dispersed in the air, they are driven to and fro by the wind. For since we see that the clouds, which are nothing but a mass of vapours drawn together into a mist (§ 261 *Phys.*), are driven by the winds, the vapours dispersed singly in the air must all the more follow their motion (§ 205 *Phys.*). Indeed, we have also already seen that a rain-wind at first brings only vapours before the clouds follow (§ 119). Now although the vapours are in themselves something so slight, and are dispersed in the air quite without order, and also have no regular motion of their own, they are nevertheless used by God in nature to bring about many remarkable things (§ 138). Now since God acts with design in everything that happens in nature (§ 1027 *Met.*), as one who has weighed everything long beforehand, before it comes to pass (§ 968 *Met.*), one recognises from this how God accomplishes a great deal through humble means, slight in themselves, which one would never have supposed could be brought about by them. How many would there be who would give credence if someone told them that from little bubbles dispersed in the air without any order, which also have no motion of their own, but [p. 246] are driven about by the wind by chance, so many wonderful things should arise as I have just related (§ 138), if we did not see all this daily, and custom had not taken away our wonder? But now since the hidden benefit of the vapours extends their use still further, the thought of this particular circumstance is thereby raised still higher. And in this way we have yet a new cause why we should investigate still further the benefit of the vapours, which is somewhat more concealed.

*Benefit of vapours in diminishing heat.*

*How they cool the air.*

*How this means is used in art.*

§ 140. The vapours which are dispersed in the air and do not hinder its clearness weaken the force of the sun, and that at a time when it is very hot (§ 238 *Phys.*). They therefore contribute something towards keeping the heat from increasing excessively. Perhaps one might object that this loss of heat is of no great consequence. Now it is indeed true that, with respect to the rest of the heat, it might perhaps not be of very great consequence, which cannot be determined more exactly here, since we have nothing to do with mathematical knowledge, to which this belongs: meanwhile, since in the effect of burning-glasses and burning- mirrors it is nevertheless quite [p. 247] noticeable (§ *cit.*), one cannot after all regard it as something of such a kind that nothing noticeable could proceed from it in the effects of heat. But besides this, the vapours can also cool the air in yet another way when it has become too warm. Water takes on more heat than air, and yet for all that remains cool (§ 108 *Pt. II Exper.*). Therefore, since a cold body takes on a part of the heat from a warmer one (§ 76 *Phys.*), the watery vapours which come into the air, or are generated in it, must also take to themselves a part of the heat of the air. Now since in this way the air becomes cool (§ 116 *Pt. II Exper.*), and, as we have just seen, the water nevertheless remains fresh, the heat is lost from the air through the vapours, so that one does not know where it has gone, and the air in this way becomes cooler. And this is also probably the cause why on hot days it becomes cooler in a hall or another chamber when one sprinkles the floor heavily with fresh water, as it evaporates again, or else fills the air with some vapours in another way, as by leaves laid in fresh water.

*Vapours serve to preserve smell longer in a chamber, and also to carry it further elsewhere.*

*Means of preserving smell in a chamber.*

[p. 248] § 141. I have already shown above that the watery vapours take on and hold together smell, which would otherwise very soon disperse and on account of its weakness become imperceptible (§ 131). And by this means a delicate smell is carried far. I have no hesitation in maintaining that the vapours rising from the sea are to be regarded as one of the causes why at sea one can smell quite distinctly, as far as 30 German miles off, the rosemary that grows in Spain not far from the shores<sup>a</sup>. And indeed we also find in spring, when the trees are in blossom and, besides this, flowers and herbs abundantly disperse their smell through the air, that the rain has a pleasant smell, which it cannot have obtained from anywhere else than from the fragrant evaporations from the blossoms, flowers and herbs which the watery vapours in the air have taken on. And so experience also shows that a smell in a chamber which passes into the air of itself is soon lost; on the other hand, if one lets fragrant waters evaporate, the smell is preserved longer. Therefore [p. 249] to this end fume-balls, or so-called *aeolipilae*, were formerly used (§ 176 *Pt. I Exper.*).

<sup>a</sup> Sturm *Phys. Hypoth. Tom. 1. sect. 2. c. 6. phæn. 2. p. 336.*

*Vapours sustain springs and rivers.*

*Particular proof of the wisdom of God.*

§ 142. Vapours sustain the springs not only by the rain-water and snow-water (§ 343 *Phys.*), but also in that during the night they separate from the air on mountains fit to produce springs (§ 345 *Phys.*). The rivers are sustained by the springs, which carry their water into the sea (§ 340 *Phys.*). And accordingly evaporation is the means by which the circulation of water in nature is maintained, namely that it moves from one place into another, and from there comes back again to the place from which it had flowed. For by this means it is brought back from the sea and the rivers to the springs, and also from the sea to the rivers; the springs, however, bring it back into the rivers, and these back into the water. In this way God ensures that water is never lacking in any place on the surface of the earth, and at the same time that it can remain in constant motion, so that it does not become stinking, as it would were it to stand still in one place for many years. This very thing most splendidly confirms the circumstance, pointed out before, of the divine [p. 250] wisdom, namely that God, according to his infinite knowledge, accomplishes very important things in nature by means that in appearance are quite slight. For that it is something very important that water is nowhere lacking on the surface of the earth, and that water is everywhere found fresh, we shall afterwards clearly recognise when we consider the benefit of water.

*Evaporation from the earth keeps the plants healthy.*

*Superfluous moisture harms the plants.*

*How much here presents itself for investigation.*

§ 143. Whoever pays attention to what I have just said will perceive something quite remarkable, and will take delight in this ray of the wisdom of God. The earth is moistened with rain so that the plants may have their nourishment, as I shall shortly show more fully. And yet rain is followed by warm sunshine, through which the moisture of the earth evaporates (§ 248 *Phys.*); indeed, the winds at times, especially in spring, likewise dry out the ground. One would therefore think that sun and winds deprived the plants of their nourishment through the evaporation which they cause, and that accordingly the means in nature were contrary to one another. But these are thoughts which occur to one when one regards things only superficially, but which vanish again at once when one considers them more closely. [p. 251] Who does not know that superfluous moisture is harmful to plants, and in general to all growing things, in that they overgrow themselves and the force goes into the leaves and the stalk which ought to be kept in reserve for the fruit and the seed? Next (and this is chiefly what I am now attending to), we know that the grass and herbs which grow on wet meadows give unhealthy, or at least sour and coarse, fodder, which the cattle eat reluctantly and from which they get a cough and consumption<sup>a</sup>. Whence comes this affliction? From nowhere else than from that in which the damp and wet meadows differ from the others. Now the difference consists in this, that in damp and wet meadows the water remains standing and spoils within the pores of the soil, whereas in the others it evaporates and is thereby kept in motion, since through evaporation the moisture is brought from the lower soil into the upper, so that it cannot become putrid and stinking; not to mention that it also appears that the water which is divided into subtle droplets through the pores [p. 252] of the motes of earth is not so easily spoiled as that which is together in great quantity, and in which the motes of earth, as it were, swim; which, however, we shall not now investigate more exactly. Accordingly grass and herbs become unhealthy from the water standing in the wide pores of the soil, which becomes putrid and stinking therein. And in this way evaporation is a means whereby one has healthy grass and healthy plants. This could give us occasion for many further good thoughts, in that we could investigate what kinds of corn and kitchen-vegetables are beneficial to the health of man, according to the difference of the soil where they have grown and of the weather in which they have grown. And I do not doubt that this consideration, if it were pursued in the proper manner, would in many respects shed great light upon the health of man. But such considerations would lead us further than we now intend; we should also, as anyone who is practised in the knowledge of nature and likes to see with his own eyes can very easily foresee, meet with various obstacles on the way which would detain us and not let us proceed until we had, by competent observations [p. 253] and experiments, which require time and opportunity, cleared them out of the way.

<sup>a</sup> Mr von Hohberg in *Georgic. Curios. part. 2. lib. 9. c. 10. f. 294.*

*Vapours refresh plants.*

*How they wilt.*

*How they become fresh again.*

*How the moisture of the air can refresh them.*

§ 144. In great heat plants wilt, if not all, at least some, as experience shows. On the other hand, when evening comes, what had wilted in the sun becomes fresh again. Plants wilt when they *transpire* too much of their moisture: which one may recognise from the fact that they become fresh again if one either moistens them, so that the water can draw itself in through the pores of the stalk and of the leaves, or stands the stalk in water, so that it can rise into the plant through the sap-vessels, especially between the bark (§ 94, 95 *Pt. III Exper.*). Therefore if a plant which still stands in the earth wilts in the sun and becomes fresh again in the evening, then either water must come out of the earth through the stalk into the leaves, or moisture must draw itself in out of the air. We see the former when we water the earth around a plant which is dry: for if it has wilted for lack of the nourishment which it ought to have from the earth, it becomes fresh again some time after it has been watered. But that this [p. 254] is not the only, nor indeed the most usual, cause in nature by which she refreshes wilted plants that the sun has oppressed too much, we notice from this, that this refreshment also takes place when the soil is fairly dry. And accordingly the moisture of the air must accomplish it. And it can also be understood how this is possible. Towards evening the air becomes moist (§ 131), and the leaves have pores on both sides, through which they can draw the moisture to themselves (§ 94 *Pt. III Exper.*). What wonder is it, then, if they draw to themselves the vapours present in the air, which make up the moisture of the air (§ 93 *Pt. II Exper.*), like other bodies (§ 94 *Pt. II Exper.*)?

*Vapours make the air fit to give signs of the weather.*

§ 145. Before fair weather changes into overcast, the air becomes moist with watery vapours, as the observations made with the hygrometer confirm<sup>a</sup>, and as is in accordance with the course of nature (§ 115). But although the vapours are in themselves invisible so long as they are scattered singly through the air, they nevertheless cause all kinds of changes from which one can judge of the impending change of the weather (§ 117 *ff.*). And [p. 255] in this way the vapours make the air fit for all kinds of signs of the weather (§ 292 *Met.*).

<sup>a</sup> *Philosophic. Transact. Num. 129. p. 715.*

*They indicate the springs.*

*How this is possible.*

*Advantage in observing.*

§ 146. It has also long since been remarked<sup>b</sup> that where there are springs under the earth, the rising vapours are to be seen like a mist in the morning before sunrise. And accordingly the vapours acquire a new benefit, namely they serve as a guide to the springs, which one would not so easily discover without their assistance. The cause is not hard to understand. Where there are springs, vapours rise without ceasing. In the morning the air is coldest, as the thermometer shows (§ 55 *Pt. II Exper.*). But in cold air the vapours are visible which vanish in warm air (§ 251 *Phys.*). Shortly before sunrise it is also light enough for one to see them. For since we see everything by means of light, the vapours in the air must also *reflect* it in sufficient abundance if we are to see them, and the eye itself must not be taken up by a stronger light, since otherwise the stronger overcomes the weaker, so that one cannot perceive it. Therefore one must also hold the eye in such a way that one has the east, whence the light comes, [p. 256] behind one's back. Or one could also look through a narrow tube, into which no light from other things at the side can fall. Whoever has command of the natural causes knows how to find many advantages which another does not hit upon in practice.

<sup>b</sup> *Vitruvius lib. 8. c. 1. f. m. 151.*

*They make heat gentler.*

*Benefit of natural things*

*is unexpected.*

§ 147. One finds a great difference in heat, according as it is conveyed by itself in dryness or with vapours. The first is far more destructive than the other, which carries with it a certain gentleness or mildness, so that it produces quite different effects from those one can expect in the first case. Of this second kind is the heat of dung, which likewise rises with a fume. And for this reason one is also accustomed to use the heat of dung and the fume of hot water in chemistry, where the heat of fire will not serve so well. We also find warm fume serviceable in medicine for the health of man in a great many cases, and very much could be adduced on this subject if we wished to enter into such particular matters, which, however, our present undertaking does not permit. It is enough that we show by such examples that the benefit of natural things is unsearchable, and shows itself in cases where one would [p. 257] never expect it: which ought therefore to spur us on to continue with all diligence in the knowledge of nature, and to investigate the benefit of natural things.

*Evaporation serves for the exact union of different materials.*

*Fumes take on solid materials.*

*Why distillation does not yield the water pure.*

§ 148. Although I do not intend to investigate exactly what benefit the evaporation of fluid materials, and in particular of water, affords in all manner of cases occurring in human life, still less to investigate what other benefit it might have which has not yet been recognised, I have nevertheless wished to adduce one more thing from chemistry, because it is a very general truth which can be used in a great many ways. I mean *distillation*, in which, by the exhalation of water or of another fluid material, one separates the subtle and volatile parts from another material and unites these with the former in the most exact manner. For example, when one distils water with rose- leaves, the vapours take on those parts which cause the taste and smell, and are united with them in such a way that there is not a single one of them that would not smell and taste of roses. All spices which are distilled with wine or brandy give just such examples. And it is clear from this that the fumes take on solid [p. 258] materials and carry them along with them wherever they move, and also, when the foreign matter is not too volatile, or when that which one has obtained by *distillation* is well stored, retain them for a long time. And this is also the cause why particles of solid bodies thus united with vapours cannot always be separated by *distillation*, and why one therefore cannot infer that a water is entirely pure, or free from all foreign matter, when it leaves nothing behind after *distillation* has taken place. For when matter so subtle is present that it passes over with the vapours, it cannot be separated by exhalation. Rain-water gives us an example. This comes from vapours which have taken on a great deal in the air (§ 396 *Phys.*): meanwhile not all the matter which has been taken on by them can be separated by *distillation*; rather, it rises up with the vapours and passes over with them.

*Evaporation is the means of making things dry.*

§ 149. Finally, I must adduce one further benefit of evaporation, which is indeed common, but nevertheless very great. In many cases of human life we need wet and damp things to become dry. What daily experience teaches everyone from earliest childhood, I have no need to show by [p. 259] examples. But this very experience also shows us that in most cases we get things dry by letting the wetness and moisture evaporate. Namely, this is necessary in all cases where the wetness and moisture has drawn into the pores of solid bodies, and we are not in a position to drive it out by pressing together the particles of the body, which may have various causes that need not be adduced here, since we know the matter without them from experience, as far as is sufficient for our present undertaking. And we also have the advantage that water and other fluid materials can be resolved into vapours not only by heat (§ 248 *Phys.*) but also by wind (§ 142) and cold (§ 250 *Phys.*).

*What purposes mist has.*

*Doubt which arises here.*

*It is removed.*

*How the purpose is to be discovered.*

§ 150. Mist consists of watery vapours which, on account of their weight, cannot rise up (§ 255 *Phys.*), and hence it also arises only at certain seasons (§ 257 *Phys.*). Since it accordingly takes away the matter from the clouds by accident (§ 261 *Phys.*), it also seems as if we could seek no particular purpose in it, but had merely to cite it as a thing which occurs along with the rest because without this accident another purpose, namely the [p. 260] generation of clouds, could not be attained. For vapours are necessary for the generation of clouds (§  *cit. Phys.*). But if the air is to be filled with vapours, it also cannot fail that a mist should arise at certain times (§ 257 *Phys.*). However, since it is always dangerous to deny anything in nature, because far too much is required when one is to prove the impossibility of a thing, and one can therefore easily make a mistake, I will not exactly deny that a principal purpose or a principal benefit of mist exists, or may perhaps in time be discovered. And this very thing ought to spur us on to search for it with all the greater diligence, the more hidden it seems to be. But if we wish to get to the bottom of it, we must begin in the following way. As often as a mist occurs, we must pay attention not only to the state of the air and the weather which follows upon it, but also to what changes show themselves thereby in the bodies of men and animals, as well as of other lifeless things. For since the purpose of mist is that which is attained in nature through it as a means (§ 910 *Met.*), we must indeed pay attention to what would fail to happen in nature if no mist arose, if we [p. 261] wish to know for what purpose God lets it come.

*What benefit and what harm mist does.*

*1. makes the air unhealthy.*

*2. cleanses the air.*

*Which benefit comes near to the principal benefit.*

*3. gives a sign of the weather.*

*4. increases the cold.*

*How it can freeze in misty weather.*

*5. serves for the generation of springs.*

§ 151. But although we cannot yet determine any principal benefit of mist, such as we meet with in other natural things, e.g. in vapours (§ 138), nevertheless not all the benefit that mist has is entirely unknown. Mist makes the air moist (§ 256 *Phys.*), and accordingly it has all the benefit that moist air can have, and also does all the harm that very moist air causes. E.g. moist air is unhealthy for man and unfit for breathing. And so one also finds that those who stay much and long in misty air suffer disadvantage to their health from it. When the mist falls, the air is cleansed of coarse vapours and thereby becomes clear (§ 255 *Phys.*). If it rises, then either the vapours disperse, or they form a rain-cloud (§ 259 *Phys.*); and in this way, in the first case, the lower air is once again cleansed. We find that mist is often very foul- smelling, and accordingly draws to itself the noxious exhalations that are in the air, as indeed we have also already seen above that vapours draw to themselves other fumes that are subtler than they are (§ 141), and have also already set this forth at another [p. 262] time (§ 40 *Pt. II Exper.*). Therefore, since the air becomes unhealthy on account of the harmful fumes that linger in it, since in and of itself it has nothing unhealthy about it, as otherwise it would have to be unhealthy at all times, the mist, whether it falls or rises, cleanses the air of fumes harmful to health. And this is a benefit which will not be far removed from the principal benefit (§ 150), especially since we know that ultimately everything changeable on the surface of the earth aims at the good of men and animals (§ 66). But since a change of the weather is also connected with the rising and falling of the mist (§ 258, 259 *Phys.*), one can use it as a sign of the kinds of weather to come (§ 292 *Met.*). Many mists that last through the day not only hinder the sun in its effect, by which it warms the surface of the earth (§ 239 *Phys.*), but they also take from the air, and consequently from other bodies (§ 79 *Phys.*), their heat, without becoming noticeably warmer themselves (§ 140). And thus they are a means of increasing the cold. One sees from this at the same time the cause why it can freeze hard in misty weather and the mist nevertheless remains moist. For it makes the air very cold, as [p. 263] we have just seen, and the cold air robs the water and wet things on the surface of the earth of their heat (§ 79 *Phys.*). Therefore what happens here is as when one makes water freeze by art in a cooling matter (§ 119 *Pt. II Exper.*); and this can happen all the more readily because mist in general does not easily freeze (§ 260 *Phys.*). Again, since vapours serve for the generation of springs (§ 343 *Phys.*), and mist consists of watery vapours (§ 255 *Phys.*), it too can contribute something to their generation in mountains suited to springs. One need not fear that the mist, when it runs together into water, or on the cold stones in the mountains between which it draws itself in (§ 344, 345 *Phys.*), freezes. For just as the cold penetrates only to a certain depth into the earth (§ 108 *Pt. II Exper.*), so also into the mountains, so that it can freeze within them as little as in a well-secured cellar. However, one cannot well say whether the mist contributes anything noticeable to this, because there is a lack of detailed observations, which in such cases are always necessary if one is to say whether a cause produces an effect in a noticeable degree or not.

*Principal purpose of the clouds.*

§ 152. The purpose of the clouds is all the clearer, the more hidden the principal purpose [p. 264] of mist is (§ 150). Everyone sees that the clouds give the rain, and that, when it has finished raining, they are no longer to be seen in the sky. And hence everyone also recognises that they are generated in nature to the end that it may rain. In winter they give snow; and so they are there in winter for the sake of the snow, just as in summer, and at other times when it does not freeze, for the sake of the rain. Nor may one doubt that this is the foremost purpose of the clouds, since rain and snow are something important in nature, on which much depends, as we shall soon see more fully hereafter. And because the clouds let themselves be driven by the winds, one can also have rain everywhere.

*Clouds cool the air, indeed make it cold.*

*A doubt is removed.*

*When the clouds make a cold summer;*

*when a fierce winter.*

*How every creature must carry out God's command.*

*Warning against wanton interpretations.*

§ 153. The clouds hinder the effect of the sun by which it makes it warm on the surface of the earth (§ 239 *Phys.*), and, because they consist of vapours (§ 261 *Phys.*) which are partly watery and even frozen (§ 268 *Phys.*), they at the same time take from the air into which they come its heat, so that it as it were vanishes, and one does not know where it has gone (§ 140). And in such a way they serve to cool the air when in summer it threatens to become too hot, to no small disadvantage of the plants of the earth, of animals and of men. [p. 265] Experience also agrees with this. For when on hot summer days the heat becomes unbearable to us, one at once finds relief and notices that it grows cool as soon as the sky becomes overcast with clouds; and whoever attends to the thermometer at that time will become still more aware of it, which we can trust more than our senses in judging heat (§ 108 *Pt. II Exper.*). It is indeed true that the sky is sometimes overcast with clouds and the air remains very sultry; but this happens at the time when a violent tempest is about to come, and this particular case must not be confused with what happens ordinarily, of which we are speaking here. If the sky is overcast with clouds for a long time at the season when the sun ought most to warm the surface of the earth, then we have a cold summer (§ 246 *Phys.*), so that for want of heat nothing can grow properly. And accordingly the clouds are a means of making a cold summer and a barren year. If in winter, with the north wind or also with the north-east, the sky is overcast for a long time, so that for a long time one cannot enjoy the sunshine, then the cold greatly increases thereby and one gets a fierce winter. An example of this [p. 266] we have in the extraordinarily severe cold in the winter of *A. 1709*, when this was one of the principal causes, as I have shown elsewhere<sup>a</sup>. And in this way the clouds are also a means of increasing the severity of the cold and of causing so fierce a winter that plants, men and animals suffer great harm from the cold. And accordingly we see that God can use the clouds to do good to men, and to punish them, or for vengeance against them (§ 108). Indeed, there is no single creature that does not stand under such subjection that it must carry out God's will, according as he deems it necessary either to show good to men or to do them harm. For among all the creatures there is none that has any effect upon the surface of the earth which cannot under certain circumstances procure benefit for man, while under others it brings harm. But who does not know that everything that happens in the world comes about according to God's counsel (§ 999 *Met.*)? I do not believe that anyone will take offence at this manner of speaking; otherwise he would also have to (which could not be done without blasphemy) take offence at Scripture [p. 267] when it says: everything comes from God, good fortune and misfortune<sup>a′</sup>, and there is no misfortune in the city which the LORD has not brought about<sup>b</sup>. For how this can be so without God's needing to contribute anything to evil, I have already set forth sufficiently in another place (§ 1056 *Met.*).

<sup>a</sup> *in Consideratione physico-mathematica hiemis A. 1709.*

<sup>a′</sup> *Sir. XI, 14.*

<sup>b</sup> *Amos III, 6.*

*Whether the clouds also promote heat.*

*When a change is easily felt.*

*How clouds increase heat.*

*Whether this increase is noticeable.*

*How this can be settled by an experiment.*

*A doubt is met.*

*Which clouds resemble a mirror.*

*When watery*

*clouds serve for this.*

*How the clouds promote heat in yet another way.*

*Remark.*

§ 154. That the clouds are a means of increasing the cold is easily understood (§ 153); indeed, experience teaches it to those who must work in the field or walk in the sun. When the sun oppresses them with its rays, they feel a change in an instant when a cloud covers the sun. The change is all the more noticeable because it happens suddenly, while we still have the preceding sensations fresh in memory, and therefore perceive the difference from the present ones quite clearly. But that the clouds are also a means by which heat on the surface of the earth is promoted is not so immediately clear to everyone. Nevertheless, it proves to be so if one reflects upon the matter. I have already shown (§ 240 *Phys.*) that the force of the sun can be strengthened when clouds, [p. 268] especially those which carry frozen vapours, *reflect* their light onto a place where it does not of itself fall from the sun, and that this light is to be regarded like that which is *reflected* by a mirror. Although I have not yet had occasion to determine by an experiment how much the force of the sun may perhaps be increased thereby, so that one could say with certainty how much greater the effect of the sun would then be, and whether this increase is noticeable, and also in which things some change is therefore to be expected which would otherwise not take place, since even the thermometers show that heat produces noticeable changes in them which we are not fit to judge by our senses: yet one can illustrate by an observation and an experiment the probability that the increase of the force must be of some consequence. We certainly observe, when the sun shines upon water, that it *reflects* the light abundantly, in such a way that one is dazzled by it when one looks into it. Indeed, one also finds that the heat of the sun on the water becomes quite unbearable when, besides the sunshine, one is also *incommoded* by the *reflected* light. A [p. 269] cloud differs from water only in density (§ 261 *Phys.*); but since it is far away, and therefore in this respect takes on the property of a thick body, inasmuch as vapours at various distances, which, however, with respect to their whole distance from the earth are to be regarded as if they did not differ, *reflect* the sunlight to one place, whereas water throws it back only from its outer surface, it is all one whether the light is *reflected* by water or by a cloud, since here everything depends only on rays being reflected from many parts to one place, which happens in both cases. Since, therefore, we have to regard the light *reflected* from the cloud no otherwise than as if it were *reflected* by a mirror, it furnishes us with the following experiment. Let one *reflect* the sunlight by a mirror to a place where the sun does not shine, and set there a body which shows a perceptible change from any heat whatever, as for example a glass ball with a tube whose opening stands in a glass of water. Let one *reflect* the same sunlight by another mirror onto this ball, as with the first; and it will appear that the heat is perceptibly increased (§ 56 [p. 270] *Pt. II Exper.*). And we may doubt all the less that a perceptible increase of heat takes place by this means, because the learned Jesuit **Kircher**, who tried the thing, even came to the thought that *Archimedes* had formerly set fire to the ships of the Romans by this artifice when they were besieging Syracuse<sup>a</sup>. But that one can regard clouds, especially those which carry frozen vapours, as a mirror is shown by the constitution of a mirror. A mirror is a body which has a smooth surface and an opaque ground (§ 2 *Catoptr.*). A frozen vapour has a smooth surface, and there are innumerably many of them beside and behind one another, which on account of their distance are to be regarded as if they were all in one surface. Therefore a cloud which carries frozen vapours represents a body which has a smooth or polished surface. Since the cloud is very thick, one can nowhere see through it, and it is therefore just the same as if it had an opaque ground. In this way the cloud in the present case can be regarded as a mirror. And in the same way it can also be proved if we suppose that [p. 271] the cloud consists of watery vapours: which, however, will seldom be the case in the present instance, because clouds which carry watery vapours ordinarily give rain. It can happen with a north- west wind, which brings broken clouds that now begin to rain down, now give no rain, and in both cases do not hinder the sun in its shining. Yet this circumstance must also be added, that the clouds do not move all too swiftly. But precisely when the clouds are not moved so swiftly that the wind thereby holds up the heavy watery vapours, they fall lower and give a rain. Therefore I said just now that a cloud which carries watery vapours will seldom serve the present undertaking. There is, however, still another case in which the clouds can promote heat. In winter the vapours which the west wind brings are warmer than our air. For as soon as this wind begins to blow, the air becomes milder. These must therefore impart something of their heat to the air: which we may call into doubt all the less, because it is not only in accordance with the doctrines of physics (§ 79 *Phys.*), and in particular the air takes on most readily of all the heat of a body which [p. 272] touches it (§ 134 *Pt. I Exper.*), but experience also confirms it through the striking-out of the cold, if one investigates the ground of it (§ 274 *Phys.*). One might indeed doubt whether the heat of the clouds could make any changes in the lower air: but it is enough that it warms the upper air, until one finds an opportunity to settle further how far the heat from the cloud can penetrate into the air, and how far the dispersion of heat in the lower air is thereby prevented. For this, mathematical knowledge is necessary, and it therefore requires other grounds than those we have explained, if we wish to bring out anything certain.

<sup>a</sup> *Artis magnæ de luce & umbra lib. 9. part. 3. probl. 16. f. 771.*

*Clouds serve to distinguish winds and kinds of weather: according to the difference 1. of the quarters from which they come.*

*2. Of the velocity of their motion.*

*3. Of the colours they have.*

*What remains to be investigated further in this.*

*Clouds are signs of the kinds of weather.*

*Encouragement to the knowledge of nature.*

§ 155. From the motion of the clouds one can see the quarter from which the wind blows (§ 83 *Pt. II Exper.*). And thus the clouds show us what wind is blowing aloft, and whether it differs from the lower wind which drives the vanes on the buildings or the weathercocks. Now the winds receive their properties from the quarter from which they blow (§ 217 ff. *Phys.*). Therefore the clouds teach us by their motion to know the properties of the winds; consequently, since the changeable kinds of weather arise for the most part from the winds (§ 246 *Phys.*), [p. 273] we can find their causes by means of the clouds, and also have the surest conjectures about them. Besides this, one can also judge from the velocity of the motion of the clouds of the strength of the wind in the upper air (§ 82 *Pt. II Exper.*), and thus they also serve to attain to knowledge of the weather in the upper air. Indeed, one could carry the judging of the weather in the upper air still further. We can judge from the colour of the clouds whether they are dense or thin (§ 264 *Phys.*), and indeed also what kind of vapours they carry, since watery clouds look different from those that have frozen vapours, or snow-clouds, such as are met with in the air even in summer (§ 268 *Phys.*). Moreover, their motion also shows whether they are far from the earth or not (§ 266 *Phys.*). Indeed, one further recognises from it whether some are farther from the earth than others (§ 84 *Pt. II Exper.*). Now since the state of the air differs according to the state of the clouds found in it (§ 261 *f. Phys.*), the former can be judged from the latter; and accordingly the clouds can lead us to the contemplation of the upper air, which without them it would not be possible for us to know. We also know that the clouds, according to their [p. 274] different constitution, take on different colours at the rising and setting of the sun, the causes of which I have to some extent investigated (§ 203, 204 *Phys.*). And accordingly they could likewise serve by this means for judging the state of the upper air. But before this can be done with certainty, one must first settle what state of the air each constitution of the clouds requires. This is done most surely by diligent observations, provided, however, that one afterwards takes pains to search out the cause of what one observes. One sees, therefore, how the clouds could bring us to much knowledge in nature, if only we were willing to make use of their help, or also knew how to use it rightly. Now since, however, the kinds of weather arise for the most part from the state of the upper air, where rain, snow, hail, lightning and the like are generated, the clouds would be able to furnish us the best signs of the kinds of weather, if we could judge sufficiently from them of the state of the upper air, and had at the same time correctly settled what change in the weather must follow according to the state of the upper, or, as one commonly says, the middle air. And from this example we see [p. 275] clearly that we could still turn natural things, even those that are so far from us that we must content ourselves with merely seeing them, to much benefit, if we took more pains over the knowledge of nature, and indeed strove after how we might attain certain knowledge. This very thing should spur us on to strive after it with greater diligence.

*Benefit of dew.*

§ 156. Dew moistens all plants and growing things on the surface of the earth during the night, especially towards morning, so that early, at the rising of the sun, one droplet is to be seen beside another on the leaves (§ 271 *Phys.*). Now the leaves have pores on the upper side too, and large ones at that, into which the water draws itself (§ 71 *Pt. III Exper.*), and I have already proved (§ 398 *Phys.*) that the dew draws itself into them. But since it is also certain that the sap can pass from the leaves into the tree (§ 94 *Pt. III Exper.*), we can hardly doubt that dew is destined by nature to the end that plants exhausted during the day may be refreshed and have nourishment at the time when the soil can afford them little. And this is the cause why, not only with us, the plants of the earth can still hold out in continued dry weather, [p. 276] but also Egypt and other places lying in the torrid zone are fertile, although it either does not rain there at all or at any rate very seldom. The abundant dew makes up for the lack of rain.

*Why May dew is more fruitful than that of another time.*

*Particular benefit of dew.*

*Why it is more fertile than rain.*

§ 157. It is a common saying that May dew is much more fruitful than that which falls at another time: whether this has been learned from competent experience or not, I will not exactly investigate. This much is certain, that the proposition is grounded in nature, and it often happens that one comes upon the truth by a fortunate error. Dew is generated from vapours in the lower air (§ 271 *Phys.*). Therefore, since the vapours draw to themselves other exhalations that are met with in the air (§ 40 *Pt. II Exper.*), the dew must also bring down with it, and consequently into the plants (§ 156), whatever of them is to be met with in the lower air. In spring the trees and flowers blossom, all kinds of sweet-smelling herbs also spring up, and the tender plants altogether, as well as the still quite young leaves of the trees, *transpire* strongly, and we have also already seen above (§ 141) how the vapours draw the sweet-smelling fumes to themselves. Accordingly the May dew must carry with it whatever has evaporated from the [p. 277] flowers, blossoms, fragrant herbs and leaves, which are always strongly scented at first. The nourishment of plants is not mere water, but what is conveyed along with it from other materials (§ 394 *Phys.*), and it is known that the blossoms and flowers in particular contain the subtlest part of the oils and salts, which is necessary for the nourishment of the fruit and of the seed, in which salts and oils are at all times found. Therefore the May dew brings with it much that serves the plants, and all that grows, for the preparation of the nutritive juice (§ 395 *Phys.*), and that at a time when they are in their best growth and have most need of it. Indeed, since the leaves prepare the best nourishment, especially for the buds and fruits (§ 399 *Phys.*), while the dew draws itself into the leaves, and the juice prepared there penetrates everywhere into the plant (§ 156), so this matter also comes to the right place, and it can hardly be otherwise than that the May dew should be so beneficial to all plants together. One sees at the same time that the dew is a means of collecting whatever good would otherwise vanish like smoke into the air, and through it this is achieved, that one plant can serve another with its superfluity. What is exhaled from one [p. 278] would be lost if the dew did not collect it and convey it to others. But one sees in general that dew must be more fertile than rain. For it is generated in the lower air, where the exhalations from animals and plants are to be found, which are precisely that changeable matter which is chiefly required for the growth of plants. It is indeed true that the vapours rise from the lower air into the upper, and hence also take with them other exhalations which by themselves would perhaps not rise so far; so too the rain falls through the lower air, and in falling can still carry with it what it meets on the way, and this is also the cause why the rain brings such matter with it, as we have already seen quite precisely (§ 141): but it is nevertheless also very easy to grasp that vapours which constantly linger in the lower air, and there flow together into droplets of water, can take up a greater quantity of the other exhalations present there than others which only sweep along with them in passing whatever stands in their way.

*Dew is sometimes harmful*

*What is still to be investigated further here.*

*What is still to be investigated further here.*

§ 158. Sometimes, too, a dew is wont to fall which is harmful either to plants in general, or at least to some among them, [p. 279] in that the leaves on which it falls are spoiled. Whoever considers what precedes (§ 157) will easily find the cause. Dew in itself consists of pure water (§ 270 *Phys.*), and is therefore not harmful: indeed, otherwise it would have to be harmful constantly, which, however, is contrary to experience, no less than to what we have already set out concerning its benefit (§ 156). If, then, it is harmful, this must come from a matter which it brings with it. Now since it is generated in the lower air (§ 271 *Phys.*), it can bring with it no other matter than what it has taken up there. And accordingly there must be matter harmful to plants in the air when it is unwholesome to them. But what kind of matter this properly is, and whence it comes, cannot be settled in general. One must first come to know the particular kind of harm which the dew does, with all possible distinctness, before one can think of a particular investigation of the cause of the harmfulness of dew. Thus here too, as in all parts of physics, much still remains in which those who draw pleasure from the investigation of nature can exercise their understanding and diligence.

*Dew gives signs of the weather.*

*When it signifies good weather.*

*When, on the other hand, rainy weather.*

*Whence the uncertainty arises in this case.*

*How the uncertainty is remedied.*

*Dew comes also from the upper air.*

*When dew falls more abundantly.*

[p. 280] § 159. When dew falls abundantly, it is a sign that the vapours have remained in great quantity in the lower air, or have even sunk down at night from the upper into the lower, and consequently that the air has been much cleansed of vapours. Now since the clouds are generated from vapours which shoot together when a diminution of the weight of the air occurs (§ 263 *Phys.*), the matter of the clouds, and consequently also of the rain, is gone (§ 275 *Phys.*). Therefore, since in the ordinary way the air is first filled with coarse vapours before rainy weather sets in (§ 119), indeed before the sky becomes overcast (§ 125), it is not without ground that one expects no rain on a day on which the dew has fallen very abundantly. And in this way it is a sign from which one can judge of the weather impending during the day. This has without doubt given occasion for rainy weather to be expected from the contrary, namely for it to be taken as a sign of rain when either no dew at all, or at least very little, falls, although it has been found that this does not always come true: hence some have afterwards wished to help the rule out, and have alleged that the dew is consumed by the [p. 281] wind<sup>a</sup>. Dew is produced when the air becomes denser through the cold (§ 271 *Phys.*), so that, namely, the droplets produced on high fall down and, in the lower air through which they fall, are enlarged still more by the union of others. If, then, from other causes the air becomes lighter than it was during the day, it cannot be sufficiently condensed by the cold, and in this way either no dew at all, or at least less, is produced than ought to be produced. When the air becomes light there is indeed at times rainy weather (§ 41 *Pt. II Exper.*), but not always: for a wind may also come (§ 44 *Pt. II Exper.*), yet only in the case where the air suddenly becomes very light (§ *cit.*). Therefore one sees that little dew, or the want of dew, affords no infallible sign of rain or of wind (§ 293 *Met.*), although it may be taken as a probable one, and may obtain its certainty if one pays attention to the state of the air in other respects, in particular to the barometer (§ 22 *Pt. II Exper.*) and the manometer (§ 45 *Pt. II Exper.*). For the rest, one sees at the same time that dew does not come merely from the lower air, [p. 282] but also falls down from on high: which agrees very nicely with the experience that the celebrated English *mathematician*, Mr Halley, had on the mountains of the island of St Helena (§ 343 *Phys.*). It also casts a new light thereby on why dew falls more abundantly in fine weather. In such weather the air is ordinarily heavy (§ 40 *Pt. II Exper.*), and hence denser than at other times (§ 124 *Pt. I Exper.*). Therefore it also lets itself be condensed more by the cold, and so more dew can be produced (§ 271 *Phys.*).

<sup>a</sup> *Florini* kluger Haus-Vater *lib. 2. c. 79. f. 463.*

*Why most dew falls in May.*

*A scruple is removed.*

*Remark.*

§ 160. In April it is commonly stormy, with rain, snow and wind mixed together: hence such weather has also received the name of April weather. Now since not only is much moisture left over from the winter in this month, but the north winds also, in particular the north-west, bring us much moisture when it begins to thaw towards the north and the snow to melt, there are many vapours in the air at the beginning of May. If fine weather now follows, the air is heavy (§ 40 *Pt. II Exper.*) and hence suited to produce dew (§ 271 *Phys.*). Thus one sees a sufficient cause why at the beginning of May, namely when the weather is fine (§ 159), more dew falls than at other times. One need [p. 283] also have no hesitation in holding this to be the true cause, out of concern that there might perhaps be yet another. For an abundance of dew requires many vapours and a state of the air such that much dew can be produced. When both are present together, there cannot fail to be an abundance of dew. Now at other seasons the air is just as heavy as it becomes in May, as the observations made with the barometer show, and at some times it becomes colder still at night than in May: therefore it must depend chiefly on the many vapours in the air. But just as the changeable kinds of weather do not bind themselves so very exactly to the month, as we see, for example, that April weather sometimes comes as early as March, but at times lingers far into April, so one cannot believe either that the greatest abundance of dew is bound so very exactly to the first weeks of May.

*When hoar- frost does harm, and why there is hoar-frost.*

*Doubt whether hoar- frost can harm plants.*

*How hoar- frost increases the cold.*

*How one can learn this by experience.*

*The error*

*of the common man is removed.*

§ 161. Hoar-frost is nothing other than frozen dew (§ 273 *Phys.*), and it is therefore not necessary to treat of it separately. For since dew was necessary (§ 156), but at a certain season it must freeze in those countries that are far enough removed from the torrid zone, hoar-frost too has thereby obtained a place in [p. 284] nature. Meanwhile it is nevertheless in accordance with experience that hoar-frost often does harm, namely when it comes at a time when the plants, or also the crops sown in the field, are still very young. It appears, however, as though hoar-frost could do the plants no harm. Dew freezes and becomes hoar-frost when too much heat escapes from it (§ 119 *Pt. II Exper.*). Therefore it seems as though the plants were colder than it, and even received heat from it. Now if the plants suffer harm from the cold, and the hoar-frost diminishes their cold, it certainly has the appearance as though they must already have taken harm from the cold before the hoar-frost comes upon them, and one merely by accident held this to be the cause of the harm, when it contributes not the least to it. I cannot deny that this may happen very often, and perhaps for the most part: but it can nevertheless also be conceived how hoar-frost may increase the cold and thereby do the plants harm. It is certain that dew can freeze from the cold of the air when it lies on the plants (§ 273 *Phys.*). Now since it then becomes very cold, it can take heat from the plant to itself and let it pass again into the [p. 285] air, which would not of itself pass out of the plant (§ 79 *Phys.*), and consequently cause the sap in plants to freeze. Therefore, since in that state the sap spreads through a greater space (§ 120 *Pt. II Exper.*), it not only alters the composition of the parts, but can also burst entirely some delicate little tubes and little bubbles (§ 94 ff. *Pt. III Exper.*) and sever them from one another, as I have also found through the microscope when I examined frozen things with it. Whether this happens in nature, and when this cause takes place rather than the other, is a matter that must be settled from experience. Here, however, not only can the weather-glass be serviceable, which must show such a degree of cold as allows freezing, if the cold of the air is to be the cause of the dew freezing on the plants, for one can also see whether moisture freezes elsewhere. If it is found that frost is perceived nowhere else than where the hoar-frost lies, one can suppose nothing other than that the dew has frozen from the cold of those bodies on which it lies, and in this case it can also of itself do the plants no harm. Indeed, it may even happen that on plants no hoar-frost at all [p. 286] is to be perceived when it lies on other cold bodies: in which case people are wont to say that the hoar-frost has not struck this or that plant, since they imagine that the case is just as with hail, that it falls in strips, because they imagine that the hoar-frost comes out of the air just as one finds it on the bodies.

*Why it rains: namely, to make the earth fertile.*

§ 162. When it does not rain for a long time, most of the plants on the surface of the earth spoil, and at least do not thrive as they would have done had it rained. Therefore everyone easily sees that rain is necessary for growth, though admittedly not absolutely, since we find that in some places God also has yet other means by which he accomplishes this in nature (§ 156). It is enough here that rain is a means of promoting growth (§ 392 *Phys.*). And since we know that God willed the earth to be inhabited by men and animals (§ 66), and for this the growth of plants and other vegetation is necessary, since these have their nourishment from it, one indeed recognises that God has ordained rain as a means of making the earth fertile, namely so that what grows out of the earth may thereby receive nourishment.

*Rain keeps the soil loose.*

*Why further benefits are adduced.*

*How rain makes the soil loose.*

*Whether the soil swells up from rain.*

*How the soil cracks open.*

*How it swells shut again.*

*Why a gentle rain is beneficial to the earth.*

*How the rain moistens the soil.*

[p. 287] § 163. But although this is the foremost purpose which God had in rain, since it is to be regarded as a means (§ 912 *Met.*) by which the principal purpose of the earth is maintained, namely that it should be a dwelling-house for men and animals (§ 66), yet rain has many further benefits, which we must go on to set forth, so that we may become fit to know how much good God does to men thereby, and to recognise the riches of his goodness (§ 1063 *Met.*) also in such cases where people commonly give it little thought. Accordingly I note first of all that rain is the means by which the soil is kept loose, so that it does not become too hard. When the soil is quite dry, it is hard almost like a stone. Experience teaches this at the time when dry weather lasts long: then the soil in the field becomes so hard that one can scarcely get into it with great effort. But as soon as it rains, it becomes loose again. Accordingly one must picture the soil like a sponge, which becomes hard when it dries out, but loose and soft when it is softened in water. In the sponge this comes from the many pores into which the water can draw itself, and in the soil the case is just the same. [p. 288] The particles of earth have many pores or little air-spaces, and are in this respect to be regarded like the small parts of a sponge. Now when the water penetrates into them, it drives the solid parts somewhat apart, and thereby the soil becomes soft (§ 64 *Phys.*) and loose (§ 37 *Phys.*). From this it appears that the soil must swell up from the rain, as we also see that a sponge is wont to swell up when it is made wet. Now to my knowledge no one has yet made any experiments on this, and I myself have not tried it either: but one can prove from an experience that it must happen. When it has been dry for a long time, it closes up again. One easily comprehends that the soil cracks as it dries out, because the particles of earth draw closer together and therefore can no longer fill the whole space as before. Accordingly, when the soil closes up again from the rain, the particles must be driven apart again, and the soil must accordingly swell shut (§ 54 *Phys.*). One easily sees, if this is to happen, that the rain must fall drop by drop onto the soil and draw itself in, without displacing the larger particles of the soil from their position, [p. 289] but merely drives the smaller ones within the larger somewhat apart. And this is the cause why a gentle rain, which reaches the soil in drops that are not too large and without a strong impact, is more beneficial to the earth than a heavy downpour; likewise why one makes the water run through little holes from the watering-cans which one is accustomed to use in the garden for watering. One can also see quite plainly how raindrops that fall singly and gently upon the soil draw themselves in as into a sponge, and make a much broader spot than the drop was. Namely, when a drop falls upon the soil and touches a pore of one or several motes of earth, the air rises upward and the water sinks down (§ 218 *Pt. I Exper.*). But by this the water is at the same time divided into smaller droplets, so that it runs apart and sinks down into other pores, since water can be divided so very subtly that the droplets nevertheless remain visible (§ 221 *Pt. I Exper.*).

*When the soil is made firm*

*by the rain.*

*How the soil freezes loose.*

*How the rain can make it firm again.*

*When the road becomes very bad towards spring.*

*How heavy downpours make the earth hard.*

*Why the soil in vessels becomes hard and dry.*

*Why in summer the soil becomes harder at one time than at another.*

*Advantage of gentle rain.*

§ 164. Now since the rain makes the soil loose only in certain cases (§ 163), I must also show when it has a contrary effect. The experience of carters and of the countryman [p. 290] furnishes us with the first circumstance. When the earth thaws towards spring, the ground is very loose, so that the waggon cuts deep into it. But as soon as a heavy rain comes, the soil settles, and when the wind dries it out, it becomes firm. For while the soil freezes, its particles are driven further apart from one another, since the droplets of water in the pores, which are what properly freeze, take up a larger space than before (§ 120 *Pt. II Exper.*). Now when the moisture that was frozen becomes fluid again, there is nothing present that would drive the particles of earth together again, and therefore the ground remains too loose. If, then, something heavy comes upon it, such as the waggon, the particles of earth which are pressed by the wheels have room enough to give way to the side, and so the wheel sinks down between them: which is precisely what one calls cutting in. When a heavy rain comes, it finds so much room in the too loose ground that the particles of earth begin to float in the water. But since they are heavier than water, they cannot float, but sink to the bottom, and so the soil settles. Accordingly, as soon as the wetness has dried up and merely some moisture remains behind, then [p. 291] the ground is firm. For when the earth is pressed, it can no more give way to the side than beneath it, and accordingly the waggon no longer cuts in as deeply as before. One sees that here the rain acts only through the quantity of water. And accordingly the abundant snow-water can also accomplish this, when the earth, which had previously been hard frozen, has been covered with much snow. When it freezes hard before any snow falls, the earth freezes deep and is therefore also loosened deep; this happens still more when little snow falls. And hence one sees the cause why the road becomes so very bad when it thaws again and we have had a winter in which it froze hard and little snow fell. Now I must also show how heavy downpours can make the earth hard. We find a similar case in gardening which can serve us by way of illustration. When one has a plant in a vessel and pours so much water on it at once that it stands above the soil and, after it has drawn itself in, runs out again abundantly below, it will be found that, if this happens often, the soil at last becomes quite hard. Now heavy watering is of exactly the same constitution as a downpour: for [p. 292] in both cases more water comes onto the soil than can draw itself in at once. Therefore in both cases there must also be the same cause why the earth becomes hard. A body becomes hard when its small particles come closer together than they were before, and the interstices either become narrower or in some places disappear altogether (§ 45 *Phys.*). Therefore the water which penetrates into the earth in great quantities must also bring the particles of the earth closer together and diminish the interstices. The earth dissolves in the water, and the water becomes turbid from it. One also sees in the vessels in which much water has been poured onto the earth at once that it gradually draws itself in, and how a slime at last settles on top from it. And everywhere one finds a slime at the bottom upon the earth, which covers it as it were with a crust, when water has stood over it for a while, whether it has gradually dried in or been dried out by heat and wind. Accordingly the water which runs through the pores of the earth carries particles of earth with it, which it leaves lodged therein, or else by its force tears off some particles in some places and carries them along to another place. [p. 293] And so there happens what is required if the soil is to become hard. Now if it soon dries out completely again from the heat and from the winds, then the other cause of hardening is added as well, and the ground thereby becomes harder still. And this very cause is found in the vessels in which precious plants are usually set in gardens, since the soil, because of the heat, which penetrates not only from above, as with the ground, but also from the sides all round through the vessel, soon dries out, and one must therefore water it almost daily. From this one now further understands why in summer, when it is very warm and rains heavily, the soil becomes much harder than at another season, when it merely rains heavily but does not at the same time dry so strongly. But when the ground is very firm, the water cannot penetrate into it so well as when it is loose. Hence one must not only loosen the soil in the field around those plants that need much nourishment, as e.g. around cole and cabbage, as is also usually done around the vines on the mountains, but one must also do this in vessels if rain and dew are to penetrate into the soil. And for this reason a gentle rain is better at such a time than a heavy one. For since the water [p. 294] cannot soon soak in, and in a heavy rain a great deal soon collects, it merely washes over the soil and flows together into hollows. The drops of a gentle rain, on the other hand, soak in and begin to loosen the ground, so that the following ones can likewise penetrate.

*Rain keeps the soil moist and makes mud.*

*How rain lays the dust.*

*How the soil becomes moist.*

*When the soil stays moist longer.*

*How mud arises.*

*Much rain can at once benefit and harm.*

§ 165. When the soil is very dry, it crumbles, and the particles rubbed off make dust, which the wind scatters through the air. Experience teaches how vexatious it is for travellers when the road is very dusty, especially where there is great heat as well. These inconveniences are removed by the rain. For even if only a slight rain falls, it lays the dust. Namely, dust consists of nothing but little motes, which lie scattered beside and upon one another on the ground. Now when a raindrop falls into the dust, it covers more than one mote in breadth, and also penetrates to more than one in depth. Accordingly the wetness soaks at once into many, which the drop surrounds on all sides.
