Since moist soil can be pressed together, while the drop, however gently it may rain, nevertheless strikes somewhat and thereby presses the moist motes together, these too close [p. 295] together, and can no longer be scattered through the air as before, since they have now become heavier, both because of the water that has soaked into them and because many have united with one another, and hence can neither be raised so easily nor float in the air as before. When the dust has been moistened enough, that is, when all the pores of the motes are filled with droplets of water, the following droplets penetrate to the ground on which it lies, and the soil is then moistened further, so that it no longer gives dust, since moist soil does not let subtle motes be rubbed off as easily as dry soil does, but is rather only pressed more firmly together when men and animals walk on it, or when the winds blow over it and dry it out. If the rain has penetrated somewhat deep into the soil, the ground also stays moist longer than otherwise. For when the upper crust of the soil has dried out, because the droplets of water contained in it have been dissolved into vapours, the vapours rise from the moist soil lying beneath and penetrate into the upper crust, so that it does not soon become too dry and able to give dust. Accordingly, if the rain does not stay away too long but soon comes again, while the upper crust [p. 296] of the soil is about to become quite dry, one has no trouble from the dust either. And thus rain is the means by which the dust on the roads is remedied, and thereby no small advantage is procured for travellers. If too much rain falls, the soil cannot take it all in. For since we have seen that a large drop divides into many small ones, and that the small droplets fill the pores in the motes of earth, one sees plainly that water can soak into the soil only until all the pores are filled. Now if more is added, it remains standing between the motes of earth and dissolves the soil, so that it floats in the water. When there is more soil than water, so that only the motes float imperceptibly in the water, but not the coarse parts that consist of many motes, it becomes muddy. And accordingly too much rain spoils the road, whereas a moderate one keeps it in good condition. Meanwhile, however, much rain can be beneficial to the fruits, and since fertility is to be esteemed more highly than the convenience of the road, one is not on that account to regard rain as something bad because it makes the road somewhat foul: which is also to be noted in other like cases with regard to other natural [p. 297] things. Whoever does not attend to this is over- hasty in his judgement and holds to be bad what is beneficial. And from this source come the over- hasty judgements concerning the benefit and harm of natural things.

*Why rain makes it cool, and how this happens.*

§ 166. Rain cannot be without clouds (§ 275 *Phys.*). Now since the clouds cool the air (§ 153), it must always become cool in rainy weather. But because the rain does not do this of itself, it does not properly belong here either. It can, however, very easily be proved that the rain also in itself cools the air, as well as the ground and other bodies found upon it. It comes about in just the same way as we proved above (§ 140) of the vapours. Water takes on more heat than the air, and nevertheless remains cool for all that (§ 108 *Pt. II Exper.*). Therefore the raindrops which fall down from the upper air must be colder than the lower air, because the upper air is colder than the lower (§ 282 *Phys.*). As soon, therefore, as they fall through the warm air, they take a part of its heat to themselves, and the air thereby becomes cooler (§ 76 *Phys.*). The same thing happens when they come to the ground or fall upon some other body. For experience teaches that it is warmer at the [p. 298] earth than at some distance from it in the air. When one stoops down on hot summer days, one can quite distinctly feel how the heat comes up towards one from the ground. Therefore when the rain falls on the ground, or on a body found upon it, it deprives it of a part of its heat, and by this very means the body becomes colder (§ 76 *Phys.*), so that it can no longer impart as much heat to the air as would otherwise happen. We also feel well enough on summer days how cool it becomes when rain comes, and how very soon the great heat is thereby tempered.

*Rain brings water everywhere it is needed.*

§ 167. The clouds cover the sky entirely when there is a true settled rain, such as the west wind is wont to bring with us. And thus it rains not only on the level country and on the mountains, but also on the rivers, lakes and other standing waters: the soil it keeps moist and loosens it again (§ 163); in forests, in the fields, meadows and gardens it nourishes what grows out of the earth (§ 162); in mountains it serves for the generation of stones (§ 376 *Phys.*); and springs (§ 343 *Phys.*), rivers and other waters standing in ponds and lakes swell from it (§ 340 *Phys.*). And in this way there comes, by means of the rain, [p. 299] the water everywhere it is needed. Thus the rain is the means by which God distributes the water over the surface of the earth, although dew also has some share in this (§ 156), since in fact it differs from rain only in the size of the droplets, but is otherwise generated, just as rain is, from the vapours in the air (§ 271, 276 *Phys.*).

*What harm rain does to plants.*

*How one arrives at a certain knowledge of it.*

*Who can perform such work.*

§ 168. Long-continuing rain is harmful to the plants in more than one way, according to the differing circumstances of the time, as the experience of country people and gardeners teaches; and it would require a more exact investigation if one wished to divide this harm into certain general classes and to show its natural causes. Something has already been adduced on this above (§ 143), where I showed that superfluous moisture is harmful to plants. But whoever wished to settle everything more exactly would have to note carefully what befell each plant from superfluous moisture in each season, and how its state differed from its state in other years, when at the same season it had had no superfluous moisture. It would accordingly be serviceable not only for this undertaking but also for other purposes, which in the course of the work would of themselves [p. 300] become apparent, if in the diaries of the weather one also entered daily the state of each plant, as often as anything changeable showed itself in it, but at the same time investigated, from the weather and other circumstances that arose, what cause the change had properly had and how it could have come about. These particular investigations, if they were continued for some years in succession, could give occasion to many useful doctrines which one would not otherwise arrive at. But because in nature many causes often act at the same time, one would afterwards have to determine by particular experiments what each of them does alone; and the former investigations would themselves suggest these experiments, as I have already given a proof of this at another time, on another occasion<sup>a</sup>. This is a work for those who either live in the country or have opportunity to visit gardens, and whose occupations allow them to apply some time to it daily. It would, however, also be good if one who is willing to undertake it had already done something in the knowledge of nature and were practised in the experiments [p. 301] of the present-day natural philosophers; for then he would make his observations with greater care, and would not, as alas! often happens in such cases, overlook the best. For this useful preparation my Experiments, published in three parts, together with the Thoughts on the Effects of Nature, and even these present Thoughts on their purposes, may be of service.

<sup>a</sup> in the Entdeckung der wahren Ursache von der wunderbahren Vermehrung des Getreydes.

*Yet other harm that rain does.*

*2. Of the downpour.*

*How God can remedy the harm again by natural means.*

*3. Of the cloudburst.*

*How it sweeps away mills.*

*How it undermines buildings.*

*How it throws them down.*

*How it washes away men and cattle.*

*How it washes the dead out of their graves.*

*Proposal of a set of directions for observing.*

*How far God can put the cloudburst to the*

*best use.*

§ 169. When heavy rain continues for a long time, the rivers swell from it to such a degree that they often overflow, especially in places where the banks are not high, and inundate fields, meadows, gardens, &c. But what harm inundations do, and what accidents they sometimes further cause by chance to men and cattle, is known from common experience, and gives us here no occasion for any natural investigation. A downpour that falls from an especially great height has a force to strike hard (§ 278 *Phys.*), and can therefore snap and batter the tender stalks of the corn, or also of other plants, and likewise the young leaves, especially of low plants where the leaves touch the ground and cannot give way. When the stalk of the corn is already somewhat hard, and the ear heavy with grains, then a [p. 302] heavy rain lays the corn flat. If the grains are already ripe, they can sprout from the wetness: for it is just the same as if they were steeped in water, whereupon (as everyone knows) they begin to germinate. Meanwhile, if the corn has merely been laid flat, nature has a means of raising it up again, namely when after the rain a wind comes that blows into it. And thus we see here a proof of how God too can by natural means restore to a good state what has been spoiled by a natural cause. And thus the connection of things in the world is in no way opposed to God's graciously averting, in consideration of prayer, the harm which he had already decreed. Such remarks are powerful grounds by which we can convince the Spinozists and other enemies of divine providence, who would contest it because everything has its natural causes, and one cause is an effect of another cause. A cloudburst not only brings at once a great quantity of water onto the ground, which cannot soon run off, but the water also falls down with great force (§ 280 *Phys.*). And from this the harm that cloudbursts cause can be explained. The cloudburst [p. 303] which fell *Anno* 1720, on 1 *Jul.*, a good mile below Annaberg, in the valley where the Wolkenstein Baths are, swept away mills and whatever came in its way, and undermined stables and buildings in those same baths<sup>a</sup>. Since the water in the valley could not spread out freely, but rather rushed together there from the heights besides, it acquired all the greater force (§ 349 *Phys.*) and all the swifter a current (§ 348 *Phys.*). Therefore it was no wonder that it swept away the mills and whatever else stood in its way. If one knew how steeply the ground slopes at that place, and how high the water stood above it, the force of the water could be *determined* more distinctly by one sufficiently practised in mathematics: which, however, does not in any case belong to our present undertaking. Since, moreover, the water in the valley could not soon run off because of the narrowness of the space, but stood as it were quite still for a while, it applied to pressing the force which it otherwise applies to flowing away (§ 194 *Pt. I Exper.*). Now since fluid materials press sideways with just the same force as downwards (§ 50 *Pt. I Exper.*), it is again no wonder that it undermines the buildings [p. 304] that have no deep foundations, particularly since it carries the earth away with it as it has flowed on, and, once a beginning has been made, the flowing water has also washed it out; just as we are also accustomed to see with rivers that they wash out the soil ever deeper wherever a beginning has once been made. For the water, which flows as broad as it finds room, presses with force where it has washed out at the side, and, when it cannot proceed in breadth, it either tears away what stands in its way, or *reflects* itself back and makes an eddy, whereby more and more is continually washed away. Since, accordingly, buildings whose foundations have been washed away no longer stand firm, they may well even be thrown down altogether by the strength of the water, as happened in the countryside in the terrible cloudburst in Tyrol on 26 *Novembr. Anno* 1719<sup>b</sup>. But that shortly before, on 4 *Nov.*, at *Messina* in Sicily, a sutler at the gate, with his wife, children and a maidservant, was driven by the flood from a cloudburst right into the sea and drowned<sup>c</sup>, [p. 305] can be understood from what I have just set out, namely whence such a sudden flood acquires a strong current, for which in the present case all the favourable circumstances are found, since the water had a great fall as far as the gate, and that from the sea side, but on the other hand found a narrow outlet at the gate. And this very thing is the cause why here with us at Marburg, in the village of Ockershausen, which lies not far from the town, a heavy rain this summer carried off a stone water-trough and took it along with it right through the village to the end of it, where the water was able to spread out more freely, although it is of such weight that it had to be drawn back again with eight horses. But what has been adduced concerning the eddy shows how it was possible that in *A.* 1717, on 13 *Sept.*, at Büttelstadt, a village in Thuringia four German miles from Naumburg, the cloudburst which swept away the houses at the same time also washed the dead out of their graves<sup>a′</sup>. Where a dead person has been laid, there is a small mound of earth, according to the custom of this country. Now since the water [p. 306] meets with a resistance there, it is at first *reflected* by it; but since the earth is loose, the water washes it away at first from the one side, and afterwards moves round in the pit in an eddy, whereby ever more earth is washed away and the pit is widened. Since the coffin with the corpse may be lighter than the water, it can, as soon as it has been worked loose, rise up in it and float, and consequently be carried away altogether with the water. How it actually came about in the earthquake mentioned cannot be explained in detail, since the circumstances needed for that are not to be found in the account. It would not be unserviceable if, for the benefit of those who do not know of themselves what they properly have to look to in observing, someone were to draw up a set of directions as to what one properly has to look to in every natural event, if the observation is afterwards to be of use in the knowledge of nature. But one sees, as far as our present matter is concerned, that a cloudburst brings harm. How far, however, God in his provident care can also turn it to the best (§ 1060 *Met.*) must be judged from the particular circumstances, which requires a very exact attention in those places where the cloudburst [p. 307] does harm, and from where the abundant water comes to it. Now since no one has yet shown himself so careful in this, nothing in particular can be determined about it at present.

<sup>a</sup> Breßlauische Samlungen *Tent. 13. p. 70.*

<sup>b</sup> Breßl. Samlungen *Tent. 10. p. 586.*

<sup>c</sup> *loc. cit.*

<sup>a′</sup> Breßl. Sammlungen *Tent. 1. p. 61.*

*What harm and benefit hailstones bring.*

*When it does great harm.*

*History of hail.*

*When it can do little.*

*How the wind diminishes its force.*

*How hail does harm by its cold.*

*Whether it can procure any advantage.*

§ 170. Hailstones or hail do great harm when they are large and partly fall from a great height, partly are hurled down by the force of the wind (§ 289 *Phys.*). For in this case the hail can effect everything that can be effected by the throw of a heavy body. Mr Thümmig has collected the remarkable features of hail from reliable reports and explained them according to nature<sup>a</sup>. He has recorded that hailstones have fallen the size of pigeons', hens' and geese's eggs, indeed some that measured 8, 9, up to 12 inches in diameter, and once even reached the size of ostrich eggs; and that with respect to weight some at times weighed 5 to 12 ounces, others a quarter of a pound, indeed some were even heavier than 6 pounds. Of the effects of hail he reports that it has beaten down the fruits in the field, smashed in the windows, once killed more than 300 persons [p. 308] and wounded still more, killed many wild pigeons and great bustards, beaten the corn deep into the ground, killed the poultry in the field, and pierced the shingles on the roofs. He has also already shown how the hail can produce these and other such effects; and whoever considers its size and weight, and weighs therewith what I have adduced concerning the force of hail (*loc. cit.*), will be able to explain them all from the general ground cited. If the hail is neither particularly large nor heavy, and is not hurled down by any wind, it has no particular force (§ 289 *Phys.*) and therefore can do no noticeable harm. Indeed the wind can sometimes even prevent hail which, on account of its weight and its high fall, could otherwise cause harm from causing any. Namely, if the hail is to have the force to strike hard, it must move swiftly (§  *cit.*). The wind, which blows almost parallel with the earth, checks it in its fall (§ 288 *Phys.*), and since it carries it further, it must overcome its force of falling (§ 669 *Met.*); consequently it is just as if it were always beginning to fall, and it has no great velocity when it reaches the [p. 309] ground or other bodies situated upon it. Therefore it cannot strike very hard either, but when it falls in quantity it merely presses down by its weight whatever cannot resist that weight. And thus the wind diminishes the violence of the hail. Since hail is frozen water (§ 286 *Phys.*), but frozen water is colder than unfrozen (§ 120 *Pt. II Exper.*), whereas, if it is to thaw, heat must pass out of the air and the earth, and also out of the other bodies on which it lies (§ 116 *Pt. II Exper.*), the hail must cool the air and the surface of the earth wherever it falls. If it comes down together with rain and is thawed by it, the rain thereby becomes colder (§ *cit.*) and cools air and earth more than at other times (§ 166). If the hail lights upon any tender plants of the earth which its cold can harm, there follows from it what may be expected from such a degree of cold. One easily sees that hail of itself brings more harm than benefit. But what advantage it may procure by reason of the wise connection of all things with one another, whereby what follows comes about for the sake of what precedes (§ 13), is not yet known, because people have so far paid no attention to it, and also the knowledge of [p. 310] nature has not yet risen so far up to the present that one could attain to it by reason.

<sup>a</sup> in the Versuche einer Erläuterung der Begebenheiten in der Natur *part. 3. sect. 12. p. 259. & seqq.*

### Das V. Capitel. Von dem Regen-Bogen / den Neben-Sonnen / und andern Lufft-Erscheinungen. — Chapter V. On the Rainbow, the Mock Suns and Other Aerial Phenomena.

*What the rainbow signifies.*

§ 171. We learn from Scripture<sup>a</sup> that God appointed the rainbow to Noah and his descendants, and consequently to the whole human race, as a sign of assurance that no universal inundation of the surface of the earth, such as the Flood was, should come any more. Therefore, as often as a Christian, or anyone else who accepts the Old Testament as divine truth, beholds the rainbow in the clouds, he can assure himself thereby that God will no longer destroy men and animals over the whole surface of the earth at once by an inundation, and he therefore has no more to fear from the clouds of heaven, in so far as [p. 311] harm is to be feared from such an inundation as must fill the whole surface of the earth. For particular inundations, in which the water does not stand so high that it could flow over every place where firm land is to be found on the earth, are not comprehended under it, since experience itself has taught in our own days that such may still occur.

<sup>a</sup> Gen. IX, 13.

*Whether there was also a rainbow before the Flood.*

*Why this question is idle.*

§ 172. Since God made the rainbow a sign of his grace, by which he wished to assure the world that no Flood should come any more, this has given occasion to ask whether there was also a rainbow before the Flood or not. The rainbow does not have this signification by its nature and essence. For however well one may understand how it arises (§ 292 *Phys.*), and however exactly one may contemplate its colours and the rest of its constitution, as much as ever one likes, one will nevertheless never thereby come upon the thought: God will no longer punish the world with a Flood, that is, he has so ordered the course of nature that no universal inundation of the surface of the earth can follow in a natural way, and he also does not desire to bring it about by any miracle (§ 630, 632, 633, 1042 *Met.*). Therefore one can hold the rainbow [p. 312] to be no natural sign (§ 293 *Met.*), and it accordingly remains merely an arbitrary sign, which has its signification from the free will of God (§ 294 *Met.*). Now since it does not receive its signification through its essence, it could have appeared before the Flood without being required to signify anything, and it was therefore not first necessary to produce it after the Flood, when it received its signification. Whatever is chosen as an arbitrary sign begins to be a sign as soon as he who made it one has made the declaration, and it contributes nothing to this whether it existed before or not. Therefore the whole question is idle, and not much better than if one wished to ask whether hailstones also fell before the Flood or not. For although one might say that God did not wish to produce it before he used it as a sign, one nevertheless finds no ground on one's side by which one could maintain it.

*Whether mock suns and similar aerial events*

*also have a signification.*

*That such a thing is not known from divine revelation.*

*Nor yet from grounds of reason:*

*Which rather seem to be against it.*

*Nor indeed from experience.*

*Why aerial events cannot signify public calamities.*

*Grounds against the signification.*

*When people paid heed to this.*

*Prodigies admit of no concept.*

§ 173. Since the rainbow arises from natural causes (§ 292 *Phys.*), and notwithstanding this God has made it a sign of a particular grace (§ 171), one indeed sees that it is not unbecoming to God to make things which arise from natural [p. 313] causes and come about in the ordinary course of nature a sign either of his grace or of his wrath. For from what happens one knows what he wills (§ 1007 *Met.*), and on the other hand he wills nothing but what is agreeable to his perfection (§ 1004 *Met.*), that is, that for which his perfection can furnish him a motive. Meanwhile, since natural things are merely arbitrary signs, as can be shown in general in just the same manner as was done above with the rainbow (§ 172), one can also take none of them for a sign until one can prove that God has made it one (§ 294 *Met.*). If, accordingly, he had declared, as he did of the rainbow, what his will is in this matter, then we could also accept every event in the air as assuring us of what it signified. But since there is no divine revelation concerning mock suns, mock moons and other such events in the air, we must prove by grounds that God has made such things a sign. Besides divine revelation, we have two further ways left of knowing the will of God (§ 1004, 1007, 1010 *Met.*); but by neither of them [p. 314] can it be made out that mock suns, mock moons and other such aerial events should have a meaning. As regards the first way, in which one infers what God wills from a consideration of his perfections, or else makes use of the perfection of the world for this purpose (§ 1004 *Met.*), no one will, I think, ask to establish in such a manner that the aerial events, especially those which occur seldom and, from ignorance of their hidden causes, appear very wondrous (§ 346 *Mor.*), have any meaning. For such meanings, as the example of the rainbow very clearly proves (§ 172), are arbitrary and have no necessary connection with the attributes of God. But who would presume to go so far as to *determine* what God may resolve to do according to his free will? And even if something could be said on this ground, it will still go no further than some probability, and will be more against the meaning than for it. Namely, God is perfectly wise (§ 1048 *Met.*), and therefore does nothing superfluous (§ 1049 *Met.*). But if one considers what meanings the aerial events are supposed to have, one will [p. 315] find that this is already known from Scripture. They can have no meaning other than that they announce either good or evil to man, according as he conducts himself well or ill. But we know not only from Scripture that God will punish evil and reward good, even in this world; we are also assured by reason that God crowns the virtue of men with good fortune and punishes vices with misfortune (§ 37 *Mor.*). And accordingly one cannot well conceive otherwise than that these signs are superfluous. But if they are merely to serve as an incitement to think of God and his perfection, and thereby to renew in us the remembrance of his grace and of his wrath, all this can happen without their needing to be particular signs of this or that good or ill fortune which is at the door. Such a thing could be granted if there were absolutely a necessity in everything that happens in the world, such that what precedes necessarily drew after it what follows: for then all events of nature would be natural signs of good and ill fortune, and in general of all the events of men (§ 293 *Met.*). But now since no such [p. 316] necessity obtains (§ 577 *Met.*), neither does any such meaning obtain. The other way of knowing the will of God consists in inferring from what happens to that which God wills (§ 1007 *Met.*). And this is the way one is wont to choose when one wishes to refer the meaning of natural events to the state of men. One observes what misfortune befalls men after a rare aerial event has occurred, and then supposes that this was a sign of the unfortunate accident and signified it. If one can bring forward many such examples, the meaning gains credit, and one supposes that there is something behind it. But commonly one forgets the examples that stand against it. For one also meets with others in which either nothing remarkable, or even fortunate accidents, followed. And if one has a single example that stands against it, the whole matter falls to the ground: which way I made use of when I showed the groundlessness of the meaning of the northern lights<sup>a</sup>, [p. 317] and others before me have gone this very same way when they sought to show the incorrectness of the meaning of comets. But I find several further things to be noted in this matter. Since aerial events are seen only in a small part of the surface of the earth, one may not count great public calamities as part of their meaning, but only such occurrences as concern those places where they were seen. And these must, besides this, also be of such a kind that they do not concern single persons in their particular state, but rather have their purpose with regard to the whole place in its public state. Now if one confines the meaning within such narrow bounds, as is only fair, one will find still less ground in experience by which one could lend the meaning an appearance of truth. We find that such aerial events often occur at a time when most people are asleep (§ 312 *Phys.*), and are therefore seen either by no one or only by a few, who are on watch and commonly perceive them only by chance. But if God wished to use them as signs of his grace [p. 318] and of his wrath, he would yet have to let something happen along with them whereby at least many would be roused to look at them; as we find that on the day of Pentecost, when he wished to make the beginning of preaching the Gospel through the Apostles, he caused a concourse of people by a strong rushing of a mighty wind<sup>a′</sup>. But nowadays, when the arts of soothsaying have fallen out of use among the learned, few will be found who pay heed to this interpretation of signs. It was in use only at the time when people did not know that these things arise from natural causes according to the ordinary course of nature, but imagined that God produced them, if not by a miracle, yet in an extraordinary manner; inasmuch as in this sense they were called prodigies, which were regarded as something distinct from miracles and from natural things, although one had no concept of such things, as indeed there can be none, since everything that occurs in corporeal things is either natural or supernatural (§ 632 *Met.*).

<sup>a</sup> Gedancken über das ungewöhnliche *Phænomenon p. 35.*

<sup>a′</sup> *Act. II, 2.*

*Purposes*

*of aerial events.*

*Why they are hidden.*

*What they consist in.*

§ 174. Why God lets mock suns [p. 319] and mock moons, together with other aerial events, actually come about in nature seems hard for us to guess, because we do not find what they properly bring in their train whereby the principal purpose of the earth, that it is to be a dwelling for animals and men, would be promoted (§ 66). For where something of this kind is found, as with rain (§ 162), the purpose at once occurs to everyone. Notwithstanding this, however, we can still find all manner of benefit which we can derive from them, and which also belongs among the divine purposes (§ 1029 *Met.*), even if it can be counted only among the subordinate purposes. The principal purpose of the whole world is that by contemplating it we are stirred up to know God's perfections (§ 2). Such unexpected aerial events, since they occur but seldom in any case, awaken wonder in those spectators to whom the causes are unknown (§ 346 *Mor.*), and on the other hand delight in the rest, who understand them to some degree (§ 307 ff. *Phys.*). Both are an occasion to think of God as the author of nature, who performs such wonderful things in it, and so they serve to awaken men. But the more singular natural events are, the more [p. 320] eager one is to know their cause, and hence they also serve to stir men up in the sciences.

### Das VI. Capitel. Von dem Blitze und andern Feuer-Zeichen. — Chapter VI. On Lightning and Other Fiery Meteors.

*What harm thunderstorms do.*

*Why one should pray and sing during thunderstorms.*

§ 175. Among the things that are generated in the air there is scarcely anything more dangerous than lightning, which causes harm in a great many ways. This is shown by its manifold and in part quite singular effects, which are both abundantly known from daily experience and have also been explained by us in detail according to their causes (§ 327 ff. *Phys.*), so that I have no need to repeat this here anew. And on account of the great dangerousness of thunderstorms it also usually happens that men in general are afraid of them (§ 476 *Met.*), though one more than another, according as he has either been accustomed to it from his youth (§ 419 *Mor.*) or else imagines the danger greater than another does (§ 441 *Met.*). This very fear has brought in the custom [p. 321] that, when a thunderstorm arises, one begins to sing and pray; which is also not to be rejected, since in time of danger one ought to turn to God. For although thunderstorms arise from natural causes, I have nevertheless already shown that the whole course of nature has been arranged by God according to his wisdom (§ 14, 15), and that he has also had regard therein to the conduct of men (§ 30 *Mor.*).

*Whether thunderstorms also bring benefit.*

*1. They cool the air.*

*How this happens.*

*2. They purify the air.*

*Why God permits thunderstorms.*

*What is to be observed concerning evil*

*in the world.*

§ 176. But although thunderstorms can do so much and such great harm where they strike, they are nevertheless not made for that alone; rather, man also has benefit from them. It is a thing that strikes everyone's eye at once, that thunderstorms cool the air. For when there is great heat and a thunderstorm comes, one perceives at once that it grows cooler. And hence one also wishes that a thunderstorm might come when one feels the burden of the heat. Indeed, when after hot days one sees sheet lightning in the evening, one is accustomed to say: the weather is cooling off. Now it is indeed true that thunderstorms are commonly accompanied by a heavy rain, and that this cools the air (§ 166); consequently it has the appearance as if it were not by the tempest, in so far as it consists of thunder and lightning, [p. 322] that the air is cooled. But since it is also ascribed to the thunderstorm by itself, and mere sheet lightning in the evening seems to confirm it, we must nevertheless investigate whether it has any ground or not. We find that there is a peculiar kind of heat in the air when a thunderstorm is about to come, and one is accustomed to say: the air is sultry. When a thunderstorm arises, not only is a mixture of various fumes present in the air brought about by all kinds of motions in the air (§ 321 *Phys.*), such a mixture being, according to chemical experience, accompanied by heat (§ 141 *Pt. II Exper.*). And the sultry air confirms that heat is produced by this mixture. But now since in thunderstorms this sultry matter scattered in the air draws together in one place (§ 321 *Phys.*) and is dispersed by the ignition, the air is thereby freed of its sultriness. From this yet another benefit appears still more clearly, namely that the air is purified by the thunderstorm of all kinds of fatty and saline exhalations. The matter of lightning is a sulphur that is generated in the air (§ 321 *Phys.*). Sulphur consists of a fatty matter and of an acid salt (§ 372 *Phys.*). Now since [p. 323] many fatty materials exhale from the bodies of animals and men, and also pass into the air when these and the plants putrefy, as likewise rise from fatty and sulphurous soil, &c., the air is purified of all these exhalations by the thunderstorm. For as they are dissolved by the ignition, they are not only distributed in the upper air, and in part also, where the storm strikes, brought down out of the air, but the subtle fumes also unite with the vapours (§ 131) and afterwards fall down with them; and in this way not only is the air purified, but the earth is at the same time made fertile (§ 399 *Phys.*). And who knows what further benefit lies hidden that we do not yet perceive. But this one alone is great enough to outweigh the harm that thunderstorms cause, and it justifies God's wisdom in having permitted some harm, because otherwise a greater benefit would have been hindered (§ 1058 *Met.*), especially since he has, moreover, known how to turn the harm to use in the government of men (§ 175). As we find it here with lightning, so it would show itself with all other natural things, if only we paid close attention to them. It would show itself everywhere that the good [p. 324] outweighs the evil, and that evil itself is applied by God to good, at least in the government of men. This is a task for those who wish to judge of the perfection of nature (§ 701 *Met.*), and it would be of great benefit for convincing worldly minds that they raise doubts without cause, either against God or against his providence and government, because in their opinion there is more evil than good in the world. But this benefit is to be esteemed all the more highly, because those who have investigated the origin of evil, and have made it their concern to answer the objections drawn from it against natural and Christian religion, must confess that these objections are, of all those brought forward by this kind of people, the most difficult to answer. I will now cite merely the celebrated court preacher of His Royal Majesty of Great Britain, Mr **John Clarke**, who has written most recently on this matter<sup>a</sup>.

<sup>a</sup> *Enquiry into the origine of moral Evil p. 1. & seqq.*

*Whether rain weakens the thunderstorm.*

*Is unknown to the Author from experience.*

*General remark.*

*Particular maxim of divine providence.*

*Is maintained as probable on grounds.*

*Encouragement to the knowledge of nature.*

*How the thunderstorm strikes.*

*Why there is sometimes only sheet lightning.*

§ 177. It is commonly believed that rain weakens the thunderstorm, and that in heavy rain the lightning does not strike so easily as [p. 325] when it storms without rain, or at least rains only weakly. I recall that, when in my youth I was still living in my native town, the storm struck in the suburb during a very heavy rain, and notwithstanding this the house burned down. But I have never paid attention to whether the thunderstorm abated and became milder as the rain increased. Therefore I cannot properly say whether the assertion is in accordance with experience or not. Now although I am not unaware that it is held that one should not concern oneself with the cause of a natural event before one is sufficiently assured that it actually takes place in nature, and although I do not reject this caution absolutely, but rather highly approve it in those cases where one would thereby only take occasion to invent causes; yet I also cannot conceal that I admit some cases where it is useful to concern oneself with the cause of alleged events, namely where by this investigation one can find out whether the event is well founded or not, and reason makes up for the want of experience, or else gives occasion to a further investigation of nature which would otherwise be omitted. Now since the present assertion seems to me to belong to the latter class, [p. 326] I shall do no wrong if on this occasion I reflect upon it somewhat, especially as we shall thereby obtain a new proof of what we maintained above, namely that God through his unspeakable wisdom has so ordered the course of nature that he makes good again, through other natural things, the harm which some cause (§ 169). Indeed, if the matter should prove correct, we could even note in it a further rule, distinct from the former, namely that God can by natural things prevent the harm which some would cause. The thunderstorm is one of the most dangerous things that we have in nature (§ 175). Now if it were certain that a heavy rain breaks its force, we should have a means by which the harm that could otherwise ensue would be forestalled. If one proceeds ever further in the knowledge of nature, in such a manner as I have begun, one will note more rules of this kind, and thereby attain to the knowledge of the maxims according to which God governs the world, and in this way be assured ever more and more that it is a work of the wisdom of God, and that no *fatality* and unavoidable necessity has place in it (§ 15, 16). Now if we [p. 327] consider the present assertion, it could easily be proved, if we were willing to grant that the sultry air is filled with sulphurous fume, and that the storm strikes in the places where this fume gathers thickly together. For then the heavy rain would drive away the fume, in that it struck it down along with itself, and in this way the fire could not descend as far as a building; consequently the danger of a strike would be past. This opinion seems to gain some further probability from the fact that it is so easily conceived how the lightning goes downward, whereas flame otherwise rises upward: for in the chemical experiment in which the martial fumes are kindled, the flame which is lit at the top of the glass goes downward from a like cause, and even divides the water (§ 141 *Pt. II Exper.*). Here the case would be no other than with a running fire, where the flame goes on as far as it meets matter that can be kindled. Nor does the example to which I appealed earlier stand entirely in the way: for besides the fact that outside the gate it perhaps did not rain quite so heavily as in the town, which is very often *observed*, I myself also cannot properly recall whether the great thunderclap [p. 328] by which the house was set on fire, happened when it was already raining heavily beforehand, or whether rather the heavy downpour only began together with it. Therefore we still have cause to investigate whether the lightning goes down because it meets in the lower air matter of its own kind, which is to be regarded, as it were, as a running fire. Should this be found certain, one would thereafter also be able to settle probable rules concerning the dangerousness of the weather; indeed, perhaps it would even be possible to avert harm. I adduce this to the end that I may encourage the lovers of nature to let no opportunity pass where something can be *observed* that could give light in this matter. It cannot be denied that the thunderstorm moves from one place to another, and that one hears it at first from afar, but afterwards it draws nearer (§ 324 *Phys.*): but this can very well hold together with the fact that the storm can strike only in the place where it finds lightning-like matter in the lower air as well, since otherwise, namely, the lightning does not reach the earth, as indeed we often see it vanish in the air, if we pay attention to how it shoots out of the cloud in serpentine fashion. And from this one can also further [p. 329] determine the cause why at times the storm-cloud gives off nothing but sheet lightning, whereas at other times a flash of lightning darts out of it as well. For if the matter of the thunderstorm, namely the sulphurous fume, is merely above the cloud or within it, then on its ignition one sees nothing but a light breaking through; but if such matter is also to be found beneath the cloud, then it too ignites at the same time in a series, one part after another, and thereby forms the lightning. Accordingly one sees that this opinion has more probability in its favour than can be objected against it. But now, even if the lightning were not carried along like a running fire, it could nevertheless still come about that heavy rain was disadvantageous to the thunderstorm. For although we cannot yet properly explain how in this case the rain could be disadvantageous to the generation of the lightning, yet neither have we any grounds by which we could prove the contrary, or even only show it to be probable. And accordingly in this case the matter remains in complete uncertainty, and must be decided by experience (§ 2, ch. 5 *Log.*). For although one could also settle it by reason if one knew the actual [p. 330] constitution of the ignition and of the other circumstances of the thunderstorm, we have nevertheless not yet got so far at present (§ 321 *Phys.*).

*What strong thunder with weak sheet lightning signifies.*

*When strong thunder signifies wind.*

*Caution to be used in this.*

*Why the lightning is stronger at one time than another.*

*Caution to be used in this.*

*Caution in judging the strength of the thunder.*

*What remains to be done here.*

*State of physics.*

§ 178. It has also long been observed that when the thunder is strong and the sheet lightning weak, a greater storm is to be expected from the side where the thunder is heard most<sup>a</sup>. The lightning excites the thunder by a motion which it communicates to the subtle corpuscles of air (§ 323 *Phys.*). Therefore, when the state of the air is the same, a strong flash of lightning must excite stronger thunder than a weak one. But now if a weak flash of lightning excites strong thunder, the cause of it must be sought in the air. When the air is denser, the sound increases, although the cause which produces it remains the same (§ 7 *Pt. III Exper.*); and in this case, too, no other cause than the density of the air can obtain, since the difference between a strong and a weak sound consists solely in this, that many or few corpuscles of air are moved at once (§ 12 *Pt. III Exper.*). If, therefore, there is little sheet lightning with strong thunder, it is an indication that on the [p. 331] side or quarter where it thunders strongly the air must be denser. Where the air is dense, its expansive force is greater than where it is thinner (§ 125 *Pt. I Exper.*). Therefore, since wind arises from this, that the expansive force of the air is stronger in one place than in another, namely in that the stronger air spreads through the weaker with a velocity proportioned to the difference of their strength (§ 74 *Pt. II Exper.*), it can very well happen that a wind comes from the quarter where it thunders more strongly than it otherwise should according to the magnitude of the lightning. And in this way the thunderstorm gives a sign of the impending wind (§ 193 *Met.*). But in this conjecture one must take care not to hold the lightning to be much weaker than it is. We also know from daily experience that a light is not so bright by day as by night, and that the darker it is by day, the more the brightness of the light also increases. This obtains with lightning as well as with other light. By night the lightning is stronger than by day, and in the dark stronger than in the light; whereas by day it is weaker than by night, and on dark days brighter than on bright ones. One night is darker in a thunderstorm than another, partly on account of the [p. 332] season, according as the day either glimmers through the night or not (§ 193 *Phys.*), partly on account of the storm-clouds, which are not as dark at one time as at another. A day, on the other hand, is darker than another solely on account of the storm-clouds. But just as the break of day, or the evening twilight, diminishes the darkness at night-time, so by day one must also consider whether the time of the thunderstorm is near noon or not, especially in summer-time, when on account of the height of the sun its light is wont to be very noticeably stronger than shortly after the rising or near the setting of the sun. But with the thunder, too, one must take care not to hold it to be stronger than it is, partly on account of the varying distance at which one hears it, partly on account of the difference of the places where one hears it; for it is well known that where there are many mountains or other things by which the sound is *reflected*, it becomes very much stronger thereby: hence in general thunderstorms with their violent thunder seem far more terrible among the mountains than on the level plain. But just as the latter is shown by the evidence of one's own eyes, if one looks about a little to see where one is and considers what the bodies lying around us [p. 333] can contribute to the echo, so the other, on the contrary, can be judged from the length of time that passes between the flashes of lightning and the claps of thunder (§ 324 *Phys.*). One could push this conjecture, as to how the wind will change after the thunderstorm, still further, namely if we accustomed ourselves to take good note of the difference of the thunder in the distance whence it comes, where we are, and at the place whither it moves when we listen after it, so that we could judge from this the difference that is found in the state of the air: for that one could conjecture from this from what quarter the wind will blow after the tempest has ended may be gathered from what precedes. Indeed, perhaps one could even bring it so far as to be able to conjecture also from the difference in the strength of the thunder whether the thunderstorm will come up or not. But here there arises a very extensive investigation, in which a great deal is to be weighed and distinguished from one another, and it is not feasible at present for us to pursue these thoughts further. At least one sees from this too that in most natural things much is still to be found which could be brought out by the careful diligence of skilful natural philosophers and applied to the benefit of human life [p. 334] if one once grew accustomed to ordering the sciences to the benefit of life, and in particular gave up the delusion that the knowledge of nature is to be regarded merely as a game by which the minds of those who love the sciences are delighted.

<sup>a</sup> *Florini* kluger Hauß-Vater *lib. 2. c. 78. f. 463.*

*Why strong lightning with feeble thunder points to rain.*

*How the state of the air is exactly judged.*

*A particular example.*

*Is explained from the observations with the weather- glasses.*

§ 179. It has moreover been observed that, when a strong flash of lightning is accompanied by weak thunder, this is a sign of rain, or points to rain<sup>a</sup>. I do not find it groundless, and it can be proved in just the same way as the preceding. The lightning excites the thunder by impressing a particular kind of motion upon the corpuscles of air (§ 327 *Phys.*), and accordingly, with the same state of the air, it produces a stronger thunder when it is strong than when it is weak. Therefore, when a strong flash excites a weak thunder, the cause must be sought in the air. In the air a stronger sound is produced by the same cause when it is dense than when it is thin (§ 7 *Pt. III Exper.*). But it can become thinner when it is not pressed so hard (§ 122 *Pt. I Exper.*), consequently when it becomes lighter (§ 22 *Pt. II Exper.*). Therefore, [p. 335] when the thunder is weak, it is an indication that the air is thin, consequently light, or else very warm (§ 133 *Pt. I Exper.*). Now since in the first case rainy weather is wont to follow readily (§ 41 *Pt. II Exper.*), it is no wonder if weak thunder with strong lightning is a harbinger of rain. One may also from this, when the thunderstorm comes up with rain-clouds, expect a heavy downpour with it. From what cause the air is thin can be determined by the manometer and the barometer; for if the barometer does not stand very low, while the manometer indicates a great thinness of the air and the thermometer at the same time much heat, then the lower air is thin more on account of the heat than on account of the lightness of the air. I will illustrate the matter by an example, so that one may see how the weather-glasses are to be used in explaining the thunderstorm. On Tuesday last, being 27 July of this year 1723, at 2 o'clock in the afternoon, there came a heavy thunderstorm, which was soon accompanied by an uncommon downpour that continued without a break for a fairly long time. In the midst of the rain it began to hail heavily: but the hailstones were no bigger than a pea, indeed even smaller, [p. 336] and because it was raining heavily at the same time, indeed the rain continued a good quarter of an hour and more, they melted again at once. When it began to hail, I went to look at the weather-glasses, and found that the mercury in the barometer did not stand especially low, namely at 29⅜ English inches, consequently that the air was not especially light (§ 25 *Pt. II Exper.*). The thermometer stood fairly high, and when the storm began the air was also very sultry. The manometer (§ 54 *Pt. II Exper.*) showed that the air was very thin. Since hail had been generated in the upper air, this must have been very cold (§ 287 *Phys.*). Therefore, since cold condenses the air (§ 133 *Pt. I Exper.*), there was also much air pressing upon the lower, consequently the air taken altogether was not especially light (§ 124 *Pt. I Exper.*). On the other hand, since the lower air was very warm, its expanding force was thereby strong enough to withstand the load above (§ 133 *Pt. I Exper.*), and it therefore remained thin. But in thin air a heavy downpour can ensue (§ 277 *Phys.*). And so one saw that here the thinness of the air arose more from the heat than from the lightness of the upper air, and likewise how it came about that so many clouds had been able to gather above [p. 337] and thereafter been able to sustain themselves in an almost unusual and continuing downpour: to which without doubt the upper wind also contributed its share, which must have been present there on account of the hailstones (§ 288 *Phys.*), in that it constantly brought clouds up swiftly, so that there was no lack of matter for the rain.

<sup>a</sup> *Florinus loc. cit.*

*Why red sheet lightning is dangerous.*

*Defects in physics.*

*A doubtful circumstance*

*Why red lightning sets fire more easily.*

*Whether the air can change the colour of lightning.*

*Why this is to be doubted.*

*The difference of colour comes from the matter.*

*What use the consideration of the*

*colour can be.*

*Why rain, and snow in particular, changes the sound of the bells.*

§ 180. Whoever pays attention to thunderstorms will find that lightning does not always have the same colour. One can observe it quite properly when two thunderstorms stand over against each other and there is sheet lightning from both places at the same time, so that one can see the light either at once, or at least immediately one after the other. But just as natural philosophers have hitherto by no means determined all the difference among natural things which one can perceive if one deliberately pays attention to them, and notes what one perceives in them at different times, so that one can afterwards compare it together: so we also do not yet find the difference of the light in sheet lightning and in lightning described according to every difference. Notwithstanding this, the country people, who are led by opportunity to some experiences of which others do not think, have long since stated that red sheet lightning is the most dangerous, and also, even in the midst [p. 338] of rain, sets on fire the place<sup>a</sup> where the thunderbolt strikes, and cannot be put out with water: whether this fire cannot be put out with water I leave undecided, since it must first be confirmed by particular examples (§ 2, ch. 5 *Log.*). But that this lightning sets things on fire more easily, and moreover that the thunderbolt, where it strikes, exerts greater violence, can very well be understood. When the lightning is pale, there is little matter gathered together that takes fire, and hence it is weak (§ 327 *ff. Phys.*). When more takes fire, the flame becomes yellow, and consequently the lightning stronger. Therefore lightning that resembles gold in colour is already held to be more dangerous. Finally, when it becomes entirely red, there must be a still denser flame, since the gold- yellow colour draws towards red when it becomes dense. Therefore in this case the lightning is strongest. Should it be the case that red lightning were a fire which also cannot be put out with water, then the colour would not only come from the density of the flame, and consequently from the quantity of the matter that is set on fire at once, but the kind of matter must also contribute something to it. Perhaps [p. 339] one might conjecture whether the air too may not produce some changes in the colour of lightning, just as we find that the light of the moon is very greatly changed by it (§ 120 ff.). And if this were so, one would have to pay attention to this as well, since we know that the effects of lightning, from which one judges its dangerousness, also arise in part from the air (§ 330, 331 *Phys.*). Indeed, what was suggested above for consideration concerning the striking of the thunderbolt (§ 177) could then also be drawn into it. But I do not yet see whether one can ascribe much to the air in changing the colour of lightning. For I have more than once quite expressly observed that at one time two thunderstorms stood towards the south, though the one more to the east and the other more to the west, and the lightning was quite different in colour. Therefore it is indeed more credible that the cause of the difference in the colour of lightning is to be sought more in the matter which takes fire than in the air through which the lightning passes. Here one finds occasion for new considerations of lightning, and of fire in general, according to its colours. If the strength of lightning could be judged exactly from its colour, once one had for some time paid [p. 340] attention to it, one would thereby obtain a means of deciding by experience whether heavy rain weakens lightning (§ 177). For although one might suppose that this could indeed be better judged by the strength of the thunder, yet several things are to be considered here which one need not regard in the colour of lightning. In the first place, the barometer and manometer show that the air can change in its weight and density during a thunderstorm: but thereby the magnitude of the thunder is changed at the same time (§ 6, 7 *Pt. III Exper.*). Next, it is also certain that water diminishes sound (§ 8 *Pt. III Exper.*), and hence in rainy weather it is not heard so strongly and so far as at other times. This is the cause why bells do not ring so clearly in heavy rain as when the air is harder, and likewise why in the first case one does not hear the clock strike so well as in the other. And since both likewise occur when it snows heavily, while snow consists of frozen vapours, and the snowflakes are moreover to be regarded as a soft body, which by experience weakens sound, one not only recognises from this the cause of it too, but also understands at the same time why snow [p. 341] alters the sound of the bells still more than rain does.

<sup>a</sup> *Florinus loc. cit.*

*Purposes of other fiery meteors.*

*Modesty of the Author.*

*Whether fiery meteors afford weather- forecasts.*

*Superstition.*

*Whether there is anything preternatural.*

*Origin of superstition.*

*What the prodigies in old histories are.*

*Whence the meaning of comets comes.*

§ 181. All fiery meteors that appear in the air are generated from such evaporations as either merely shine or are actually kindled (§ 337 *Phys.*). Since most of them vanish again at once, and moreover take up no great space in the air, not much change can follow in it merely from its being freed of these evaporations. It is indeed commonly said that, when by night one sees stars as it were falling down from the sky, the air is purifying itself: but whether this purification signifies much is another question. The air is certainly purified of fat and oily evaporations: but if there are few of them in it which draw together into a fiery meteor, this too cannot render the ensuing state of the air much changed from the preceding one. Whoever considers this will not find that any particular purpose is attained in nature by this fiery meteor. However, since we do not yet understand the whole connection of natural things, we are not opposed if someone should in time find out what we, by the light which now shines upon us in [p. 342] the knowledge of nature, are not yet able to discern. By our present knowledge we set no bounds to anyone in his knowledge, nay, we thereby set none to ourselves; but we shall endeavour to go ever further, so long as we have powers and opportunity for it from God. But since something changeable must nevertheless go on in the air if even the few evaporations or fumes present in it are to be brought together and fiery meteors generated from them; and since the kinds of weather arise from the changes in the air (§ 246 ff. *Phys.*), as the barometer in particular also shows, since it properly indicates only the changes in the air (§ 22 *Pt. II Exper.*), but by means of these also the changes of the weather (§ 39 ff. *Pt. II Exper.*): it may well be that a weather-forecast can be taken from such fiery meteors, and hence the conjecture is not without ground when people, for example, if many stars seem to fall down from a clear starry sky, are wont to say that the weather will change. What those who regard these events as something preternatural invent belongs to superstition, the ground of which I have already shown elsewhere (§ 333 [p. 343] *Phys.*). But since superstition is nothing good, it cannot be regarded as a purpose which God sought to attain (§ 1056 *Met.*), although he permits it, because he can turn it to good (§ 1057 ff. *Met.*). The preternatural has no proper concept, but is regarded only as something intermediate between the natural and the supernatural, namely as something that does not happen through any miracle, and yet would not follow of itself in a natural way according to the course of nature unless God or a spirit lent a hand. That there is anything of this kind among the fiery meteors no one will be able to prove: but that such preternatural events have been admitted in nature may be gathered from what has been written of prodigies; for it is precisely this kind of events that, by way of distinction from miracles, has been called prodigies<sup>a</sup>. Nowadays, since natural events have been investigated more exactly, nothing more is known of such prodigies. Meanwhile, since from ignorance it was imagined that the fiery meteors did not all arise merely from natural [p. 344] causes, but were produced either by God, or by an angel, or else by the devil, or finally by his instruments, it was also supposed that God has particular purposes for which he either stirs them up himself or through an angel, or else lets the devils and their instruments, the witches, stir them up; and meanings were accordingly ascribed to them. In particular they were regarded as harbingers of the future wrath of God and of the punishments following on account of it, and, to maintain this, whatever could be found on the subject was gathered together from history. Most of what has been passed off as prodigies, and of which the histories are everywhere full, can be held to be nothing other than aerial phenomena (§ 305 ff. *Phys.*) and fiery meteors; and it would not be unserviceable if all such narratives were sought out from history and each supposed prodigy were assigned its place among the aerial phenomena and fiery meteors. Since formerly, when the Aristotelian philosophy was current, comets were reckoned among the aerial events and regarded as prodigies, it came about for this very reason that a meaning was ascribed to them too, the groundlessness of which, however, I have shown (§ 169 *Phys.*).

<sup>a</sup> *Vid. Garcæi Meteorologia c. 4. p. 8. & seqq.*

*Whence that of the northern lights.*

*How we are to turn such events to our good.*

*What freedom is to be allowed in this.*

*Caution.*

*Mischief of those who do not know nature.*

*What Anthropomorphists are.*

[p. 345] For this very reason it has come about that a meaning has been ascribed to the so-called northern lights, the most wonderful among the fiery meteors (§ 334 *Phys.*), and that they have been regarded as a harbinger of the impending misfortune which God intends to bring upon men as a punishment; of which I have likewise shown the groundlessness<sup>b</sup>. Meanwhile, since these events too are to be reckoned among the works of God which come about according to his counsel (§ 999 *Met.*), and God has made the world and all that is in it, and lets all that happens in it come to pass, to the end that men should take occasion from it to think of him and to arrive at his perfections (§ 8), so it is also in accordance with his will that we take occasion at such events to think of him; and it is not to be rejected if each person, moreover, according to his concept, forms such edifying thoughts as his devotion gives him, and thereby encourages himself to godliness (§ 671 *Mor.*). Meanwhile, since nothing in this matter has been ordained by God, and the nature of things does not bring with it any *determinate* devotion, so each person must also leave the other [p. 346] his freedom, and not wish to force his own upon him. A simple person pictures the matter to himself according to his concept; but a person of understanding and a philosopher sees further, and purifies his thoughts of the imperfection which, on account of the weakness of their understanding, clings to the common man and to those who understand no more than he does in such things. But one must not suppose that I therefore approve of confirming people in their error, for this would be to speak in favour of superstition, which is not needed for the promotion of godliness (§ 671 *Mor.*). It is one thing to overlook the faults of the weak when he himself turns them to good, but another thing to contribute something to his error. The latter is wrong, but not the former. This is shown by the example of God, who permits evil to happen because he can turn it to good, but contributes nothing to it (§ 1056, 1057 *Met.*). But those go astray still further who, having senses unpractised in the knowledge of nature, look upon the world as a piece of clockwork in which God as the craftsman, and angels and devils as well, move the hand now this way and now that, that is, who represent God to themselves in a human manner, and who wish to force upon the philosophers [p. 347] the demand that they too, together with them, should think and speak of God in a human manner, although he has granted them the ability to think and speak of him in a manner befitting his great perfection. Formerly those were called **Anthropomorphites** who ascribed to God a human body; but by way of distinction one may call those **Anthropomorphists** who look upon God as a human soul and, besides his power by which he preserves all things and has ordained from eternity how everything is to come about (§ 999, 1054 *Met.*), ascribe to him yet a particular kind of effect, whereby he moves bodies, as it were, in a natural way, as, in their opinion, the soul moves our body, or as a created spirit moves bodies.

<sup>b</sup> In the Gedancken von dem ungewöhnlichen *Phænomeno p. 29, & seqq.*

*What benefit will-o'-the- wisps and other fiery meteors like them have.*

*Origin of superstition.*

*Disadvantage of ignorance.*

§ 182. Of the will-o'-the-wisps, the fiery meteors of mariners, and the flames which sometimes appear on animals and men (§ 336 ff. *Phys.*), no benefit can as yet be determined either. Why will- o'-the-wisps lead people astray I have already set out in that very place, and it has also long since been remarked by others. Namely, it happens accidentally, and the fault lies solely with us, in that we mistake them for lights in the village and follow [p. 348] them, or make towards them. What superstition has invented about them found credence no longer than until their causes were understood. And the common man, who imagines something dangerous in them, likewise does so for no other reason than because he does not know that it arises from natural causes, and so gives room to his imagination of a preternatural effect. And it is precisely this that strikes fear into him, because he supposes that an evil spirit is present there which produces it. Since fright can do harm, one sees that ignorance in natural things is something bad, whereas knowledge is something good for everyone. And accordingly each person should strive after knowledge as much as is ever possible for him in his circumstances.

### Das VII. Capitel. Von dem Wasser auf dem Erdboden. — Chapter VII. On the Water on the Surface of the Earth.

*Water makes things moist and wet.*

*How water makes things moist.*

*How this is confirmed by experience.*

*What experiments can contribute to this.*

*How water makes things wet.*

§ 183. Water makes things moist, as common experience teaches; and if one comprehends how this comes about, one also understands which bodies become moist, [p. 349] indeed, what water accomplishes thereby in nature. There are in bodies interstices which are filled with air (§ 64 *Pt. III Exper.*), and some of them indeed wide, out of which the air can be pumped by the air-pump (§ 110 *Pt. II Exper.*). Now when water comes upon these wide pores, where air and water can give way to each other, which does not happen in quite narrow ones (§ 31 *Pt. I Exper.*), the air, which is very much lighter (§ 86 *Pt. I Exper.*), rises upwards, and the water on the other hand sinks down (§ 212 ff. *Pt. I Exper.*). That bodies become moist in no other way than by the water penetrating into the pores and driving the air out of them is confirmed both by common experience and by that which is deliberately sought. If one lays a body that has wide pores in water, one sees that bubbles attach themselves to it all round. No one doubts that these bubbles are air. And since they can come from nowhere but out of the pores of the wood, where air is found in abundance (§ 161 *Pt. I Exper.*), one sees from this plainly that the water drives the air out of the pores of bodies. As regards experiments, I have already shown by more than one example (§ 161 ff. *Pt. I Exper.* [p. 350] and § 66, 71 *Pt. III Exper.*) that, when the air is pumped out within the water, the water penetrates very abundantly into the pores. One can try it with all kinds of bodies and note various circumstances which give occasion to know their inner constitution more clearly. For example, if one carefully weighs the bodies or materials with which one makes the experiment both before and afterwards, it will appear into which the water penetrates more strongly and more easily. If one takes pieces of different thickness and makes the experiment with them, it will appear, when one cuts them through or breaks them apart, how far the water can penetrate into the pores. Indeed, according as one pumps the air cleanly out of the receiver, or else leaves much or little behind, one can find out by experience whether the water penetrates easily or with difficulty. And since experience teaches that water draws itself into some bodies slowly, inasmuch as they must lie in it a long time, one must also observe how in the course of time more water gradually draws itself in: which one perceives by weighing it from time to time to see whether it has become heavier than before. Again, since [p. 351] heat rarefies the air, while cold condenses it again (§ 133 *Pt. I Exper.*), one can also pay attention to the difference that shows itself according as one first warms the materials or makes them cold, and this all the more, since it is known what this circumstance contributes in other cases to the air going out of a material more easily or with more difficulty (§ 154 *Pt. I Exper.*). Wetness differs from moisture only in degree. For one says that a body or a material is wet when one can either see with one's eyes that much water has drawn itself into it, or else the hands or another body take something of the water to themselves by touching it. For when a body has too much moisture about it, it imparts some of it to another. That is, when a body has too much moisture, the droplets of water stand out above the uppermost pores, and likewise above the imperceptible depressions in the uneven surface; some also hang here and there on the little fibres that are found on the surface. Therefore, when another body touches it, it is just as much as if droplets of water were laid upon its pores, and accordingly it is no wonder that they either draw themselves in, or at least remain hanging in the [p. 352] depressions and on the little fibres.

*Water makes many changes in bodies.*

*Effect of water in the seed.*

*What it does in dyeing.*

*How separation comes about through water.*

*How it spoils composition.*

*That it unites materials.*

*Benefit of water in mixtion.*

§ 184. By water penetrating into the pores of bodies, much change takes place in them. It drives their small parts apart from one another, and thereby some materials swell up (§ 54 *Phys.*), others become soft (§ 64 *Phys.*), and some are indeed entirely dissolved. And nature makes use of this means in many kinds of cases, where she either intends to dissolve bodies again in their decay, or else has the design of producing others anew. For example, the first beginning in the growth of plants rests on this ground. For the seed draws the water into itself and, having swollen up from it, begins to germinate. Since water dissolves the parts of bodies, it also draws out colours and other subtle materials, in particular the salts found in them. And one can therefore use it in manifold ways in the arts. Indeed, it is possible in this way to bring very finely divided matter out of one body into another, as we see with colours. For as the water draws itself into the narrow pores, it carries in with it the matter dissolved in it: but when it dries out again, then [p. 353] the matter that was dissolved in it remains behind. Of the latter there is by no means any doubt. When solid matter has been dissolved in water, and one lets the water stand so that it gradually evaporates, the dissolved matter separates from it again and remains behind on the bottom of the vessel. When bodies frequently swell up through wetness and contract again in drying, their inner composition can often be spoiled thereby. We have a clear example in wood which stands in water and at times becomes dry, and which at length rots or at least becomes quite brittle. On the other hand we also find that water unites divided dry matter together, of which dough and gypsum can furnish an example. All that I have said occurs now here, now there in nature, as everyone can perceive for himself who deliberately pays attention to everything that happens there. We are accordingly to regard water as a matter which provides very great and manifold benefit in the dissolution of materials and in their mixing and uniting. And assuredly more wonderful effects lie hidden behind this than one would suppose. And nature uses water [p. 354] in this respect for very important operations.

*Indispensable benefit of water in human life.*

*1. In eating and drinking.*

*2. In the cleaning of household goods and clothing &c.*

*3. For the convenience and recreation of life.*

*Why not all men recognise the goodness of God in water.*

*What is to be done here.*

*What occasional devotions should be like.*

*Equity to be observed therein.*

§ 185. How much benefit water provides us in human life lies before everyone's eyes and need not be described at length. Water serves men and animals as a universal drink, and although men prepare a drink for themselves by art, yet this cannot be done without water. Beer is brewed from water and malt, and it is the water that quenches the thirst. Wine, which is prepared from grapes, could not have grown without the help of water, and the same is the case with the drink which is prepared from fruit in England and in other places. All plants which serve for the nourishment of men and animals must attain their growth by the help of water, and in general we cannot do without water in the preparation of food. In this way neither men nor cattle could have their nourishment without water, nor find their dwelling on the surface of the earth. Therefore, since God has so arranged the earth that men and animals can dwell upon it and find everything that belongs to the necessities and convenience of their life [p. 355] (§ 66), he uses water as a means by which the earth is made a fit dwelling for men and animals (§ 912 *Met.*). And this becomes still further evident when we consider what advantage water affords us besides in the cleaning of our household goods and our clothing, and in other cases; whereby the manifold use of water in the arts is by no means to be forgotten, by which is prepared whatever we use for the convenience, and also for the recreation, of human life. This benefit would be very lengthy to recount; but this lengthiness is not necessary. Everyone need only look about him among the craftsmen and artists, and he will find it in abundance. Few men recognise the good they enjoy from water, because they are not accustomed to pay attention to everything that occurs daily, much less to consider at the same time what advantage they enjoy from it. Whoever, therefore, would know the goodness of God (§ 1063 *Met.*) from water must, on every occasion where he sees water used, pay attention to the work which is prepared by means of it, and afterwards weigh what convenience he would have to do without, [p. 356] if we did not have that work. For example, when one comes into a grinding-mill, one sees that the grindstone is constantly moistened with water, consequently that water is necessary for grinding knives, scissors and other instruments. Let one therefore consider what advantage we have from sharp knives, scissors and other instruments which are ground sharp in the grinding-mill, and one will thereby obtain a greater concept of the benefit of water. If one now goes further in one's thoughts and regards water as a means by which God has sought to attain this use as a purpose (§ 1029 *Met.*), one will find occasion therein to delight in the wisdom (§ 14) and goodness of God (§ 21). And in my judgement these are the right occasional thoughts which a man can and should have of God (§ 8): for these can also stand before the rigour of reason, and cannot be mocked as simple-minded notions by those who fancy themselves to be strong in understanding above others. Meanwhile we can also very well suffer a simple person to form edifying thoughts according to his simplicity; only one must [p. 357] not force them upon those to whom God has given the gift of likewise arranging their occasional thoughts of God for the promotion of godliness (§ 671 *Mor.*) and of their virtue (§ 673 *Mor.*) in such a way that they need not fear the judgement-seat of reason.

*Why the water flows about everywhere on the surface of the earth.*

*Proof of God's provident care.*

*Why and how the water is kept*

*in motion.*

§ 186. Now since water is in the highest degree necessary for plants, men and animals, and also for the generation of other bodies, it is also so distributed over the surface of the earth that one nowhere finds a lack of it. And where it cannot come of itself, thither it is brought by rain and dew, and also by art (§ 16 *Hydraul.*). It is enough that there is everywhere on the surface of the earth such a supply at hand that rain and dew can be maintained and artificial wells can be abundantly provided. And from this one recognises the provident care of God, who has prepared the earth with wisdom (§ 1036 *Met.*). For the earth is the dwelling-house of men and animals (§ 66). But it is also so prepared that nothing is lacking to it which makes it habitable. Common experience teaches, and it is therefore a thing known to everyone, that water becomes foul and stinking when it stands still for a long time, especially [p. 358] when the great heat of summer is added to it. Therefore it was expedient that the water should be kept in constant motion. And to this end we find that in the rivers it constantly flows onward. But so that it can flow, the springs from which the rivers rise lie very high, and the bed of the rivers slopes towards the sea in such a way that it comes incessantly ever somewhat nearer to the centre of the earth the farther one goes down along the river. From which one sees that the lands where the springs are, from which the rivers rise, lie highest, whereas those where the sea is found, into which all rivers finally pour, lie lowest.
