But since the water constantly flows past, and yet the sea is not to become fuller, lest it overflow and inundate everything, it was necessary that the water should come back again from the sea to the springs: which also in fact happens (§ 342 *Phys.*). And so the springs and rivers never lack water, while the sea, on the other hand, does not receive too much of it. This *circulation* of the water on the surface of the earth is the means by which the dry land, which is inhabited by men and animals, is constantly supplied with water, and the water [p. 359] itself is kept fresh and convenient for use.

*Benefit of the rivers.*

§ 187. Besides the fact that the rivers carry the water everywhere and keep it in constant motion, so that it cannot spoil (§ 186), they have yet another benefit, which they afford to men who know how to make use of them to their advantage. For who does not know that one can sail on the rivers from one place to another, and that this happens with great advantage, partly because one can travel faster by water than by land, since the ship, especially when there is a good wind, goes much more quickly than a laden waggon on land, partly because it is done at less cost, since the fodder for the horses, which one does not need on the water, costs a great deal? Again, it is known that one drives mills by flowing water, and can thereby accomplish at less cost and more quickly what could be done only far more dearly and slowly with the help of men and animals. For that only flowing water, and not water standing still in lakes, is fit to drive mills has been set out in another place (§ 145 *Mech.*).

*Benefit of the sea.*

*Whether there are rivers in the moon.*

*Benefit of the knowledge of the purposes.*

*Yet another benefit.*

*The sea affords rain and snow.*

*That there is a sea in all the planets.*

§ 188. Since all the water in the rivers constantly flows onward (§ 186), there must [p. 360] also be a great reservoir in which it is finally collected together again. And from this the necessity of the sea, or of the great ocean, into which all rivers pour, is evident. Therefore, since we meet with something of this kind in the moon too (§ 136 *Phys.*), it may be inferred from this that there are rivers there as well. And here we have a new proof that from the purposes of natural things, too, some matters can be determined which we should otherwise not be able to guess. Therefore I also have the confidence in general that what I show in this work concerning the purposes of natural things will give occasion to a greater knowledge of nature, since from it, as from sound grounds, one derives things to the knowledge of which one would otherwise perhaps not attain. Indeed, it is also pleasing when one can confirm through the purposes what one has come to know of natural things in another way, since in this one tastes the wisdom of God (§ 4), which on account of its perfection must necessarily delight one (§ 404 *Met.*). We find that the wind which blows over the sea brings the rain: the others, which come over the dry land, bring no rain- clouds with them, unless perhaps they merely [p. 361] drive back those that another wind has brought hither. From this one recognises sufficiently that rivers and ponds cannot supply as many vapours as are required for a lasting settled rain. Yet rain is nevertheless most necessary, as experience teaches us in dry years, when for want of rain almost everything is ready to wither and, as it were, to burn up on the ground, and as we also learn more than sufficiently from what has been taught concerning the benefit of rain (§ 163 *ff.*). Accordingly the sea must supply the matter for rain and snow. And so we see from this a new benefit of the sea, or of the great ocean. Namely, God has made it to the end that vapours may rise in great quantity, which are carried by the winds over the firm land for the generation of clouds. And this purpose of the sea confirms that in the planets too, even in the secondary planets of Jupiter, and consequently in those of Saturn as well, there must be an open sea. For we find great clouds in them (§ 146 *Phys.*); but these, as we have seen, cannot be generated except where a great open sea is present.

*Benefit of water with respect to fish.*

§ 189. In general, however, water, both in rivers and in the sea, also has the benefit that the fish find their abode, [p. 362] since, as is well known, they cannot last long out of the water, although one kind can always hold out longer than another. Accordingly a great multitude of living creatures would not be found on the surface of the earth if the water were not collected together in great reservoirs, that is, if rivers, lakes and seas were not found on the surface of the earth. The difference between fish and the other creatures living in the water does not belong here, where we are speaking merely of the benefit of water in general, in so far as it is collected by nature in great reservoirs.

*Benefit of the motion and of the salt in sea- water.*

§ 190. The water of the sea is not only strongly moved by storm-winds, but also has a constant motion from east to west (§ 353 *Phys.*), and over and above this the motion towards the shores and from them back again into the open sea, which is commonly called ebb and flood (§ 355 *Phys.*). Now since motion keeps water fresh and good, this must also be maintained by it in sea-water. It is known besides that salt also preserves water from putrefaction, which would otherwise very easily spoil. Therefore, since sea-water is likewise salty, the salt also serves to ensure that it cannot spoil.

*How water serves for punishment.*

*How God uses water to oblige men.*

*Mark of the goodness of God.*

*Why unfortunate accidents are to be regarded as divine punishment.*

[p. 363] § 191. When rivers have swollen high, they sometimes sweep away bridges, break through banks and dams, or else pour over their banks and cause great harm by their inundation. The very same is wont to happen when inundations arise from the sea, because the wind holds the same course as the flood, so that the flood becomes higher than the shores, since the wind increases the velocity of the water, so that during the time of the flood it can flow in in greater quantity, and in some places even changes the direction of the water, so that it must flow where it would otherwise not come. But what uncommon harm is done by such inundations the history of floods teaches, and we ourselves have examples in our own days. Now since we can regard the unfortunate accidents that occur in nature as divine punishments and chastisements (§ 37 *Mor.*), namely as punishments for those who do evil, and as chastisements for those who would in future turn aside to evil, we see from this how God can use water also for vengeance against the ungodly, and as a means of obliging men to desist from evil and to do good. But since such harmful inundations are nevertheless rare [p. 364] and the water too swells excessively in the rivers only now and then, one nevertheless sees that God shows more good to men through water than he inflicts misfortune upon them: which is to be regarded as a sign of his goodness (§ 1063 *Met.*), and shows in a proof the superiority of this world over others in which inundations would be more common. But we find in other cases too that the creature is used by God more to promote the good of men than to their harm. And so much the more can one gather from the latter that we may regard the latter as divine punishments without, as some imagine, doing violence to reason (§ 37 *Mor.*). Those who censure such things do indeed fancy themselves strong in reason; but they still have too little reason, since they do not see deeply enough into the connection of truths (§ 368 *Met.*). One may also reckon here what concerns the harm that excessive rain does (§ 168), and that cloudbursts in particular cause (§ 169): for rain for the most part causes the harm in so far as it is water, and cloudbursts have most things in common with inundations, since through them a great quantity of water pours out over land that is otherwise dry, [p. 365] as is wont to happen in inundations (§ 280 *Phys.*). Only, with rain and with cloudbursts there is this further difference, that the water falls heavily from on high and can also cause some harm by this heavy fall, which a mere inundation does not occasion; though this is commonly wont to be the least of it (§ 169).

*How water serves man for recreation.*

*How far such pleasure is to be approved.*

§ 192. There remains yet one benefit of water in which art takes part, namely the amusement of the senses, in particular of the eyes and of the hearing. This delight is caused by fountains and cascades, or waterfalls, of which one finds an account in the Hydraulics, and which delight the eye particularly in gardens, mazes, grottoes and pleasure-groves. The pleasant rushing between the rocks in grottoes and grotto-works, as likewise at the cascades, especially when the water pours down from some height into the *bassin* or basin, gives the ear no less delight than the rushing in natural brooks. And when in the heat of summer one enjoys the shade beneath a waterfall, one at the same time enjoys a pleasant coolness from the water. Here belongs also the delight that one takes on the water, when [p. 366] one sails to and fro upon it for pleasure, or travels from one place to another. No less must we reckon here the pleasure that is made by fireworks on the water.<sup>a</sup> How far one may enjoy such pleasure without acting contrary to the *intention* of God I have shown in another place (§ 471 *Mor.*): namely, all transitory pleasure must be so enjoyed that it does not become ruinous, that is, does not turn to our vexation and aversion, so that we must pay for a small pleasure with a great vexation, which we would never have paid for it had we known it beforehand. Whoever rejects it outright goes too far, and shows that he neither rightly knows the nature of man nor has a right insight into what is right and wrong, what is permissible and impermissible. But I have all the less need to set this out more fully here, since I have already shown elsewhere how far in the commonwealth regard is to be had to this amusement of the senses as well (§ 302, 389 ff. *Pol.*).

<sup>a</sup> Buchner *Artiller. part. 2. f. 26. & seqq.*

[p. 367]

### Das VIII. Capitel. Von der Erde / und des Erdbodens Beschaffenheit. — Chapter VIII. On Soil and the Constitution of the Ground.

*Benefit of garden soil.*

*1. In making plants firm.*

*2. In providing for them.*

*How the rain rises again from the lower soil into the upper.*

*How the wind dries things out.*

*An objection is met.*

*How the soil promotes growth.*

*Benefit of different kinds of soil.*

§ 193. Since good soil, such as one finds in gardens and wherever anything is wont to grow, draws water into itself like a sponge and retains it, and also holds firmly together (§ 363 *Phys.*), it is fitted for plants to be able to grow in it. For on account of its firmness they can take root, so that they stand firm and the winds cannot easily tear them out; as experience testifies not only in that it often costs much trouble before one can tear a plant out with its root, but also in that violent storm-winds very seldom throw down the trees in the forests. But since it is nevertheless loose at the same time, especially when the rain has soaked into it and loosened it, the root can continue to grow within the soil and spread itself out, so that the tree and other plants stand firmer when their load increases with their increasing size. Finally, since it retains the rain within itself and does not let it penetrate deeper [p. 368] (§ 363 *Phys.*), but rather the rain that has penetrated deepest is afterwards drawn up again by the upper soil, the plants too can have their nourishment little by little, and be provided for a considerable time by a single rain. That the rain which has penetrated deep into the soil rises up again when moisture is lacking above, I prove in the following way. The soil dries out above and is still moist below. And it cannot well be otherwise. For the heat either of the sun or of the air dissolves the water into vapours (§ 248 *Phys.*), which are carried away by the air, which is in constant motion. But the heat is greater at the top of the soil than further down, since for this very reason it penetrates down only to a certain depth, because it continually decreases little by little (§ 108 *Pt. II Exper.*). When the wind dries things out, its force likewise does not penetrate into the soil, but remains only in the upper part, which it touches. For the cause why the wind dries is surely none other than this. The air is made lighter by the wind, so that it no longer presses so strongly as before (§ 43 *Pt. II Exper.*). Now since there is much air in water (§ 148 *Pt. I Exper.*), it must expand further, since it is just the same [p. 369] as if one had rarefied the outer air around it (§ 80 *Pt. I Exper.*). In this way the droplets of water become larger than they were before, and for the most part project out of its pores above the surface of the body. Therefore the wind can carry them away with it. But when the soil is dry above, it nevertheless dries out ever deeper. Therefore, since the lower moisture must likewise pass into the air, but cannot get into it otherwise than through the upper, the water too must draw itself upward from below. If one would ask how this comes about, there is surely no other way left than that the lower heat in the soil dissolves it into vapours, which rise in its interstices and finally pass into the air. For even if one would say that the soil is like a sponge, or some other loose body, in which the water rises higher than the body stands in the water, this nevertheless does not apply in the present case. For with such bodies the case is just the same as with subtle capillary tubes. In these the water does indeed rise higher than they are immersed; but when one takes them out of the water, it does not draw itself further upward out of the lower part and leave it empty. But we find yet another particular circumstance which is to be [p. 370] noted: how the soil promotes growth. This happens, namely, in that it does not afford the root superfluous moisture, and also furnishes the moisture beforehand with other nitrous and oily particles before it penetrates into the roots (§ 395 *Phys.*). But what this signifies is shown by the difference between a plant which grows in soil and another of the very same kind which one sets in water and lets grow therein; the latter commonly grows rank and comes near the other neither in smell nor in taste. And it would not be unprofitable if one were to investigate this difference by further experiments set up deliberately to that end: for what has hitherto become known to me rests only on observations which have offered themselves by chance. Now although it is indeed certain that the soil of itself contributes little or nothing to the nourishment of plants (§ 393 *Phys.*), it is nevertheless evident from the present matter that it affords plants a great advantage in their growth, and must therefore be regarded as a most necessary part of it. But finally we have also to note this circumstance, that, since not every plant needs as much moisture and heat for its growth as another, we also find such [p. 371] a difference in soils as is required in order that all kinds of plants may thrive well.

*Benefit of other kinds of soil.*

*1. In building.*

*2. In household goods.*

*Why such things are adduced.*

*3. In medicine.*

§ 194. The benefit of other kinds of soil shows itself in common life, if one pays attention to what is made from them. From brick-earth are moulded the bricks which give us great benefit in building (§ 258, 361 *Arch. civ.*), so much so that even in most ancient times good bricks were preferred to the best stones<sup>a</sup>. In particular, tiles are of great benefit for roofing buildings, since nature can afford us no material that would equal them. Apart from tiles, buildings are roofed either with straw, or with shingles, or with slate, or with metal. Straw and shingles are very dangerous in case of fire, so that for this reason in well-ordered republics one does not willingly allow houses in the villages, let alone in the towns, to be roofed with straw and shingles. Slate is not very durable, and in a great conflagration becomes red-hot, cracks, flies off through the air and sets things alight, as much as shingles ever may, as sad experience has more than [p. 372] once testified. Copper, it is true, is very convenient for roofing and gives durable roofs; but it is too costly, and therefore seldom finds use. Iron, even if it is tinned, rusts easily in the wet and is eaten away by the rust, especially where the water remains standing and cannot run off at once. Rain-water likewise, and especially snow-water, eats into lead when it stands on it, so that it becomes quite porous like a sponge and can hold water no longer, as those have learned to their harm who have covered balconies with leaden plates. Other stones which can be cut into slabs are too heavy for roofing. And accordingly there is no more convenient material with which one could roof houses than tiles. One also uses loam in buildings, which is convenient not only for partition walls but also for ceilings, even when the walls are built of brick or stone: not to mention that it also serves for entire regular buildings that are erected of timber. It is likewise known that one uses it for stoves, baking-ovens, threshing- floors in barns and other things. From clay one prepares all kinds of vessels which we, in the preparation of food and for its keeping, and also for other use in the house, [p. 373] need: about which, however, as things known to everyone, it is not necessary to adduce more. Meanwhile, since we intend to consider the wealth of nature which stands at our service and by which we promote the convenience of life, we must also at the same time direct our thoughts to that which is known, and indeed all the more, the less we are accustomed to pay attention to what hovers daily before our eyes, and the less we reflect on the advantage which we have to enjoy daily, because we let it pass as a thing that must be so. But if we wish to know all the goodness of God which he shows us through the creature that he has created for our good, then we must also pay attention to the commonest things that we enjoy daily (§ 21), and that all the more willingly, because we thereby receive the more occasion to think of the goodness of God, since the beginning of our thoughts about it must always spring from what falls into the senses (§ 846 *Met.*). The benefit which some particular kinds of soil have in various diseases is described in medicine, and cannot well be set forth here, unless we have grounds enough to explain it.

<sup>a</sup> *Vitruvius lib. 2. c. 8. f. m. 29.*

*Benefit of the soil's alternating in layers.*

*Why chance events are a work of the wisdom of God.*

*Proof of the goodness of the Meta-*

*physics of the Author.*

[p. 374] § 195. If one and the same kind of soil were found throughout in one place, one would have to do without various benefits that one has from soil, or at least obtain at some cost what one did not have in one's own place. Now since we find that the soil in one place alternates in layers, and in this way all sorts of kinds of soil are to be met with together (§ 364 *Phys.*), one may also everywhere enjoy the benefit that can be had from soil. It is indeed true that this alternation of the strata is to be ascribed to inundations (§ *cit.*), and thus seems to be an effect of nature by chance; but since nothing happens in nature which God had not previously considered and decreed should come to pass (§ 999 *Met.*), nothing is by chance with respect to him; and, since one must reckon every benefit of natural things among the divine purposes (§ 1029 *Met.*), one must also regard as a work of his wisdom whatever arises from inundations and, in general, from chance events (§ 1036 *Met.*). And this is to be noted above all measure, so that one does not grant less room to the wisdom and goodness of God than is fitting. From this, however, one recognises the goodness of my metaphysical grounds, since through them, on every occasion in nature, [p. 375] the perfection of God is exalted, as ought rightly to happen by virtue of the principal purpose which God had in the world (§ 8). If as much time and diligence were applied to understanding my writings as some apply to slandering them, one would be amply convinced of this. It is enough, however, that there are more who see it than who cannot, or will not, see it. Let him who will be blind be blind, by all means.

*Benefit of the unevenness of the earth for the rivers.*

*How rain increases the rivers.*

*How the water in rivers becomes turbid.*

*How it clears again.*

*Another cause why the water becomes turbid.*

*Third cause.*

§ 196. If the earth had the perfect figure of a sphere, all points on its upper surface would be equally far from the centre of the earth (§ 27 *Geom.*). And thus it would not be possible for the water in the rivers to flow on (§ 346 *Phys.*). Indeed, if heavy rain came, the water could not even run off at all, but would remain standing on the fields, pastures and meadows and in the forests: which would be very disadvantageous to the plants, and would cause much trouble if one had to draw it off by art, a trouble from which we are at present spared. The rivers are maintained by the springs (§ 340 *Phys.*). It is known, however, and the origin of springs also shows it, that they cannot arise and run on anywhere except where there are mountains [p. 376] (§ 342 ff. *Phys.*). And thus there is a new benefit that the mountains have, namely that they give the springs their origin and thereby supply the rivers with water. Besides this, the rain-water too, and, when it thaws in spring, the snow-water runs from the mountains and other elevated places into the rivers, and in many places the mountains prevent it from pouring out over the level land to the disadvantage of agriculture and of the growth of the meadows. Hence we see that in continuous rain the rivers rise, and most of all when it thaws. From the mere rain that falls into the rivers, and the mere snow that lies on the ice, they would not rise so much. And whoever has not been in such places where the mountains lead the water into the rivers, so that we can see it with our eyes, can infer it from the fact that in summer the water in the rivers is very turbid when it rises in continuous rain, because it sweeps away with it the dust from the soil which has collected in dry weather, and also, by its swift motion, washes away soil here and there from the mountains themselves, where it rushes down. When the snow melts at the end of winter, the soil is hard frozen, and the water flows over [p. 377] the ice and away. Therefore it is no wonder if at such a time it remains clear and bright. But just as the soil differs in different places, especially in mountains, so the turbid water also has a different colour, according as it has dissolved this or another kind of soil. And whoever pays attention to what colour the turbid water has, and what kind of soil settles in it, can also find from this from what mountains the water has run together, if the regions whence the river comes are exactly known to him. The further the water flows, the clearer it becomes, because the soil which makes it turbid gradually settles, and thereby the bed of the river is gradually raised: which can give occasion for it to spread further in breadth and sometimes, in time, even to leave its old place altogether. For although this does not amount to much in a short time, yet in many years it can become noticeable. Besides this, one must also consider here that the water becomes turbid not only because it rushes down from mountains and pours through hollow ways into the rivers, and also runs here and there from other elevated places into brooks and rivers, but also because by its force it washes the soil out of the banks here and there, and even [p. 378] tears them away in some places: which I mention so that one may not be surprised if the turbid water at one place in the river does not always have the same colour. To this, however, this too may contribute something, that the heavy rain which washes away the soil and makes the river turbid does not always pour down at one place in the mountains or on certain elevated places, whereas it is known that different soil is to be met with at different places.

*Mountains serve against inundations.*

§ 197. Now although inundations are often caused by the rain which runs down from mountains and other elevated places pouring itself into rivers, whereby, according to the constitution of the regions and of the season, no small harm is sometimes done to the fruits of the field, to men and to cattle; nevertheless the mountains also serve against many inundations which would occur if they were not present. This advantage they provide chiefly by the sea, where the water is driven by violent storm-winds, so that, especially at the time of the flood, when the water moves towards the shores (§ 355 *Phys.*), it would easily overtop or break through the shores and cause an inundation.

*Mountains break the force of the winds.*

*How experience confirms it.*

[p. 379] § 198. But just as the mountains withstand the force of the raging sea, so they have a like benefit with respect to the winds, whose force they break. The force of the wind comes from the velocity with which it moves (§ 215 *Phys.*). Now where there are mountains against which the wind strikes, there it cannot proceed further, but is *reflected*, so that it moves back again. The surface of the mountain is not perpendicular but oblique. Hence the wind does not strike against it with its whole force (§ 396 *Mech. Lat.*) and is therefore also not so strongly *reflected*. And in this way the wind is weakened by the *reflection*. To this is further added that, on account of the unevenness of the mountains, not all the wind is *reflected*, but a part is lost. It also happens, since the wind blows in gusts, that the *reflected* wind blows against the other, and thereby the force of the wind is broken. Whoever is not sufficiently practised in the doctrine of motion to be able to grasp all this distinctly may also gather it from experience, if he has opportunity to stay near mountains: for he will find that the wind up on the mountain, where it has freedom, is much stronger than below in the valley, where it strikes against the mountains. [p. 380] But one can also learn it in other places where there are no mountains. For in the first place it is known that the wind outside the gate in the open field is always stronger than in towns, especially when there are large and high buildings in them. Here one can assign no other cause than that on the open, level field it has freedom to move, whereas in the town it strikes here and there against the buildings. But it is all one whether it strikes against houses or against mountains: for both stand in the way of its motion and hinder it from sweeping on freely, so that it must be *reflected*. Next, we notice the difference quite distinctly at the top of a high tower, where the wind has its freedom, just as on the mountains. Now since the mountain holds the wind back, so that it cannot proceed in one course, it cannot extend itself so far as would otherwise happen if it had freedom to continue its course unhindered.

*How mountains can excite a wind, or also strengthen it.*

*Remark.*

§ 199. Meanwhile it can also be understood that in some cases the wind is excited and strengthened by mountains, although from what I shall presently say it is quite readily understood that this seldom happens. When mountains lie on both sides and run on for a considerable [p. 381] distance, while between them there remains a plain that is not too broad, the wind can thereby be strengthened, if it keeps the same direction as the valley lying between them. For in this case the wind drives much air together into the narrow valley, which cannot all get through at once. Now since the air is thereby compressed and becomes denser than it was before (§ 122 *Pt. I Exper.*), its expanding force must also increase (§ 123 *Pt. I Exper.*), and in this way it must spread out with all the greater velocity at the end of the mountains: which increases the wind (§ 75 *Pt. II Exper.*). Indeed, since it is warmer in a valley between mountains than in open places, and heat increases the expanding force of the air (§ 133 *Pt. I Exper.*), this too can contribute something to increasing the force of the wind. How much these causes amount to would have to be settled by mathematical calculations together with experiments and experiences made for the purpose.

*Mountains increase the variety of things: 1. On account of the*

*herbs.*

*2. Of the vine.*

*3. Of fruit.*

*4. Of the metals.*

*5. Of the minerals.*

*How one is made more certain of this.*

§ 200. We also find that herbs and trees grow on mountains and in elevated places which do not thrive so well in others, namely those which love a dry and warm soil. And accordingly, through the mountains, our earth has been put in a position [p. 382] to bring forth more kinds of herbs and plants than could otherwise well have been done. In particular we find that the vine is best produced on the mountains: partly because in too great wetness it easily overgrows itself and bears only sour grapes, but keeps no force left over for the fruit; partly because for its growth it requires heat, which it can best have on the mountains, especially where the ground is stony as well, so that the heat is not only increased by the reflection but also maintained by the stones, since the sun can shine upon the mountains longer than upon the flat country, and its rays are also more powerful upon them, because they do not fall so obliquely upon them as upon the level country; indeed, nothing stands in the way there which by its shadow might hinder the sun from shining upon it. So also, for this reason, the finest fruits can be produced on the mountains, and it is therefore also customary for vineyards to be furnished with fruit-bearing trees. The metals, which yield so great a benefit to the human race, as we shall soon hear at greater length, must be fetched out of the fissures of the mountains, and these are the great storehouses which supply us with them abundantly. Besides this, the mountains also afford us [p. 383] many minerals, and with them stones in a quantity such as we meet with nowhere else so abundantly. And all this shows sufficiently that by the help of the mountains both the variety and the number of natural things are increased. Whoever wishes to be more fully convinced of this need only consult the herbals and those who have written on mining matters, or who have also described the history of nature in the parts belonging here; then the quantity and variety of what the mountains afford will become more comprehensible to him. And accordingly one sees here a benefit of natural history which one cannot obtain from natural science. Therefore it is also fitting, with this purpose in view, that one connect history with science.

*Mountains make regions agreeable.*

§ 201. Among the benefits of the mountains we may also rightly reckon this, that through them regions become agreeable, because the eye is thereby feasted. Those who dwell in an elevated place have freedom to look about them, and the difference of mountain and valley which they survey makes an agreeable prospect for them. We also find that when green mountains, or else mountains furnished with forests, lie round about us, [p. 384] the eye again obtains an agreeable prospect. Indeed, at times even the bare mountains contribute to it, when, at the sight of other things which strike the eye along with them, they make an agreeable variation. It is indeed true that those who are constantly in such regions do not perceive this so much as another who comes there from some other place: but it is so, and not otherwise, with all things that delight the senses. For if one is to know a thing, one must notice its difference from others. This one perceives more easily when one can set it against others of its kind than when one looks at it alone.

*How the mountains moderate and increase the heat.*

*Cause of the moderation of the heat.*

*Cause of the increase of the cold.*

*First cause of the increase of heat.*

*Second cause.*

*Third cause.*

*Fourth cause.*

*How experience confirms everything.*

*Cause of the heat in summer towards the north.*

§ 202. On the mountains it is always colder than on the level country (§ 282 *Phys.*), and this is also the cause why there the fruits of the field, as well as those of gardens and trees, ripen more slowly, unless one makes good the deficiency of nature by art. Accordingly, when winds blow over the mountains, they are cool (§ 220 *Phys.*) and moderate the heat in the place to which they blow (§ 214 *Phys.*). And in this way the mountains serve to moderate the heat. Indeed, since in winter the mountains are very cold, and consequently cold winds blow from them [p. 385] (§ 220 *Phys.*), they must actually increase the cold: as we also find it to be in accordance with experience. Notwithstanding this, they nevertheless also contribute much to the increase of heat. For the mountains reflect the sun's rays back into the valley and onto the level country, especially when the sun stands high and shines at its warmest, so that the reflected rays strike the soil in the valley. But by this the heat is increased. Since the sun lies upon the mountains which face the south the whole day, or at least for the greatest part of the time in which it shines warmly, they too become very hot from it, especially when they are sandy and stony, since it is well known that sand and stone become much warmer than soil. Therefore the air on the mountains becomes very warm, and since it is thereby further expanded (§ 113 *Pt. I Exper.*), the heat also penetrates into the other air in the valley. And in this way the heat is again increased. Besides this, it is clear of itself that the air cannot pass through the valley so freely as over the level field, where everything is open, and whoever desires the cause of this can easily find it. The motion of the air proceeds from the wind (§ 205 *Phys.*). But the mountains hold back many winds, so that they cannot come into the valley, because they [p. 386] stand in their way. But it seldom happens that the wind blows exactly against the valley, or that the valley runs straight on, so that the wind should not strike against the mountains everywhere. Air that is calm becomes uncommonly warmer than that which is moved by winds (§ 103). Therefore the mountains serve in this respect too for the increase of heat. And to this is added further that the heat cannot be driven away by the winds, nor has freedom to spread, and therefore lasts longer: which again counts for not a little in the increase of the heat of the sun (§ 130 *Phys.*). Experience confirms all this which has been established by various grounds, and this is precisely the cause why in the valley everything grows and ripens more quickly than on the mountain, and why it is very fertile in warm valleys. Indeed, by this means, that the heat is greatly increased by the mountains, especially by sandy and stony ones, it also becomes warmer in the lands lying towards the north, during their long days, than we have it in our own lands, and therefore what requires more time with us can grow and ripen there in a short time.

*How the constitution of lands arises from the mountains.*

§ 203. Since the mountains can, under certain circumstances, increase heat and cold [p. 387] and diminish them (§ 202), they contribute not a little to the weather in the lands where they lie (§ 226 ff. *Phys.*), and alter the properties of the winds which these would otherwise have (§ 216 ff. *Phys.*). According as the kinds of weather in a land are constituted, so it is also fertile or barren, which we will here assume as a matter grounded in experience, although we have in part already set it out above (§ 114). And this is the cause why at times in one land there is a failure of crops in a thing which has prospered well in another, indeed why in some places certain kinds of plants do not thrive at all which grow well in others. For example, early fruits seldom prosper in such places where late night-frosts can come. Likewise the weather contributes not a little to the health of man, and the constitution of the winds can very greatly *alter* the body of man: which is best known to the *physicians*, who are accustomed to pay exact attention to all accidents. In elevated places, which however do not lie all too high, so that they are not *incommoded* by excessive cold, but which on the other hand have level country around them, the air can pass through freely, and all evaporations [p. 388] are carried away. Hence one has there a pure and healthy air. On the other hand, in valleys where the ground is wet, and which are narrow, so that the wind cannot well pass through, the air remains damp and becomes unhealthy. In order that one might better recognise the difference of lands which arises from the mountains, it would be very serviceable if one noted the actual constitution of the kinds of weather in various lands and, by comparison of them, the difference.

*Whether the mountains hold the earth together.*

§ 204. The learned Jesuit **Kircher**<sup>a</sup> has besides a quite particular opinion of the benefit of mountains, for which he took occasion from geography. He is of the thought that the mountains are to be regarded like the bones in the body of man, which give the earth its firmness and hold it together, so that it does not fall apart. He is led to these thoughts in that he represents to himself from geography how the mountains extend in a line from one place to another, which for brevity's sake I will not set out. But since the benefit which he ascribes to them on this account does not yet distinctly appear from this, I find [p. 389] as yet no sufficient ground before me why I could agree with him in this respect. Moreover it has not yet been proved whether the mountains have always been so, or whether under another constitution too they had a similar situation on the surface of the earth (§ 365 *Phys.*). We therefore leave this point set aside for further investigation. Meanwhile it seems to me that one could show a similar benefit of the mountains, and indeed with greater certainty than **Kircher**, and therefore I consider it necessary to adduce something further about it.

<sup>a</sup> *in Mundo subterraneo lib. 2. c. 10. §. 1. f. m. 70.*

*Benefit of the mountains for the level balance of the earth.*

*What is to be investigated further here.*

§ 205. The earth moves about its axis within 24 hours (§ 175 *Phys.*), and indeed with unchanged velocity: which latter one can infer from the fact that one perceives nothing at all of its motion. For if a noticeable change were to occur in its velocity, one would perceive it. One can experience this if one travels in a ship and shuts one's eyes. So long as the ship goes on with equal velocity, one cannot notice that one is moved. On the other hand, if it begins to move faster or more slowly, one perceives it at once. Now if the earth is to move about its axis with equal velocity, then the parts [p. 390] on the one side must either constantly outweigh those on the other in one and the same way, if the motion were to arise from the inequality of weight, which, however, does not well hold here, unless we wished to derive the motion about the axis from its gravity towards the sun; or they must always remain in level balance on both sides, so that it allows itself to be turned evenly by the external force. Now since the earth does not consist of one and the same matter, and also has great caves in some places, the equilibrium of it all round can also best be maintained by the mountains. Yet this benefit too deserves further investigation, by considering more exactly the turning of spheres in which gravity counts for nothing, that is, where the sphere does not merely overturn by its gravity after it has been set in motion, and moreover attending to what the matter of a mountain signifies in this respect with regard to the whole terrestrial globe. Therefore we can put forward this benefit as nothing more than a conjecture, which gives occasion to further investigations.

[p. 391]

### Das IX. Capitel. Von dem Nutzen des Feuers und der Wärme. — Chapter IX. On the Benefit of Fire and Heat.

*Why fire and heat are treated together.*

§ 206. Fire and heat differ from one another only in degree, in such a way that one may regard fire as a *concentrated* heat, and heat as a spread- out and rarefied fire (§ 128 *Pt. II Exper.*): just as experience accordingly also teaches that the effects of fire and of heat differ from one another merely in degree. Therefore it is fitting that we treat the benefit of heat and of fire together. But it is also necessary that we treat of them in particular, because we know that in nature, for the sake of heat and fire and on account of their effects, there exists a particular matter, which is distributed everywhere in bodies and passes from one into another (§ 104 *Pt. II Exper.*).

*Heat is necessary to the life of man.*

§ 207. Without heat men and animals cannot live, just as both also freeze to death in great cold. Life consists in the motion of the blood, and with it life ceases (§ [p. 392] 455 *Phys.*). The blood coagulates and loses its fluidity from the cold, since we see that blood which has been let from the veins congeals as soon as it grows cold. When the blood is no longer fluid, it can also no longer move in the veins and arteries. Therefore heat is necessary to men and to animals to keep their blood fluid, and thus in the state in which it can be moved. Hence we also seek to maintain heat by art where it is lacking by nature.

*Is maintained by fire.*

*Magnitude of the vital heat.*

*How the heat of summer warms.*

*How strongly a chamber is to be heated.*

*How the right degree is determined in this.*

*How the heat in the body increases.*

*When the body takes on heat from outside.*

*Which heat one soon loses.*

*External heat does not penetrate deep into the blood.*

*Why the loss of heat need not be replaced from out-*

*side.*

*How one grows warm in a cold bed.*

§ 208. And herein appears the first benefit of fire, that by it we maintain the necessary vital heat in cold weather. The inner parts of the body are always warmer than the outer air, even in summer, provided we are not standing in the sun. This can be proved without prolixity by means of weather- glasses. For on the hottest summer days one can, in the hand or even in the mouth, drive the *spiritus vini* in the weather-glass higher than it stands when the weather-glass hangs in the shade, although the sun shines into the room, indeed lies constantly upon the windows until the afternoon, and the chamber is moreover shut up, in which case it must become very warm. On the other hand [p. 393] one can nevertheless never drive it so high by the heat of the body that the glass bursts, as happens when one places it in the sun. Now it is known, however, that greater heat drives the *spiritus* higher than lesser, and that the heat from which the weather-glass actually bursts must be the greatest (§ 59 *Pt. II Exper.*). Now since the body has more heat than the air in summer, which is warmed by the sun, the latter cannot give the body any heat either, but merely prevents too much of its heat from escaping from it (§ 76 *Phys.*). Therefore, if fire is to take the place of the sun, it too must warm the air in the chamber as much as is necessary so that not so much heat escapes from the body that the loss of it becomes noticeable. This can be determined quite easily with the help of the weather-glass, and it could therefore be of great benefit if one were willing to use it for this purpose. This much is certain, that great heat is harmful to the body, because we then *transpire* too strongly, and therefore more goes off than should: hence we also feel a lassitude and loss of strength when we have been too hot. On the other hand it is no less known from experience that cold becomes burdensome to us when [p. 394] one has too little heat. Whoever, therefore, wished to moderate the artificial heat in his chamber in such a way that the air served merely for the preservation of the necessary vital heat, but increased it to the disadvantage of the body as little as it allowed it to be diminished through, say, too great a loss, would have to observe in summer when the air is so constituted that he feels neither cold nor heat, even when he pays attention to it, and at the same time note the degree of heat by the weather-glass. For if in winter, or when it grows cool, he again warms the air in the chamber so much that the *spiritus* in the weather-glass reaches the same degree and remains standing there, he has in like manner this degree of heat, and can be certain that neither cold nor heat will bring any disadvantage upon his body. It is indeed true that, when one has been out in the cold in winter-time, one's heat escapes, and one accordingly feels a loss of the necessary vital heat, which shows itself in one's feeling cold. But it is not necessary to replace this loss by art. It is enough if we check the further loss; then nature herself afterwards replaces it little by little. Both happen if one merely comes into a chamber of moderate [p. 395] heat. For then no more heat goes out of the body, and in the body new heat is continually produced by the motion of the blood. The first is clear from the general doctrines of heat (§ 76 *Phys.*); the second one learns from the fact that the heat increases with the velocity of the motion of the blood. For example, when a man becomes very angry, the blood moves faster than before, as one can gather from the violence of the pulse. But then he also grows warm, and many even begin to sweat. Meanwhile it is indeed also possible that heat is communicated to the chilled part of the body and the loss thereby replaced; but this heat one either loses again at once, if one is in the cold, or the heat soon becomes unbearable, if one is in air so warm that the loss may be replaced by it. That the chilled parts of the body can be warmed by external heat can best be seen when one takes hold of a warm body, e.g. a warm stone, with cold hands: for the stone becomes cold, and the hands, on the contrary, become warm, as must happen when heat is communicated to one body from another (§ 76 *Phys.*). Indeed, one feels [p. 396] it so too when one has grown cold in winter and steps up to a fire: but the former experience shows the matter still more clearly than this one. Now if one is in cold air, the heat at once goes off into it again, and indeed all the faster the colder that air is (§ 79 *Phys.*); and for this reason one does not long enjoy that heat. And although the blood circulates round the body (§ 415 *Phys.*), yet little of this heat comes into the other parts of the body which are in the cold air. For those who sit by a fire in the chimney-place, where the air in the room is cold, are cold at the feet and in the back while they can scarcely bear the heat of the fire on their face. On the other hand, if one is in warm air, the heat of the body, whether it has come into it from outside or has a cause within, does not go away so immediately (§ 76 *Phys.*). Now since it lasts for a while, so much inner heat can meanwhile, before it entirely passes away, have collected again that one can no longer feel the loss of the heat received from outside. And from this it is evident that one need not take care how the loss of heat in cold air is replaced again from outside; rather, it is enough if one comes into such air where [p. 397] the further loss of the inner heat is checked, and the inner heat cannot fall into such a loss as one perceives: for the inner heat, which is excited by the motion of the blood, collects again little by little, so that one no longer feels any cold, which consists in a mere loss of heat. And from this one sees the cause why one grows warm in a cold bed, even though one is quite frozen when one lies down in it. The bed, which is cold, can give us no heat: rather, we must warm it if it is to become warm. But it prevents the heat from passing into the air, since it holds it back and does not let it through. Now since, after one has lain down, more and more heat is continually produced in the body by the motion of the blood, this heat first passes into the bed and makes it warm (§ 76 *Phys.*): when this has become warm and does not let the heat through, the heat in the body and under the bedding must continually increase.

*Similar benefit of fire in the preservation of the vital*

*heat of plants.*

*Why plants freeze.*

*How this is prevented.*

*How transpiration is to be promoted in greenhouses.*

*How to renew the air in rooms.*

*How the degree of heat in greenhouses is determined.*

§ 209. Trees, plants and growing things, by whatever further names they may be called, likewise need a certain degree of heat, without which their life cannot be preserved. For we find in experience that in the cold [p. 398] plants and trees, as well as men and animals, freeze, although one can always endure more cold than another. The cause why trees freeze is no other than this, that the sap freezes and, as it expands, bursts the sap-vessels (§ 121 *Pt. II Exper.*), as microscopes plainly show when one brings the frozen wood under them<sup>a</sup>. The sap freezes when too much heat escapes from it (§ 119 *Pt. II Exper.*), and the sap-vessels are easily burst when they are either soft and tender in themselves or else very full of sap. And from these grounds one may judge why some plants and trees freeze more easily than others, and why they freeze more easily at one time than at another. Accordingly, if a tree or plant is not to freeze, its vital heat must be preserved in the cold by artificial heat, as I explained earlier concerning men. And for this end, for foreign plants which cannot endure the winter in our cold air, one uses greenhouses, in which by means of fire one maintains a proportionate [p. 399] heat. If one even wishes that trees and plants should thrive at that time as well, and not stand still in their growth, then the degree of heat must be driven so high by increasing the fire that it equals the heat of the sun at which the plant enjoys good growth. But since plants also *transpire* strongly in such heat (§ 394 *Phys.*), and the air, thereby made damp, also when it stands still long enclosed, and especially since it itself suffers all manner of changes through the change of heat (§ 133 *Pt. I Exper.*), easily grows foul, or at least the plants can no longer *transpire* as well as in pure air, because the air takes up only a certain quantity of perspiration; so it is serviceable to arrange the greenhouse so that the air can circulate through pipes. Namely, the air must come into the chamber through pipes which pass through the fire, and through others drive out the old air. Such a greenhouse, or rather a stove to heat it, was devised by a certain Englishman,<sup>b</sup> John Evelyn, and from some of his works printed together, which treat of the cultivation of forests [p. 400] and gardens, it has been described in the *Acta Eruditorum*<sup>c</sup>. And it is well known that this renewal of the air is *recommended* for other reasons as well, also in the heating of dwelling-chambers for men (§ 390 *Archit. civ.*), and that it has already been introduced by some with good advantage. But since it is also necessary that the heat in a greenhouse is not driven higher than the growth of the plants requires, one can, as before (§ 208), *determine* the degree of heat by a weather-glass, just as the aforementioned Englishman, **Evelyn**, already *recommends* the weather-glass for this end. Mr **Leutmann**, who has applied the spare hours left free to him by the duties of his office to the advancement of the mathematical and physical arts with laudable diligence, and who, on account of the laudable diligence he has shown in the mathematical and physical arts, has obtained a place as *Professor Mechanicæ practicæ* at the Imperial Academy of Sciences at St Petersburg, has likewise<sup>d</sup> described a stove which can be used in greenhouses and glass frames, and in which he [p. 401] has had regard not only to the useful renewal of the air but at the same time also to the necessary safety from fire at night-time.

<sup>a</sup> *Vid. Dissertatio de hieme A. 1709. sect. 2. §. 25. p. 26.*

<sup>b</sup> *A discourse of forest-trees &c.*

<sup>c</sup> *A. 1707. p. 496.*

<sup>d</sup> *in Vulcano famulante c. 16. p. 93. & seq.*

*Benefit of fire in promoting rapid and early growth.*

*Proofs of early growth.*

*Proofs of rapid growth.*

*What gave occasion to glass*

*frames.*

*How one may carry it further by experiments.*

*Very rapid growth.*

*How lettuce is produced in 2 hours.*

*Why such a thing is possible.*

*What doubt arises about it.*

§ 210. Since the heat of fire can be used instead of the heat of the sun for the growth of plants (§ 209), it is also possible to force plants in greenhouses and glass frames at a time when they could not otherwise grow on account of the season. And gardeners know how to make use of this means when they wish to have flowers, fruits and garden plants in winter, or at least early in spring, when nature alone cannot yet grant them, or also to bring to ripeness such plants as do not otherwise attain it in our region. In particular the celebrated *Professor Physices* at Leipzig, Dr **Lehmann**, has made it his concern to bring forth, by the right use of fire, in every season and even in winter itself, all the kinds of flowers that one has in spring and by which this season is made more agreeable than the others, and has given sound proofs of it. As regards rapid growth, there is likewise already one proof or another on which one can rely, although here and there various things are still put forward, [p. 402] the correctness of which is still open to some doubt, so long as it has not been established by sufficient proof. Whoever is not inexperienced in garden matters knows that, when gardeners wish to have some garden produce earlier than nature grants it, especially when adverse weather occurs, they commit the seed to the earth in greenhouses and glass frames and drive the plant on by artificial heat, so long as the sun does not yet give heat enough; but afterwards transplant it into the open air in the garden as soon as the sun shines warmly enough for it to be able to grow on further. For since in this way it has a considerable start over other plants of the same kind which are raised merely in the garden, it is no wonder if it also comes much sooner to full growth and bears its fruit before the others. Common experience teaches that, when we have many warm days one after another, everything grows much more quickly than at other times: as I have indeed already remarked above that the increase of heat is the means by which nature, in the lands lying towards the north, brings to ripeness in a short time what with us requires more time for it. And such common experiences have given occasion to glass frames, [p. 403] and likewise to hotbeds and other inventions by which the heat is increased and growth is hastened. This anyone easily comprehends who has insight into the true constitution of inferences of reason (§ 333 ff. *Met.*) and their use in invention (§ 363 *Met.*). But how far nature may be compelled by heat has not yet been settled by reliable experiments. Now if one wished to undertake such experiments, it would chiefly be serviceable to try it with one and the same kind of plant in different degrees of heat, each of which would be constantly maintained by fire whenever the sun was not sufficient. Should one fear that the heat would be greater than a common weather-glass could bear, one need only use a weather-glass of quicksilver, which can bear a greater heat without harm than a common one that has been filled with *spiritus vini*. In the common books of arts and secrets, of which there are several in our language, one finds a process by which one can make a lettuce, or another herb of that sort which grows quickly, grow within 2 hours. I remember having read the like in a book which, under the title *Newly Opened Gate of the Arts*, at Nuremberg [p. 404] came out. The celebrated *Bayle*<sup>a</sup> relates that Mr Wilde in England had some persons as his guests and, when they were about to sit down to table, sowed lettuce in a soil prepared in a particular manner. Before two hours had passed, the lettuce, with its root, had already grown a thumb's length. I remember that an old *student* once told me he had seen such an experiment at Greifswald, in which the lettuce grew so swiftly from the seed that had been committed to the earth that one could almost see the motion. *De La Brosse*, a skilful *botanist* at Paris, describes a like experiment<sup>b</sup>, and indeed in detail how he claims to have performed it, without keeping back the tricks of the art; and this agrees fairly well with what I had previously read in the book of arts and secrets cited above. He repeatedly watered ashes of tree-moss and dung with liquid manure and each time let them dry again in the sun, until a rich and loose soil was formed from them. After he had moistened this soil in a vessel, [p. 405] he set it over a coal fire and gave it such a heat as is usually found on hot summer days in *July*. Into it he sowed lettuce seed and purslane which had been steeped for 24 hours in liquid manure, and, whenever the soil was about to become dry, watered it with lukewarm rain-water. In this way he claims to have produced in two hours a lettuce that was fit to eat. I do not indeed see how one can reject the testimony of those whom I have cited as simply unfit, especially since here everything that is required for the growth of plants (§ 405 ff. *Phys.*) is to be met with in superabundant measure, which must certainly be so where one wishes to hasten growth; yet I still have this doubt about it, how in so short a time the preparation of the nutritive juice (§ 399 *Phys.*), and in particular the *transpiration* (§ 394 *Phys.*), could have taken place, which is necessary in order that the plant may acquire its proper firmness. The latter seems to me all the more questionable because it is known from common experience that whatever grows in a damp cellar, where, precisely because of the dampness of the air, the plant cannot *transpire* sufficiently, overgrows itself in length, or at the same time remains all too watery [p. 406] and acquires no proper firmness, so that it even spoils in the open air and cannot bear it. If the experiment succeeds as it is reported, I nevertheless suppose that close inspection would still show various things in which such a forced plant differs from another of its kind; and least of all does it seem probable to me that the plant can be driven on so quickly to its perfection that it blossoms and bears seed. And for this reason I have chosen to speak of this benefit of fire and heat still somewhat doubtfully.

<sup>a</sup> Republiq. des lettres T. I. A. 1685. p. 319.

<sup>b</sup> De la Nature & vertu des plantes lib. 1. c. 18. p. 128. & seqq.

*Benefit of heat in the generation of fowls.*

*An oven that hatches fowls.*

*Whether the hen contributes anything more than heat in hatching*

*The doubt is removed*

*Why it is not credible that the hen contributes more than heat.*

§ 211. Birds and all poultry hatch their eggs by mere heat (§ 444 *Phys.*), and for this very reason one can also put in place of the natural heat of a bird another heat maintained by fire: as it is indeed also related that the fowls in Egypt are hatched by means of an oven. This gave Mr **Leutmann**<sup>a</sup> occasion to think of an oven in which young fowls can be hatched; and, since he is best able to make the experiment with his oven himself, he will perhaps also yet make a trial of it, so that that truth may be [p. 407] confirmed which is made evident by the experiment cited elsewhere (§ 444 *Phys.*), that the hen in hatching imparts nothing further to the egg than heat; especially since he now has time and opportunity for such work (§ 209), inasmuch as the great Monarch of the Russian Empire not only pays the members of the Academy most handsomely, but also furnishes the costs of all experiments, in order to promote the advancement of the sciences and arts most vigorously. Mr **Leutmann** has indeed very well observed that the heat of the hen, like all heat that is given off from living animals, is mixed with moisture, and to that end he also indicates how one may make the heat in the hatching-oven moist. But **Lange's** experiment mentioned before, unless some circumstances of it have been held back, makes it almost clear that this is in vain. Therefore one ought to investigate whether perhaps, besides heat, something of the matter which *transpires* from the hen also penetrates through the pores of the eggshell: which in my opinion could most conveniently be done if one weighed the eggs when putting them under the hen and recorded the weight of each, but during the brooding took them away little by [p. 408] little and weighed them, to see whether perhaps their weight had noticeably increased. For if nothing of any other matter entered into the egg, the concern to make the heat moist might well be for the most part in vain. Should one wish to maintain that even a little matter, which one could not detect in weighing, could nevertheless contribute something in hatching to the formation of the fruit, then one would have to have some eggs hatched by art and others by a hen, and after this observe whether some difference would be found among the young fowls which could be explained from this. But should something of what *transpires* from the hen enter into the egg and give the young fruit some force, I nevertheless do not hold that this could be replaced by mere moisture with an artificial heat, since it is known that what *transpires* from a living body is no mere moisture. Meanwhile it does not seem credible to me that anything more than heat is necessary for hatching, even if something more should also penetrate into the egg; for we see daily in vermin, for example in caterpillars and spiders, and indeed in general in fish and other creatures that live in the water, that they do not themselves hatch their eggs, [p. 409] but rather they are hatched merely by the heat of the sun. That here too, however, neither the sun nor perhaps the air furnishes anything else for it than heat, one can see from this, that caterpillars creep out of their eggs even when one brings them in cold weather into a warm room, and uses for hatching the mere heat excited by fire.

<sup>a</sup> in Vulcano famulante c. 32. p. 127.

*Benefit of fire in the household economy.*

§ 212. Besides our using fire in cold weather to heat our rooms, it is also of manifold benefit in the household economy, both in the preparation of food and of drink, and also in cleaning the vessels and other things that we use daily. Of this, however, as of things well known, there is no need to speak, although one must point it out to him who wishes to weigh the benefit of natural things (§ 194). Indeed we also use fire in the evening and at night, when the sun is not with us, to make it light: for in some places one burns pine-wood, as is customary in Poland and in neighbouring places, and candles and lamps give light by their flame. Since the sun is an actual fire (§ 112 *Phys.*), our candles and fires make it light in just the same way as the sun (§ 121 *Phys.*).

*Also in the arts.*

*What benefit is to be expected here.*

[p. 410] § 213. With the benefit of fire in the household economy there agrees in some measure the benefit which fire has in the arts. Here belongs what one sees in mines in the smelting of metals; in chemistry in its processes; in the workshops of artists and craftsmen in the preparation of their works: wherein a natural philosopher finds much to consider, if he is to investigate the causes of all the various effects of fire which show themselves there. But one also finds here much concerning the degrees of heat that can be obtained by fire, if one knows how to handle it rightly; and what has hitherto been written on the art of saving wood, namely how one is to obtain a great degree of heat with little wood, has probably for the most part taken its origin from this source. And I do not doubt in the least that still more would come of it in this respect, if one investigated all these uses of fire more circumstantially. Indeed one would thereby also learn to know the properties of fire more exactly, and obtain occasion for experiments to represent them clearly, just as we have hitherto made the properties of the air comprehensible by manifold experiments. And since fire is of such manifold benefit, there would also be no [p. 411] doubt that the knowledge of nature would thereby gain much advantage, just as we see that an uncommon light has dawned in it since the properties of the air and their effects have been made known by evident experiments.

*Benefit in war.*

*Whether one can reckon this use*

*of fire among the benefits.*

*Advantage*

*of gunpowder in war.*

*Where one is precipitate in judging.*

§ 214. Since gunpowder was invented, fire has acquired an uncommon benefit in war, which is evident in particular from what is taught of military fireworks in the art of fireworks, and from what one finds here and there in descriptions of sieges. One need only open Mr von Fleming's Complete Soldier, and one will be sufficiently convinced of it. It is indeed true that fire was used in sieges even before the invention of gunpowder, as may be seen, among others, also from the Military School of Dilich, who has described both ancient and modern war: but at that time the use of fire was very poor in comparison with that which it has obtained since the invention of gunpowder, especially since the art of fireworks has risen ever higher, and in particular bombardment, which subdues all fortresses, has been invented. Perhaps some will not agree with me that I reckon this too among the benefits of fire which the [p. 412] human race enjoys from it. They will suppose that one ought rather to hold it for a harm, and the whole art of fireworks will be reckoned by them among those which it is better not to know than to have known. But it seems to me that there is some precipitancy in this judgement, and that it is not exactly difficult to show it. In war it is not only permitted, but one is even obliged to use all means which they find necessary for averting the harm that foreign enemies wish to do them (§ 500 *Polit.*). And accordingly one cannot reject the use of fire either on the ground that great harm is thereby done to the besieged, such as that their houses and goods are set on fire and many even lose their lives in it. For although it is the case that even in war one ought not to seek to obtain by a greater harm what one ought to obtain by a lesser (§ 500 *Polit.*), yet one cannot maintain from this that the use of military fireworks is not to be employed in war: for who will maintain that the old manner of making war was carried on with less harm than the newer, in which, since the invention of gunpowder, one compels most things by fire? One now accomplishes in a short time what formerly [p. 413] went on tediously, and therefore restores peace all the more quickly, whereas formerly the all-consuming war lasted all the longer. Indeed one need only weigh what misery there was formerly when a city was starved out, of which the history, known to everyone, of the destruction of Jerusalem gives an example, and compare it with the state in which nowadays a city is compelled by bombardment to a speedy surrender, and one will see sufficiently that the latter is more tolerable than the former. But he to whom both manners of making war are sufficiently known, and who withal knows how to estimate exactly the harm to be feared from each, will find his way into the matter still better. Precipitate judgements occur among people who like to have something to censure, and most of all in such cases where something happens that has not been before. This happens not only in the present case, but is so general that it occurs even with the best and most useful things when they are invented.

*Benefit of fire for the amusement of the mind.*

§ 215. Just as military fireworks have their benefit in war, so fireworks for pleasure, on the other hand, afford their benefit in peace: for they serve for the amusement of the mind, in that they present the eye with a delightful spectacle. It is indeed true [p. 414] that fireworks are costly; but for that very reason they remain a pleasure of great lords, who can spend more upon it than others, and meanwhile many thousands of others enjoy it along with them, who likewise serve as spectators of it. Moreover, it is known that, according to the present manner of waging war, one must at all times also have people who understand the art of fireworks. Now since there is not always occasion for military fireworks, such that one could have the apprentices prepare them for use in war, fireworks for pleasure give them a convenient exercise, and while they give proof of their skill in handling fire, they can at the same time thereby afford others a delight. He who has something to spend on other pleasures, and may do so (§ 457, 511 *Mor.*), can spend it as well on fireworks as on anything else. And what has already been said of pleasures of this kind in a similar case (§ 192) holds good here.

*Harm that fire causes.*

*Causes of the dangerousness of a*

*conflagration.*

*Fire-spewing mountains do harm.*

*Whether the benefit of fire outweighs the harm.*

*Proof of the goodness of God.*

§ 216. Fire also does much harm when a conflagration arises, either through carelessness, or through the malice of those who set it, or else through an accident, e.g. when lightning strikes. And this is so much the more dangerous, the more combustible matter the fire finds, and the more easily the kindled matter flies from one place to another; [p. 415] as the wind, too, increases the danger of fire, partly when it blows upon the fire, partly when it carries the flying sparks to places where they kindle and whither they would not otherwise come of themselves, partly when it drives the flame against other buildings, so that it catches and kindles where it would not otherwise kindle. Among the accidents that are to be ascribed to fire belongs also the harm which the fire from fire-spewing mountains causes in the fields and in villages and towns when it reaches them, of which one must seek information from history; and since such fire- spewing is commonly accompanied by earthquakes, one thing and another will be noted on this occasion hereafter. Furthermore, we also see from fire that natural things which procure ever so much benefit for the human race can nevertheless at times inflict violent harm. But if one weighs the benefit against the harm, it will be found here too that the benefit far outweighs the harm: from which one again has a proof of the divine goodness (§ 21), which shows itself precisely in this, that the creature must serve man more than it can harm him, especially if one deducts what man brings about through his own fault.

[p. 416]

### Das X. Capitel. Von dem Nutzen derer Dinge/ die sich in der Erde befinden. — Chapter X. On the Benefit of the Things Found in the Earth.

*Benefit of stones and sand.*

*Why sand increases heat.*

*Use in common life:*

*in chemistry.*

*Benefit of stones in architecture.*

*Benefit of lime.*

*Benefit of stones in paving the roads:*

*also in sculpture,*

*and for many ornaments of architecture, and for household goods.*

*Why works of art should be described.*

*Benefit of flints.*

*How one can strike fire with them.*

*Advantage that one has from this.*

§ 217. What benefit some kinds of soil that are commonly dug out of the earth have, has already been set forth above (§ 193). So too some benefit of stones and of sand, which these bodies have in increasing heat, has in part already been touched upon (§ 202). And in fact sand and stones also sometimes have the advantage in the field that they increase the heat there, and they are of particular service to such places as cold winds can sweep freely. Thus in gardening, too, one sometimes mixes sand into the soil when a plant requires a warm ground. The cause is easy to grasp. Sand and stone become warmer than soil. Lay sand, a stone and soil in the sun, or place them in winter by a warm stove, or else on the hearth by the fire; and the difference will show itself so distinctly that one can distinguish it even with one's hands. [p. 417] Moreover, when a stone and sand have become thoroughly warm, they also retain the heat longer than soil. Both serve to increase thereby the heat of the ground in a place. And this very thing has given occasion for the custom of using stones in winter to warm beds and chilled limbs, or also the body in such afflictions where heat is of service to it, either for the relief of pain or to remedy the ailment, since in particular a stone can easily be made warm in water without itself becoming wet thereby. Sand is also used in chemistry for maintaining heat, when, for example, one distils in warm sand. In common life stones have manifold benefit. They are used for walls (§ 258 *Arch. civil.*), and one kind of them for lime (§ 63 *Arch. civ.*): for not all can be turned into lime, but some melt in great heat and become glass, as one can also find by *experiment* with the great burning-glasses (§ 138 *Pt. II Exper.*) and burning-mirrors (§ 136 *Pt. II Exper.*). Lime was at first invented chiefly for building walls, and in this it still has its foremost, or at least one of its foremost benefits, [p. 418] since, together with sand, it provides the material by which stones and bricks are bound to one another in walls (§ 251 *Arch. Civ.*), and is also the chief ingredient in the plaster with which walls and partitions are coated (§ 314 *Arch. Civ.*); but it has since acquired still further benefit both in the arts and in medicine. For example, certain kinds of leather are dressed with lime, and in Paris trials have been made of the internal use of lime in certain diseases, of which one finds an account in the History of the Royal Academy of Sciences<sup>a</sup>. Everyone knows what benefit stones have for paving streets or lanes, which would otherwise be spoiled by the rain and by much walking and driving, and become unfit for both; he who understands building knows how to distinguish the benefit of stones better than another, since the various cases are known to him in which they contribute something both to firmness and to ornament, and sometimes also to convenience, as being the three points to which one must look in building (§ 15, 17, 18 *Arch. civ*). In particular stones have a [p. 419] by no means small benefit in sculpture, by which statues are made from them, which serve as an ornament of churches, palaces, gardens, avenues, grottoes and so forth, whereby the eye is delighted and the mind is cheered and roused, according as the various circumstances in which they are used require. To the benefit of stones in architecture I reckon all works that are made of stone and have their foundation in architecture. An example is the artificial fountains which serve as an ornament of towns in the open squares, of which Böckler<sup>a′</sup> describes a great many. Besides this, all kinds of vessels and other things that one uses in the household are made from some kinds of stone: all of which are indeed things well known among us, but which must nevertheless be considered in this place as well (§ 194), and deserve to be described exactly in writings, for the remembrance of posterity, where one describes the arts existing among us (§ 305 *Polit.*), since it very easily happens that through the length of time arts which one has possessed are lost again, and one is thereby deprived of a benefit [p. 420] which one formerly had. Since we here only indicate the benefit of things in general, we must also content ourselves with that. Yet I must still mention one benefit of stones which, though exceedingly common, is nevertheless greater than one is wont to esteem it, because it is all too common. We use a kind of stone to strike fire, which for that reason we are also wont to call flints, and this has given occasion for them to be fitted also to small firearms, so that one can discharge them without fire, since by the flint one strikes fire and thereby kindles the gunpowder in the pan, which through the touch-hole kindles the rest in the firearm with which it has been loaded. One strikes with such a stone at an oblique angle down along a steel or iron, and the sparks which then leap off are glowing little pieces of iron, or sometimes also a melted little piece of the flint that has been turned into glass, since this stone belongs among those that are turned into glass by the force of fire (§ 133 *Pt. II Exper.*). It is therefore just the same as if one touched a body with a glowing iron, or with molten glass that likewise glows, where a [p. 421] spark falls upon it. Now everyone knows that when one brings a glowing iron, or also molten glass, nay glass merely inclined to melt, to a body that easily catches fire, this body is singed and begins to smoulder, and also that gunpowder is kindled by either, nay must be kindled. Therefore it is no wonder that the sparks struck with the flint from the iron or steel kindle the tinder and the gunpowder. But how great the advantage is that one can have fire so easily by this means, he is able to judge who not only considers what difficulties there would be if one had to procure fire in another manner, but also attends, in human life, to those cases in which the readiness to strike fire stands us in very good stead.

<sup>a</sup> *Histoire de l'Acad. Roi. des Scienc. A. 17. p. m.*

<sup>a′</sup> *in Archit. Curiosa part. 3.*

*Benefit of precious stones.*

*Serve for magnificence.*

*Give pleasure to the eye.*

*Inner worth of the diamond.*

*Cause of nature's great store.*

§ 218. Precious stones indeed have no greater benefit than that they serve man for delight, and are necessary in those cases where one must display a magnificence above others for political reasons (§ 466 *Polit.*). And here, to be sure, it depends for the most part on the imagination when one sets a value on them according as they are rare and not to be had in such quantity as other things.
