Such things in general also procure the advantage [p. 422] in the commonwealth, that wealthy people distribute a part of their superfluity among the others who need money in order to support themselves by it (§ 211 *Polit.*). Meanwhile, however, precious stones that are well cut and set can also, by their lustre, give the eye a pleasure which does not consist in mere imagination, and in this respect the diamond retains a pre-eminence over all the rest, partly on account of its flashing light, partly on account of the exceedingly agreeable colours with which it plays (§ 162 *Pt. II Exper.*). He who does not value this pleasure must leave it to those who make something of it. For it is precisely on this account that nature has so great a store of things, and art seeks to increase it still further, so that everyone may find something that delights him after his own fashion, and that there may also be no want of variation, since transitory pleasure loses its charm if it is not enjoyed with change.

*Whether precious stones are of benefit in medicine.*

*Why not all grounds convince everyone equally.*

*Examination of Boyle's grounds.*

*Whether mineral motes mix with them.*

§ 219. Some are also wont to ascribe to precious stones a force in medicine: but what is brought forward on this point is not yet grounded on such experiences that one could find one's satisfaction in them. At least none such are known to me, and it may be that some, for their own part, believe they possess such [p. 423] experiences, which might not appear so convincing to me as to them; just as I have found, in such things as I maintain by grounds, that to some the proofs do not appear convincing, although they know of nothing to object against them, notwithstanding that, after a carefully conducted examination, they satisfy me. It depends on how far a man has exercised his understanding, that he is convinced in this or in another way. The famous *Boyle*, who in the investigation of nature spared neither diligence nor expense, sought to maintain this force of precious stones by grounds and to free it from objections<sup>a</sup>: but I am of the opinion that, even if one grants him everything he alleges, nothing more than a very slight force can be ascribed to them. He assumes that, since precious stones are produced from a fluid, or at least a soft, matter, various mineral motes were mixed with it in the earth. This may very well be granted, not only on account of the various colours which various kinds of precious stones have, but also on account of the difference of colour within one kind of precious stone, such [p. 424] as are met with in very great variety even in the diamond, although it is properly uncoloured, like a clear crystal (§ 379 *Phys.*). Now he further assumes that minerals have a benefit in medicine, and this is unknown to no one, provided only he has concerned himself with the preparation of chemical medicines: on which one may read the famous *Lémery*'s *Cours de Chymie*, which has also been translated into the German language. But since the mineral parts are to be met with very sparingly in precious stones, which one can very easily discover if one deprives one of them of its colour in the fire and pays attention to the loss of weight; so the force too, which must be proportioned to the quantity of the mineral matter, cannot amount to much. Should anyone maintain that the least part of the mineral matter went off with the colour, and that on the contrary a quite considerable part remained behind, he would have to prove it. But I do not see how he means to prove it. For that the colour is mineral matter is certain (§ 379 *Phys.*): but what more is present cannot yet be said with certainty. But even if more is present, from which the precious stones have received their solidity in the mixture; still one does not see why that which is invisibly distributed should [p. 425] amount to more than the other, which strikes the eye, although it too is very finely distributed through the whole matter of the stone, as the microscopes show, by which the colour in precious stones is indeed weakened, yet one can discover no parts that would be uncoloured: from which one sees that all the motes which the microscope can discover must still have some mineral matter in them. But should anyone derive the medicinal force from something other than the mineral matter, he would be alleging what he could maintain by no grounds, and would fall back upon occult qualities, which are no longer admitted nowadays, since nothing is granted but what can be distinctly explained, where one means to maintain something from grounds.

<sup>a</sup> *in Tract. de gemmarum origine & virtutibus sect. 2. p. m. 30. & seqq.*

*On the benefit of minerals,*

*1. in medicine.*

*2. in human life:*

*as of salt,*

*sulphur,*

*vitriol,*

*amber.*

*How one should rightly value the benefit of the things that occur daily.*

*Why one has to take pains over this.*

*How one comes to know the manifold benefit of minerals in the arts.*

*Benefit of the history of science and of the arts.*

*Examples of the use of minerals.*

*How one may discover more benefit.*

§ 220. Minerals have their benefit as well in medicine as in human life and in the arts. The benefit they have in medicine must be learned partly from chemical writings, partly from the writings of those who have treated of the kinds of diseases and of the means proved against them. But he who understands nothing either of chemistry or of medicine can [p. 426] gain no thorough insight into this. Indeed, if one wished to learn anything of this benefit, one would first have to become exactly acquainted with the minerals as a whole, and set up the experiments necessary for investigating their properties. The benefit in human life is known from common experience, and one need only look about in it, and one will perceive it without trouble. For example, we use salt with all our food to make it savoury, for which reason it is also called the best seasoning. And since it checks putrefaction, it is also customarily used as a means of preserving against putrefaction whatever one wishes to keep. Sulphur we use daily when we wish to make a fire or light a candle, because it melts very easily and, as soon as it melts, takes fire. The tinder, however, which catches the fire that we strike, is to be regarded only as something glowing, which may well melt something but cannot kindle it. Vitriol we use for the ink with which we write, since it colours black everything among plants that has an astringent taste. For although we commonly use gall-nuts for it, it succeeds just as well with *tea*, rose-leaves and other such things. Amber serves for fumigation, because [p. 427] it easily melts on glowing coals and can be resolved into smoke. But just as I have already reminded more than once that, if we wish rightly to know how to value the benefit of the things that occur daily, we must not only attend to it in all cases where they are used, but must also in particular consider how we should fare if at such a time we did not have this or that thing for our use, so I must here repeat this once more also, in order that we may rightly know how to value the benefit that might seem slight to us, especially since this leads us to a greater knowledge of the goodness (§ 21) and wisdom of God (§ 14), and thereby gives occasion, in everything that comes before us, to think of God not merely by chance but with good ground, in which the right kind of godliness consists (§ 670 *Mor.*). Whoever wishes to know the manifold benefit of minerals in the arts, by which in turn they procure advantage in human life in a hidden way, namely unknown to those who do not understand the arts, must look about among the artists and craftsmen. And this benefit will then become better known when one shall at last have a complete description of all arts and crafts [p. 428] and, upon that, at the same time a science of them. But from this there appears a new benefit that such a history and science will have, namely that from it we shall learn to know better the good that we enjoy from God through nature. Yet whoever has looked about even a little in the workshops of the craftsmen will not be ignorant of the use of minerals. For example, alum is used in many ways with colours, and painters make their oil-colours for the most part from minerals. Sulphur and saltpetre are the chief *ingredients* of gunpowder, and are found necessary in all matters of fireworks. Yellow ochre is used for chamois leather, and so on. But although the benefit of minerals in the arts is already very manifold, there is nevertheless no reason whatever to doubt that still more would come to light if one not only made oneself acquainted with that already at hand, but at the same time investigated its natural ground: for the latter it would be necessary to investigate the properties of the minerals by all kinds of deliberately conducted experiments, and the benefit hitherto customary would itself give occasion for these experiments. As in all sciences and arts, so in physics in particular, there is lacking [p. 429] nothing but labourers; were there these, a rich harvest would not be in doubt.

*On the benefit of metals in medicine.*

*What uncertainty attends gold tinctures.*

*Why so much effect has been ascribed to gold medicines.*

*Doubt that arises here.*

§ 221. Metals have just as many kinds of benefit as minerals. Medicines against diseases are prepared from them; all kinds of things for use in human life are made from them; and one has much to do with them in the arts. What medicines are made from metals one must again learn from chemical writings. Those prepared from iron are the best known; those from gold have at all times enjoyed the greatest renown and have been highly praised. But no chemist, or even alchemist, has yet shown in an intelligible manner how gold is to be resolved into its elements, that is, into the simpler materials from whose mixture it arose in nature (§ 32 *Phys.*). It is indeed true that many have boasted, and still boast, of possessing this secret, because such supposed medicines, which have the prejudice of so many ages on their side, bring their possessors much money: but since no one has yet *communicated* such a process that it could be judged on chemical grounds, one cannot be certainly assured that either simplicity does not conceal a self-deception under it, or covetousness a wilful [p. 430] deception of others under it. Indeed, even if one wished to prefer one's advantage to the common good and to conceal the secret, it would be enough if one could prove by infallible proofs that in gold tinctures and gold powders, which are supposed to be made from opened gold, such opened gold is actually to be found. Such proofs, however, no one has as yet given. And accordingly the profit, and the *interested* appearance sometimes connected with the pretended discovery of such singular and hidden medicines, make the matter very doubtful (§ 12, ch. 7 *Log.*). The benefit of the medicines that take their origin from metals one must learn from medical writings, and one cannot deny their benefit to those which have long experience on their side. But as regards the gold medicines of which we have just spoken, it is certain that their benefit was extolled before anyone pretended to possess them, and it was precisely the hope set upon a singular effect that drove the alchemists to take pains over them. That people came upon such thoughts, however, arose from this: that they had seen the effect of the medicines made from other metals and minerals, but held gold [p. 431] to be the most perfect of all, which on account of its perfect mixture would also be so hard to resolve. But if gold medicines are on that account to be highly esteemed, and a singular effect ascribed to them above others, then one would perhaps have to hold gold in itself to be more potent than gold opened into its elements, whereby the mixture is removed. This opinion, however, finds little approval nowadays, since things are now customarily investigated with greater insight than was perhaps the practice formerly, when people were more hasty. Yet I cannot deny that it has a great deal of probability that the medicaments made from opened metals must be of greater and more penetrating force than the rest. I have no doubt that there are in the metals salt, sulphur and a volatile substance, such as has been called mercury; not only because one cannot indiscriminately call into doubt the experience of those who claim to have opened the metals and found these chemical elements in them, but also because I believe I have found a proof by which this can be shown from the properties of the metals that are observed in them: of which there will be occasion to speak at some time in [p. 432] my Latin works. It is known that the mixture of the elements in metals takes place in the most subtle particles of all, not only because the greatest magnification effected by microscopes shows nothing but metallic particles, but also because the metals are generated by the most subtle fume of all<sup>a</sup>: for which very reason they are so exceedingly hard to resolve. Accordingly the chemical elements found in the metals must be subtle and volatile beyond all measure. But who does not know that subtle, volatile materials are exceedingly penetrating and can produce great changes in other bodies into which they penetrate? And so one has a proof by which the true metallic oils and salts can be distinguished from others. They must show a particular penetrating force in a small quantity. Where this does not happen, one can be assured that a deception lies hidden under it, whether someone has deceived himself out of lack of understanding, or wished to deceive others from *interested* motives.

<sup>a</sup> *Vid.* Hencke in the Pyritologie *c. 5. p. 349. & sq.*

*What services the metals*

*render us in common life.*

*Benefit of iron in architecture.*

*Benefit of tin in architecture.*

*Particular benefit of iron in stone cement.*

*Benefit of iron in the household economy and in*

*husbandry, &c.*

*Benefit of tin in common life.*

*Why it is preferred to other metal.*

*Why tin keeps the advantage over copper and brass in common life.*

*Where these metals are used with benefit.*

*Use of silver in common life.*

*Why gold is to be highly esteemed.*

*Harm of alchemy.*

*Use of metals in clothing.*

*How use and misuse are to be distinguished from one another.*

*Whether the wearing of gold and silver should be permitted.*

*When it is not permitted.*

*1. Cause.*

*2. Cause,*

*3. Cause.*

*When it is permitted.*

§ 222. In common life the metals [p. 433] render men a great deal of benefit, yet always one more than another. None among them all performs more services for man than iron, and it is to be ascribed to the wise (§ 14) and benevolent provision of God (§ 2) that it is found more abundantly than others whose benefit is lesser, although these are held in higher value because they are not so common. We use it in building to anchor the walls, where one must fear that they might part from one another on account of their great weight; to fasten together the large ashlars and other building-work composed of large stones, which cannot be bound together like bricks and small stones; for the fittings of doors and windows, for locks and keys, for nails with which woodwork is fastened, for stoves, at chimneys, and in other cases. I do not think it necessary to cite the passages from architecture that belong here out of my Elements, since everyone can find most of it for himself if he looks about him in a house; indeed, from the experience that hovers before his eyes hourly, nay every moment, without his ever properly paying heed to it, he will have noticed so much that he needs only a slight reminder if he is to recollect it (§ 238 *Met.*). There is [p. 434] also some benefit of tin in architecture. For where iron is not to rust, because it may become wet or the damp air may pass over it, which latter is still more harmful than the former, it is customary to tin it. A particular benefit of iron in building, which not everyone can think of so readily as of the foregoing, is that it is used as an ingredient of the stone cement with which the stones are bound together in such places as rain and snow can reach (§ 314 *Arch. civ.*): for how much this cement serves for firmness is shown by sad experience through the harm that ensues when it is not used where it should be. I will cite only one example here. On large buildings it is customary to cut the gutters into the stone cornice, so that they are hidden there and cause no unsightliness in the building. Now if these gutters cut into the stone are not lined with copper, and one forgets to join the parts of the cornice together with stone cement, the snow-water gradually eats its way through and penetrates down to the wall where the beams rest. Since here in particular the wetness cannot dry out, where no free air comes, the ends of the beams begin to rot away, [p. 435] and the wall is torn loose by the weight of the projecting cornice. In this way the buildings split at the corners and at the partition walls, and great cracks appear. Now because it is supposed that the foundation is to blame, giving way so that the wall settles, no attempt is made at once to remedy the defect, and great dilapidation may thereby ensue in the building. I speak from experience, as in other cases where I put something forward with great confidence: which the reader should well note. More cases of this kind occur in human life, where a thing has an exceedingly great benefit on account of the harm that is avoided by it, but this benefit is not recognised, because one does not at the same time see the harm that is prevented by it. We must also reckon here all the instruments of iron that are used in building, and which must be judged from the history and science of the arts, which, however, is as yet still to be awaited. In the household economy all sorts of instruments are made of iron which are used in the kitchen and on other occasions, such as fire-tongs, oven-forks, steels for striking fire, axes, saws and so forth. And in husbandry still more of them occur, [p. 436] which, no more than the foregoing, can all be individually enumerated and described, since this again belongs to the history and science of the arts. The use of iron in carting is likewise well known, as also in riding and on other occasions besides. Now whoever wishes to see into this very manifold benefit must observe what we have *recommended* on other occasions of the kind, and what must also be observed in what follows (§ 220). The benefit of tin in common life shows itself for the most part in the vessels which we are accustomed to make for the enjoyment of food and drink; although other things which we need for our use also consist of it, such as candlesticks, warming-bottles &c. And because in these cases the tin is not left pure, but receives an admixture of lead, lead thereby at the same time also gains its benefit. This metal is preferred to others for this use because liquids do not attack it, nor does it take on anything from them, and in this way neither are the vessels spoiled by them, nor is the taste of food and drink altered: for which reason vessels made of iron, copper and brass are also tinned on the inside [p. 437] as a rule. Besides this, tin can also easily be cleaned, and takes on nothing of the smell and taste of what has been in it, except what has merely clung to the surface and can therefore soon be removed again. Why copper and brass are less commonly used may be gathered from the advantage of tin over them. From both metals water, and especially acid water, draws a vitriolic substance which spoils the taste and effect of food and drink. Yet there are some cases where one need not regard this, and there copper and brass too are used with benefit, e.g. the former for kettles, brewing-pans &c., the latter for candlesticks, kettles for preserving fruit &c. Silver is costly in value and serves for the rich, who can display superfluity (§ 510 *Mor.*). Yet since it has the same property as copper, from which it also receives its alloy, tin is to be preferred to it in this respect; and a drink that stands long in tin also tastes better than one that has stood in silver. And this is the reason why silver vessels are customarily gilded heavily, so that acid and wet things cannot attack them so much. The benefit of silver, and of the copper that is [p. 438] alloyed with it, shows itself especially in money, since it has those properties which are required in money (§ 917 *Mor.*). And one can see how burdensome it is where one has mere copper money, in that the money is often heavier to carry home than the wares one sells were to bring to market. And gold in particular, on account of its costly value, is to be highly esteemed for this reason, that one can carry a great sum of money about one without any burden, which procures a great advantage for travellers and in war. Hence it has also long been recognised that the art of alchemy, were it known and did it become common, would bring more harm than benefit. For if gold were as common as brass, and were to be had in as great a quantity as other metals, it would no longer retain its value, which it now has merely because it is rare. But then we should lack a means of having much money together in a small compass, and we should lose a great advantage which we now enjoy from gold merely because it is rare. To the benefit which metals have in human life belongs also their use in clothing. I leave aside that benefit [p. 439] which iron affords in making the tools that are used where one would make clothes, since this too belongs to their benefit in the arts: here it is enough to attend to those cases where they furnish material for clothing, as, e.g., for buttons. Silver and gold in particular are much used for this, although what is commonly called gold here is nothing other than gilded silver. Since in clothing one has to look not only to necessities and convenience but also to one's station and means (§ 492 *Mor.*), one cannot pronounce it wrong when persons of rank and means wear gold and silver. On the other hand, it is a vanity when some seek only display in it who could forgo it without harm to their station, and wrong when anyone squanders his means on it (§ 542 *Mor.*). And in this case the use of silver and gold belongs under misuse. Now in general the use and the misuse of things can be very well distinguished: for the former can subsist with the duties incumbent upon us, and does not infringe the least of them; the latter, on the other hand, cannot subsist with them, and is contrary to them. Whoever, therefore, would distinguish the right use of things from [p. 440] misuse must be practised in ethics, for which we have given sufficient instruction in our Reasonable Thoughts on the Actions of Men. But it is yet another question whether it be conducive to a state that the wearing of gold and silver be permitted. This question cannot be answered absolutely with yes or no; rather, one must look to the different state in which the country finds itself. Where there is a lack of money, so that trade and traffic cannot on that account be properly carried on, and thereby not only does money stay out of the country which could be brought into it by trade, but also money in general does not pass sufficiently from one hand into another for everyone to be able to earn his living; there one should not only rather coin money from silver and gold, or keep within the country the money which is paid out for spun gold and silver, than permit the wearing of gold and silver. For in this case it is disadvantageous to the country. In like manner one sees that the wearing of gold and silver must be forbidden when people fall into poverty by it, as also in times of war, when much money must be taken out of the country for the maintenance of the troops. On the other hand, where no such circumstances are found, [p. 441] but it rather serves to make money circulate better among the people, indeed one even brings more money into the country with it by trade; there silver manufactures are not to be forbidden, nor does one find any ground why silver and gold should not be allowed to be worn.

*Benefit of metals in the arts.*

*How one comprehends it.*

*Works of art made from metal, as clocks.*

*Bells,*

*Cast statues,*

*Musical instruments.*

§ 223. If we go through the workshops of artists and craftsmen, we shall not find a single one in which we do not meet with tools made of metal. Meanwhile it remains certain that of all the metals none has a greater benefit in this than iron, and the steel made from it. And one would never believe how badly things would stand with us if only we had no iron. This we shall again know more fully when we have a history and science of the arts and crafts, which we still lack at present, although there is no end of the writing of books. But for comprehending this benefit there serves not only what I have already noted above (§ 220), that in every case that occurs one should consider what is made by the instrument, and after that attend to where we use the finished work, and then reflect on what we should not now have if we did not [p. 442] have it; but one may also profitably consult the travel accounts on this point, especially those of peoples among whom sciences and arts do not yet flourish, by observing how they perform with greater difficulty and vexation what we accomplish more quickly with the help of certain tools, and no less what they lack in convenience, since they are without some instruments which we have the enjoyment of. Here one may also reckon the works of art that are made from metal. What advantage do we not have from clocks, not only from the large ones used on towers, but also from the small pocket-watches that one can carry about one? For how much it matters to us that we know the time exactly is best understood by those to whom it matters that they know the time exactly in their occupations. What advantage do we have from bells, by which the signal is given when one is to assemble for divine service, so that one comes neither too early nor too late; when a conflagration breaks out, so that it is soon made known over the whole town, and people can the better run together to extinguish it and to arrange whatever else is necessary in such [p. 443] cases; when the clock is to make the hour known to those who cannot see the hand, or at a time when one cannot see it, and whatever else there is of this kind. Statues cast from metal serve for magnificence and lend dignity to the residence of a monarch (§ 466 *Polit.*). Not to mention that they also delight the mind, by affording the eyes a pleasant pasture. And for the very same reason the musical instruments made of metal have their place here, such as trumpets, trombones, hunting- horns, organs, instruments strung with metal strings, and whatever else there is of this kind. Whoever will go through everything he finds made of metal will come upon still more examples by himself, by which it may be maintained that the metals, through the works of art made from them, both relieve the necessities of human life and also serve its convenience and recreation.

*Benefit of metal in war.*

*How one comes to know the use of metals in war.*

*Benefit in fortification,*

*In the artillery.*

*Whether the use of metal in war is to be held a misuse.*

§ 224. In war the metals have at all times been of great benefit, both in ancient times, before gunpowder was invented, and especially in more recent times, since it has been invented. Of the benefit in former times there is no need to speak, since, as a lesser one, it has been driven out by [p. 444] a greater. But whoever should nevertheless wish to know something of it must read those writings which have described the manner of the ancients in making war, and in particular have represented the *machines* and instruments used in war; among which are to be reckoned, of the ancients, *Vegetius*<sup>a</sup> and *Vitruvius*<sup>b</sup>, and among the moderns Dilich, who in his Military School has described the old and the new at length, and has also illustrated it with the appropriate copperplates. But whoever wishes to know the use of metals in war has to attend (1) to fortification, (2) to the *artillery* with which fortresses are stormed and *defended*, (3) to the tools used in a siege. In fortification we meet with iron most of all, and the benefit of any other metal will show itself in few cases, whether we attend to the tools used in building or to the various parts of a fortress which consist partly of metal, such as the mountings and bolts of the gates, the chains on drawbridges, the portcullises and so forth; of which Dilich [p. 445] gives an account both in his Military School and in his *Peribologia*, or Report on Fortress-Buildings, and the like may also be obtained from others who have written on fortification. In the *artillery* we meet not only with the heavy and light ordnance, such as cannon, mortars, howitzers, petards and muskets or firelocks, but also with all kinds of instruments customary in the *artillery* for various uses; concerning all of which one must seek instruction from those who have written on the *artillery* and its use in sieges, and whom I cite in the Appendix to the Elements of the Mathematical Sciences (§ 120 ff.). It is also known that bombs, which are so excellent a means of reducing fortresses, are hollow balls made of iron which are filled with gunpowder (§ 125 *Artill.*). Finally, in a siege one also needs other tools for constructing various works, for which a great deal of ironwork in particular is required. Here also belong all the weapons used besides the ordnance, which again one finds described by those *Authors* who take the *artillery* in a somewhat wider sense, [p. 446] and are accustomed to describe everything that is needed in war and is kept in arsenals as a store for war, as among the French *Surirey de Saint-Remy* has done<sup>c</sup>, whose book, as the best that exists in the French language in this respect, has been printed more than once. Whoever would pass off the use of metal in war as a misuse, because much harm is done by it and many men are made unhappy, may be brought to better thoughts by what we have set out above concerning the use of fire in war (§ 214). For whatever in the ordnance customary nowadays otherwise seems to be too dangerous to be used in war with a good conscience depends on the force of the gunpowder, and consequently on the use of fire, of which we have spoken above. In judging such things one forgets how far it is permitted to ward off harm (§ 866 ff. *Mor.*).

<sup>a</sup> *de re militari.*

<sup>b</sup> *lib. 10. c. 15. & seq. conf. inprimis versio Gallica Peraltii.*

<sup>c</sup> *Memoires d'Artillerie.*

*What metals contribute to the advancement of the sciences.*

*Through astronomical instruments.*

*and for the knowledge of the cosmic bodies;*

*also of the whole edifice of the world, and of the encouragement arising therefrom*

*to godliness.*

*2. by physical instruments,*

*to the knowledge of nature.*

*3. by anatomical instruments, to the knowledge of men and animals.*

*How one learns to see this benefit properly.*

§ 225. All that I have hitherto adduced concerning the benefit of metals are things that strike everyone's eyes at once: hence I have also indicated them rather than [p. 447] described them, and shown the ground of them. But there yet remains one benefit which is more hidden and does not strike everyone's eyes at once, and accordingly I must speak of it more circumstantially. All kinds of *instruments* are made from metals which contribute a very great deal to the advancement of the sciences, so much so that we may maintain that we should not know the greatest part of what we hold as certain in the disciplines if we had no metal. I here adduce first the astronomical *instruments*, because these are the oldest that can be named here, and also among the most eminent of those which show the necessity of metal for the advancement of the sciences. Without metal one cannot make a serviceable astronomical *quadrant*, *sextant* or *octant*. Therefore *Tycho Brahe* too, although he made these *instruments* of wood so that they might be moved more easily, nevertheless covered them with brass where the division was to be made, because in wood, which is easily altered by wetness and the moisture of the air, no exact division can be obtained, not to mention that divisions can in general be made more finely in brass than in wood. Of the ironwork which, besides this, [p. 448] he needed for his *quadrants* and *sextants* we will not now make mention. He himself described his *instruments* in a work of its own<sup>a</sup>, and after his death descriptions of them also came out together with his *observations*<sup>b</sup>. *Hevelius* made most of his *instruments* entirely of metal, as the descriptions show which he himself gave of them<sup>c</sup>, and the moderns likewise use nothing but metal for them (§ 98 *Astron. Lat.*). The ancients had, on various occasions, further special *instruments*, such as their *armillas æquatoriales, regulas parallacticas &c.*, which have nowadays gone out of use, but which likewise could not have been made without metal in such a way that they could have been used for serviceable *observations*. Accordingly, had we had no metal for astronomy, chronology, geography, gnomonics and navigation at sea, then the glorious science of astronomy, from which many others in turn descend, such as chronology, geography, the art of sundials and the art of navigation at sea, would have remained entirely unknown, and we should by no means have been able, with such [p. 449] certainty, to know the motion of the stars that we could have calculated it in advance for future and past times. The nature and constitution of the heavenly bodies we owe, it is true, for the most part to telescopes (*Pt. 2, ch. 2 ff. Phys.*): but if we had no metal, we should also lack telescopes, and most of all such as we can employ for the contemplation of the heavens. For telescopes are put together from ground glasses. To grind glasses one needs dishes of copper or brass, and the *machines* which are necessary for grinding and polishing require lead and ironwork (§ 468, 472 *Dioptr. Lat.*). And the micrometers which are fitted to telescopes for measuring small quantities are likewise made of metal (§ 239 *Astron.*). Whoever considers this within himself, and is especially experienced in astronomy and in that part of physics which treats of the edifice of the world and makes up the second part of our Reasonable Thoughts on the Effects of Nature, will recognise quite properly how we should have neither the philosophical (§ 5 *Prol. Log.*) nor the mathematical knowledge of the edifice of the world (§ 15 *Prol. Log.*) if we had no metal. Therefore, [p. 450] since this knowledge also leads us to the knowledge of God (§ 23 ff.), and thereby further to godliness (§ 670 *Mor.*), all these good thoughts would likewise be wanting, and none of those good motions would follow in the mind which now arise in us when, with this knowledge, we either behold the stars or hear them spoken of (§ 238 *Met.*). Whoever has learned, from the experiments which I have published in three parts, or from other writings extant on such subjects, how much has been discovered of the properties of the air, and of the changes which it causes in other bodies, by means of the air-pump, which cannot be made otherwise than of metal, since wood and other materials either do not hold air (§ 64 *Pt. I* & § 65 *Pt. III Exper.*), or cannot withstand the pressure of the outer air when they are exhausted (§ 109 ff. *Pt. I Exper.*), or at least not everything that is needed for an air-pump can conveniently be made from them, as if one wished to make everything of glass, for which we should nevertheless again require instruments of iron; and who has moreover seen from my Thoughts on the Effects of Nature how very much the experiments made with the air- pump contribute to the knowledge of nature: [p. 451] he will again comprehend how we should not understand the greater part of what we know with certainty in physics if we had had no metal, especially where he at the same time pays attention to the connection of truths with one another, and therefore knows what we should not know if we had not been able to make experiments with the air-pump. Moreover, it is more than too well known to those who deliberately consider the experiments which I and others have published, that besides the air-pump one also has other instruments and *machines* which either are made of metal, or could not have been made of other material without tools made of metal, or in which metal also has its use together with other things, and by which various experiments have been made that give us some light, now here, now there, in the knowledge of nature. One must further reckon here the anatomical instruments, without which one could have dissected neither wood and plants nor the parts in the bodies of men and animals in such a way that it would have been possible to know their proper structure, without which it would nevertheless have been impossible to explain with any reliability anything of the changes that belong to them. Should [p. 452] one now go further, and investigate still further what knowledge of other things has proceeded from this knowledge of nature, or to which at least it has given occasion, we should perceive still more properly the benefit of which I speak. And from this one may gather how much more fortunately one advances herein than others, if one learns all truths in a constant connection with one another, and makes the connection visible by continual *citations*, such as are as yet to be found nowhere but in my writings. And it is precisely this that makes them liked by people of understanding, since these recognise how they come to know everything with certainty and obtain a greater insight into the truth, as it were imperceptibly and without particular effort.

<sup>a</sup> *Mechanica cœlestis.*

<sup>b</sup> *in Historia cœlesti.*

<sup>c</sup> *in Machina cœlesti Tom. I. integro.*

*Why metals have so great a benefit in the arts and in human life.*

*Why they serve for things which are to be durable.*

*2. which need a great weight in a narrow space.*

*3. which differ only in size and figure.*

*4. which have an even and polished surface.*

*Why iron has the precedence over other metals.*

§ 226. Having indicated the various benefits which metals have in the arts and in human life (§ 222 ff.), it is also incumbent upon us to show further why they can have this benefit (§ 5 *Prol. Log.*). Now since we have treated of it merely in general, but by no means set it out in particular, as something which we referred to the history of the arts, we need only indicate in general that wherein the ground of the benefit is to be sought, and refer the rest [p. 453] to the science of the arts. Metals are hard bodies, and can endure heat and cold, moisture and dryness, though always one more than another. And accordingly they are serviceable for things which are not to be fragile and which have to hold out in the kinds of weather of the air. Indeed, according as one metal has a benefit over another with respect to this property, so it is also to be preferred to the other where one has to consider either the solidity of the work, or its durability in the air and its various kinds of weather, or also in water and fire. For example, where one has to cover a terrace on which the rain- and snow-water remains standing, one prefers copper to lead and iron, because the last two metals are corroded by snow-water, but copper is not. Metals are heavy bodies (§ 188 *Pt. I Exper.*) and therefore serve for such things as need a great weight in a narrow space; and where nothing else is to be considered, those of a heavier kind are in this respect to be preferred to those of a lighter. An example is given by the balls which are made of lead, because after gold it is the heaviest metal, or, where one has to fire against solid bodies, such as the revetment walls of a [p. 454] rampart, from iron, since the soft lead flattens itself against the hard wall. For whoever understands motion knows that the force of a body arises not only from the velocity of the motion, but also from the quantity of matter which is moved with them and is estimated by the weight (§ 656 *Met.*), and also that a body which changes its figure in the impact retains less force for effects upon the other (§ 658 *Met.*). They can be hammered and melted, and hence one can easily give them all kinds of figures. Therefore they are fit for all kinds of things, and in particular such manifold instruments can be made from them, since the works of art which are made from one and the same kind of matter differ from one another in nothing but figure and size, as one can not only perceive at once when one takes pains to determine the difference in particular, but it can also be proved that no other difference can be brought by art into a matter whose parts we judge to be similar to one another than that which consists in figure and size, since we can do nothing further here than that either some parts are taken from it, or those present in it are differently [p. 455] arranged, whereby, however, no other difference can arise than in figure and size (§ 21, 54 *Met.*). Thus an axe and a hatchet differ only in size, for in figure the two agree with each other; nor is there any other difference between the two and the blade of a knife. An iron stove and an axe, however much they differ from each other at first sight, yet have no further difference than that which comes from size and figure and can be fully explained by these two. Metals can be made smooth and polished. Therefore one can use them for such things as must have a flat and polished surface. An example is seen in steel buttons, and likewise in the mirrors made of steel by a craftsman<sup>a</sup>. But iron in particular has the precedence over other metals in instruments on account of its hardness, which it still retains even when it is beaten very thin, ground very sharp and brought to a very subtle point, wherein no single metal equals it. For on this account instruments can be made from it with which one can cut, hew, pierce, dig, and so forth. But one must not always seek the cause why a metal is used for a [p. 456] work of art in one property of it only, but sometimes in several. For example, that the balls for small arms, such as muskets and pistols, are made of lead happens not only on account of its weight, but also on account of its softness, for the sake of which it is easily melted: for in this way less trouble is needed to cast the balls.

<sup>a</sup> *Vid. Acta Erudit. A. 1714. p. 203.*

*Whence the acidulous and medicinal springs come.*

§ 227. Nature also uses minerals and metals for the acidulous and medicinal springs, which for this reason are commonly called mineral waters. In them, mineral and metallic corpuscles are indeed not present in such a form that one could separate them from it; yet subtle volatile parts are found in them which make themselves known by other proofs. For example, if a volatile vitriolic substance is contained in a water, it becomes like ink when one throws powdered gall-nuts into it, or steeps rose-leaves and tea in it, or, better still, pours into it an extract prepared from them with hot water; although here one must take care not to drive off the mineral substance by boiling. But besides this there are still other waters which carry with them mineral or even metallic corpuscles in so large a form that they can be separated from them in their true [p. 457] form. Both differ according to the various kinds of minerals and metals, and not only has *Varenius* treated of them in general<sup>b</sup>, but a great many particular writings on them have also appeared in various languages, which one meets with here and there. What is to be met with here and there on this matter in the *Philosophical Transactions*, which the *Secretary* of the Royal Society of Great Britain in England is accustomed to publish, has been collected by *Lowthorp*<sup>c</sup> and *Motte*<sup>d</sup> in their abridgement of them. The waters which flow through the fissures of the earth either dissolve the mineral materials and the metallic soil; or the subtle vapours, which are driven upward by the internal heat of the earth through its pores and have been separated by other causes from mineral and metallic earths, mix with them. When a water is of both natures, so that it has coarse and volatile parts together, then both causes also come together. But since it is not exactly our design at present to treat at length of the origin of mineral waters, we may let the matter rest with this general explanation, [p. 458] as this is enough to show how the minerals and metals can have the benefit which we have here ascribed to them.

<sup>b</sup> *Geogr. gener. part. 1. sect. 4. c. 16. prop. 4. p. m. 191. & seqq.*

<sup>c</sup> *Vol. 2. p. 331. & seqq.*

<sup>d</sup> *Vol. 2. p. 217. & seqq.*

*What harm and benefit earthquakes do.*

*Their harm.*

*How nature thereby changes the state of the earth.*

*Examples of such changes.*

*How an earthquake can swallow up a mountain.*

*Why one cannot see where a mountain has gone.*

*How a standing lake can arise in place of a mountain or a country.*

*How islands and mountains arise suddenly.*

*Further causes of the changes of the earth.*

§ 228. Earthquakes indeed cause great and dreadful damage, namely all that which is to be expected from violent shocks (§ 383 Phys.), and are therefore a means which God can use for punishment (§ 104); meanwhile, if one considers the various accidents that accompany them, one cannot deny them all benefit either. They are the foremost means which nature uses to change the state of the earth. The difference which we meet with round about the outside of the earth shows itself in this, that in one place there is water, in another dry land; that the dry land is either level or mountainous; and that both either continue in one unbroken stretch, or are separated from one another by the water and surrounded by it, the latter of which one is accustomed to call islands. Accordingly the change which can occur on the surface of the earth takes place either when water arises where previously there was none, or when the water is lost and dry land comes forth where formerly there was a sea; when mountains vanish or become level with the earth, or also when, in a level place, [p. 459] mountains arise; when mainlands are torn away and become islands, while islands, on the other hand, are joined on to a mainland. That changes of this kind occur on the surface of the earth, and consequently that the present earth is no longer the same (§ 17 Met.) as it was in the beginning, the learned Jesuit **Kircher**<sup>a</sup> has proved as a matter grounded in the experience of all ages. He not only cites general testimonies on this from *Aristotle*, *Ovid*, *Seneca* and *Lactantius*, but also confirms it by particular examples from ancient as well as modern history. It falls to us to cite some of these, in which some sudden and notable change has come about through earthquakes (§ 2, ch. 5 Log.). *Pliny*<sup>b</sup> relates that in his time a high mountain called *Cibotus*, together with the city of *Curite*, was swallowed up by the earth, so that not the least of it was to be seen any more. He there cites still other examples of the same kind. That the cause was an earthquake may be gathered from *Pliny* himself, since he speaks of it in the place where he had undertaken to describe the wonderful effects of earthquakes; and the matter itself also shows that nothing [p. 460] other than an earthquake can have been the cause of it. For if a high mountain together with a whole city situated on it is to sink into the earth, there must be so great a cave there within the earth that the mountain with the city has room in it; and in order that it may fall down with the city, it must be separated together with it from the surrounding land. An earthquake is fitted to bring about such a thing. For in it the earth is lifted up so violently that it bursts (§ 383 *Phys.*), and through such bursting the mountain with the city is separated from the rest of the land. But that one did not see where the mountain and the city had gone can be ascribed to no other cause than that the mountain and the city sank deep down and were covered over with earth: which again is quite comprehensible from an earthquake. For this shakes the mountain and the city, and causes the heavy materials, such as stones and walls, to separate from the lighter ones, such as common earth is. Now when things begin to fall into the earth, the stones fall first, and whatever large pieces of wall there are, while the rubble remains behind. For although some of it also falls down together with the heavy things, yet the greater part remains behind. The cause is [p. 461] this, that the collapse does not happen all at once but little by little, since, for example, the stones in a mountain separate little by little through the shaking, and then give way on the side where the earth begins to fall into the cave, and hang down until at last they themselves fall after it. **Kircher** cites from modern history that Mount *Pico*, which was one of the highest, was swallowed up by an earthquake, and that in its place a standing lake arose which had as great a circumference as the mountain had had before; likewise that *A. 1556* in China a whole country with mountains, cities and all its inhabitants sank, and a great standing lake took its place. One sees without my pointing it out that in such cases there was water under the earth, which, when the earth burst and fell in through the great shaking, rose up, while the earth, on the other hand, sank under together with what had been on it. On the contrary, *Pliny*<sup>d</sup> already noted that islands and mountains have suddenly arisen in the sea where there were none before. **Kircher** cites similar examples from more recent times. For example, *A. 1638*, near the island of St Michael in the Atlantic Ocean an island 5 German miles in breadth [p. 462] came into being through the subterranean fire throwing out stones in great quantity in one heap. He cites yet another example, in which a whole mountain arose in a single night in the midst of the sea; likewise how a fire-spewing mountain brought forth a small one beside itself, merely by the ashes and stones that were thrown out with the fire. So too, only as recently as *A. 1707*, on 23 *Mart.*, a new island arose out of the midst of the sea in the *Archipelago*<sup>e</sup>. To say nothing of other examples. Certainly, besides earthquakes and the raging of the fire-spewing mountains connected with them, rivers and inundations are also a cause of the outward shape of the earth being changed, of which *Pliny* again cites examples, and **Kircher** adds others from more recent history. Indeed, the sea too has its place here, in that it retreats and withdraws ever further from the mainland, as **Mr Swedenborg** has observed in Lapland<sup>f</sup>. But this does not belong in the present place.

<sup>a</sup> *Mundi subterranei lib. 2. c. 12. f. m. 76.*

<sup>b</sup> *lib. 2. c. 91. p. m. 70.*

<sup>d</sup> *lib. 2. c. 87. 88.*

<sup>e</sup> *Philos. Transact. Num. 332. p. 354. & seqq.*

<sup>f</sup> *in Discurs. prælim. Prodromi Principiorum rerum naturalium p. * *.*

[p. 463]

### Das XI. Capitel. Von dem Nutzen der lebendigen Creaturen. — Chapter XI. On the Benefit of Living Creatures.

*What is understood by living creatures.*

§ 229. By living creatures we understand plants, animals and men. Here, however, we ask nothing more than to what end God created plants, animals and men, and how one benefits the other; on the other hand, we do not concern ourselves in this place with the parts of the members which these creatures have, since we shall shortly<sup>a</sup> speak in particular of the benefit of those parts, and shall at the same time show how through all of them together the principal purpose is attained which God had with each kind of them.

<sup>a</sup> This has been done in the third part of the Physics, which has appeared under the title of the vernünfftigen Gedancken von dem Gebrauche der Theile in den Thieren und Pflantzen.

*Plants serve men and cattle for food.*

*What difference occurs here.*

*Remark on vermin.*

*Why this knowledge affords us delight.*

§ 230. Experience teaches us that men and animals are nourished by what grows out of the earth. Now since neither can live without nourishment, inasmuch as they *transpire* without ceasing (§ 422 *Phys.*) and this [p. 464] loss must be made good by nourishment, the plants of the ground are the only means by which men and animals can be preserved (§ 912 *Met.*). It is true that some animals are nourished by animals, and man too is himself nourished by them; but if nothing grew out of the earth, there would also be no animals at hand on which others could be nourished. And accordingly one may well say that God causes the plants to come forth out of the earth chiefly so that men and cattle may have their nourishment. Plants have roots, leaves, blossoms or flowers, and fruits and seeds. Some animals are nourished by the leaves and soft stalks, others by roots, others by blossoms and flowers, others again by fruits and seeds. We find among plants a great difference, which extends through all the parts we have here enumerated, and experience shows that one animal chooses this for its nourishment, another, however, that. And in this way it comes about that neither do all animals stay in all places, nor does one encroach upon another in its nourishment. We find this to be so even with caterpillars and other vermin; hence those who have written of this kind of [p. 465] animals, if they wish to note everything exactly, have also specified the flowers and herbs on which they find their nourishment. And it is therefore remarkable that vermin too have their fixed times in which they live, namely each kind comes at the time when the earth brings forth its nourishment. Now since we know that nothing in the world happens by chance, but that everything has been considered by God from eternity, whether it shall come to pass or not (§ 999 *Met.*), we can regard all this as things that have been so ordered by God's wisdom (§ 14). And whoever looks upon all this with such eyes that he at the same time beholds God's wisdom therein, and does not overlook the goodness, either, which he shows to every creature (§ 21), finds, in this sight of the divine perfections, a singular delight in it as well (§ 404 *Met.*). And this delight makes the knowledge of nature dear to us (§ 450 *Met.*).

*What grows out of the earth serves man for recreation.*

*First cause of the delight.*

*Second cause.*

*What art contributes to it in this respect.*

*Why this holds.*

*Third cause of the delight.*

§ 231. Inasmuch as men and animals find their nourishment in what grows out of the earth (§ 225), the necessities of life are thereby provided for, since they need nourishment for the preservation of their life. But with man we have yet another benefit: by it the senses [p. 466] are gratified, that is, there are produced in his organs of sense such sensations as he draws a delight from. The eye is regaled by the sight of the forests, the fields and meadows, especially when, besides the green, other colours are present from the multitude of flowers, which make a difference. One need not doubt that men take delight in this: for on this account they go walking out into the green, and count these walks among their delights. The figure, size and colour of the fruits afford no less pleasure to the eye. And since in spring the blossoms, after this the leaves, and finally in summer until the autumn the fruits are to be seen on the trees, there is a pleasant alternation, such as is necessary in a transient pleasure, because otherwise one soon grows weary of it (§ 470 *Mor.*). To this alternation it also belongs that not everything blossoms at the same time, and accordingly every season of the year has its flowers, as well in the gardens as in the meadows. This pleasure of the eyes is increased by art in the gardens, where one brings about by diligence what nature of itself does not afford. In the gardens one raises plants, all kinds of flowers and fruits, which either [p. 467] nature does not produce at all in our countries, or of which very few grow in the same place, or which would also not be preserved of such quality without our diligence. One plants them in an order which we can survey, such as does not occur by nature, and which for that very reason is pleasing to us: to which last the art of gardening contributes not a little, which is distinct from gardening, the latter showing itself in diligence in the due tending of the plants. But that in well-laid- out gardens one can have a pleasant pasture for the eyes, which one does not find in places where everything grows by nature, and which accordingly is all the more serviceable for an alternation, one can not only know by experience but also prove by grounds. We find in gardens plants, flowers and fruits which we do not meet with in other places, and so art here increases their variety, whereby in particular an alternation is maintained, partly in the gardens themselves, partly also when one comes into the pleasure-gardens from places where things grow of themselves, or where that is produced which we use for our necessities. In gardens one sees to it that at all times there is always something else [p. 468] at hand, and, when a flower for example passes away, another comes again in its place: which constant alternation nature of itself does not show in every place. In gardens one takes pains that plants, flowers and fruits should in everything have an advantage over what nature of itself affords in other places, whereby a change is produced. But the ear, too, has something to enjoy at times, namely the pleasant rustling of the leaves when the trees are moved by a not too violent wind. And finally we can also find something for the touch, namely the pleasant shade which the trees give on hot summer days: to which in particular art contributes its part with the green summer-arbours in the pleasure-gardens.

*Herbs have their benefit in medicine.*

*Where they are found described.*

*How one has been able to discover them.*

*Whether it was learned from the animals.*

*How men could have hit upon it by chance, and found it out by experiment.*

*At what time this happened.*

*An objection is answered.*

*How things are brought by degrees to a great perfec-*

*tion without great understanding.*

§ 232. The herbs which grow of themselves out of the earth in forests, in the meadows and on the mountains, or also in other places, likewise the foreign ones which are raised in gardens, together with the fruits of the trees, have particular forces for driving away diseases, in such a way that one can say there is not a single disease which can befall men or cattle for which a herb has not also grown. And this benefit has been [p. 469] known from the most ancient times, and medicines were for a long time prepared solely and alone from herbs, until the chemical ones came into use, which are prepared from minerals and metals (§ 220, 221). Those who have written herbals have described their benefit in this respect at length, among which *Jacobus Theodorus Tabernaemontanus*'s *Complete Herbal* is well known, which this Electoral Palatine *physician* compiled with great diligence over a full 36 years. Since everything that is praised of the benefit of herbs in this respect is taken from experience, and this knowledge is nevertheless so manifold and extensive, one not unjustly wonders how one has been able to discover the virtues of so very many herbs. It cannot be denied that the wild animals, just as, without any instruction, among so great a multitude of plants and fruits as come before them, they nevertheless know how to pick out that which serves them for their nourishment, so also recognise among the herbs that which serves them in their disease. Thus it is said of the stag that it drives shot arrows out of its body by white dittany, heals itself inwardly in the body with brown wild marjoram, uses hart's-orache [p. 470] in order to become strong and fat, and that game in general soothes the pain of its shot wounds with orchis. The hind strengthens her young by the smell of ant-hills, with water-cress, moss and all kinds of bark from the trees; and when a stag lets blood through its nose, it sometimes staunches the blood with moss from ash-trees or red beeches; indeed, in general it purifies its blood in spring by means of herbs. Many more observations of this kind concerning other animals are found in those who have described their history. And this has brought some to the thought that men learned the benefits of herbs from the animals. Now although it may readily be granted that men may have taken occasion from this to use herbs in diseases, and also to learn the virtues of some of them, it is nevertheless certain that they can have obtained by this means only the smallest part of their knowledge of the benefit of herbs; not only because the number of herbs, all of whose virtues are nevertheless commonly praised, is exceedingly great, and very few of them are used by animals, but also because most of the ailments against which herbs are commonly used are not met with in animals. It is [p. 471] therefore credible that men came to know the benefit of a herb partly by chance, as indeed still happens even today; partly found out various things by experiment, since they used some things in vain and afterwards kept to that which helped; partly, for want of a herb whose benefit was known, took another which they had and which resembled it either in smell or in taste or else in its outward shape; and partly found out the virtues of some herbs by reflection. For when we consider the inventions that are met with in human life alone, such as, for example, agriculture, it does not seem incredible, what **Stevin**<sup>a</sup> maintains, that there was a time in the world when men had much understanding, a time older than all those of which the historians give us an account, since astronomy in particular furnishes excellent grounds for this. And perhaps much of what is known of the virtues of herbs is also to be derived from those same times. Let no one object, as though I made **Stevin's** age of wisdom into an unintelligible cause [p. 472] and invented something where one was not in a position to state the cause, so as thereby to conceal one's ignorance, as was formerly done in the Scholastic philosophy: for in this age of wisdom we admit nothing but what people endowed with understanding, wit and diligence can still find out thereby in our own days, in such a way as I have shown, in my Thoughts on God, the World and the Soul of Man, that man can pass from one thought to another; and it is all the same to me whether one would draw the ages of wisdom together into a narrow compass, or so distribute them over many ages that now and then some were driven by necessity to use their natural understanding and wit, as that by which a very great deal in the world has been found out by degrees which would otherwise have required a very great understanding and wit, and much knowledge besides, if, without necessity having given occasion for it by degrees to various persons, it had had to be found out by one man all at once through reflection. Military architecture and the art of fireworks are new arts, which were not known before, and whose origin and progress are not hidden from us. Whoever, then, [p. 473] wishes to know how an invention which is at first in a poor state can by degrees, through various persons, by the correction of many false steps which experience has taught and to which necessity has driven them, at length be brought into such a state that it gains the appearance of having been found out by the greatest understanding, since it is also possible that an ingenious mind may accomplish the like: he need only consider the origin and progress of the art of fireworks and of military architecture, from the writings which have appeared on these subjects by degrees and are not merely taken wholly from those that went before, according to the grounds which I have shown concerning the sequence of thoughts in my Metaphysics, where I have described the faculty of knowing in detail. Besides, this makes clear what I have said, why even in ancient times necessity was called the best teacher.

<sup>a</sup> *Geogr. lib. 1. def. 6. Oper. f. 106.*

*Benefit of wood: 1. in maintaining fire and heat.*

*An objection is answered.*

*2. in household goods.*

*3. in architecture.*

*in navigation.*

*in mills.*

*in machines.*

*in works of art.*

§ 233. Trees, on account of their wood, have yet a particular benefit beyond the other plants of the earth. We use it, both in human life and in the arts, to make and maintain fire. Now since we have found that fire, and the heat accompanying it, are in human life [p. 474] so greatly needed for preserving the vital heat of men (§ 208) and of plants (§ 209), for promoting quick and early growth (§ 210), in the preparation of food and drink and for other benefits in the household economy (§ 212), and in manifold operations in the arts which cannot be performed without fire (§ 213); so we also recognise from all this the benefit of wood, without which that fire is neither made nor maintained. It is indeed true that wood is not the only means of making and maintaining fire, since we also use peat and pit- coal for this purpose, of which the former is customary chiefly in Holland, the latter in London, and in part also among us in salt-boiling; yet this does not abolish the benefit of wood: for that a thing has this or that benefit cannot become untrue because other things exist which afford the very same benefit. To this is further added that wood is used more than peat and pit-coal, and that one does not think of these where one can have wood enough. There is also a difference between the fire which is maintained by wood and the other which is maintained by pit-coal, [p. 475] and it therefore still remains to examine the preference of the one over the other; which, however, on account of the all too manifold differences where fire is used, is not a matter that can be carried out here, where we always content ourselves with what is general. Besides this, many household goods in the household economy are made of wood, and one need only look about everywhere in the house to be more than sufficiently convinced of it. And how many craftsmen do we not have who work solely in wood and make from it such things as we need in human life for necessities and convenience? Wood is used in architecture, both in the buildings themselves and also for the scaffolding, even with those buildings which are raised of stone. Military architecture likewise cannot do without wood, as one can see at once in a fortress if one merely walks over the bridges and through the gates. Whoever will inspect the works at a siege, and how they are prepared, will find the benefit of wood here too: not to mention that it is also used for many of the tools which one needs partly in the artillery, partly otherwise in [p. 476] the siege, and it is required for stocking the muskets, also for the carriages of the cannon and mortars, and whatever more there is of this kind. Finally, a great benefit further shows itself in navigation, since the principal material for ships is wood, both for the small ones on rivers and standing waters and for the large ones at sea. Now the benefit which is to be expected from navigation, especially that at sea, must be ascribed to wood all the more, the less any other material exists which one could use for ships instead of wood. There is yet another such benefit of wood, which aims in some measure at the same end as navigation. Namely, all mill-works, by whatever force they may be driven, and all kinds of other *machines* are for the most part made of wood. These, however, serve to make much work, which would otherwise be hard to perform with human hands for the necessities and convenience of life, easy to do: whereby what men need for their nourishment and clothing, and otherwise for their convenience, is procured for them at a lower price than they would otherwise have it. And precisely such an [p. 477] advantage is afforded by navigation, whereby not only are foreign wares brought into a country which one would otherwise not have there, but these are also brought at less cost to places to which it would cost much to carry them by wagon: for which latter reason trade is carried on by sea, even though one could also reach the same places by land. Besides this, *machines* serve the advancement of manufactures, because by means of them these can be produced in greater quantity and at less cost, as has long since been remarked by others in many ways; and for this very reason Mr Leupold, *Commercial* Councillor at Leipzig, as a skilful *mechanic*, makes it his business to bring mechanics to greater advancement, since the *Theatra Machinarum*, or theatres of *machines*, available hitherto are not yet so arranged that anything reliable and complete, let alone anything thorough, can be learnt from them. By manufactures, however, trade is promoted, which is so necessary in a commonwealth, so that money comes into the country, and in particular also from one hand into another, and from one country into another (§ 488, 489 *Polit.*). And in this [p. 478] navigation, especially the great navigation by sea, likewise agrees with it, since its benefit in trade is known from daily experience. But many things are also made from wood which serve merely for delight: to these belong sculptors' work, such as statues carved from wood, the frames of pictures made from it, the artful works of turners, which one has merely in order to delight in their art, such as the skilful monk in France *Plumier*<sup>a</sup>, who has described the art of turning at length, mentions, and other such things more.

<sup>a</sup> *L' Art de tourner en perfection part. 9. f. 167. & seqq.*

*That plants have still more benefit besides that described.*

§ 234. Although the benefit described hitherto, which we men in particular have from that which grows out of the earth, is very manifold and great (§ 230 ff.), we shall nevertheless meet with much more if we look about us in the workshops of the craftsmen. In dyeing we meet with herbs, barks, rasped wood, berries &c., with which one dyes; and whoever wishes to comprehend the extent of this benefit need only look into what is extant on the art of dyeing in [p. 479] books, of which he will meet with several both in our German language and in French. In the same way one uses the bark of oaks and of birches to prepare the leather which we use for shoes.

*Whether animals are to serve one another also as nourishment.*

*Whether man may eat the flesh of animals.*

*An objection is answered.*

*Why one man may not devour another.*

*When it is permitted to eat human flesh.*

*Why homicide is forbidden.*

*A means of multiplying the kinds of animals.*

§ 235. Animals, of whatever kind they may be, from the largest four-footed ones down to the smallest vermin, have no understanding and reason, no will and no freedom (§ 892 *Met.*), and are therefore incapable of knowing God (§ 1076 *Met.*). Therefore God also cannot attain through them his principal purpose for which he made the world (§ 8), and accordingly he cannot have placed them in the world to the end that they should know his perfection from it. Now since God and nature, the latter of which acts according to his will and his purposes, do nothing in idleness (§ 1049 *Met.*), he must also have had a purpose in letting nature bring forth such animals, especially since, as a supremely rational being (§ 974 *Met.*) and as a wise God (§ 1048 *Met.*), he cannot act without purposes (§ 911, 914 *Met.*). Accordingly God must have intended the benefit which the animals have in nature [p. 480] (§ 1029 *Met.*). Therefore, since we find that one animal feeds on another, indeed that some exist which feed solely on animals and also cannot tolerate any other food, we have no cause to doubt that God made the animals also to the end that one should become the nourishment of another. In like manner it is evident that man too may use the flesh of animals for his nourishment, which is conducive to his health, this being what we have to look to in all food (§ 450 *Mor.*). The objections which some make on the ground that animals are God's work, and that no one else is at liberty to ruin the work of a craftsman, are not to the point here. For when man feeds on animals, he does not ruin the work of God, but uses it for that for which it was made by God. If the work of a craftsman cannot be used otherwise than by being destroyed through its use, then it is also destroyed by him who uses it, and one does not ask whether it is very artfully made or not. For example, a bomb is the work of a craftsman, and of the same kind are all fire-balls, indeed all display-scaffolds [p. 481] at fireworks. One cannot use the bomb and fire- balls for that for which they were made without destroying them, nor attain the end intended by a display-scaffold at a firework without its perishing. But precisely for this reason no one finds anything to object to in one's destroying the former or letting the latter perish; indeed the craftsman himself finds nothing for which he could complain on that account; he would rather complain if it did not happen, since one would then not be willing to use his work for that for which he made it. On the other hand, however, one easily comprehends that, although it is not wrong when one animal devours another, and man himself feeds on their flesh, it can nevertheless by no means be approved if one man wished to devour another, since he was created by God for another end, as we shall learn in more detail shortly hereafter (§ 241). Meanwhile, if someone felt no disgust at human flesh and, in necessity, where he could otherwise have no other food, satisfied his hunger with men slain in war, one could reprove this as little as when he satisfies his hunger with the flesh of animals, since a dead [p. 482] man, just like cattle, is not in a state in which God can attain his principal purpose through him. But if one were to kill men in order to be able to satisfy oneself with their flesh, one would act against the law of nature, which forbids taking anyone's life precisely because a rational man is in a state in which God can attain through him the principal purpose for which he made this world (§ 8). And Scripture also adduces this reason when it forbids homicide on the ground that God made man in his own image<sup>a</sup>, that is, because he is endowed with understanding and reason, and God can therefore attain his principal purpose through him. Now since we find that there are particular kinds of animals which can be nourished by nothing other than other animals, as, for example, the swallow feeds solely on flying vermin, this is a means by which God multiplies the kinds of animals in the world, namely that he lets one serve another as food. And in this way he gives matter more figures and forms than it would otherwise assume: which contributes [p. 483] not a little to his principal purpose, which he had in the world (§ 13, 19, 20, 21).

<sup>a</sup> *Gen. IX. 6.*

*Why there is so great a multitude of all sorts of kinds of animals.*

*First cause.*

*Second.*

*Third.*

*Fourth.*

§ 236. And from this very fact it is evident why God has brought forth so great a multitude of kinds of animals. For the earth was to be a theatre in which one would have occasion to think of God's perfection (§ 8). Therefore, since the great difference which is found among the various kinds of animals serves to let us know from it God's infinite understanding (§ 13), his immeasurable power (§ 19), the loftiness of his reason (§ 20) and the greatness of his goodness (§ 21), together with his unsearchable wisdom (§ 14), God attains his purpose all the more perfectly the more kinds of animals he has brought onto the surface of the earth. And since we know from the use of the parts in animals that each, by its essence, leads us in a particular way to the knowledge of the divine perfections, we have as many proofs of them as we meet with kinds of animals, and of creatures in general, in the world, and can accordingly assure ourselves of the divine perfections in many ways. This serves not only to make us, concerning them, [p. 484] all the more certain, after we have been convinced of them by so many proofs, but also to keep us from growing weary of delighting in God, since in the knowledge of them something variable is to be met with each time, namely in the manner in which we arrive at it, and we always find occasion to think of them, since the knowledge of him is connected with the beholding of the creature, and therefore occurs to us as soon as we catch sight of the latter (§ 238 *Met.*). Now since this is a great means to godliness (§ 671 *Mor.*), and consequently also to all virtue (§ 673 *Mor.*), one understands how God has multiplied the number of the various kinds of animals for this end too, that he might make godliness, and through it virtue, easier for us: which perhaps has not been sufficiently recognised up to now, and hence this so excellent means has also not been used as it ought to have been.

*Animals serve man for clothing.*

*What benefit the spider could provide.*

§ 237. From animals we also obtain most of what we need for clothing, although in fact there are only a few kinds of animals which are of the greatest benefit in this respect. The wool of sheep is one of the foremost materials that we use for clothing, since everyone knows that [p. 485] not only cloth and all kinds of stuffs, but also hats and stockings are made from it. The skins of sheep and calves, likewise the hides of cattle and horses, and also of some other animals, are worked in various ways and applied to all kinds of clothing. Many shaggy skins of all kinds of wild animals are used against the cold. All this is known among us from common experience, and when the history and science of the craftsmen has been established (§ 300 *Polit.*), all this will become still more evident. Silkworms are a lowly little creature, in appearance nothing other than a kind of caterpillar, and notwithstanding this they spin so much silk that men can make so many stuffs and other wares from it.
