In France, too, reliable experiments have been made at the Academy of Sciences showing that common spiders spin just as good a silk as the silkworms: but it has been found that silkworms are far better suited to this than spiders, since they spin more silk than these, and one therefore does not need so great a multitude of [p. 486] workers, and moreover they are easier to feed, and in particular also easier to keep, because they do not devour one another like the spiders, which for that reason dare not remain together, except at the time when the male wishes to propagate its kind with the female<sup>a</sup>. The feathers of animals and some of their hair are also sometimes used; hair, however, less than wool, skins and silk. I say deliberately that most clothing comes from the animals: for much linen cloth is also made from flax, and much is manufactured from cotton, which belongs to the benefit of plants. All this must again be learned more fully from the history and science of the crafts and arts.

<sup>a</sup> *Memoires d' l'Acad. Roy. des Scienc. A. 1710. p. m. 504.*

*How animals serve man in other ways besides.*

§ 238. The bones of animals are also used in the arts, and in particular all kinds of things are turned from them. The horn of cattle has the very same benefit, and is the most usual material that we use for combs. Bristles are taken for all kinds of brushes, feathers for [p. 487] beds. From the shells of mussels one burns lime, which is used in building, and whatever else of this kind one comes upon when one searches through the workshops of craftsmen and artists, or also looks through the wares with which trade is carried on. In particular, animals also have their manifold benefit in medicine, since various things from them find their use in all kinds of ailments: concerning which one must seek information in the writings of those who describe the medicines that can be used against the different kinds of diseases. All these are, to be sure, common things which everyone can see for himself: yet one needs to think of them where one wishes to set forth the benefit that we have from animals, and therefore it is not to be regarded as superfluous that we are reminded of it in the present place. I could also mention here that the fields have their manure from the animals: for which purpose it is customary in husbandry to combine cattle-breeding with agriculture. Finally, it must not be forgotten here that man uses animals in manifold ways to lighten his tasks, which would otherwise [p. 488] be burdensome to him, and takes his pleasure with them. An example is given by horses, which are used for riding, for driving, for ploughing and for turning mills. In like manner dogs are used to guard the house, for hunting, or indeed also for pleasure, especially when on account of their docility they have been trained to all kinds of tricks. Birds delight the ear with their agreeable singing, and wild animals, when they are baited, the eye, not to mention what other pleasure men, each according to his inclination, take with animals.

*Why animals and men are of two sexes.*

*Proof of God's provident care.*

§ 239. We find that all animals, from the greatest down to the smallest, propagate their kind by copulation: for if we look about us in nature and pay diligent attention to everything, we shall become aware of this even in the meanest vermin. Although transformations take place among them, one nevertheless sees that the transformed creatures come together again and beget such little animals as they themselves were before, which by a like transformation become their like. From this one sees sufficiently that copulation is the means by which every animal propagates its kind. And accordingly one also recognises that God, to [p. 489] the end that none of them might perish so long as the world stood, brought forth two sexes of every kind. It was, to be sure, necessary for this that males and females should always come intermixed: for if far too many came of one sex and very few of the other, it could at length happen that the kind died out altogether. Experience also testifies that both come in almost equal number: for where two animals pair together and tend their young with each other, there every female, when she has need, finds her male; but where one male can serve many females at once, there many are also always found to one. And here one has a manifest proof of the wise provident care of God (§ 1035 *Met.*).

*What harm animals do to man.*

*1. Harm from vermin.*

*2. From large animals.*

§ 240. Although among animals vermin are the very meanest, they can nevertheless do man the greatest harm: for since they feed on those leaves, blossoms and fruits, they also eat the trees bare, so that they bear no fruit, nay they sometimes also spoil the fruits in the field and consume the corn heaped up in the granary. Since vermin are very small and gathered together in great multitude, and are also often shut up within the [p. 490] fruits, so that one does not perceive them until they eat their way through, they cannot be driven away so easily as the large wild animals when these do harm. And hence they are fit to execute God's punishment when he finds it advisable (§ 103). The harm which men can suffer from large animals consists mostly in this, that these either injure them in their bodies, or even take their lives, or else lay waste their fruits of field and garden and destroy their tame cattle: to which the experience of country people bears witness who live in such regions where there are wild animals, and who at times have unruly cattle in their yards and in their stalls. But if one gathers together all the harm that we have from animals and weighs it against the benefit that we enjoy from them, we shall clearly see that the latter far outweighs the former, and hence that in this respect too the good outweighs the evil.

*To what end God made man.*

*That man is obliged to the sciences;*

*likewise to arts and crafts.*

§ 241. Among all the living creatures that we meet with on the surface of the earth, man alone is the one who can know God from himself and from the other creatures (§ 1076 *Met.*). Therefore he is also the only visible creature on the surface of the earth [p. 491] through which God can attain his principal purpose for which he made the world. And accordingly there is no doubt that God created man principally to the end that he should know him and honour him as a God, since his honour cannot be separated from a convincing knowledge of God (§ 658 *Mor.*). Now since this knowledge is to be attained through the knowledge of the creature (§ 8), man is also made to the end that he should know nature, pay attention to what goes on in it, and investigate its causes. Now we know that the knowledge of nature cannot subsist without other sciences, as is sufficiently evident if one reads through with consideration what I have written on the effects of nature and have also let flow into the present writing: therefore man is also made to the end that he should pursue other sciences. Whoever has but looked about in the Astronomy and in the three parts of the Experiments published by me will readily comprehend that without arts and crafts the sciences cannot be brought to any advancement. Therefore man is also made to the end that he should pursue arts and crafts: which latter is also evident from this, that only in such [p. 492] a way can he first turn the creature to his benefit (§ 230 ff.). But how far each person is obliged to apply himself to sciences, arts and crafts I have already set out in another place (§ 255, 368 *Mor.*).

*How far everything is for the sake of man; but man is made to please God.*

§ 242. If we assume that all the planets are inhabited like our earth (§ 149 *Phys.*), and that the fixed stars are nothing but suns which have their planets about them (§ 153 *Phys.*), then the suns are for the sake of the earths. Everything that is on the surface of the earth serves man for manifold benefit; indeed, whatever he merely beholds of the heavenly bodies from afar he can apply to some benefit, as is evident from the whole treatment of the present writing. And to this extent one may say that everything is for the sake of men. On the other hand, since man is the only creature through which God can attain the principal purpose that he had with the world, namely that he be known and venerated as a God (§ 242), it is clear from this that God made him for his own sake.

THE END.

[p. 493]
